### [1] [The Coin Flip Judge? Reliability and Bias in LLM-as-a-Judge Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13685)
*Abel Yagubyan*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: LLM-as-a-Judge evaluations suffer from substantial run-to-run unreliability: pairwise preferences flip 13.6% on average (up to 56%), cross-judge agreement is only 76%, and prompt-template changes alter majority outcomes 25% of the time. The authors recommend multi-trial aggregation, position randomization, and explicit uncertainty reporting as standard practice.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM-as-a-Judge is widely deployed for ranking model outputs, training reward models, and populating leaderboards, yet its run-to-run reliability remains poorly characterized. The authors aim to quantify the degree of within-judge instability, cross-judge disagreement, and sensitivity to experimental parameters (temperature, prompt templates, decoding strategy) to assess fitness for high-stakes evaluation.

Method: The authors conduct repeated identical evaluations across 29 tasks (10 categories) using two OpenAI judge models (GPT-4o-mini and GPT-4.1-mini), with 50 pairwise trials and 50 pointwise trials per question. They supplement this with temperature ablations, prompt-sensitivity tests (semantically equivalent templates), deterministic decoding comparisons, and a reliability curve analysis to determine how many repeated trials are needed for a stable majority verdict.

Result: Pairwise preferences flip 13.6% on average across judges, with 28% of questions exceeding 20% flip rate and one reaching 56%. GPT-4o-mini shows a significant first-position bias (72% A-majority, p=0.024). Mean pointwise score gaps are small (0.19–0.36 on a 10-point scale) and not statistically significant in aggregate, revealing a pairwise–pointwise gap. Cross-judge agreement is only 76% (κ=0.51). Semantically equivalent prompt templates change majority outcomes in 25% of cases. Deterministic decoding reduces but does not eliminate inconsistency. Reliability curve analysis shows 11 repeated trials are needed on average (15 for high-variance questions) to recover the 50-trial reference verdict with 95% probability.

Conclusion: Single-trial LLM judging is too noisy for high-stakes evaluation. Multi-trial aggregation, position randomization, and explicit uncertainty reporting should become standard practice. Because both judges come from a single provider (OpenAI), cross-provider replication is a critical next step.

Abstract: LLM-as-a-Judge is now widely used to rank model outputs, train reward models, and populate public leaderboards, but its run-to-run reliability remains under-characterized. We study repeated identical evaluations on 29 tasks spanning 10 categories using two OpenAI judge models (GPT-4o-mini and GPT-4.1-mini), with 50 pairwise trials and 50 pointwise trials per question, supplemented by temperature and prompt-sensitivity ablations. Across judges, pairwise preferences flip on average 13.6% of the time, with 28% of questions exceeding a 20% flip rate and one question reaching 56%. GPT-4o-mini also exhibits a significant first-position bias (72% A-majority, p = 0.024). At the same time, mean pointwise score gaps are small (0.19--0.36 on a 10-point scale) and not statistically significant in aggregate, producing a pairwise--pointwise gap: judges frequently choose a winner even when their own scalar scores provide little evidence of a meaningful quality difference. Beyond within-judge instability, cross-judge agreement is only 76% ($κ= 0.51$), semantically equivalent prompt templates change majority outcomes in 25% of tested cases, and deterministic decoding reduces but does not eliminate inconsistency. A reliability curve analysis shows that, in our dataset, 11 repeated trials are needed for a majority vote to recover the 50-trial reference verdict with 95% probability on average, rising to 15 for high-variance questions. These findings suggest that single-trial LLM judging is often too noisy for high-stakes evaluation, and that multi-trial aggregation, position randomization, and explicit uncertainty reporting should be standard practice. Because both judges are from a single provider, cross-provider replication remains an important next step.

</details>


### [2] [Benchmarking Web Agent Safety under E-commerce Deceptive Interfaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13686)
*Zijing Shi,Meng Fang,Ling Chen*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: WebDecept is a plugin framework for injecting deceptive interface patterns into web environments, revealing that current multimodal web agents are highly susceptible to manipulation and that prompt-based safeguards are insufficient.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As autonomous web agents are increasingly deployed for real-world tasks, their vulnerability to deceptive interfaces poses a critical safety risk. There is a need for controlled, reproducible evaluation frameworks to systematically study this vulnerability.

Method: The authors introduce WebDecept, a lightweight, configurable plugin framework that injects seven deceptive interface patterns (e.g., targeted ads, domain redirection, shopping manipulation) into web frontends during task execution. Multiple multimodal web agents are then evaluated under these controlled conditions.

Result: Current web agents are highly susceptible to multiple classes of deceptive interfaces. Prompt-based constraints often fail to mitigate these failures. The design choices of deceptive patterns significantly influence manipulation success rates.

Conclusion: Significant safety challenges remain for web agent deployment, particularly around robustness to deceptive interfaces. These must be addressed before scaling web agents toward real-world use.

Abstract: As autonomous web agents are increasingly deployed to perform real-world tasks, ensuring their safety has become a critical concern. In this work, we study web agent behavior under realistic deceptive interfaces in the e-commerce domain. We introduce WebDecept, a lightweight and configurable plugin framework that enables controlled injection of deceptive interface patterns into existing web environments. Using WebDecept, we instantiate seven deceptive patterns commonly observed on the open web, including targeted advertisements, domain redirection, and shopping manipulation. By injecting these patterns into the frontend during task execution, we perform controlled evaluation of multiple multimodal web agents. Our results show that current web agents are highly susceptible to multiple classes of deceptive interfaces, and that prompt-based constraints are often insufficient to mitigate these failures. We further analyze how the design choices of deceptive patterns influence the success of such manipulations. These findings highlight safety challenges that should be addressed as web agents are scaled toward real-world deployment.

</details>


### [3] [Which Models Perform Better in Inheritance Reasoning?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13751)
*Mohammed Amine Mouhoub,Chahinez Bouchekif*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Team PSL evaluated commercial and open-source LLMs on Arabic Islamic inheritance reasoning (QIAS 2026 Shared Task), finding commercial models (especially Gemini 2.5 Flash, MRE=0.989) significantly more reliable than open-source ones in multi-step legal reasoning and fractional computation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To systematically compare commercial vs. open-source LLMs on a structured legal reasoning task requiring legal interpretation, multi-step deduction, and precise numerical computation, using minimal task-specific adaptation.

Method: Unified prompting strategy applied across both commercial and open-source models on the QIAS 2026 Shared Task dataset, evaluating performance on heir identification, exclusion rule application, reasoning consistency, and fractional share computation.

Result: Commercial models consistently outperformed open-source models in identifying eligible heirs, applying exclusion rules, and maintaining reasoning consistency. Open-source models showed instability, particularly with dependent legal decisions and fractional share adjustments. Best performance: Gemini 2.5 Flash with MRE of 0.989.

Conclusion: A clear reliability gap exists between commercial and open-source LLMs for structured Islamic inheritance reasoning; commercial models are currently more trustworthy for this domain without extensive task-specific fine-tuning.

Abstract: This paper presents the participation of team PSL in the QIAS 2026 Shared Task on Arabic Islamic inheritance reasoning. The task evaluates the ability of large language models to solve inheritance cases that require legal interpretation, multi-step reasoning, and precise numerical computation. We compare \textit{commercial} and \textit{open-source} models under a unified prompting strategy to assess their effectiveness in structured legal reasoning with minimal task-specific adaptation. \\ Our results show a clear gap in reliability between the two model families. Commercial models demonstrate stronger performance in identifying eligible heirs, applying exclusion rules, and maintaining consistency across reasoning steps. In contrast, open-source models exhibit greater instability, particularly in cases involving dependent legal decisions and fractional share adjustments. The best performance is achieved by \textit{Gemini 2.5 Flash}, with an MRE of $0.989$.

</details>


### [4] [QIAS 2026: Overview of the Shared Task on Islamic Inheritance Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13756)
*Abdessalam Bouchekif,Somaya Eltanbouly,Samer Rashwani,Shahd Gaben,Mutaz Al-Khatib,Heba Sbahi,Emad Mohamed,Mohammed Ghaly*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The QIAS 2026 shared task evaluated LLMs on end-to-end Islamic inheritance reasoning using 12,500 Arabic cases from the MAWARITH benchmark. Sixteen teams employed prompting, RAG, and fine-tuning, with results showing Islamic inheritance remains highly challenging, particularly for precise legal interpretation and structured numerical reasoning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional QA benchmarks fail to capture the complexity of domain-specific legal-religious reasoning. The shared task was motivated by the need to assess whether LLMs can perform the full pipeline of Islamic inheritance calculation — from identifying eligible heirs to assigning precise shares — a task requiring both legal interpretation skills and structured numerical reasoning.

Method: The shared task was built around three core components: (1) the MAWARITH benchmark containing 12,500 annotated Arabic inheritance cases with intermediate reasoning steps and final answers; (2) the MIR-E evaluation metric, which measures performance across multiple stages of the inheritance reasoning process rather than only final-answer accuracy; and (3) a multi-team experimental design where 16 participating teams explored prompting-based methods, retrieval-augmented generation (RAG), and fine-tuning strategies for tackling the problem.

Result: Sixteen teams participated and investigated diverse approaches. The results demonstrated that Islamic inheritance remains a highly challenging benchmark for current language models. The greatest difficulties emerged in stages requiring precise legal interpretation (e.g., identifying eligible heirs and determining exclusion rules) and structured numerical reasoning (e.g., computing fractional shares and resolving cases like 'Awl and Radd).

Conclusion: Islamic inheritance reasoning represents a formidable open challenge for LLMs. The gap between current system performance and the task demands highlights the need for further advances in combining legal-domain knowledge with structured multi-step reasoning. The MAWARITH benchmark and MIR-E metric provide a robust foundation for continued research in this area.

Abstract: This paper presents a comprehensive overview of the QIAS 2026 shared task, organized as part of the OSACT7 Workshop and co-located with LREC 2026. The shared task was designed to evaluate the ability of large language models to perform complex reasoning in the religious and legal domain of Islamic inheritance. Unlike conventional question-answering benchmarks, QIAS 2026 focuses on end-to-end reasoning from natural language cases, requiring systems to perform the full inheritance calculation process, from identifying the eligible heirs to assigning the correct share to each beneficiary. To support this evaluation, the task was based on the MAWARITH benchmark, a dataset of $12{,}500$ Arabic inheritance cases annotated with intermediate reasoning steps and final answers. System submissions were evaluated using MIR-E, a multi-step metric that measures performance across the main stages of inheritance reasoning. A total of $16$ teams participated in the shared task, investigating a range of approaches, including prompting-based methods, retrieval-augmented generation, and fine-tuning strategies. The results show that Islamic inheritance remains a highly challenging benchmark for current language models, especially in stages that require precise legal interpretation and structured numerical reasoning. This overview summarizes the task design, dataset, evaluation framework, participating systems, and main results.

</details>


### [5] [The Culture Funnel: You Can't Align What isn't in the Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13808)
*Ananya Sahu,Mehrnaz Mofakhami,Daniel D'Souza,Thomas Euyang,Julia Kreutzer,Marzieh Fadaee*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This paper identifies a "cultural data funnel" in modern LLM pipelines, where explicit cultural signals decline sharply during post-training phases. Using a multidimensional tagging framework across 5.6M samples, the authors demonstrate that geographic diversity in cultural knowledge diminishes despite multilinguality, and that improving cultural tagging enhances downstream benchmark performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current cultural alignment methods operate at inference time under the flawed assumption that models already contain sufficient cultural knowledge. The authors challenge this assumption by investigating how cultural representation evolves across the full LLM training pipeline — pretraining, fine-tuning, alignment, and reasoning — hypothesizing that post-training stages systematically erode cultural signals.

Method: The authors develop a multidimensional tagging framework to annotate cultural signals across four stages of the LLM data pipeline: pretraining, fine-tuning, alignment, and reasoning datasets. Using this framework, they tag 5.6M samples to quantify the prevalence and geographic distribution of cultural content at each stage. They analyze how multilinguality interacts with geographic diversity, and validate their tags by measuring improvements on downstream cultural benchmarks.

Result: Explicit cultural signals drop sharply during post-training (fine-tuning, alignment, reasoning). Post-training data is dominated by geographically concentrated, task-specialized content. While multilinguality improves geographic diversity of cultural knowledge, it does not guarantee balanced cultural representation. The authors' cultural tags demonstrably improve performance on downstream cultural benchmarks.

Conclusion: Advances in cultural alignment require shifting focus from inference-time interventions to the training data pipeline itself. The release of the 5.6M-sample culturally tagged dataset (CultureMarkers) aims to facilitate future research in this direction.

Abstract: Current cultural alignment approaches focus on inference-time interventions, assuming models already contain sufficient cultural knowledge. We argue modern LLM pipelines suffer from a cultural data funnel. Using a multidimensional tagging framework across pretraining, fine-tuning, alignment, and reasoning datasets, we show explicit cultural signals decline sharply during post-training, while geographically concentrated, task-specialized data dominates. Multilinguality enhances geographic diversity of cultural knowledge but does not ensure balanced representation. Our tags improve downstream cultural benchmark performance, demonstrating that advances require shifting focus in training data pipelines. To facilitate future research, we release our culturally tagged dataset with 5.6M samples at https://huggingface.co/datasets/CohereLabs/CultureMarkers.

</details>


### [6] [When Plausible Is Not Realistic: Evaluating Human Mobility in LLM-Based Urban Simulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13835)
*Gustavo H. Santos,Aline Carneiro Viana,Thiago H. Silva*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces a validation framework for evaluating LLM-based urban simulators against real-world mobility data, revealing a significant gap between narrative plausibility and empirical mobility realism across multiple dimensions, and contributes open-source infrastructure for reproducible evaluation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM-based generative agents are increasingly deployed in urban simulators, but it is unclear whether they reproduce empirically realistic human mobility patterns or merely generate plausible-sounding narratives. There is a lack of rigorous validation frameworks to assess mobility realism against ground-truth data.

Method: The authors propose a multi-dimensional validation framework using mobility laws, temporal rhythms, network motifs, semantic activity transitions, and behavioral mobility profiles. They evaluate two simulators — AgentSociety and CitySim — against real-world mobility datasets from Greater Paris and Shanghai. They also contribute scalable LLM-driven infrastructure for map generation, simulation, mobility-metric computation, and traffic simulation.

Result: The simulators capture some high-level semantic activity distributions but fail to reproduce core spatial and temporal constraints: realistic trip-length distributions, origin-destination flows, dwell times, and transition dynamics. Realistic mobility diversity proves unstable under default prompting and may require explicit profile-aware initialization.

Conclusion: There is a substantial gap between narrative plausibility and empirical mobility realism in current LLM-based urban simulators. Rigorous empirical validation is essential, and the contributed open-source infrastructure provides practical tools for building more realistic and reproducible urban simulation systems.

Abstract: LLM-based generative agents are increasingly used in urban simulators, yet it remains unclear whether they reproduce empirically realistic human mobility patterns or merely generate plausible mobility narratives. We introduce a validation framework for evaluating the mobility of generative agents of LLM-based urban simulators against real-world mobility data. For this, we use mobility laws, temporal rhythms, network motifs, semantic activity transitions, and behavioral mobility profiles. Using datasets from the Greater Paris region and Shanghai, we evaluate AgentSociety and CitySim across multiple dimensions of mobility realism. Our analysis reveals a substantial gap between narrative plausibility and empirical mobility realism. Although the simulators capture some high-level semantic activity distributions, they struggle to reproduce core spatial and temporal constraints, including realistic trip-length distributions, origin-destination flows, dwell times, and transition dynamics. We further observe that realistic mobility diversity is unstable across default prompting configurations and may require explicit profile-aware initialization. To support reproducible evaluation, we also contribute scalable and open LLM-driven infrastructure for regional-scale map generation, observability-enhanced simulation, mobility-metric computation, and traffic simulation. Our findings highlight the need for rigorous empirical validation of LLM-based urban simulators and provide practical tools for building more realistic and reproducible urban simulation systems.

</details>


### [7] [Hybrid Classical-Quantum Variational Autoencoder for Neural Topic Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13852)
*Ivan Kankeu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: A hybrid classical-quantum VAE for topic modeling achieves state-of-the-art coherence (Cv=0.71, NPMI=0.20) on AgNews using only 10 qubits, enabled by a modified Gaussian Softmax posterior that decouples latent dimensionality from topic count.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Neural topic models are scalable but unexplored on quantum hardware. The authors aim to demonstrate that hybrid quantum-classical VAEs are viable for topic modeling even under current NISQ-era resource constraints.

Method: A hybrid VAE architecture with parameterized quantum circuits in the inference network and a classical topic-word decoder. A modified Gaussian Softmax posterior decouples latent space dimensionality from the number of topics, enabling operation on a low-resource 10-qubit device. A fully classical variant is also constructed for comparison.

Result: The hybrid VAE achieves Cv=0.71 and NPMI=0.20 on AgNews, outperforming state-of-the-art NTMs while preserving high topic diversity. The fully classical variant also surpasses existing models and exhibits clear class separation in latent space.

Conclusion: Hybrid quantum-classical VAEs are computationally viable on NISQ-era devices and represent a promising direction for quantum-enhanced topic modeling.

Abstract: Neural topic models enable scalable semantic discovery, but their integration with quantum hardware remains largely unexplored. We present a proof-of-concept hybrid classical-quantum variational autoencoder (VAE) for topic modeling, embedding parameterized quantum circuits within the VAE inference network while retaining a classical topic-word decoder. To address the resource constraints of quantum hardware, we propose a modified Gaussian Softmax posterior that decouples latent space dimensionality from the number of topics to be extracted, enabling the model to operate with a low-resource 10-qubit quantum device. On the AgNews dataset, the hybrid VAE outperforms state-of-the-art neural topic models (NTMs), reaching a $C_v$ coherence score of 0.71 and an NPMI score of 0.20 while preserving high topic diversity. For comparison, we also construct a fully classical variant, which also outperforms state-of-the-art models on AgNews and exhibits clear class separation in the latent space. These results demonstrate that hybrid VAEs are computationally viable even on NISQ-era devices and represent a promising direction for quantum-enhanced topic modeling.

</details>


### [8] [SANA: What Matters for QA Agents over Massive Data Lakes?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13904)
*Austin Senna Wijaya,Jiaxiang Liu,Haonan Wang,Eugene Wu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: SANA is a diagnostic ablation framework that decomposes end-to-end EQA agent performance into search, planning, data analysis, and action policy components, revealing that data analysis is a consistent bottleneck across benchmarks while search limitations vary by data-lake scale.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: End-to-end accuracy metrics cannot isolate where EQA agents fail — whether in search, planning, data analysis, or action policy decisions. A systematic diagnostic method is needed to attribute failures and enable targeted improvements in data-lake agents.

Method: SANA transforms EQA tasks into runtime profiles (gold source sequence, sanitized subquestions, execution records) and constructs idealized oracle versions of search, planning, and data-analysis tools. By ablating each component (replacing it with an oracle) and measuring the residual accuracy gap, SANA quantifies the contribution of each module and identifies policy failures.

Result: Evaluated on LakeQA and KramaBench with lightweight and mid-sized agents: (1) data analysis is a consistent bottleneck across both benchmarks; (2) planning is less of a limitation; (3) search is a major failure point in LakeQA (large data lake) but not in KramaBench (smaller scale).

Conclusion: SANA enables fine-grained diagnosis of EQA agent failures, moving beyond aggregate accuracy to component-level attribution. It provides a reusable framework for systematically comparing progress across search, planning, data analysis, and agent policy design.

Abstract: Exploratory question answering (EQA) over data lakes requires an LLM agent to discover relevant sources, analyze retrieved data, and adapt its actions based on intermediate results. End-to-end accuracy alone cannot distinguish failures in search, planning, data analysis, or the agent's Action Policy: its decisions about what to do next and when to submit an answer. We present SANA (Search Agent Navigation Ablation framework), a diagnostic ablation framework that transforms EQA tasks into runtime profiles containing gold source sequence, sanitized subquestions, and execution records. SANA uses these profiles to construct idealized search, planning, and data-analysis tools, allowing each component to be ablated; the residual gap is diagnostic evidence for policy failures.
  To illustrate SANA as a reusable evaluation framework, we adapted two recent EQA benchmarks, LakeQA and KramaBench, and evaluated lightweight and mid-sized agents under fixed prompts, budgets, data lakes, and runtimes. Across both benchmarks, data analysis is a consistent bottleneck while planning is less so. Search is a major limitation in LakeQA's large data-lake setting, but less so for the smaller-scale KramaBench. SANA thus deconstructs end-to-end task accuracies into a diagnosis of where data-lake agents fail, and allows for systematic comparisons of progress in search, planning, data analysis, and agent design.

</details>


### [9] [DLawBench: Evaluating LLMs Through Multi-Turn Legal Consultation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13931)
*Li Zhang,Yuzhen Shi,Yiran Hu,Jingwen Zhang,Wenbo Lv,Yubo Ma,Wei Wang,Rongyao Shi,Yuanyang Qiu,Xinran Xu,Yuemeng Qi,Linlin Miao,Jaromir Savelka,Yun Liu,Kevin Ashley,Bing Zhao,Hu Wei,Lin Qu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: DLawBench is a diagnostic benchmark evaluating LLMs' ability to conduct multi-turn lawyer-client consultations across four client personality types (Cooperative, Dependent, Withdrawn, Adversarial). It comprises 461 real-case-based dialogues, tests 26 LLMs, and reveals significant performance gaps — the best model (GPT-5.5) scores only 0.562 — exposing both sycophancy and a paradox where models perform worst when clients need guidance most.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing legal benchmarks focus on static legal reasoning (e.g., reading comprehension, judgment prediction) but overlook the interactive, multi-turn nature of lawyer-client consultations. Effective legal consultation requires LLMs to strategically elicit material facts, adapt to diverse client personalities, and guide clients toward disclosing sufficient and truthful information — capabilities no prior benchmark measures.

Method: The authors construct DLawBench with: (1) four client behavior archetypes — Cooperative, Dependent, Withdrawn, and Adversarial — grounded in realistic client psychology; (2) 461 cases spanning Chinese and U.S. law, yielding 5,532 paired fact entries, 3,411 inquiry rubrics, and 3,348 issue-resolution rubrics; (3) a structured evaluation framework assessing both the quality of information elicited and the legal reasoning applied to that information; (4) evaluation of 26 representative LLMs under realistic consultation conditions.

Result: Systematic experiments reveal substantial performance headroom: the top model, GPT-5.5, achieves only 0.562 on consultation-grounded legal reasoning. Two critical failure patterns emerge: (1) sycophancy — models default to agreeing with clients rather than challenging incomplete or misleading statements; (2) a guidance paradox — model performance degrades precisely when clients (e.g., Withdrawn or Adversarial types) need the most proactive guidance and strategic questioning.

Conclusion: DLawBench demonstrates that current LLMs are insufficient for real-world legal consultation, lacking the strategic, adaptive, and truth-seeking interactive capabilities the task demands. The benchmark provides a foundation for developing LLMs that can conduct effective, ethically sound lawyer-client interviews.

Abstract: Lawyer-client consultation is a critical starting point for legal services. Effective legal assistance hinges on eliciting sufficient and truthful information from clients in order to devise strategies that best protect their interests. This task requires Large Language Models (LLMs) not only to perform robust legal reasoning, but also to strategically elicit material facts through multi-turn interactions and effectively guide clients with diverse personalities. Yet existing legal benchmarks overlook this interactive capability. To fill this gap, we introduce DLawBench, a diagnostic benchmark for real-world legal consultation. Drawing on realistic client behavior, we characterize lawyer-client interactions into four types: Cooperative, Dependent, Withdrawn, and Adversarial. Using dialogues grounded in real cases, DLawBench evaluates whether LLMs can effectively conduct legal consultation under realistic conditions. DLawBench comprises 461 cases from Chinese and U.S. law, 5,532 paired fact entries, 3,411 inquiry rubrics, and 3,348 issue-resolution rubrics, and evaluates 26 representative LLMs. Systematic experiments show substantial headroom: the best-performing model, GPT-5.5, achieves only 0.562 on consultation-grounded legal reasoning. More importantly, DLawBench exposes both sycophancy in legal consultation and a paradox: models perform worse when clients need guidance most.

</details>


### [10] [Can Post-Training Turn LLMs into Good Medical Coders? An Empirical Study of Generative ICD Coding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13940)
*Ziqing Wang,Weihao Li,Shijie Chen,Yuan Luo,Kaize Ding*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: LLMs are underrated for ICD coding because prior evaluations relied on prompting alone. Through post-training (SFT, GRPO, and the novel PHI curriculum), generative LLMs substantially close the gap with discriminative baselines, with the main bottleneck being adaptation for full-taxonomy recall, not the generative formulation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Generative LLMs are often dismissed as weak medical coders, but this conclusion is based almost entirely on inference-time techniques (prompting, retrieval, reranking). The role of task-specific post-training (SFT, RL) for generative ICD coding remains underexplored, and no prior work has studied RL-based post-training for this task.

Method: A controlled empirical study comparing discriminative baselines against generative LLM coders under a unified protocol. Three post-training stages are evaluated: (1) supervised fine-tuning (SFT), (2) group relative policy optimization (GRPO) reinforcement learning, and (3) PHI — a novel diagnostic curriculum extending GRPO that specifically targets missed-code cases to improve macro-level performance.

Result: Prompting-only evaluation significantly underestimates LLM potential. SFT provides the largest capability jump. GRPO yields further improvements in code-set prediction beyond SFT. PHI delivers targeted gains on macro-level performance, addressing weaknesses in rare and missed codes.

Conclusion: The primary bottleneck in generative ICD coding is not the generative formulation itself, but how the model is adapted and optimized for full-taxonomy recall. With appropriate post-training, generative LLMs become competitive medical coders.

Abstract: Automated International Classification of Diseases (ICD) coding is a core medical-coding task for billing, epidemiology, and clinical decision support. Generative large language models (LLMs) are often reported as weak medical coders, but this finding mainly comes from inference-time settings such as prompting, retrieval, reranking, or tool use, leaving the role of task-specific post-training underexplored. We present a controlled empirical study of post-training for generative ICD coding, comparing discriminative baselines with LLM coders across prompting, supervised fine-tuning, and reinforcement learning under a common protocol and metric set. To our knowledge, this is the first study to evaluate RL-based post-training for generative LLM coders in ICD coding. We further introduce PHI, a diagnostic curriculum that extends GRPO to refine missed-code cases. Our results show that prompting-only evaluation substantially underestimates the potential of LLMs for ICD coding. SFT provides the main capability jump, GRPO further improves code-set prediction beyond SFT, and PHI provides targeted gains on macro-level performance. These findings suggest that the main bottleneck is not the generative formulation alone, but how the model is adapted and optimized for full-taxonomy recall. We release our code, data splits, and checkpoints at https://github.com/AlexandreWANG915/LLM4ICD.

</details>


### [11] [LLMs Contain Multitudes: How Deployment Context Reshapes Model-Level Preferences and Values](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13944)
*Filip Trhlik,Aoife O'Flynn,Angela Yu,Arduin Findeis,Paula Buttery*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: LLMs do not have stable, model-level value systems. When deployment context (task framing) is varied, both preference rankings and utility judgements shift dramatically — far more than from prompt paraphrasing or temperature. Claimed model-level preferences are actually context-conditioned measurements, undermining safety guarantees that rely on fixed value assumptions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior evaluation work characterizes LLMs as having stable, model-level preferences and value systems, but robustness checks are limited to superficial prompt perturbations (syntax, option order). The authors test whether these properties hold when the broader deployment context changes, as happens in real-world applications.

Method: The authors conducted large-scale experiments across five LLMs using over 1.2M pairwise decisions in two paradigms: (1) ranking country preferences, and (2) eliciting utility judgements. The controlled variable was "deployment context" — the high-level task framing (e.g., writing a Reddit post vs. a news article) while the model makes value-dependent choices. Results were compared against prompt paraphrasing and temperature variation as baselines.

Result: Deployment context produced variation far exceeding that of paraphrasing or temperature controls. In country rankings (15 countries), context induced statistically significant rank shifts, and Global North favouritism reported in prior work proved context-dependent. In utility elicitation (50 outcomes), broad cross-category ordering was preserved but fine-grained rankings varied substantially; cardinal exchange rates between outcomes shifted by a factor of 2.47 at the median.

Conclusion: Reported model-level preferences and utilities are better understood as context-conditioned measurements rather than fixed model-level properties. Consequently, safety guarantees derived under one framing provide limited assurance when the deployment context changes.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly characterised in recent evaluation work as having stable, model-level preference and value systems. However, accompanying robustness checks are limited to incidental prompt perturbations such as syntax variation and option reordering. This leaves open whether the measured properties survive when the surrounding task context changes, as it does in most real deployments. We test this directly across two established pairwise paradigms: ranking country preferences and eliciting utility judgements. In both, we make the deployment context -- the high-level task the model is performing while making concrete value-dependent choices -- our controlled variable, varied across framings such as writing a Reddit post or a news article. Across five LLMs and over 1.2M pairwise decisions, deployment context produces variation far larger than prompt paraphrasing and temperature controls. In country preference rankings over 15 countries, context induces widespread, statistically significant rank shifts; the aggregate Global North favouritism reported in prior work is itself context-dependent, with each model's bias shifting systematically across contexts. In utility elicitation over 50 outcomes, broad cross-category ordering is preserved, but fine-grained rankings within domains vary substantially, and cardinal exchange rates between outcomes (e.g. how many lives in one region equal one in another) shift by a factor of 2.47 at the median. Reported model-level preferences and utilities are therefore better understood as context-conditioned measurements than fixed model-level properties: safety guarantees obtained under one framing provide limited assurance in another.

</details>


### [12] [A Computational Audit of Demographic Association Encoding in ClinicalBERT Language Predictions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14460)
*Kehinde Temitayo Soetan*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: ClinicalBERT systematically amplifies demographic biases rather than merely inheriting them from training data — 65.6% of statistically significant biased outputs contradict actual corpus frequencies, with Black patient representations and agency attribution showing amplification rates of 80–87.5%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Despite widespread deployment of transformer-based clinical LMs in decision support systems, the computational mechanisms by which demographic associations propagate from medical documentation into model outputs remain poorly characterized. The authors aim to empirically distinguish between bias inherited from training data distributions and bias generated through model-internal amplification.

Method: Two complementary probing approaches are employed: (1) Log Probability Bias Analysis (LPBA), quantifying demographic descriptor-induced shifts in masked token probability distributions across behavioral/evaluative semantic categories; (2) Masked Language Model (MLM) probing of internal representations for demographic agency attribution encoding, using 98 clinical sentence templates across eight intersectional race–gender combinations. Corpus frequency analysis benchmarks model outputs against empirical MIMIC-III term frequencies to operationalize the distinction between statistical disparity and bias amplification.

Result: Of 32 statistically significant findings, 65.6% contradict observed corpus distributions. This contradiction rate rises to 80% for Black patients and 87.5% for agency attribution under MLM probing, providing strong evidence that representational bias in ClinicalBERT operates predominantly through model-internal amplification rather than passive training data inheritance.

Conclusion: Representational bias in ClinicalBERT is not simply a reflection of training data statistics but arises primarily from model-internal amplification mechanisms. This has significant implications for the safe deployment of clinical LMs in high-stakes settings and underscores the necessity of computational auditing that goes beyond corpus-level fairness assessments.

Abstract: Transformer-based clinical language models are increasingly integrated into high-stakes clinical decision support pipelines, yet the computational mechanisms through which demographic associations encoded in medical documentation propagate into model probability distributions remain empirically underspecified. We present a systematic computational audit of representational bias in ClinicalBERT (Alsentzer et al., 2019), a BERT-based model pretrained on MIMIC-III discharge summaries, employing two complementary probing methodologies: Log Probability Bias Analysis (LPBA), which quantifies demographic descriptor-induced shifts in masked token probability distributions across behavioral and evaluative semantic categories, and Masked Language Model-based analysis (MLM), which probes internal representational structure for demographic agency attribution encoding across 98 real clinical sentence templates and eight intersectional race-gender combinations. Corpus frequency analysis operationalizes the distinction between statistical disparity and bias amplification by benchmarking model outputs against empirical term frequencies in the MIMIC-III training corpus. Of 32 statistically significant findings, 65.6% contradict observed corpus distributions, rising to 80% for Black patients and 87.5% for agency attribution under MLM probing, providing direct empirical evidence that representational bias in ClinicalBERT operates predominantly through model-internal amplification rather than training data inheritance. Keywords: natural language processing, clinical documentation, algorithmic auditing, representational bias, health equity 1

</details>


### [13] [MedLatentDx: Latent Multi-Agent Communication for Cross-Hospital Rare-Disease Diagnosis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13945)
*Ziqing Wang,Lili Zhao,Kaize Ding*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: MedLatentDx enables privacy-preserving cross-hospital rare-disease diagnosis via latent multi-agent communication, where hospital agents exchange compact latent KV blocks instead of raw clinical text, with support for both same-backbone and cross-backbone LLM deployments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Rare diseases affect 300M+ patients across 7,000+ conditions, but no single hospital sees enough cases for reliable diagnosis. Cross-hospital collaboration is needed but prevented by privacy regulations restricting identifiable clinical text sharing. Existing medical agents rely on text exchange, while raw latent states (hidden states, KV caches) can still leak prompt-derived clinical information.

Method: MedLatentDx is a latent multi-agent communication framework where hospital agents retain private records locally and transmit only compact latent KV blocks to a host diagnostic agent. Two deployment modes: (1) same-backbone hospitals use latent KV distillation, (2) different-backbone hospitals use cross-family latent alignment. Evaluated on CrossRare-Bench, a self-constructed large-scale rare-disease benchmark with hospital-level data partitions.

Result: MedLatentDx improves cross-hospital diagnostic performance on CrossRare-Bench while reducing reconstructable clinical content compared to raw-latent communication baselines.

Conclusion: Latent communication with KV distillation and cross-family alignment offers a viable privacy-preserving pathway for multi-institutional rare-disease diagnosis, balancing diagnostic accuracy with reduced clinical content leakage.

Abstract: Rare diseases affect over $300$ million patients across more than $7{,}000$ conditions, yet no single hospital encounters enough cases of any one condition for reliable diagnosis. Cross-hospital collaboration could help by allowing a diagnosing institution to use distributed, case-specific diagnostic evidence, but privacy regulations restrict the transmission of identifiable clinical text across institutional boundaries. This setting raises two challenges: existing medical agent systems often rely on textual evidence exchange, while raw latent states such as hidden states and KV caches may still reveal prompt-derived clinical content. We introduce MedLatentDx, a latent multi-agent communication framework in which hospital agents keep private clinical records and retrieved cases local, and send compact latent KV blocks to a host agent for rare-disease diagnosis. MedLatentDx supports two deployment settings: same-backbone hospital agents use latent KV distillation, while hospitals with different LLM backbones use cross-family latent alignment. On CrossRare-Bench, a self-built large-scale rare-disease benchmark with hospital-level partitions, MedLatentDx improves cross-hospital diagnostic performance while reducing reconstructable clinical content relative to raw-latent communication baselines.

</details>


### [14] [Creative Integration: A Decidable Criterion of Creativity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13977)
*Yoshinori Nomura*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes an operational criterion for "creative integration" (CI): it occurs when resolving a genuine conflict between A and B strictly reduces description length (compression ratio C > 1), with the reduction localized in the conflict itself. The criterion is made decidable via four binary gates, validated through falsifiable computational tests rather than inter-rater agreement, and distinguished from pseudo-integrations via a taxonomy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: "Integrative" solutions are widely praised but lack an operational definition. There is no rigorous way to distinguish genuine integration — one that makes the world cheaper to describe — from mere tidy re-description. The paper aims to close this gap.

Method: The authors build on the compression-as-intelligence lineage. They define creative integration (CI) as: resolution of a real conflict between A and B where description length strictly shrinks (C = L_pre / L_post > 1), with reduction localized in the conflict. Decidability is achieved through four binary, conjunctive gates. A taxonomy of pseudo-integration names and rejects look-alikes. Validation uses four falsifiable tests: independent computational check, discrimination against hard negatives, out-of-sample prediction, and description-language robustness — all tested on a curated multi-domain corpus.

Result: All four falsifiable tests pass with margin: the computational check confirms compression; hard negatives are discriminated; out-of-sample predictions hold; and results are robust across description languages. The criterion thus proves decidable, discriminative, and empirically grounded.

Conclusion: The contribution is not merely "creativity is compression" but the decidability, discrimination, and corpus supporting that claim. Genuinely creative moves compress a conflict, with novelty and value as downstream symptoms. Whether all creativity fits this model is left as an explicit conjecture. The result provides a citable primitive for a broader research program; the framework judges but does not generate creative integrations.

Abstract: "Integrative" solutions are widely praised but rarely defined: we lack an operational way to tell a genuine integration -- one that makes the world cheaper to describe -- from a tidy re-description. Building on the lineage that
  treats creativity and intelligence as compression, we give such a criterion for creative integration (CI): the resolution of a real conflict between A and B is CI if and only if, under a fixed description language, the description
  length strictly shrinks (C = L_pre/L_post > 1), with the reduction located in the conflict itself. We make the judgment decidable through four binary, conjunctive gates, and we fix its extension through a taxonomy of
  pseudo-integration that names and rejects the look-alikes. We back the criterion with a curated, multi-domain corpus and -- crucially -- validate it not by human inter-rater agreement but by four falsifiable tests it could fail: an
  independent computational check, discrimination against hard negatives, out-of-sample prediction, and description-language robustness; all pass with margin. The contribution is not "creativity is compression" but its decidability,
  discrimination, and corpus: on this account, what makes a move genuinely creative -- rather than merely novel -- is that it compresses a conflict, with novelty and value as downstream symptoms; whether all creativity is so
  constituted we state as an explicit conjecture. We claim only the sign of C-1; we judge, not generate. The result is a citable primitive for a broader program.

</details>


### [15] [Fusing Stylometric and Embedding Systems to Estimate Authorship Likelihood Ratios in Japanese](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13991)
*Praju Ghatpande,Satoru Tsuge,Shunichi Ishihara,Wataru Zaitsu,Mitsuyuki Inaba*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: First application of the likelihood ratio framework to Japanese authorship attribution, demonstrating that fusing stylometric features with LLM embeddings improves calibration, discriminability, and log-likelihood-ratio cost (Cllr = 0.32484).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The likelihood ratio framework is the gold standard for forensic evidence evaluation but has been restricted to English texts. Authorship attribution has separately advanced along two tracks — traditional stylometric features and modern contextual embeddings from LLMs — yet no work has fused these heterogeneous systems within the likelihood ratio paradigm, nor applied it to non-English (specifically Japanese) digital texts.

Method: The authors applied likelihood ratio-based forensic text comparison to ~1,000-character excerpts from Japanese blogs. They built two types of systems: (1) a stylometric-feature system using traditional features, and (2) an embedding-based system leveraging pre-trained large language models. These were fused, and performance was evaluated using calibration metrics and log-likelihood-ratio cost (Cllr), assessing both consistent-with-fact and contrary-to-fact likelihood ratio magnitudes.

Result: The fused system maintained excellent calibration while: (1) increasing likelihood ratio magnitudes for same-author pairs (consistent-with-fact), (2) decreasing magnitudes for different-author pairs (contrary-to-fact), and (3) improving overall discriminability. The best fusion achieved a Cllr of 0.32484.

Conclusion: The likelihood ratio framework is feasible and effective for Japanese forensic text comparison. Fusion of heterogeneous systems — stylometric and embedding-based — enhances evidential value beyond either system alone, supporting broader cross-linguistic and cross-method adoption in forensic authorship analysis.

Abstract: The likelihood ratio framework is widely recognized as the logically and legally sound basis for evidential analysis across forensic sciences, and its importance is increasingly acknowledged in analyses of authorship in textual evidence. To date, however, its application has been confined to English-language texts. Meanwhile, authorship attribution has traditionally relied on a diverse array of stylometric features, even as the rise of pre-trained large language models enables new contextual-embedding approaches. Combining these diverse approaches through fusion promises enhanced performance, yet it has not been applied to integrate stylometric-feature systems with embedding-based systems within the likelihood ratio paradigm. This study is the first to apply likelihood ratio-based forensic text comparison to Japanese digital texts, using ~1,000-character excerpts from blogs, to 1) evaluate system performance and likelihood ratio magnitudes and 2) assess the impact of fusing stylometric-feature systems with embedding-based systems. The results demonstrate that the fused system maintains excellent calibration while 1) increasing consistent-with-fact likelihood ratio magnitudes; 2) decreasing contrary-to-fact likelihood ratio magnitudes and 3) improving overall discriminability. The best-performing fusion achieved a log-likelihood-ratio cost of 0.32484, illustrating both the feasibility of likelihood ratio framework for Japanese and the benefits of fusion across heterogeneous systems.

</details>


### [16] [The Holistic Storage of Verb+Up Phrases in Text-based and Audio-based Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13993)
*Zachary Nicholas Houghton,Yu Zhou,Dan Pluth,Vijay K. Gurbani*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This study probes whether language models (text-based LLMs and ASR models) develop holistic, stored representations for V+up phrasal verbs, finding that frequency and predictability drive distinct internal representations, consistent with usage-based linguistic theories.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While prior work has extensively examined abstract, rule-based knowledge in language models, the holistic storage of multi-word units—a key component of the storage-vs.-abstraction trade-off in human language—remains underexplored. The authors aim to fill this gap by investigating whether models store phrasal verbs as whole units.

Method: The authors probe internal representations in text-based large language models and an automatic speech recognition (ASR) model, focusing on V+up phrasal verbs (e.g., "pick up," "give up"). They analyze how the representational distinctness of these constructions varies as a function of token frequency and semantic predictability/compositionality.

Result: All tested models exhibit evidence of holistic storage for phrasal verbs, with the degree of representational distinctness modulated by both frequency (higher frequency → more holistic) and predictability (lower predictability → more holistic), mirroring patterns observed in human language processing.

Conclusion: The findings provide computational support for usage-based theories of language, which posit that frequent and idiosyncratic multi-word expressions become entrenched as holistic units. This demonstrates that the storage-abstraction trade-off emerges in neural language models through statistical learning.

Abstract: A crucial aspect of linguistic capability is the ability to trade off between stored representations and abstract knowledge: one must retrieve learned representations, but also generate novel ones by applying productive rules. While recent work has examined abstract knowledge in language models, holistic storage of multi-word units has received far less attention. We probe internal representations in text-based LLMs and an ASR model, testing whether V+up phrasal verbs develop distinct representations as a function of frequency and predictability. All models show evidence of holistic storage driven by frequency and predictability, further supporting usage-based theories of language.

</details>


### [17] [Dialogue SWE-Bench: A Benchmark for Dialogue-Driven Coding Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13995)
*Brendan King,Jeffrey Flanigan*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Introduces Dialogue SWE-Bench, a benchmark for evaluating coding agents' ability to solve software engineering problems through user dialogue, featuring a persona-based user simulator, dialogue quality metrics, and a schema-guided agent that improves dialogue performance by 3-14%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing coding agent benchmarks evaluate agents as fully-autonomous systems, ignoring the interactive, dialogue-driven nature of real-world coding assistance. Dialogue capability is an understudied dimension of agent performance.

Method: The authors create Dialogue SWE-Bench by augmenting SWE-Bench tasks with a persona-grounded user simulator that engages in dialogue. They propose automatic dialogue quality evaluation metrics and a schema-guided agent architecture to enhance off-the-shelf coding agents' conversational abilities.

Result: The schema-guided agent achieves 3-14% improvement over strong baselines. Results also reveal that stronger coding models do not necessarily yield stronger dialogue models, indicating dialogue capability is a distinct performance dimension.

Conclusion: Dialogue capability is a critical yet underexplored dimension of coding agent performance. The proposed benchmark and schema-guided approach provide a foundation for advancing interactive, human-in-the-loop coding assistance.

Abstract: AI coding agents have rapidly transformed software engineering, powering widely used interactive coding assistants. Despite their interactive real-world use, existing benchmarks evaluate them as fully-autonomous systems. In this work, we introduce Dialogue SWE-Bench, an automatic benchmark dataset for evaluating the ability of coding agents to resolve real-world software engineering problems through dialogue with a user. We design a novel, persona-grounded user simulator to support our task evaluation, and augment our task evaluation with automatic evaluations of dialogue quality. We also propose a new schema-guided agent, aimed at improving the dialogue capabilities of off-the-shelf coding agents, which improves over strong baselines by 3-14%. Our results indicate that better coding models do not always correspond to better dialogue models, suggesting that dialogue capability is a distinct and currently understudied dimension of coding agent performance.

</details>


### [18] [Right or Wrong, Models Comply: Directional Blindness in LLM Moral Judgment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14037)
*Jihye Kim,Jeffrey Flanigan*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This paper introduces "Compliance Asymmetry" (A = BCR/HCR), a metric comparing how LLMs respond to helpful vs. harmful nudges. It finds that models show selectivity on factual questions (following helpful nudges more) but are direction-blind on moral questions (following both equally), identifying this as a critical alignment failure mode.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing alignment evaluations treat compliance as unidirectional—measuring whether models resist pressure, not whether they resist it selectively. The authors argue that true alignment requires models to update appropriately in response to helpful corrections while resisting misleading ones, motivating a bidirectional diagnostic framework.

Method: The authors define Compliance Asymmetry as the ratio of Beneficial Compliance Rate (BCR) to Harmful Compliance Rate (HCR). They test 9 models across 972,000 nudge-condition responses spanning factual and moral judgment domains, using helpful nudges (steering toward correct answers) and misleading nudges (steering toward incorrect answers). They also evaluate how chain-of-thought prompting and identity-based prompting modulate compliance patterns.

Result: On factual questions, models exhibit positive asymmetry (A = 1.58), following helpful nudges more than harmful ones. On moral questions, asymmetry collapses to near unity (A = 1.04), indicating direction-blind compliance. Chain-of-thought prompting amplifies both helpful and harmful compliance symmetrically, while identity-based prompting suppresses both by nearly identical margins. These patterns persist across model families, scales, and nudge types.

Conclusion: Direction-blind moral compliance constitutes a distinct alignment failure mode in current LLMs. The authors argue that alignment efforts should target directionally calibrated updating—improving the ratio of helpful-to-harmful compliance—rather than merely reducing overall compliance rates.

Abstract: As language models take integrated roles across many domains, the response of LLMs to user pushback becomes a critical alignment property. Yet many existing evaluations treat compliance as unidirectional, measuring whether models resist pressure but not whether they resist it selectively. We introduce Compliance Asymmetry (A = BCR/HCR), a bidirectional diagnostic that compares beneficial output change under helpful nudges with harmful change under misleading nudges. Across 9 models and 972,000 nudge-condition responses, we find that this selectivity differs in factual and moral judgments: models follow helpful nudges more than harmful ones on factual questions (A = 1.58), but follow both directions at nearly identical rates on moral questions (A = 1.04). This phenomenon persists across model families, capability levels, and nudging types. Interestingly, we also find that chain-of-thought prompting amplifies helpful and harmful compliance together, while identity-based prompting suppresses both by nearly identical margins. These results identify direction-blind moral compliance as a distinct failure mode in current LLMs and suggest that alignment should target directionally calibrated updating rather than lower compliance alone.

</details>


### [19] [Harsher on Male? Evaluating LLMs on Gender-Asymmetric Moral Framing Across Diverse Conflict Scenarios](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14068)
*Guangzong Si,Dong Wang,Zhenhao Li,Yifan Yu,Panwang Pan,Wentao Zhu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: GAMA-Bench introduces a gender-mirrored benchmark of 1,298 scenarios revealing that LLMs consistently apply harsher, more punitive framing to male actors and more empathetic, therapeutic framing to female actors for identical misconduct.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work on gender bias in LLMs has focused on stereotypes, occupational associations, and explicit harmful outputs, but has not systematically examined whether LLMs apply consistent response standards to identical negative behaviors when the actor's gender is swapped. This work addresses that gap.

Method: The authors construct GAMA-Bench, a benchmark of 1,298 gender-neutral misconduct scenarios spanning intimate relationship and public social conflicts. Scenarios are built via controlled grids with cross-model review to ensure neutrality, then compiled into paired first-person prompts with matched actor-gender and role-reference variations. Responses are evaluated using a structured response-framing protocol measuring five dimensions: punishment, empathy, escalation, instruction, and blame.

Result: Experiments across 10 representative LLMs reveal a consistent male-disadvantaging asymmetry: male actors receive significantly more punitive, escalatory, and blame-centered framing, while female actors receive more therapeutic and empathy-oriented responses for the same misconduct. This pattern holds across model families, scenario tracks, model scales, and explicit chain-of-thought reasoning.

Conclusion: LLMs exhibit a systematic double standard in judging misconduct based on actor gender, disadvantaging male actors. This reveals a deeper fairness concern beyond surface-level stereotyping, suggesting that response-framing biases are pervasive and robust across diverse model configurations.

Abstract: Existing studies on gender bias in LLMs have largely focused on stereotypes, occupational associations, or explicit harmful outputs. In this work, we ask whether LLMs apply consistent response standards to the same negative behavior under matched male-actor and female-actor conditions. We introduce GAMA-Bench, a gender-mirrored benchmark of 1,298 scenarios covering intimate relationship and public social conflicts. It constructs gender-neutral misconduct templates through controlled grids and cross-model review, then compiles them into paired first-person prompts with matched actor-gender and role-reference variations. We further design a structured response-framing protocol to measure how models allocate punishment, empathy, escalation, instruction, and blame. Experiments on 10 representative LLMs reveal a consistent male-disadvantaging asymmetry: male actors receive more punitive, escalatory, and blame-centered framing, whereas female actors receive more therapeutic and empathy-oriented framing for the same misconduct. Further analyses show that this pattern persists across model families, scenario tracks, model scale, and explicit thinking-style reasoning. The official code is available at https://github.com/xufeiqiong/GAMA-Bench.

</details>


### [20] [Beyond Perplexity: UTF-8 Validity in Byte-aware Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14122)
*Sangwhan Moon,Daisuke Oba,Youmi Ma,Tatsuya Hiraoka,Naoaki Okazaki*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Byte-level tokenized language models can produce invalid UTF-8. This paper shows that UTF-8 validity converges much later than perplexity during training (~2× more tokens), and surprisingly, rare characters achieve higher structural validity than common ones in context-free generation, suggesting reliable UTF-8 generation is a distinct capability requiring separate evaluation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While byte-level tokenization allows language models to process any Unicode input, these models can generate invalid UTF-8 byte sequences when handling rare or unseen characters. The authors aim to understand how training scale affects UTF-8 generation reliability, a question previously unexplored.

Method: The authors trained a 355M-parameter model on 80B tokens from a balanced multilingual corpus (English, Japanese, Korean, Chinese) using byte-level tokenization. They designed multiple evaluation protocols that cleanly separate UTF-8 structural validity from language modeling quality. They tracked both perplexity and UTF-8 validity throughout training to compare their convergence behavior, and conducted context-free generation experiments to isolate character-level structural validity.

Result: UTF-8 validity convergence lags perplexity by roughly a factor of two: perplexity stabilizes after 2.1B tokens, while UTF-8 validity requires 4.2B tokens. In context-free generation, rare characters unexpectedly exhibit higher structural validity than common characters, indicating over-specialization of frequent character representations that harms structural correctness.

Conclusion: Reliable UTF-8 generation is a distinct capability that cannot be inferred from perplexity alone and requires dedicated evaluation. The counterintuitive finding that rare characters are structurally more valid than common ones highlights the need to consider token frequency effects when designing and evaluating byte-level models.

Abstract: Byte-level tokenization enables language models to handle any Unicode input, but models can generate invalid UTF-8 sequences when encountering rare or unseen characters. We investigate the relationship between training scale and UTF-8 generation reliability with a 355M parameter model trained on 80B tokens from a balanced multilingual corpus of English, Japanese, Korean, and Chinese. We introduce multiple evaluation protocols that isolate UTF-8 structural validity from language modeling. UTF-8 validity convergence lags perplexity by a roughly a factor of two: perplexity stabilizes after 2.1B tokens, but UTF-8 validity requires 4.2B tokens. In context-free generation, rare characters achieve higher structural validity than common characters, suggesting over-specialization of frequent character representations. Through experiments, we observed that reliable UTF-8 generation is a distinct capability requiring evaluation beyond perplexity.

</details>


### [21] [Implicit Reasoning for Large Language Model-based Generative Recommendation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14142)
*Yinhan He,Liam Collins,Bhuvesh Kumar,Jundong Li,Neil Shah,Donald Loveland*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: PauseRec proposes a lightweight implicit reasoning paradigm for LLM-based generative recommendation, avoiding costly explicit rationale generation. It addresses three limitations of explicit reasoning pipelines—weakened world-knowledge verbalization, SID-natural language embedding misalignment, and rationale quality sensitivity—achieving up to 6.22% performance gains, 65% training cost reduction, and 71.3% faster inference over standard Chain-of-Thought methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM-based generative recommendation (GR) suffers from a fundamental tension: items are represented as Semantic IDs (SIDs) unseen during pretraining, disrupting the natural-language reasoning interface. Existing multi-stage explicit reasoning pipelines (rationale generation + alignment) are expensive and poorly understood, with no clear analysis of when or why each stage is necessary.

Method: The authors first systematically decompose explicit reasoning training pipelines to identify three failure modes. They then propose PauseRec, a lightweight implicit reasoning paradigm that operates without reasoning trace acquisition or reasoning alignment training. The approach injects learnable "pause" tokens into the LLM's forward pass, enabling internal deliberation without generating explicit rationales, thereby preserving the LLM's native embedding space and avoiding SID misalignment.

Result: PauseRec outperforms standard explicit CoT methods by up to 6.22%, reduces training cost by up to 65% GPU hours, and accelerates inference by up to 71.3%.

Conclusion: Implicit reasoning via PauseRec is a more effective and efficient alternative to explicit rationale generation for LLM-based generative recommendation, circumventing the inherent limitations of SID-based reasoning pipelines.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly adopted as backbones for Generative Recommendation (GR), promising access to pretrained world knowledge. Yet reliably invoking this knowledge for GR remains poorly understood. A key obstacle is that LLM-based GR typically represents items with Semantic IDs (SIDs), disrupting LLMs' natural-language reasoning interface because these tokens are unseen by the LLM during pretraining. Existing approaches address this with expensive multi-stage pipelines that ground SIDs and elicit explicit rationales, but offer limited insight into when and why each stage is necessary. In this work, we systematically decompose explicit reasoning training pipelines for LLM-based GR, revealing three key limitations: weakened world-knowledge verbalization, misalignment between SID and natural-language token embedding spaces, and sensitivity to rationale quality, all of which hurt explicit reasoning performance. To circumvent these issues, we propose PauseRec, a lightweight implicit reasoning paradigm tailored for GR. PauseRec is exceptionally practical, avoiding costly reasoning trace acquisition and reasoning alignment training, leading to a multitude of benefits: (1) it outperforms standard explicit CoT methods by up to 6.22%, (2) it reduces training cost by up to 65% GPU hours, and (3) it speeds up inference by up to 71.3%. These results position PauseRec as a lightweight alternative to explicit rationale generation, enabling more effective and efficient LLM-based GR.

</details>


### [22] [Personal Care Utility: Health as Everyday Infrastructure](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14145)
*Mahyar Abbasian,Elahe Khatibi,Saba A. Farahani,Nitish Nagesh,Arshia Ilaty,Hooman Sajjadi,Amir Rahmani,Ramesh Jain*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes the Personal Care Utility (PCU), a layered event-driven architecture that serves as the missing infrastructure for everyday health management outside clinical settings. It organizes continuous personal signals into meaningful life events, estimates dynamic health states, reasons about cause and context, and routes guidance while separating clinical decision logic from LLM-based communication. The framework is instantiated for Type 2 Diabetes management.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Healthcare is built around ~1 hour/year of clinical contact, but the 8,759 hours outside clinical settings—where eating, sleeping, movement, medication, and stress shape long-term health—lack comparable infrastructure. The bottleneck for personalized health is not data or reasoning capability but the absence of this infrastructure layer.

Method: PCU employs a layered, event-driven architecture with several key components: (1) a Personicle that organizes continuous personal signals into semantically meaningful life events; (2) dynamic health state estimation against personal baselines; (3) causal and contextual reasoning; (4) an orchestrator that separates clinical decision logic, behavioral strategy selection, and natural-language expression—enabling LLMs to support reasoning/communication while keeping safety-critical clinical decisions grounded in validated evidence.

Result: The framework is instantiated for Type 2 Diabetes, converting CGM, meal, activity, medication, sleep, stress, and clinical data into glycemic events, individualized state estimates, causal explanations, and knowledge-grounded interventions. A day-in-the-life scenario demonstrates the infrastructure producing context-appropriate outputs: real-time nudges, weekly summaries, medication check-ins, silence, or deterministic safety alerts depending on risk.

Conclusion: PCU generalizes to other chronic conditions and surfaces governance questions for always-on personal health utilities. The result is a blueprint treating personalization as an architectural property rather than merely a final messaging layer.

Abstract: Healthcare is essential, expert, and episodic by design - built around the roughly one hour per year a person spends with a clinician. The 8,759 hours outside clinical settings, where eating, sleeping, movement, medication, and stress actually shape long-term health, have no comparable infrastructure. The bottleneck for personalized health is not raw data or reasoning capability; it is the absence of that infrastructure layer. This paper introduces the Personal Care Utility (PCU): a layered, event-driven architecture proposed as the missing utility for everyday health, in the way that payments, networks, and power are utilities for their domains. PCU organizes continuous personal signals into semantically meaningful life events through a Personicle, estimates dynamic health state against personal baselines, reasons about cause and context, and routes guidance through an orchestrator that separates clinical decision logic, behavioral strategy selection, and natural-language expression. This separation lets large language models support reasoning and communication while keeping safety-critical clinical decisions grounded in validated evidence. We instantiate PCU for Type 2 Diabetes - turning CGM, meal, activity, medication, sleep, stress, and clinical data into glycemic events, individualized state estimates, causal explanations, and knowledge-grounded interventions. A day-in-the-life scenario shows the same infrastructure producing real-time nudges, weekly summaries, medication check-ins, silence, or deterministic safety alerts depending on context and risk. We close with how PCU generalizes to other chronic conditions and the governance questions any always-on personal health utility must address. The result is a blueprint that treats personalization not as a final messaging layer, but as an architectural property of everyday health guidance.

</details>


### [23] [CacheRL:Multi-Turn Tool-Calling Agents via Cached Rollouts and Hybrid Reward](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14179)
*Md Amirul Islam,Sumiran Thakur,Huancheng Chen,Su Min Park,Jiayun Wang,Gyuhak Kim*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: CacheRL trains small agent foundation models for tool-calling via hybrid reasoning trajectories, a fuzzy cache (CacheAgentLoop), and cache-aware rewards, achieving 92% accuracy vs. GPT-5's 94% with 100× less compute. Reinforcement learning adds stability but limited gains beyond strong SFT.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Practical agent training faces three bottlenecks: (1) transferring tool-calling knowledge from large models at scale, (2) enabling RL without costly live tool execution, and (3) learning robustly from noisy cached environments. CacheRL targets these to produce small yet competitive agent models.

Method: CacheRL combines three innovations: (1) a hybrid thinking trajectory pipeline that annotates trajectories with LLM-generated reasoning traces (teaching "what" and "why"); (2) CacheAgentLoop, a three-tier fuzzy cache eliminating live execution costs while preserving fidelity via token-level masking; (3) cache-tier-aware reward that adjusts answer-quality weights to account for cache-induced limitations. Training proceeds iteratively via supervised fine-tuning (SFT) and Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO), applied to Qwen3-4B-Thinking.

Result: CacheRL improves Qwen3-4B-Thinking's validation reward from 0.43 to 0.78. On public agentic tool-calling benchmarks, it achieves 92% process accuracy vs. GPT-5's 94% with ~100× less compute. Ablations: removing knowledge transfer reduces performance by 41%; cache-aware rewards contribute +17%. RL improves stability but yields marginal gains beyond strong SFT.

Conclusion: CacheRL demonstrates that small agent models can approach frontier-model tool-calling performance through effective knowledge transfer, cached execution, and reward design. Data quality and reward engineering outweigh complex optimization methods for practical small agents; RL primarily aids stability rather than driving large performance jumps post-SFT.

Abstract: We present CacheRL, a system for training small agent foundation models that achieves 92 percent process accuracy on multi-step tool-calling tasks, approaching GPT-5's 94 percent while requiring 100 times less compute. Our approach addresses three challenges in practical agent training: transferring tool-calling knowledge from large models at scale, enabling reinforcement learning without costly live tool execution, and learning robustly from noisy cached environments. CacheRL introduces three key innovations. First, a hybrid thinking trajectory pipeline augments agent trajectories with LLM-generated reasoning traces, producing training examples that teach models not only what tools to call but also why. Second, the CacheAgentLoop eliminates live execution costs through a three-tier fuzzy cache while preserving trajectory fidelity using token-level masking. Third, a cache-tier-aware reward dynamically adjusts answer-quality weights to avoid penalizing models for cache-induced limitations. Through iterative supervised fine-tuning (SFT) and Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO), CacheRL improves Qwen3-4B-Thinking's validation reward from 0.43 to 0.78. On public agentic tool-calling benchmarks, our model achieves competitive performance against frontier models such as GPT-5. Ablation studies show that removing knowledge transfer reduces performance by 41 percent, while cache-aware rewards contribute a 17 percent improvement. Interestingly, reinforcement learning improves training stability but yields limited gains beyond strong supervised fine-tuning, suggesting that data quality and reward design play a more important role than complex optimization methods in building practical small agent models.

</details>


### [24] [OdysSim: Building Foundation Models for Human Behavior Simulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14199)
*Xuhui Zhou,Weiwei Sun,Weihua Du,Jiarui Liu,Haojia Sun,Qianou Ma,Tongshuang Wu,Yiming Yang,Maarten Sap*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: OdysSim presents SOUL, a taxonomy and benchmark for behavioral foundation models, and trains an 8B open model (OSim) that outperforms frontier models on 8/23 human simulation tasks while producing more human-like outputs and mitigating reward-hacking in LLM-as-judge RL.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLMs are post-trained for helpfulness, creating an overly agreeable "assistant register" that diverges from authentic human behavior—a behavioral Sim2Real gap. The authors aim to systematically investigate and close this gap by building behavioral foundation models specifically trained to simulate human behavior at scale.
