Method: The authors (1) propose SOUL, a taxonomy spanning five capability axes (CONV, SS, COG, ROLE, EVAL) unifying 62 datasets and 23 tasks; (2) curate the OdysSim corpus (21.4M interactions, 10B tokens, with back-generated social contexts); (3) construct the SOUL-Index benchmark; and (4) develop a training recipe combining midtraining, task-specific RL, and expert distillation to produce the open 8B OSim model. They also introduce detectors to mitigate reward-hacking patterns from LLM-as-judge RL.

Result: OSim ranks first or tied-first on 8 of 23 tasks, surpassing any individual frontier model by this count, with strongest gains on conversational and social tasks. Outputs are more human-like in length, formatting, and word choice. Zero-shot transfer to τ-bench user simulation achieves 93.2 reaction alignment vs. 93.5 for real users. LLM-as-judge RL was shown to induce reward-hacking, mitigable by their detectors.

Conclusion: Behavioral foundation models require rethinking the standard LLM training paradigm. Training specifically for human simulation—rather than helpfulness—yields models that better mimic authentic human behavior across diverse social, conversational, cognitive, role-playing, and evaluation tasks.

Abstract: Large language models are increasingly deployed as human simulators for interactive evaluation and social simulation. Yet helpfulness-driven post-training pulls them toward a homogeneous, overly agreeable assistant register, creating a behavioral Sim2Real gap. We present OdysSim, the largest open systematic investigation of behavioral foundation models, i.e., models trained to simulate human behavior at scale. We propose SOUL, a taxonomy of five capability axes (CONV, SS, COG, ROLE, EVAL) that unifies 62 datasets and 23 benchmark tasks under one framework. Specifically, we curate the OdysSim corpus (21.4M interactions, 10B tokens, retrofitted with back-generated social contexts), construct the SOUL-Index benchmark, and develop an end-to-end training recipe combining midtraining, task-specific RL, and expert distillation. The resulting open 8B OSim model ranks first or tied-first on 8 of 23 tasks, outperforming any individual frontier model by this count, with the strongest gains on conversational and social tasks. Its outputs are also more human-like in length, formatting, and word choice, and it transfers zero-shot to out-of-distribution user simulation on $τ$-bench, nearly matching real users on reaction alignment (93.2 vs. 93.5). We further show that LLM-as-judge RL induces reward-hacking patterns, and that our detectors can mitigate them during post-training. Together, our findings suggest that behavioral foundation models require rethinking the LLM training paradigm. We release all artifacts to support future research.

</details>


### [25] [Detecting undisclosed LLM-generated content in parliamentary texts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14209)
*Minerva Suvanto,Andrea McGlinchey,Peter J. Barclay,Mattias Wahde*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: An interpretable classifier trained on pre-LLM and LLM-generated parliamentary texts reveals a steady increase in undisclosed LLM use in UK and Swedish parliamentary texts from 2022 onward.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While AI disclosure norms exist in journalism and academia, parliamentary guidelines on disclosing LLM use are vague, threatening transparency and public trust. The authors aim to quantify the extent of undisclosed LLM-generated content in parliamentary texts.

Method: Training an interpretable (glass-box) text classifier on pre-LLM authentic parliamentary texts and LLM-generated versions of such texts; applying the classifier to a test set of recent parliamentary motions from the UK and Sweden to detect undisclosed AI-generated content.

Result: A steady increase in undisclosed LLM-generated content was detected in both the UK and Swedish parliaments, beginning in 2022.

Conclusion: Undisclosed use of LLMs in parliamentary texts is a growing phenomenon in both countries, highlighting a need for clearer disclosure guidelines to preserve transparency and public trust in democratic institutions.

Abstract: In this paper, we evaluate the extent of undisclosed LLM-generated content in texts from the parliaments of the United Kingdom and Sweden. In many areas, such as in journalism or in academic writing, there are often requirements to clearly disclose whether AI tools, such as LLMs, have been used. In the case of parliamentary texts, the guidelines on disclosure of AI use are more vague. However, in order to maintain transparency and retain public trust, it is generally recommended that parliamentarians should state whether or not they have used AI when writing texts, such as parliamentary motions. Here, we train an interpretable (glass-box) text classifier using pre-LLM parliamentary texts and LLM-generated versions of such texts. We then apply the classifier to a test set containing recent parliamentary texts, finding a steady increase in undisclosed LLM use, in both parliaments, from 2022 onwards.

</details>


### [26] [Decoupled Mixture-of-Experts for Parametric Knowledge Injection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14243)
*Baoqing Yue,Weihang Su,Qingyao Ai,Yichen Tang,Changyue Wang,Jiacheng Kang,Jingtao Zhan,Yiqun Liu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: DMoE proposes a modular architecture that decouples both expert modules and the router from the base LLM, enabling parametric knowledge injection without modifying base model parameters. It uses a lightweight uncertainty-aware router to selectively activate independently updatable experts attached only to the final FFN layer, preserving KV-cache reuse during inference.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing knowledge injection methods face a fundamental trade-off: retrieval-augmented generation offers flexibility but only prompt-level enhancement, while post-training approaches (e.g., fine-tuning) encode knowledge into shared parameters but risk catastrophic forgetting, knowledge conflicts, and expensive updates. The authors aim to achieve both flexibility and deep integration simultaneously.

Method: DMoE consists of three key components: (1) external knowledge corpora are converted into independently updatable MoE expert modules, fully decoupled from base model parameters; (2) a lightweight uncertainty-aware router is trained to detect when the base model lacks knowledge and selectively activate relevant experts; (3) experts are attached exclusively to the final-layer feed-forward network to preserve KV-cache reuse during autoregressive generation, enabling parameter-level augmentation without disrupting inference efficiency.

Result: Experiments on knowledge-intensive benchmarks demonstrate that DMoE consistently outperforms both retrieval-based and adapter-based baselines in answer quality.

Conclusion: DMoE offers an effective parametric knowledge injection paradigm that combines the flexibility of modular, independently updatable experts with the integration depth of parameter-level augmentation, all while maintaining inference efficiency through KV-cache preservation.

Abstract: Knowledge injection aims to equip large language models (LLMs) with external, domain-specific, or time-sensitive knowledge. Existing approaches typically face a trade-off between flexibility and integration: retrieval-augmented generation keeps knowledge outside the model but only provides prompt-level augmentation, whereas post-training based methods encode new knowledge into shared parameters but may introduce catastrophic forgetting, knowledge conflict, and costly updates. In this paper, we propose Decoupled Mixture-of-Experts (DMoE), a modular architecture for parametric knowledge injection that decouples both experts and the router from the base model. DMoE converts external knowledge corpora into independently updatable expert modules and uses a lightweight uncertainty-aware router to activate relevant experts only when the base model lacks sufficient knowledge during generation. To support efficient auto-regressive inference, DMoE attaches experts only to the final-layer feed-forward network, preserving KV-cache reuse while enabling parameter-level knowledge augmentation. Experiments on knowledge-intensive benchmarks show that DMoE consistently improves answer quality over retrieval and adapter-based baselines.

</details>


### [27] [The Linguistics Olympiads: Towards a New Corpus for Linguistics Research?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14257)
*Vlad A. Neacsu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This paper evaluates over 1800 linguistics olympiad problems (LOPs) as potential data sources for academic linguistics, discussing their strengths, limitations, and proposing criteria for their responsible research use, aiming to bridge the gap between linguistics olympiads and mainstream linguistics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Despite LOPs' worldwide popularity (43 territories in IOL 2025), their connections to academic linguistics (typology, relativity, fieldwork) and recent use as LLM benchmarks, they remain unintegrated into mainstream linguistics research. The paper aims to fill this gap by formally evaluating LOPs as linguistic data sources.

Method: The study critically examines a dataset of over 1800 LOPs, offering a structured evaluation of their potential as a novel corpus. It analyzes their strengths and limitations as research tools and maps the linguistic subfields into which they could fit, culminating in proposed criteria for their responsible academic use.

Result: The paper establishes a theoretical framework for LOPs, identifying their direct relevance to fields such as linguistic typology, linguistic relativity, and fieldwork. It also acknowledges their emerging role as LLM benchmarks in computational linguistics, while delineating criteria for responsible usage.

Conclusion: LOPs represent an underexplored but promising data source for multiple areas of linguistics. The proposed framework and criteria lay the foundation for integrating these puzzles into rigorous academic research, bridging the longstanding gap between linguistics olympiads and the academic community.

Abstract: Linguistics olympiad problems (LOPs) are a category of self-sufficient puzzles consisting of a scaled-down corpus representative of certain linguistic phenomena, from which the solver must deduce a primitive set of rules of the language and then translate a new set of elements. The linguistics olympiads (LOs) have become a worldwide phenomenon with 43 different territories taking part in the International Linguistics Olympiad (IOL) 2025. While the typology and solving strategies of LOPs have been analysed, their scientific facet and connections to academic linguistics have yet to be explored. LOPs are directly connected to many linguistic fields, e.g., linguistic typology, linguistic relativity, and linguistics fieldwork. Recently, LOPs have become a research focus as benchmarks for large language models, thus highlighting their usefulness in computational linguistics. Nevertheless, they have not yet been integrated into mainstream linguistics research. This paper attempts to open new directions of including this particular type of puzzle in academic research by offering a structured evaluation of LOPs as linguistic data sources and proposes criteria for their responsible use in academic research. Starting from a set of over 1800 LOPs, this study critically examines the potential of LOPs as a novel corpus for linguistics research by discussing their strengths and limitations as tools, as well as the areas of linguistics into which these problems could fit. This work forms the foundation for a broader initiative aimed at bridging the gap between LOs and academic linguistics, by establishing a robust theoretical framework for LOPs.

</details>


### [28] [Does the Judge Prefer English? Evaluating Language-Switching Invariance in LLM-as-a-Judge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14278)
*Shaojie Yin*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Judge-LS is a lightweight meta-evaluation protocol that tests whether LLM judges are sensitive to the language of presentation. By transforming English response pairs into Chinese and Chinese-English mixed variants, the study finds 10.7–14.4% preference flips, with all judges performing best in English — though translation-equivalent probes show no systematic English bias.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs are increasingly used as automatic evaluators for open-ended tasks, but their reliability across languages is underexplored. The authors ask whether an LLM judge evaluates answer quality or is influenced by the language in which comparisons are presented, raising concerns about fairness and robustness in multilingual evaluation settings.

Method: The authors propose Judge-LS, a meta-evaluation protocol that transforms the LLMBar benchmark's 419 response-pair items into three conditions: English, Chinese, and Chinese-English language-switched variants. Four API-accessible judges produce 13,408 pairwise judgments. Analysis includes preference flip rates, accuracy comparisons, translation-equivalent tie probes, confidence intervals, paired significance tests, and a sensitivity analysis excluding mechanically flagged high-risk variants. No training is required; only API calls are used.

Result: Chinese and language-switched presentations cause 10.7–14.4% preference flips relative to English across all judges, and every judge achieves its highest accuracy in English. However, translation-equivalent tie probes do not reveal a systematic English preference: most are judged as ties, and non-tie decisions more often favor Chinese.

Conclusion: LLM judges exhibit measurable language sensitivity, undermining their reliability in cross-lingual evaluation. Preference flips are significant, yet the absence of systematic English bias in tie probes suggests the effect is nuanced. Judge-LS offers a practical, low-cost protocol for auditing judge robustness across languages.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are now widely used as automatic judges for open-ended instruction-following evaluation. This practice is convenient, scalable, and often more semantically aware than reference-based metrics, but it also introduces a new reliability question: does a judge evaluate the quality of an answer, or does it also react to the language in which the comparison is presented? We propose Judge-LS, a lightweight meta-evaluation protocol that transforms LLMBar response-pair items into English, Chinese, and Chinese-English language-switched variants. A reliable judge should preserve its preference under label-preserving language transformations and should not prefer a language when two answers are translation-equivalent. We evaluate four API-accessible judges on the full 419-item LLMBar benchmark, producing 13,408 successful pairwise judgments. Across models, Chinese and language-switched presentations induce 10.7--14.4% preference flips relative to English, and all judges achieve their highest accuracy in English. However, translation-equivalent tie probes do not reveal a systematic English preference: most probes are judged as ties, and non-tie decisions more often favor Chinese. We add confidence intervals, paired significance tests, and an automatic transformation audit with a sensitivity analysis that excludes mechanically flagged high-risk variants. The experiment requires no model training, uses only API calls, and is feasible on modest local hardware.

</details>


### [29] [Retrospective Progress-Aware Self-Refinement for LLM Agent Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14302)
*Xinbei Ma,Congmin Zheng,Jiyang Qiu,Jiale Hong,Yao Yao,Xiangmou Qu,Jiaxin Yin,Xingyu Lou,Jun Wang,Weiwen Liu,Weinan Zhang,Zhuosheng Zhang,Hai Zhao*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: RePro trains LLM agents to retrospectively self-assess task progress, combining a retrospection warmup and composite reward optimization to improve long-horizon task performance without continuous external supervision, achieving up to 12% absolute gains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM-based RL agents lack metacognitive awareness of task progress, which hinders long-horizon scaling. Online progress prompting degrades performance, and retrospective progress assessment cannot emerge from outcome-reward training alone.

Method: RePro uses a forward-then-reflect rollout paradigm: agents execute actions online, then retrospectively reassess step-wise progress given the completed trajectory and outcome. Training includes (1) Retrospection Warmup using minimal external demonstrations to teach reflection format, and (2) RePro-PO with a composite reward that leverages self-generated progress signals, eliminating the need for continuous external supervision.

Result: Experiments on WebShop, ALFWorld, and Sokoban show that RePro enhances Qwen family models, achieving up to 12% absolute success rate gains.

Conclusion: Retrospective progress-aware training effectively instills metacognitive capabilities in LLM agents, enabling better long-horizon task performance through self-generated progress signals rather than external supervision.

Abstract: LLM-based agents trained with reinforcement learning optimize step-wise action prediction but lack metacognitive awareness of task progress, inducing a gap that hinders long-horizon scaling. A pilot study reveals that online progress prompting hurts performance while retrospective demonstrations help, yet this capability cannot emerge from outcome-reward training alone. We present RePro, Retrospective Progress-Aware Training, a framework that trains agents to self-generate progress signals via a forward-then-reflect rollout paradigm: the agent executes actions online, then retrospectively reassesses its step-wise progress given the completed trajectory and known outcome. RePro initializes with a Retrospection Warmup that teaches reflection format from minimal external demonstrations, then further trains through RePro-PO with a composite reward that produces self-generated signals without continuous external supervision. Experiments on WebShop, ALFWorld, and Sokoban show that RePro enhances the Qwen family's performance, with up to $12\%$ absolute success rate gains.

</details>


### [30] [Achieving Precise Text-To-Cypher Via Grounded Knowledge Graph Data Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14325)
*Francesco Cazzaro,Jessica Lennon,Ariadna Quattoni*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This paper presents a synthetic data generation method for fine-tuning small LLMs on Text-to-Cypher parsing, demonstrating that augmented small models can rival large proprietary models on major benchmarks while preserving data sovereignty in local deployments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Property Graphs are increasingly used for heterogeneous data, but querying them requires conversational interfaces based on Text-to-Cypher parsers. Deploying large proprietary LLMs locally raises data sovereignty concerns, while small LLMs typically underperform. The authors aim to bridge this gap without costly manual annotation.

Method: An automatic synthetic data generation method designed specifically for the Text-to-Cypher task, used to fine-tune small LLMs by augmenting training data without human annotation campaigns.

Result: Experiments across all major Text-to-Cypher benchmarks show significant performance improvements for small LLMs, enabling them to compete with much larger proprietary models.

Conclusion: Synthetic data generation enables small, locally deployable LLMs to achieve competitive Text-to-Cypher accuracy, ensuring data sovereignty without sacrificing performance or requiring expensive annotation efforts.

Abstract: Property Graphs are rapidly being adopted as database frameworks for representing heterogeneous data sources. To enable precise access to the information contained in them we need conversational interfaces based on Text-To-Cypher (Text2Cypher) parsers. This paper presents an automatic synthetic data generation method that can be leveraged to fine-tune small LLMs for this task. We conduct experiments on all the major Text-To-Cypher benchmarks, demonstrating that with our synthetic data generation approach we can significantly increase the performance of small LLMs, allowing them to compete with much larger proprietary models. This means that in settings in which models must be locally deployed we can ensure data-sovereignty without sacrificing accuracy and without costly annotation campaigns.

</details>


### [31] [Learning to Hear Hesitation: Continual Learning for Disfluency-Aware ASR](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14391)
*Henri-Leon Kordt,Theresa Pekarek Rosin,Jae Hee Lee,Stefan Wermter*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This paper addresses disfluent speech recognition by introducing explicit disfluency tokens into a pretrained ASR model and using continual learning to train on multiple datasets with varied disfluency distributions, analyzing the trade-off between marker learning and ASR performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current state-of-the-art ASR systems typically omit disfluencies (e.g., repetitions, false starts, filled pauses), causing information loss and hallucinations. Prior verbatim transcription approaches suffer from catastrophic forgetting when fine-tuned on limited disfluency datasets. The authors aim to preserve general-domain ASR knowledge while learning to handle diverse disfluency patterns.

Method: The authors introduce explicit disfluency tokens into a pretrained ASR model to establish stable token-level mechanisms. They then apply continual learning (CL) methods to sequentially train on additional datasets with varying disfluency distributions, avoiding catastrophic forgetting. They conduct a detailed analysis of model dynamics, examining the trade-off between disfluency marker learning and overall ASR performance, and investigate cross-attention head mechanisms shared across CL approaches.

Result: The analysis reveals a fundamental trade-off between learning disfluency markers and maintaining general ASR performance. Additionally, a consistent cross-attention head mechanism is identified that is shared across different continual learning methods, suggesting a common underlying model adaptation pattern when handling disfluencies.

Conclusion: Continual learning with explicit disfluency tokens is a viable approach for adapting ASR models to disfluent speech without catastrophic forgetting. The identified trade-off and shared cross-attention mechanisms provide insights for future work on robust, disfluency-aware ASR systems.

Abstract: Despite advances in large-scale Automatic Speech Recognition (ASR), disfluent speech remains challenging, as state-of-the-art systems are often optimized to omit disfluencies, leading to information loss and hallucinations. Prior work has focused on verbatim transcription and the integration of disfluency markers, but adapting models on limited datasets can lead to catastrophic forgetting of general-domain knowledge. We address this gap by leveraging continual learning (CL) with explicit disfluency tokens. We first introduce these tokens into a pretrained ASR model to establish stable token mechanisms, and then continue training on additional datasets with varying disfluency distributions. Through a detailed analysis of model dynamics during training, we identify a trade-off between marker learning and ASR performance, and a consistent cross-attention head mechanism shared across CL methods.

</details>


### [32] [Coping in Crisis: Computational Modeling of Coping Styles in Digital Crisis Discourse During the 2023 Turkiye Earthquake](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14420)
*Şevval Çakıcı*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Using over 1M Turkish tweets from the 2023 Türkiye earthquake, the study operationalizes Lazarus & Folkman's coping theory via a BERTurk classifier to detect problem-focused, emotion-focused, and meaning-making coping across crisis phases. The classifier (macro F1=0.693) reveals distinct temporal trajectories and shows anger correlates most with meaning-making, offering actionable insights for humanitarian response.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To determine whether coping theory can be reliably operationalized at scale using real-time social media data during disasters, and whether such detection can inform humanitarian organizations about a population's psychological state in politically polarized contexts.

Method: A multi-label BERTurk classifier was developed to detect three coping styles (problem-focused, emotion-focused, meaning-making) grounded in Lazarus & Folkman's (1984) theory, applied across four crisis phases. The model was trained and evaluated against a zero-shot mDeBERTa baseline, then applied to a corpus of over one million Turkish-language tweets posted after the February 6, 2023 earthquake.

Result: BERTurk achieved a macro F1 of 0.693 (vs. 0.324 for mDeBERTa). Temporal analysis showed problem-focused coping dominates early (urgency phase) then declines sharply; emotion-focused coping rises and stabilizes; meaning-making increases monotonically. Anger correlated most strongly with meaning-making (Spearman r=0.387), suggesting anger drives blame attribution rather than practical action.

Conclusion: Coping theory can be reliably operationalized in real-world digital crisis data. Detecting coping styles at scale can help humanitarian organizations tailor interventions to a population's actual psychological state throughout the disaster lifecycle.

Abstract: How do people cope when disaster strikes and can we detect it at scale, in real time, from what they write? This study addresses that question using over one million Turkish-language tweets posted in the aftermath of the February 6, 2023 earthquake in Turkiye, which unfolded in a deeply polarized political context just months before a national election. Drawing on Lazarus and Folkman's (1984) coping theory, we develop a multi-label BERTurk classifier to detect three coping styles (problem-focused, emotion-focused, and meaning-making) across four theoretically motivated crisis phases. BERTurk achieves a macro F1 of 0.693, substantially outperforming a zero-shot mDeBERTa baseline (macro F1 = 0.324). Applied to the full corpus, the classifier reveals a clear temporal trajectory: problem-focused coping dominates the urgency phase and declines sharply, emotion-focused coping rises and stabilizes, and meaning-making increases monotonically. Anger correlates most strongly with meaning-making (Spearman r = 0.387), suggesting it functions as a mobilizing force toward blame attribution rather than practical action. These findings demonstrate that coping theory can be reliably operationalized in real-world digital crisis data and that doing so can help humanitarian organizations tailor their responses to where a population actually is.

</details>


### [33] [MoDiCoL: A Modular Diagnostic Continual Learning Dataset for Robust Speech Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14459)
*Theresa Pekarek Rosin,Matthias Kerzel,Stefan Wermter*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces MoDiCoL, a modular diagnostic dataset and continual learning curriculum for studying ASR robustness under co-occurring distribution shifts (linguistic content, speaker traits, acoustic conditions), and evaluates how robustness is acquired, transferred, and forgotten across three continual learning strategies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing ASR benchmarks isolate individual distribution shift factors (e.g., noise, accents, speech impairments), failing to capture their real-world co-occurrence. Moreover, robustness is typically evaluated statically rather than as a dynamically evolving capability. The authors aim to fill this gap by enabling controlled, compositional analysis of multiple shift factors and studying how robustness develops over time through continual updates.

Method: The authors propose MoDiCoL, a Modular Diagnostic Continual Learning dataset that factorizes variation into three dimensions — linguistic content, speaker characteristics, and acoustic environments — allowing controlled combinatorial shifts. They also design a real-world-inspired continual learning curriculum that simulates sequential domain exposure, and benchmark three continual learning strategies (not further specified in the abstract) to analyze robustness acquisition, transfer, and catastrophic forgetting.

Result: The evaluation of three continual learning strategies yields detailed empirical insights into how ASR robustness evolves under non-stationary, multi-factor distribution shifts, including patterns of robustness acquisition, cross-condition transfer, and forgetting.

Conclusion: Robustness in ASR should be framed as a dynamic, continually developing capability. The MoDiCoL benchmark and curriculum provide a principled framework for diagnosing and improving ASR robustness under realistic, co-occurring distribution shifts.

Abstract: Modern Automatic Speech Recognition (ASR) systems have made remarkable progress on standard benchmarks, yet performance gaps have emerged under real-world distribution shifts, caused by recording conditions, accents, speech impairments, and noise. Existing datasets and benchmarks typically isolate these factors, which overlooks their co-occurrence in real-world applications. In this paper, we argue that model robustness can be treated as a dynamic capability that continually develops, and we introduce MoDiCoL, a Modular Diagnostic Continual Learning dataset designed for controlled analysis of linguistic content, speaker characteristics, and acoustic environments. Furthermore, we propose a real-world-inspired continual learning curriculum to simulate incremental updates and study how robustness is acquired, transferred, and forgotten. We evaluate three continual learning strategies and provide detailed insights into robustness under evolving conditions.

</details>


### [34] [Fodor and Pylyshyn's Systematicity Challenge Still Stands](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14512)
*Michael Goodale,Salvador Mascarenhas*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper argues that recent neural network models (specifically Lake & Baroni's meta-learning for compositionality) fail to meet Fodor & Pylyshyn's systematicity challenge. The authors show the model struggles with out-of-distribution rules and behaves unsystematically even on within-distribution tasks, concluding the challenge remains unmet.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Recent claims in cognitive science assert that neural networks have resolved Fodor and Pylyshyn's classic systematicity challenge — the argument that human cognition exhibits systematic biconditional dependencies (e.g., understanding "John saw Mary" iff one understands "Mary saw John"), which symbolic systems naturally explain but neural networks do not. The authors aim to scrutinize whether these claims, particularly Lake and Baroni's meta-learning for compositionality protocol, genuinely demonstrate human-like systematicity.

Method: The authors critically evaluate Lake and Baroni's meta-learning for compositionality model through empirical testing. They assess the model's ability to generalize to rules that are out-of-distribution relative to its training data, and examine whether the model behaves systematically on within-distribution problems as well.

Result: The model struggles to learn rules that deviate even slightly from the training distribution. Additionally, the model exhibits unsystematic behavior on many within-distribution problems, contrary to claims of human-like systematicity.

Conclusion: Fodor and Pylyshyn's systematicity challenge to neural networks remains unmet. Claims that neural networks have resolved this foundational puzzle in cognitive science are premature.

Abstract: The recent successes of neural networks producing human-like language have caused significant stir in cognitive science, with many researchers arguing that classical puzzles about human cognition and challenges to artificial intelligence are being solved by neural networks. A notable case is the argument from systematicity due to Jerry Fodor and Zenon Pylyshyn, argues that humans display systematic biconditional dependencies. For example, someone can understand the sentence "John saw Mary" just in case that they understand the sentence "Mary saw John." Symbolic systems explain this systematicity of language and thought, while neural networks offer no immediate explanation. Several recent articles argue that this challenge has now been met by neural networks. In particular, Brenden Lake and Marco Baroni argue that their meta-learning for compositionality protocol matches and perhaps explains human systematicity. We demonstrate that these conclusions are premature. Among other results, we found that their model struggles to learn rules that are even slightly out of distribution compared to their training data. Furthermore, the model behaves unsystematically even on many within-distribution problems. We conclude that Fodor and Pylyshyn's challenge to neural networks remains unmet.

</details>


### [35] [BayLing-Duplex: Native Full-Duplex Speech Dialogue with a Single Autoregressive LLM](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14528)
*Qingkai Fang,Shoutao Guo,Yang Feng*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: BayLing-Duplex is a native full-duplex SpeechLM that uses a single autoregressive LLM with a few special tokens to handle simultaneous listening and speaking, eliminating external VAD modules. Fine-tuned from GLM-4-Voice with only 400K samples, it achieves 92% turn-taking success and 100% interruption success while improving speech-response quality over Moshi and matching turn-based counterparts on standard benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing SpeechLMs (e.g., LLaMA-Omni, GLM-4-Voice) are turn-based and depend on external Voice Activity Detection (VAD) to mark turn endings, which fundamentally limits their ability to handle natural conversational phenomena like overlap, hesitation, and barge-in. The goal is to create a truly real-time, full-duplex spoken chatbot without auxiliary turn-taking modules.

Method: The authors introduce a native full-duplex SpeechLM where a single autoregressive LLM internally decides listening, speaking, and stopping states. The approach adds only a few special tokens to the standard vocabulary, enabling transfer across LLMs without architectural changes. Starting from the public GLM-4-Voice checkpoint, they fine-tune on 400K full-duplex samples, followed by a lightweight Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) stage.

Result: BayLing-Duplex achieves 92% turn-taking success and 100% interruption success on InstructS2S-Eval. The speech-response score improves from 2.17 (Moshi) to 3.39. It matches or surpasses its turn-based counterpart on Llama Questions, Web Questions, and Alpaca-Eval, demonstrating that full-duplex modeling does not degrade response quality.

Conclusion: Native full-duplex speech interaction can be achieved with minimal modifications to existing SpeechLM architectures — just a few special tokens and moderate fine-tuning data. This approach eliminates the VAD bottleneck, supports natural conversational dynamics, and preserves response quality on standard benchmarks.

Abstract: Real-time, full-duplex speech interaction is a key feature of next-generation spoken chatbots, allowing the model to listen and speak at the same time and to handle natural phenomena such as overlap, hesitation, and barge-in. Existing speech language models (SpeechLMs) such as LLaMA-Omni and GLM-4-Voice are still turn-based and rely on an external Voice Activity Detection (VAD) module to mark the end of the user's turn, which fundamentally limits their interactive ability. In this paper, we introduce BayLing-Duplex, a native full-duplex SpeechLM where a single autoregressive LLM decides when to listen, when to speak, and when to stop, with no auxiliary turn-taking module. The design adds only a few special tokens to the standard vocabulary, so it transfers across LLMs and reuses existing training and serving stacks with no architectural adaptation. Starting from the public GLM-4-Voice checkpoint and using only 400K full-duplex samples for fine-tuning followed by a lightweight DPO stage, BayLing-Duplex reaches 92% turn-taking success and 100% interruption success on InstructS2S-Eval, while improving the speech-response score from 2.17 to 3.39 over Moshi. BayLing-Duplex also matches or surpasses its turn-based counterpart on Llama Questions, Web Questions, and Alpaca-Eval, showing that simultaneous listen-and-speak modeling does not sacrifice response quality.

</details>


### [36] [SIMMER: Benchmarking Latent Failures in LLM Executable Planning with a World Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14574)
*Xiaoxin Lu,Ranran Haoran Zhang,Rui Zhang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: SIMMER is a benchmark for evaluating latent failures in LLM-generated plans for household tasks, using a symbolic kitchen world model. Experiments show even frontier LLMs achieve ≤17% error-free plans, with up to 56% containing latent failures (many irreversible). Counterfactual foresight simulation reduces latent failures by up to 72%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM planning benchmarks only evaluate immediate execution failures but ignore latent failures—flaws that don't halt execution immediately yet silently compromise goal achievement or cause irreversible harm. This gap motivates a dedicated benchmark to systematically assess and mitigate such failures.

Method: The authors construct SIMMER, a human-curated symbolic world model for the kitchen domain with 77 actions, 262 objects, and ~46,800 semantically realistic interactions derived from real-world cooking scripts. A state machine executor validates plans against this model, detecting three failure types: immediate precondition violations, latent hazards, and irreversible failures. Six LLMs are evaluated. A mitigation strategy using explicit state reasoning via counterfactual foresight simulation is also tested.

Result: Frontier LLMs achieve at most 17% error-free plans. Up to 56% of plans contain latent failures, with the majority leading to irreversible consequences. Counterfactual foresight simulation reduces latent failures by up to 72% and irreversible cases by up to 75%.

Conclusion: Latent failures are a critical, underexamined problem in LLM-based planning. Incorporating explicit state reasoning through counterfactual simulation significantly improves plan robustness, offering a promising direction for building safer autonomous agent planners.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed as planners for autonomous agents in household environments. While existing benchmarks evaluate whether LLM-generated plans execute successfully, they overlook a critical type of failure: latent failures. Unlike immediate failures that trigger instant feedback at execution time and enable timely correction, latent failures do not immediately halt plan execution but silently compromise goal achievement. In severe cases, they cause irreversible harm. To address this gap, we introduce SIMMER, a benchmark for evaluating latent failures in LLM planning through a human-curated symbolic world model grounded in the kitchen domain. SIMMER defines a world model comprising 77 actions, 262 unique objects, and approximately 46,800 possible interactions that are semantically realistic, derived from real-world cooking scripts. It then leverages a state machine executor that validates plans against the world model and detects immediate precondition violations, latent hazards, and irreversible failures. Experiments across six LLMs show that even frontier models achieve at most 17% error-free plans. Moreover, up to 56% of plans contain latent failures, the majority of which lead to irreversible consequences. We further demonstrate that explicit state reasoning via counterfactual foresight simulation can reduce latent failures by up to 72% and irreversible cases by up to 75%, suggesting a promising direction for more robust LLM planners.

</details>


### [37] [Persuasion Index: A Theory-Guided Framework for Persuasion Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14580)
*Liancheng Gong,Zhiyang Wang,Yiwei Xu,Julia Mendelsohn*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces Persuasion Index (PI), a theory-grounded taxonomy of 15 rhetorical dimensions with 55 lexicon- and rule-based sub-features, evaluated on four public datasets to show it provides interpretable, lightweight predictive signal for persuasion-related outcomes across domains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Identifying persuasive rhetorical cues is critical for detecting information manipulation, improving AI safety, and advancing public health communication, yet existing approaches lack a principled, theory-grounded, and transparent feature space for cross-domain analysis.

Method: The authors propose PI, a modular taxonomy of 15 dimensions rooted in persuasion theories (psychology and communication), operationalized via 55 sub-features using lexicons and rule-based detectors. They evaluate PI on four public datasets spanning different domains, styles, and outcome measures, using linear models to assess predictive signal and conducting dimension-level analyses to identify recurring and domain-specific patterns.

Result: PI features carry meaningful predictive signal via linear models while remaining computationally lightweight. Dimension-level analyses reveal both recurring associations between PI dimensions and persuasion outcomes across datasets and topic- and stance-specific variation.

Conclusion: PI offers a shared, interpretable feature space for principled and auditable rhetorical analysis across human and AI-mediated communication, released as an open-source package and web interface.

Abstract: Identifying persuasive rhetorical cues is critical across domains, from detecting information manipulation and improving AI safety to advancing public health communication. We propose Persuasion Index (PI), a taxonomy of 15 dimensions grounded in persuasion theories from psychology and communication, and one transparent implementation using 55 sub-features built from lexicons and rule-based detectors. The taxonomy is modular: individual detectors can be replaced while preserving the theoretical structure. By evaluating PI on four public datasets varying in domain, style, and outcome measures, we show that PI provides a shared feature space for interpreting rhetorical patterns associated with persuasion-related outcomes. Linear models show that PI features carry meaningful predictive signal while remaining computationally lightweight. Dimension-level analyses reveal recurring associations between PI dimensions and persuasion outcomes across datasets, while also highlighting topic- and stance-specific variation. We release PI as an open-source package and web interface for principled and auditable analysis of human and AI-mediated communication.

</details>


### [38] [LoSoNA: A Benchmark for Local Social Norm Adaptation in Group Conversations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14600)
*Mateusz Winiarek,Maksymilian Bilski,Mateusz Jacniacki*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: LoSoNA is a benchmark testing whether LLMs can infer hidden local conversational norms from group-chat transcripts and apply them in a one-turn response. Evaluating eight models under varying prompting conditions, only Gemini 3.1 Pro (84.2%) and Claude Fable 5 (81.6%) perform well with explicit norm-aware prompting; naive prompting yields limited results, with uneven gains or regressions across models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Online group chats operate under unstated, emergent local conversational norms. Current LLM evaluations largely overlook whether agents can recognize and adapt to such implicit social conventions. LoSoNA addresses this gap by providing a structured benchmark to test pragmatic social inference in multi-party dialogue settings.

Method: LoSoNA presents each model with a curated group-chat transcript where non-subject participants exhibit a hidden local norm through their interactions. A final "elicitor turn" then forces the subject model to respond in a way that reveals whether it has inferred the norm. Eight frontier and open-weight models are tested under four prompting conditions that vary how explicitly the model is instructed to treat prior conversation as normative evidence.

Result: Under naive prompting, most models perform poorly. Explicit norm-aware prompting yields uneven improvements: Gemini 3.1 Pro achieves 84.2% accuracy, Claude Fable 5 reaches 81.6%, while several other models show only marginal gains or even performance regressions.

Conclusion: LoSoNA demonstrates that inferring local conversational norms from precedent is a challenging task for current LLMs, with performance highly sensitive to prompting strategy and model choice. The benchmark contributes a novel evaluation framework for LLM social capabilities, highlighting a significant capability gap in pragmatic social reasoning.

Abstract: Online group chats are social spaces with local conversational norms that are rarely stated explicitly. The ability and willingness of LLM-based agents to recognize and adapt to these norms remains mostly unexplored. We introduce LoSoNA, a benchmark for local social norm adaptation in multi-party chat. Each scenario gives a subject model a curated group-chat transcript in which non-subject participants demonstrate a hidden local norm, followed by a final elicitor turn that forces a response revealing whether the subject has inferred that norm. We evaluate eight frontier and open-weight models under four prompting conditions that vary how explicitly the model is told to treat the prior conversation as evidence for how it should answer. Naive prompting remains limited for most models; explicit norm-aware prompting helps unevenly, with Gemini 3.1 Pro reaching $84.2\%$ and Claude Fable 5 reaching $81.6\%$, while several other models show small gains or regressions. LoSoNA contributes to recent calls for evaluating LLM social capabilities by testing whether models can infer local conversational norms from precedent and use them in a one-turn group-chat response.

</details>


### [39] [Characterizing Cultural Localization in AI-Generated Stories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14626)
*Shaily Bhatt,Supriti Vijay,Jeremiah Milbauer,Fernando Diaz*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This paper introduces a method to detect "templated localization" in AI-generated stories — where only surface-level cultural markers (names, locations) vary across nationalities while the underlying narrative remains identical. Analyzing stories from 5 models across 193 nationalities and 125 topics, they find only 9-17% of vocabulary accounts for cross-national variation, with repeated multi-word sequences remaining after marker removal, confirming a shared culturally-agnostic template. Additionally, cultural markers for 19 Global South countries are found to be stereotypical and offensive on average.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As AI systems are increasingly used globally, there is growing interest in their capacity for cultural localization. However, true cultural localization involves holistic adaptation of plots, values, and themes — not merely swapping surface-level markers. The authors aim to develop a systematic method to distinguish between superficial ("templated") localization and genuine cultural adaptation, motivated by concerns that current models may produce culturally flattened, stereotypical, or inauthentic content under the guise of localization.

Method: The authors propose a two-step method: (1) Identify lexical tokens that maximally distinguish stories across nationalities (the "cultural markers"), and (2) Measure the similarity of the residual narratives after removing those tokens. They generate stories using five models on 125 topics for 193 nationalities. They then quantify what proportion of vocabulary accounts for cross-national variation and examine whether residual narratives contain repeated multi-word sequences indicative of a shared template. Finally, they characterize the identified cultural markers for stereotypicality and offensiveness using human evaluation or existing toxicity/ stereotype classifiers.

Result: Only 9-17% of the total vocabulary distinguishes stories across nationalities, confirming that a small set of cultural markers drives surface-level variation. After removing these markers, narratives contain repeated multi-word sequences, strongly suggesting a shared, culturally-agnostic narrative template across all nationalities. Furthermore, cultural markers associated with 19 countries — predominantly in the Global South — are on average rated as offensive, revealing a systematic bias in how models represent non-Western cultures.

Conclusion: Current AI models predominantly perform templated rather than holistic cultural localization, relying on a small set of stereotypical and sometimes offensive cultural markers while recycling a uniform narrative template. This raises concerns about cultural authenticity, representation, and fairness in globally deployed AI systems. The proposed detection method offers a systematic tool for auditing and improving cultural localization in generative models.

Abstract: The global use of artificial intelligence has increased interest in assessing the ability to generate culturally localized content, including stories. Cultural localization in stories often occurs through either templated localization -- the use of cultural markers (e.g., names, locations) in a generic narrative -- or holistic localization -- the variation of plots, values, and themes, in addition to cultural markers. We propose a method to measure the degree to which content was generated through templated localization. Specifically, we identify the lexical tokens that distinguish stories across nationalities and measure the similarity of the narratives that remain after removing them. In stories generated by five models on 125 topics for 193 nationalities, our method is able to detect that only a small subset (9-17%) of the vocabulary accounts for the variation across nationalities and that the narratives that remain after removing them contain repeated multi-word sequences, suggesting the presence of a shared culturally-agnostic narrative template. Finally, we characterize the cultural markers for their stereotypicality and offensiveness, finding that markers from 19 countries, mostly located in the Global South, are on average offensive.

</details>


### [40] [AgentSpec: Understanding Embodied Agent Scaffolds Through Controlled Composition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14674)
*Jixuan Chen,Jianzhi Shen,Haoqiang Kang,Zhi Hong,Qingyi Jiang,Soham Bose,Yiming Zhang,Leon Leng,Amit Vyas,Lingjun Mao,Siru Ouyang,Kun Zhou,Lianhui Qin*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: AgentSpec is a modular framework for LLM-based embodied agents that standardizes interfaces between perception, memory, reasoning, reflection, action, and learning components, enabling systematic analysis of how scaffold design and module interactions — rather than isolated component strength — govern overall agent performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLM agent systems embed scaffolds (reasoning, memory, reflection, etc.) in tightly coupled pipelines, making it impossible to isolate contributions of individual components, compare alternative designs, or understand interaction effects between modules. A controlled, modular framework is needed for principled agent analysis and design.

Method: AgentSpec is proposed as a typed compositional framework where agents are defined as compositions of reusable policy components with standardized interfaces for perception, memory, reasoning, reflection, action, and optional learning. Components can be swapped and recombined under controlled conditions. The framework is instantiated and evaluated across four embodied environments: DeliveryBench, ALFRED, MiniGrid, and RoboTHOR, analyzing reasoning, memory, reflection, and RL modules across multiple model backbones.

Result: Key findings: (1) structured multi-granularity memory significantly improves long-horizon state tracking; (2) reasoning and memory interact non-uniformly across environments; (3) reflection modules exhibit a trade-off between correction capability and computational cost; (4) RL-trained policies compose best when optimized with deployment-time scaffold structure. Overall, scaffold compatibility and interaction effects, rather than isolated module strength, govern agent performance.

Conclusion: AgentSpec provides a controlled, modular foundation for studying, comparing, and designing composable LLM agents. The framework reveals that scaffold architecture and inter-module dynamics are more critical to performance than individual component quality, offering principles for principled agent system design.

Abstract: LLM agents are increasingly built not as single model calls, but as scaffolded systems that combine reasoning, memory, reflection, action execution, and learning. While such scaffolds often improve performance, they are often embedded in tightly coupled pipelines, making it difficult to isolate component contributions, compare alternative designs, or understand how module interactions shape agent behavior. We introduce AgentSpec, a modular specification framework that represents embodied agents as typed compositions of reusable policy components with standardized interfaces. AgentSpec standardizes the interfaces among perception, memory, reasoning, reflection, action, and optional learning, enabling components to be swapped and recombined under controlled conditions. We instantiate this framework across DeliveryBench, ALFRED, MiniGrid, and RoboTHOR, and analyze reasoning, memory, reflection, and reinforcement-learning modules across model backbones. Our results show that agent performance is governed by scaffold compatibility and interaction effects rather than isolated module strength. In particular, structured multi-granularity memory improves long-horizon state tracking, reasoning and memory interact non-uniformly across environments, reflection trades off correction and cost, and RL-trained policies compose best when optimized with deployment-time scaffold structure. AgentSpec provides a controlled foundation for studying, comparing, and designing composable LLM agents. Our code, baselines and interactive playground are publicly available at https://agentspec-embodied.github.io.

</details>


### [41] [CORA: Analyzing and bridging thinking-answer gap in Multimodal RLVR via Consistency-Oriented Reasoning Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14691)
*Jiayue Cao,Zhicong Lu,Xuehan Sun,Wei Jia,Hongling Zheng,Changyuan Tian,Zichuan Lin,Wenqian Lv,Nayu Liu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper analyzes the problem of semantic inconsistency between reasoning traces and final answers in RLVR-trained large vision-language models (LVLMs), then proposes CORA—a method integrating a consistency reward model with Hybrid Reward Advantage Splitting to align reasoning with answers, improving both task performance and reasoning faithfulness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing RLVR methods for multimodal scenarios focus on visual coverage and hallucination reduction but overlook thinking-answer semantic inconsistency, where the reasoning process contradicts or diverges from the final answer. The authors demonstrate this issue persists throughout GRPO training and at inference time, motivating a dedicated solution.

Method: The authors propose Consistency-Oriented Reasoning Alignment (CORA), which has two key components: (1) a lightweight plug-and-play consistency reward model that quantifies semantic alignment between reasoning traces and final answers, integrated into the RLVR reward signal; and (2) Hybrid Reward Advantage Splitting (HRAS), a mechanism that stably balances task reward optimization and consistency reward optimization during GRPO training by splitting advantage estimates.

Result: Extensive experiments across representative multimodal reasoning benchmarks and mainstream LVLMs demonstrate that CORA improves task performance while effectively mitigating thinking-answer inconsistency, yielding more faithful reasoning traces compared to baseline RLVR approaches.

Conclusion: Thinking-answer semantic inconsistency is a significant and under-addressed problem in RLVR for LVLMs. CORA provides an effective, lightweight solution by explicitly optimizing for consistency alongside task performance, resulting in more reliable and faithful multimodal reasoning.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) has successfully elicited the reasoning capabilities of large language models, motivating its extension to multimodal scenarios. Existing methods primarily focus on improving the visual coverage of reasoning traces and mitigating visual hallucinations, but underestimate the semantic inconsistency between the reasoning process and the final answer. In this paper, we delve into thinking-answer inconsistency in RLVR for large vision-language models (LVLMs), showing thorough analyses of rollouts collected throughout Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) training process and post-RLVR evaluation outputs that this issue persists during training and remains present during inference. Motivated by the analysis, we propose Consistency-Oriented Reasoning Alignment (CORA), which introduces thinking-answer semantic consistency into RLVR through a lightweight plug-and-play consistency reward model, and further incorporates Hybrid Reward Advantage Splitting (HRAS) to stably coordinate task and consistency optimization. Extensive experiments across representative multimodal reasoning benchmarks and mainstream LVLMs show that CORA improves task performance while effectively mitigating thinking-answer inconsistency, leading to more faithful reasoning traces.

</details>


### [42] [AdaSR: Adaptive Streaming Reasoning with Hierarchical Relative Policy Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14694)
*Junlong Tong,Wenqi Xu,Yingqi Fan,Anhao Zhao,Xuan Lu,Yang Tan,Xiaoyu Shen*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: AdaSR proposes an adaptive streaming reasoning framework that lets models think during input streaming and deliberate after, using Hierarchical Relative Policy Optimization (HRPO) to learn when and how much to compute, balancing accuracy, efficiency, and latency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing large reasoning models follow a rigid read-then-think paradigm unsuitable for dynamic streaming scenarios (audio/video). Current streaming reasoning methods rely on supervised imitation of pre-constructed trajectories, lacking flexibility in deciding when to think and how much computation to allocate.

Method: AdaSR enables reasoning during streaming and final deliberation post-stream. The core is Hierarchical Relative Policy Optimization (HRPO), which decomposes policy optimization into streaming reasoning and deep reasoning phases with fine-grained advantage assignment (rather than uniform sequence-level advantage). HRPO combines format rewards, accuracy rewards, and adaptive thinking rewards to enforce valid reasoning protocols, preserve task performance, and encourage latency-aware computation allocation.

Result: Experiments show AdaSR achieves a better balance among reasoning accuracy, computational efficiency, and streaming latency compared with supervised fine-tuning baselines.

Conclusion: AdaSR with HRPO effectively optimizes hierarchical reasoning in streaming contexts, enabling models to flexibly decide when and how much to think, yielding superior accuracy-efficiency-latency trade-offs.

Abstract: Large reasoning models typically follow a read-then-think paradigm: they observe the complete input, reason over a static context, and then produce the answer. Yet many real-world scenarios are inherently dynamic, such as audio and video stream, where information arrives as a continuous stream and models must reason, update, and respond under partial observations. Recent streaming reasoning methods allow models to think while reading, but they largely rely on supervised imitation of pre-constructed trajectories, which limits their flexibility. In this paper, we propose AdaSR, an adaptive streaming reasoning framework that enables models to reason during input streaming and perform final deliberation once the stream is complete, learning when to think, and how much computation to allocate across different stages. To optimize this hierarchical reasoning process, we introduce Hierarchical Relative Policy Optimization (HRPO), which decomposes policy optimization into streaming reasoning and deep reasoning phases, providing more fine-grained advantage assignment instead of uniformly distributing a single sequence-level advantage over all tokens. HRPO integrates format, accuracy, and adaptive thinking rewards to enforce valid reasoning protocols, preserve final task performance, and encourage latency-aware computation allocation. Experiments show that AdaSR achieves a better balance among reasoning accuracy, computational efficiency, and streaming latency compared with supervised fine-tuning baseline. We release our code at https://github.com/EIT-NLP/StreamingLLM/tree/main/AdaSR.

</details>


### [43] [GAGPO: Generalized Advantage Grouped Policy Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.13217)
*Siyuan Zhu,Chao Yu,Rongxin Yang,Zongkai Liu,Jinjun Hu,Qiwen Chen,Yibo Zhang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: GAGPO is a critic-free RL method that addresses credit assignment in multi-turn LLM agents by constructing non-parametric grouped value proxies from sampled rollouts, enabling TD/GAE-style temporal advantage computation without auxiliary value models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multi-turn LLM agent training suffers from sparse, trajectory-level rewards, making it hard to attribute success/failure to individual actions. Existing solutions rely on costly auxiliary value models (critics). A critic-free method for step-level credit assignment is needed.

Method: GAGPO constructs a non-parametric grouped value proxy from sampled rollouts, computes TD/GAE-style temporal advantages by recursively propagating outcomes backward, and applies group-wise advantage normalization with action-level importance ratios to extract stable, step-localized optimization signals.

Result: On ALFWorld and WebShop, GAGPO outperforms strong RL baselines, showing faster early-stage learning, improved interaction efficiency, and smoother optimization dynamics.

Conclusion: GAGPO provides a simple yet effective critic-free framework for multi-turn agentic RL, successfully addressing temporal credit assignment without auxiliary value models.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning has become a powerful paradigm for post-training large language model agents, yet credit assignment in multi-turn environments remains a challenge. Agents often receive sparse, trajectory-level rewards only at the end of an episode, making it difficult to determine which intermediate actions contributed to success or failure. As a result, propagating delayed outcomes back to individual decision steps without relying on costly auxiliary value models remains an open problem. We propose Generalized Advantage Grouped Policy Optimization (GAGPO), a critic-free reinforcement learning method for precise, step-aligned temporal credit assignment. GAGPO constructs a non-parametric grouped value proxy from sampled rollouts and uses it to compute TD/GAE-style temporal advantages, recursively propagating outcome supervision backward through time. Combined with group-wise advantage normalization and an action-level importance ratio, GAGPO extracts stable, localized optimization signals directly from multi-turn trajectories. Experiments on ALFWorld and WebShop show that GAGPO outperforms strong reinforcement learning baselines. Further analyses demonstrate faster early-stage learning, improved interaction efficiency, and smoother optimization dynamics, suggesting that GAGPO offers a simple yet effective framework for multi-turn agentic reinforcement learning.

</details>


<div id='cs.LG'></div>

# cs.LG [[Back]](#toc)

### [44] [Can Editing 1 Neuron Fix Repetition Loops in LLMs?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13705)
*Aristotelis Lazaridis,Aman Sharma,Dylan Bates,Brian King,Vincent Lu,Jack FitzGerald*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Gemma 4 models exhibit a reproducible failure mode ("doom looping") on long factual enumeration tasks, collapsing into repetition. The authors localize this behavior to a small set of MLP neurons (or routed experts in MoE models) and show that static weight edits can eliminate the loops while preserving benchmark performance. However, under longer thinking budgets, a residual non-convergent failure persists, which they argue is a knowledge-precision problem that weight surgery cannot fully solve.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper is motivated by the observation that Gemma 4 instruction-tuned models reliably collapse into repetitive loops (up to 95% rate) when prompted with long factual enumeration tasks (e.g., listing all 151 original Pokémon). The authors seek to determine whether this pathological behavior can be localized to specific parameters and surgically removed via weight editing, and to understand the limits of such an approach.

Method: The authors employ a multi-stage localization and intervention pipeline: (1) per-layer ablation to identify which layers contribute to looping; (2) per-neuron attribution to pinpoint individual MLP neurons (or routed experts in the 26B-A4B MoE model) responsible; (3) full-generation sweeps to confirm candidate neurons; (4) static weight edits — including sign inversion of a single neuron in the E2B model — to suppress the identified neurons; and (5) evaluation on both loop-induction prompts and general-purpose benchmarks to measure specificity. They also study behavior under extended thinking budgets to probe residual failure modes.

Result: Key findings: (1) Looping behavior localizes to a small set of MLP neurons — as few as one neuron in the E2B model. (2) The number of neurons requiring editing grows with model scale. (3) Surgical weight edits eliminate loop patterns at normal generation budgets while preserving general benchmark scores. (4) Under longer thinking budgets, larger models exhibit "doom looping" — non-convergent self-correction over unrecallable facts — which the same edits reduce but do not eliminate. (5) The residual failure is framed as a knowledge-precision problem: weight surgery can delete a loop but cannot supply missing facts.

Conclusion: The paper demonstrates both the feasibility and the limits of neuron-level weight surgery for fixing generation pathologies. While concrete looping behaviors can be localized and edited out with minimal side effects, the underlying problem — the model's inability to recall precise factual knowledge under extended reasoning — is not addressable by removing circuits. This delineates a boundary: weight edits can delete harmful patterns but cannot inject missing knowledge.

Abstract: Yes. Can it cure doom loops? Probably not.
  The Gemma 4 instruction-tuned models share a reproducible failure: on long factual enumeration prompts, such as listing every episode of a TV series, the 88 IAU constellations, or the 151 original Pokemon, they collapse into repetition, either a tight verbatim loop or a list whose entries decay onto a single answer. These loops occur at rates as high as 95% and survive prompt rewording, inference-engine changes, and most sampling adjustments. In this paper we explore whether this behavior is localized enough to remove by weight edits. To localize the cause, we use per-layer ablation and per-neuron attribution, then confirm the strongest candidates with full-generation sweeps. The loops trace to a small set of MLP neurons (or, in the 26B-A4B Mixture-of-Experts model, a few routed experts) which we suppress with static weight edits. These "surgeries" can be as small as a single sign-inverted neuron (in the E2B model). The size of the effective edits grows with model scale, but in all cases, the loop patterns can be addressed at normal generation budgets while preserving general-purpose benchmark scores. However, the edits do not solve everything: we also study longer thinking budgets, where the two larger models most visibly enter doom looping, i.e. a non-convergent regime in which the model self-corrects in circles over a fact it cannot recall, exhausting the budget without committing to a final answer. We show this residual failure is reduced but not eliminated by the same edits, and argue it is fundamentally a knowledge-precision problem rather than a removable circuit; weight surgery can delete a loop, but it cannot supply a missing fact. Our results are both a feasibility demonstration, that is, evidence that a concrete generation pathology can be localized to a few parameters and edited out, and a delineation of where that approach stops.

</details>


### [45] [Efficient On-Device Diffusion LLM Inference with Mobile NPU](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13740)
*Tuowei Wang,Yanfan Sun,Ju Ren*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: llada.cpp is an NPU-aware inference framework for diffusion LLMs on smartphones that uses Multi-Block Speculative Decoding, Dual-Path Progressive Revision, and Swap-Optimized Memory Runtime to achieve 17x-42x latency reduction while preserving generation quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Diffusion LLMs enable parallel token generation suited for mobile inference, but repeated denoising creates significant compute demands. Mobile NPUs offer dense matrix throughput but are challenging to exploit due to: (1) shrinking per-block effective workloads from token commitment, (2) KV cache reuse complications from token revision, and (3) high remapping/data transfer overheads from limited NPU-visible address space.

Method: llada.cpp introduces three key techniques: (1) Multi-Block Speculative Decoding that fills late-stage decoding workload gaps by speculatively decoding future-block tokens; (2) Dual-Path Progressive Revision that keeps committed tokens revisable until stable and uses a CPU-side path to refresh unstable tokens without stalling NPU execution; (3) Swap-Optimized Memory Runtime that compacts NPU-visible address layouts and overlaps data staging with NPU computation to minimize remapping and transfer overheads.

Result: Evaluated across diverse hardware platforms and dLLM workloads, llada.cpp reduces LLaDA-8B generation latency by 17x-42x over the CPU baseline with prefix KV cache reuse, while preserving generation quality.

Conclusion: llada.cpp demonstrates that careful co-design of dLLM inference algorithms and NPU execution characteristics can unlock significant mobile acceleration, making diffusion LLMs practical for latency-sensitive on-device deployment.

Abstract: Diffusion large language models (dLLMs) accelerate generation by denoising multiple tokens in parallel, making them attractive for latency-sensitive mobile inference. However, repeated denoising introduces substantial computation on smartphones. Mobile neural processing units (NPUs) offer high-throughput dense matrix computation, but efficiently exploiting them remains challenging: token commitment shrinks per-block effective workloads, token revision complicates KV cache reuse, and limited NPU-visible address space incurs costly remapping and data transfer overheads.
  In this paper, we propose llada.cpp, the first NPU-aware inference framework for accelerating dLLMs on smartphones. llada.cpp aligns block-wise dLLM inference with the execution characteristics of mobile NPUs through three techniques. (1) Multi-Block Speculative Decoding fills the shrinking workload in late-stage current-block decoding with speculative future-block tokens. (2) Dual-Path Progressive Revision keeps committed tokens revisable until stable and refreshes unstable tokens through a CPU-side path without stalling dense NPU execution. (3) Swap-Optimized Memory Runtime compacts NPU-visible address layouts and overlaps data staging with NPU computation to reduce remapping and transfer overheads. We implement llada.cpp as an end-to-end framework and evaluate it across diverse hardware platforms and dLLM workloads. llada.cpp reduces LLaDA-8B generation latency by 17x-42x over the CPU baseline with prefix KV cache reuse, while preserving generation quality.

</details>


### [46] [High-Frequency Pricing at Scale for E-Commerce](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13741)
*Stefan Birr,Tobias Huelden,Mones Raslan,Adele Gouttes,Andreas Schmitt,Mateusz Koren,Johannes Stephan,Robert Streek,Manuel Kunz,Tim Januschowski*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Zalando developed a forecast-then-optimize pricing tool using gradient-boosted trees and multi-objective optimization for fashion e-commerce sales campaigns. The system reduces pricing decision time from hours to minutes and, validated across 23 A/B tests in 12 markets, achieves ~6% higher profit while maintaining sales/revenue. It now handles most algorithmic pricing for sales campaigns at Zalando.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing pricing systems at Zalando operated at weekly granularity, which was inadequate for the volatile demand patterns and rapid decision-making required during sales campaigns. The prior manual-algorithmic hybrid approach was slow (hours per pricing cycle) and failed to optimally balance short-term revenue with long-term profitability at scale (5M+ articles). The authors sought to automate and accelerate this process while improving profit outcomes.

Method: A forecast-then-optimize architecture is employed: (1) daily-resolution demand forecasting using gradient-boosted trees, and (2) a multi-objective optimization framework that jointly maximizes long-term profit and net merchandise value. The optimization takes forecasted demand as input and determines optimal price adjustments across the entire article catalog. The system was validated through 23 A/B tests across 12 markets during 2023-2024 sales campaigns.

Result: The new system reduced pricing decision time from hours to minutes. Across 23 A/B tests in 12 markets, it achieved approximately 6% higher profit while maintaining statistically equivalent performance on sales volume and revenue compared to the previous manual-algorithmic hybrid baseline. Based on these results, the algorithm was deployed to production and now handles the majority of algorithmic pricing decisions for sales campaigns at Zalando.

Conclusion: The forecast-then-optimize framework effectively addresses the unique challenges of fashion e-commerce sales campaigns. The combination of gradient-boosted tree demand forecasting with multi-objective optimization proves to be a practical and scalable solution for real-time, fine-grained pricing at industrial scale, delivering measurable profit improvements without sacrificing other business metrics.

Abstract: This paper presents the design, development, and implementation of a specialized forecast-then-optimize algorithmic pricing tool for sales campaigns in fashion e-commerce. Sales events present unique challenges for pricing including volatile demand patterns, rapid pricing decisions, and the need to balance short-term revenue with long-term profitability. We describe our approach combining daily-resolution demand forecasting using gradient-boosted trees with a multi-objective optimization framework that maximizes both long-term profit and net merchandise value for more than 5 million articles. Our solution addresses key limitations of existing weekly-granularity systems by implementing a forecast-then-optimize architecture that reduces pricing decision time from hours to minutes. We validate our approach through 23 A/B tests across 12 markets during 2023-2024 sales campaigns at Zalando, one of Europe's leading online fashion retailers. Experimental results demonstrate that the new pricing system achieves approximately 6% higher profit while maintaining equivalent performance on sales and revenue compared to the previous manual-algorithmic hybrid approach. Based on these results, the algorithm was successfully deployed to production and now handles the majority of algorithmic pricing decisions for sales campaigns at the company.

</details>


### [47] [A fully GPU-based workflow for building physics emulators of hypersonic flows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13742)
*Fabian Paischer,Dylan Rubini,Deniz A. Bezgin,Aaron B. Buhendwa,David Hauser,Florian Sestak,Johannes Brandstetter,Sebastian Kaltenbach,Nikolaus A. Adams*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Introduces a GPU-based workflow combining differentiable CFD solver (JAX-Fluids) with neural emulators, using residual-based refinement and uncertainty quantification to achieve physically consistent predictions of hypersonic flows including shock waves, even beyond training distributions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional reduced-order models and neural emulators fail to capture steep gradients (e.g., shock waves) with physical consistency in supersonic/hypersonic flows — a critical capability for industrial engineering design. There is a need for surrogates that remain reliable beyond their training distribution.

Method: A fully GPU-based workflow integrating: (1) JAX-Fluids, a differentiable high-fidelity solver, for rapid dataset generation; (2) neural emulator training with multiple architectures analyzed for scaling behavior; (3) residual-based refinement using the differentiable solver to improve physical consistency, operable with only mesh and input parameters; (4) uncertainty quantification augmentation.

Result: Architecture scaling analysis reveals strengths and shortcomings of various model designs. Residual-based refinement substantially reduces residuals and improves physical consistency, enabling training even when only mesh and input parameters are available. The combined approach yields reliable predictions beyond the training distribution.

Conclusion: The integration of differentiable simulation with residual-based refinement produces physics emulators sufficiently robust for real-world engineering design loops, representing a significant advance over traditional neural surrogates for complex compressible flows.

Abstract: The ability to resolve complex physical phenomena with high fidelity and at low computational cost is central to addressing key challenges in modern engineering. A prime example lies in hypersonic flows, where the precise prediction of the full flowfield topology, in particular with respect to shock wave location and intensity, is critical. Yet supersonic and hypersonic flows continue to be a stumbling block for traditional reduced-order models and neural emulators that struggle to capture steep gradients in flow states with physical consistency in applications of industrial relevance.
