To that end, we introduce a fully GPU based workflow that integrates accelerated data generation with the training of neural emulators augmented by uncertainty quantification and physics-aware refinement. Our workflow is enabled by a differentiable high-fidelity solver (JAX-Fluids) which we employ for rapid dataset creation and residual-based improvement of the neural emulator to enhance physical consistency. Building on this framework, we first present a suite of model architectures and analyze their scaling behavior to expose their strengths and shortcomings. We then show that residual-based refinement enables training on cases where only mesh and input parameters are available, substantially reducing residuals and improving physical consistency. Together, differentiable simulation and residual-based refinement yield physics emulators that remain reliable beyond their training distribution, a key requirement for deploying surrogates in real-world engineering design loops.

</details>


### [48] [FedSPC: Shared Parameter Correction for Personalized Federated Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13748)
*Kannanthodath Induchoodan Ajay Menon,Christian Prehofer,Yunfei Xu,Toru Hirano*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: FedSPC introduces a modular control-variate correction applied solely to shared parameters in personalized federated learning, preventing inconsistent shared updates caused by differing local objectives, and boosting performance across multiple PFL settings and methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In PFL methods that split models into shared and personalized parameters, shared parameters are updated by clients optimizing divergent local objectives, causing inconsistent gradient updates that degrade the quality of the shared representation.

Method: FedSPC is a modular correction technique that applies control-variate correction exclusively to the shared parameters of any PFL method, leaving personalized parameters untouched. It is compatible with three common PFL paradigms: shared feature extractors, shared classifiers, and fully shared models with local regularization.

Result: Experiments on CIFAR-100 and Tiny-ImageNet using ViT, ResNet-34, and VGG-11 demonstrate that FedSPC consistently improves the performance of representative PFL methods: FedPer, FedRep, FedBABU, LG-FedAvg, and Ditto.

Conclusion: FedSPC provides a simple yet effective, plug-and-play correction mechanism that mitigates the shared parameter inconsistency problem in PFL, yielding consistent accuracy gains across diverse architectures, datasets, and PFL algorithms.

Abstract: Personalized federated learning (PFL) is one of the important approaches in federated learning for addressing statistical heterogeneity while enabling client-specific adaptation. Many PFL methods split the model into shared and personalized parameters, which are jointly trained on each client. However, this creates an optimization issue: shared parameters are updated by clients optimizing different local objectives, which can lead to inconsistent shared updates and weaken the shared representation. To address this problem, we propose Federated Shared Parameter Correction (FedSPC), a modular correction method for PFL. FedSPC applies control-variate correction only to the shared parameters of a given PFL method, while leaving personalized parameters unchanged. It can be integrated into three common PFL settings: shared feature extractors, shared classifiers, and fully shared models with local regularization. Experiments on CIFAR-100 and Tiny-ImageNet with ViT, ResNet-34, and VGG-11 show that FedSPC improves performance across representative PFL methods, including FedPer, FedRep, FedBABU, LG-FedAvg, and Ditto.

</details>


### [49] [The Weight Norm Sets the Grokking Timescale: A Causal Delay Law](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13753)
*Truong Xuan Khanh,Doan Hoang Viet,Luu Duc Trung,Phan Thanh Duc*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Grokking delay is causally determined by weight norm: when the norm is clamped at a fixed multiple of a critical value Wc, the grokking time follows an exponential law T_grok ∝ exp(α ρ), with α ≈ 7.5, rather than being prevented by large norms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Resolve a dispute in the grokking literature: some works claim a critical weight norm triggers grokking, while others observe grokking without any fixed norm. The authors aim to establish causality by intervening on the norm during training, rather than passively observing it.

Method: The authors train neural networks on modular arithmetic tasks with weight decay (free training) to identify a critical weight norm Wc (stable across seeds and learning rates, scaling as a power law with modulus). They then intervene by clamping the weight norm to fixed multiples ρ of Wc and measuring grokking delay T_grok. They also ablate with LayerNorm to decouple weight scale from network function.

Result: Under free training with weight decay, grokking occurs when weight norm reaches Wc (CV 1–2%). When norm is clamped at ρ·Wc, T_grok ∝ exp(α ρ) with α ≈ 7.5 fits across four moduli (R² = 0.996). Held norm shifts delay ~19× across the swept range vs. ~2× for learning rate. Clamping above Wc slows rather than prevents grokking. Removing LayerNorm restores the exponential law; with LayerNorm, the norm dependence vanishes.

Conclusion: Weight norm causally controls grokking delay via an exponential relationship when held fixed, complementing the logarithmic delay predicted for freely decaying norms. This provides a unified mechanistic picture and settles the dispute: norm matters, but the functional form depends on whether it is free or pinned.

Abstract: Grokking is the delayed onset of generalization in neural networks, arising long after they fit the training data. Whether the weight norm causes this delay is disputed: some studies report a critical norm at the transition, others observe grokking with no fixed norm at all. We settle this by intervening on the norm during training rather than only observing it. Under free training with weight decay, networks grok when the weight norm reaches a value Wc that varies little across seeds and learning rates (CV 1 to 2 percent) and grows with the modular base as a power law. When we instead clamp the norm to a fixed multiple rho of Wc and hold it there, the network still groks, but the delay follows T_grok proportional to exp(alpha rho). One exponent, alpha near 7.5, fits this delay across four moduli (R^2 = 0.996). Over the swept ranges the held norm moves the delay by about 19x and the learning rate by only about 2x, and holding the norm above Wc slows grokking rather than preventing it. A final LayerNorm removes the dependence by decoupling weight scale from the network function; without it the exponential law returns. This pinned-norm delay is the exponential counterpart to the logarithmic delay predicted for a freely contracting norm.

</details>


### [50] [D2H-AD: A Hybrid Model Utilizing Hyperdimensional Computing for Advanced Anomaly Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13754)
*Ghazal Ghajari,Elaheh Ghajari,Ashutosh Ghimire,Saeid Ataei,Faris Alsulami,Fathi Amsaad*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: D2H-AD is a hyperdimensional computing framework for anomaly detection that integrates distance-based similarity and density-aware encoding, outperforming five baselines (HDAD, ODHD, One-Class SVM, Isolation Forest, Autoencoders) across benchmarks while being lightweight, interpretable, and suitable for TinyML/edge deployments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional ML/DL anomaly detection methods require large labeled datasets, incur high computational costs, and face scalability challenges in edge and high-dimensional settings. Existing HDC-based anomaly detection approaches lack unified distance-density encoding, limiting their representational power.

Method: D2H-AD proposes a unified HDC framework combining distance-based similarity and density-aware encoding. Ablation studies isolate the contribution of hyperdimensional encoding by comparing ROC-AUC gains against applying the same density-distance scoring in the original feature space.

Result: Hyperdimensional encoding alone yields up to 5.4% higher ROC-AUC than original feature space scoring. D2H-AD consistently outperforms HDAD, ODHD, One-Class SVM, Isolation Forest, and Autoencoders on all evaluated datasets, with superior F1 and ROC-AUC, plus robustness to class imbalance, noise, and data complexity.

Conclusion: D2H-AD demonstrates that HDC can deliver accurate, interpretable, and energy-efficient anomaly detection. Its small memory footprint, binary computations, and low-latency operation make it particularly attractive for resource-constrained, real-time TinyML and edge AI deployments.

Abstract: Anomaly detection is a fundamental component of intelligent systems with applications in healthcare, cybersecurity, smart grids, and IoT environments. Although conventional machine learning and deep learning methods have demonstrated effectiveness in identifying anomalies, they often rely on large labeled datasets, incur high computational costs, and face scalability challenges in edge and high-dimensional settings. This paper presents D2H-AD, a novel anomaly detection framework based on Hyperdimensional Computing (HDC), a brain-inspired paradigm that represents information using high-dimensional distributed vectors. Unlike existing HDC-based methods, D2H-AD integrates distance-based similarity and density-aware encoding within a unified framework, improving anomaly representation and detection performance. Ablation studies show that hyperdimensional encoding alone yields up to 5.4% higher ROC-AUC than applying the same density-distance scoring directly in the original feature space. Furthermore, D2H-AD consistently outperforms five established baselines, namely HDAD, ODHD, One-Class SVM, Isolation Forest, and Autoencoders, across all evaluated datasets. The framework is lightweight, interpretable, and computationally efficient, making it suitable for resource-constrained and real-time applications. We validate D2H-AD on five benchmark datasets and demonstrate superior F1-score and ROC-AUC performance, together with robustness to class imbalance, noise, and data complexity. In addition to improved accuracy, D2H-AD offers scalability, a small memory footprint, and low-latency operation enabled by binary computations and a compact design. These properties make it particularly attractive for TinyML and edge AI deployments. The proposed framework highlights the potential of HDC for accurate, interpretable, and energy-efficient anomaly detection in dynamic environments.

</details>


### [51] [Contract-Based Compositional Shielding for Safe Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14130)
*Omar Adalat,Edwin Hamel-De le Court,Francesco Belardinelli*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper addresses safe coordination in multi-agent reinforcement learning where global safety constraints cannot be enforced unilaterally. It proposes a decentralized shielding approach using shared LTL_safe specifications decomposed into local obligations, with a non-stationary multi-armed bandit selecting among obligation tuples at learning time to optimize team reward while preserving end-to-end safety guarantees.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In multi-agent systems, purely factorized (per-agent) safety permissions often rule out team-optimal behaviors that are safe only through inter-agent coordination. Existing decentralized shields cannot capture such coordinated safe behaviors, while centralized runtime control is undesirable. The authors aim to recover team-optimal safe behavior under fully decentralized execution without centralised runtime control.

Method: The approach uses a shared global LTL_safe specification φ, decomposed into tuples of local LTL_safe obligations whose conjunction implies φ. Agents treat each other's local obligations as assumptions, with the entire contract tuple certified simultaneously and projected into local action masks. At learning time, a non-stationary multi-armed bandit algorithm selects among a library of local obligation tuples to maximize team reward, while maintaining deterministic safety guarantees throughout training and deployment.

Result: The method was evaluated across 6 environments and 15 algorithmic variants, demonstrating its effectiveness in recovering team-optimal safe behaviors that factorized approaches cannot achieve, all while preserving end-to-end safety guarantees under decentralized execution.

Conclusion: Decentralized shielding with coordinated obligation tuples, combined with multi-armed bandit selection at learning time, successfully enables team-optimal safe behavior in multi-agent RL without requiring centralized runtime control, bridging the gap between permissive safety and optimal coordination.

Abstract: Safe coordination problems surface in multi-agent reinforcement learning when global safety cannot be enforced by any agent unilaterally: the admissibility of one agent's action may depend on the dynamics of other agents. Decentralised shields can enforce safety at runtime, but purely factorised permissions often exclude optimal team behaviour that is safe only through coordination. We study deterministic safety guarantees for agents trained and deployed under decentralised execution, recovering team-optimal safe behaviour without centralised runtime control. Agents have a shared global specification $φ$ in the safety fragment of Linear Temporal Logic ($\mathsf{LTL}_{\mathsf{safe}}$ ), and select among tuples of local $\mathsf{LTL}_{\mathsf{safe}}$ obligations whose conjunction implies the global specification $φ$. Each agent may rely on the other agents' local obligations as assumptions because the whole contract tuple is certified simultaneously and allows projection into local action masks. At learning time, a non-stationary multi-armed bandit chooses among a library of local $\mathsf{LTL}_{\mathsf{safe}}$ obligations to select the tuple that optimises team reward, all without forgoing end-to-end safety. We evaluate the approach across 6 environments and 15 algorithmic variants.

</details>


### [52] [Beyond LoRA: Is Sparsity-Induced Adaptation Better?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13767)
*Elijah Cadenhead,Cristian McGee,Xin Li,El Houcine Bergou,Aritra Dutta*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper surveys LoRA and its variants, proposes sparse extensions (Cheap LoRA and c³LA) that fix or sparsify one low-rank factor, derives information-theoretic generalization bounds, and empirically benchmarks 11 methods across 10 models and 14 datasets, showing competitive performance with up to 10% faster training and 15% less GPU memory.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Despite LoRA's efficiency, questions remain about how structural restrictions on low-rank updates affect generalization and whether further parameter reduction is possible without sacrificing performance. The authors aim to provide theoretical generalization guarantees—an underexplored area—and propose even cheaper, sparser LoRA variants.

Method: The paper introduces Cheap LoRA (cLA), which trains only one low-rank factor while fixing the other (deterministically or stochastically), and chained circulant c³LA, which imposes circulant structure. These are framed as structured asymmetric LoRA with controlled column-subspace restrictions. Information-theoretic generalization error bounds are derived for these variants. Empirically, 11 PEFT methods are evaluated on 10 pre-trained models across 14 datasets using loss landscapes and spectral analysis.

Result: The proposed sparse, structured column-space restrictions remain competitive with parameter-matched baselines while reducing training time up to 10% and peak GPU memory up to 15%, even with a naïve sparse implementation. The derived generalization bounds offer a more principled analytical framework than commonly used tools.

Conclusion: Restricting LoRA-based adaptation to a sparse, structured column space is a viable, cost-effective strategy. The theoretical and empirical generalization measures provide a principled, consistent approach for evaluating PEFT methods, advancing beyond conventional analytical tools.

Abstract: Low-rank adaptation (LoRA) and its variants provide a memory- and compute-efficient alternative to full fine-tuning of pre-trained models. However, questions remain about the comparative generalizability of these approaches and how the structural restrictions on low-rank updates preserve effective adaptation performance. We present a historical framing, covering the past (full fine-tuning and original LoRA), the present (different variants of LoRA), and propose simpler, cheaper, parameter-efficient extensions by inducing sparsity within existing LoRA variants: Cheap LoRA (cLA), training a single low-rank factor with the other fixed (deterministically or, in its randomized variant, stochastically), and the chained circulant variant, ${c}^3$LA. We frame cLA as a structured instance of asymmetric LoRA, serving as a controlled column-subspace restriction of full fine-tuning. We derive information-theoretic generalization error bounds for these variants, marking one of the first endeavors in this area. Empirically, we evaluate 11 fine-tuning methods across 10 pre-trained models and 14 datasets, analyzing the fine-tuned models' performance and generalization using tools such as loss landscapes and spectral analysis. Despite the sensitivity of fine-tuned models to the pre-trained model, datasets, and other factors, our study suggests that restricting LoRA-based PEFT methods' adaptation to a sparse, structured column space remains competitive across tasks with their parameter-matched baselines while reducing up to 10% training time and peak GPU memory up to 15%, even with a naïve, non-optimized, sparse implementation. Our theoretical and empirical generalization measures provide a more consistent and principled approach to their cost-effective adaptation than commonly used analytical tools. Overview and code are available at: https://elicaden.github.io/Beyond_LoRA/.

</details>


### [53] [Diffusion Policy Optimization without Drifting Apart](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13795)
*Haozhe Jiang,Haiwen Feng,Pieter Abbeel,Jiantao Jiao,Angjoo Kanazawa,Nika Haghtalab*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: DiPOD addresses the instability in diffusion policy optimization caused by the "double-drift" phenomenon—where the ELBO separates from the true log-likelihood, misaligning proxy and true policy gradients. The solution interleaves self-distillation with policy-gradient updates via an on-policy ELBO regularizer, achieving stable training and higher rewards across diffusion language models and continuous control.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing diffusion policy-gradient methods suffer from training instability and unreliable policy improvement. The authors identify the root cause as "double-drift": optimizing a variational surrogate causes the ELBO (evidence lower bound) to drift away from the true log-likelihood, which in turn causes the proxy policy gradient used for optimization to become misaligned with the true policy gradient of expected return. This fundamental misalignment prevents consistent policy improvement.

Method: DiPOD (Diffusion Policy Optimization with Distillation) maintains tight-bound behavior between the ELBO and true log-likelihood throughout training. The core mechanism interleaves self-distillation with policy-improving gradient updates. Concretely, this is implemented as a simple algorithmic modification: augmenting each diffusion policy-gradient update with an on-policy ELBO regularizer that prevents the variational bound from drifting away from the true likelihood.

Result: DiPOD substantially stabilizes training compared to previous diffusion policy-gradient methods and achieves higher rewards. These improvements are demonstrated across two domains: diffusion language model post-training and continuous-control diffusion policies.

Conclusion: The double-drift phenomenon is a key failure mode in diffusion policy optimization. DiPOD offers a simple yet effective remedy—an on-policy ELBO regularizer grounded in self-distillation—that yields both stability and improved final performance, making it a practical framework for RL-based post-training of diffusion policies.

Abstract: RL post-training has become increasingly pivotal for improving diffusion policies, but existing diffusion policy-gradient methods are often unstable and cannot achieve reliable policy improvement. We identify the cause as the double-drift phenomenon: optimizing a variational surrogate can let the ELBO separate from the true log-likelihood, which then makes the resulting proxy policy gradient misaligned with the true policy gradient of expected return. We propose \textbf{DiPOD}, a diffusion policy optimization framework that maintains tight-bound behavior throughout training by interleaving self-distillation with policy-improving gradient updates. This leads to a simple and practical algorithm: augmenting each diffusion policy-gradient update with an on-policy ELBO regularizer. Across diffusion language model post-training and continuous-control diffusion policies, DiPOD substantially stabilizes training and reaches higher rewards than previous methods.

</details>


### [54] [Neural Variability Enhances Artificial Network Robustness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13801)
*Robin Preble,Praveen Venkatesh,Stefan Mihalas,Kameron Decker Harris*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper investigates whether structured, correlated noise in neural activations—akin to cortical trial-to-trial variability—improves artificial neural network robustness to adversarial attacks and naturalistic image corruptions. Findings show that structured noise enhances robustness, with structure from adversarial attacks generalizing across attack types, while structure from naturalistic modifications is more specialized.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Cortical neurons exhibit substantial trial-to-trial variability (correlated noise) whereas peripheral sensory neurons respond consistently. Prior work suggests noise and signal correlations may optimize discrimination in biological systems, and ANN studies hint at benefits of noise—though largely ignoring correlations. The authors aim to determine whether correlated, structured noise improves ANN robustness to adversarial attacks and naturalistic image modifications, bridging a gap between biological observations and machine learning practice.

Method: The authors leverage the covariance of ANN activations computed under modified (adversarially perturbed or naturally corrupted) versus clean inputs. They inject structured noise derived from these covariance patterns into network activations and measure the resulting change in classification robustness. They compare different noise structures—those derived from adversarial attacks versus those from naturalistic modifications—and test cross-generalization (e.g., whether noise structure from one attack type transfers to defending against another).

Result: Structured noise significantly improves network robustness over unstructured noise baselines. Robustness to naturalistic image modifications benefits most from matched noise structure, but this structure transfers poorly across different naturalistic modification types. In contrast, noise structure derived from adversarial attacks generalizes well to defending against other kinds of adversarial attacks. This indicates a qualitative difference in how noise covariance patterns relate to different perturbation categories.

Conclusion: Structured noise in ANN activations generally improves robustness, providing a biologically plausible strategy that relies only on local information for creating more robust artificial neural networks. The differential transfer properties suggest that adversarial and naturalistic perturbations engage distinct representational geometries, with implications for both neuroscience and robust machine learning.

Abstract: Neural responses in cortex exhibit substantial trial-to-trial variability in response to repeated stimuli, while peripheral sensory neurons respond far more consistently, leading many to wonder whether stochasticity may carry meaning. Existing work has argued that noise and signal correlations may be optimized for discrimination in animals, whereas artificial neural network (ANN) studies have shown similar benefits of noise in machine learning tasks, although most ANN work has neglected the effects of correlations. Here we investigate whether correlated noise improves the robustness of artificial neural networks to adversarial attacks and naturalistic image modifications. Using the covariance of activations under modified versus clean inputs, we find that structured noise may significantly improve network robustness. Robustness to naturalistic image modifications benefits most from structure, but this structure transfers poorly across modification types. In contrast, noise structure from adversarial attacks can generalize to other kinds of attacks. These results suggest that structured noise in ANN activations generally improves robustness, establishing a biologically plausible strategy for creating robust artificial neural networks that only relies on local information.

</details>


### [55] [Neural Slack Variables for Shape Constraints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13803)
*Ruben Wiedemann,Antoine Jacquier,Lukas Gonon*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Neural slack variables convert functional constraint enforcement into a regression problem using a jointly learned auxiliary network that provides valid targets, achieving zero measured violations on monotonicity/convexity tests where penalty and primal-dual methods fail.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing approaches for enforcing functional constraints (monotonicity, convexity) in neural networks are inadequate: penalty/primal-dual methods yield fragile, incomplete satisfaction, while architecture-guaranteed methods are limited to simple cases and impose unwanted inductive biases. There is a need for a method that reliably enforces constraints without compromising model flexibility.

Method: The authors propose "neural slack variables," a primal-side method where an auxiliary neural network is jointly trained alongside the primary network. The auxiliary network learns to output constraint-satisfying values that serve as regression targets for the primary network's constraint quantities (e.g., partial derivatives). This recasts constraint enforcement as a supervised regression problem, inducing both feasibility and regularity without dual variables or architectural restrictions.

Result: Neural slack variables achieve zero measured violations on dense-grid monotonicity and convexity test cases, whereas penalty-based and primal-dual baselines leave residual violations. The method also successfully enables arbitrage-free learning of volatility surfaces, addressing a longstanding open challenge in quantitative finance.

Conclusion: Neural slack variables provide a robust, deep-learning-native framework for enforcing functional inequality constraints, outperforming classical penalty/primal-dual approaches in satisfaction quality and matching the feasibility guarantees of architectural methods without their restrictive inductive biases.

Abstract: Enforcing functional inequality constraints such as monotonicity and convexity in neural networks is a fundamental challenge in many industrial and scientific applications. Classical one-sided penalty methods, along with primal-dual methods gated by complementary slackness, provide constraint gradients only at violated locations, resulting in fragile satisfaction. Architectures that guarantee feasibility by construction, on the other hand, remain largely limited to elementary cases and impose additional inductive biases. We introduce neural slack variables, a deep learning native primal-side approach that converts constraint enforcement into a regression problem by coupling the primary network with a jointly learned auxiliary network. The auxiliary network serves as a valid target for the primary network's constraint quantities, inducing feasibility and regularity. Neural slack variables achieve zero measured violations on dense-grid monotonicity and convexity test cases, where penalty and primal-dual baselines leave residual violations, and enable arbitrage-free learning of volatility surfaces, an open industrial challenge in quantitative finance.

</details>


### [56] [Uncertainty Estimation and Generalization Bounds for Modern Deep Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13818)
*Luis A. Ortega*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This thesis develops Bayesian methodologies (DVIP, VaLLA, FMGP) for uncertainty quantification in deep learning and provides a unified PAC-Bayesian/large-deviation framework explaining generalization in overparameterized networks through diversity, smoothness, and stochasticity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Neural networks achieve strong predictive performance but suffer from poorly understood generalization and uncertainty quantification. The thesis aims to bridge this gap by applying Bayesian principles to both improve uncertainty calibration in pretrained networks and theoretically explain why overparameterized models generalize well.

Method: Methodologically: (1) DVIP — a scalable deep Bayesian framework extending implicit processes to deep architectures via variational inference; (2) VaLLA — a variational post-hoc linearized Laplace approximation; (3) FMGP — a fixed-mean Gaussian process for calibrated uncertainty from pretrained networks. Theoretically: a unified probabilistic framework combining PAC-Bayesian bounds and large-deviation theory, formalizing three generalization mechanisms — diversity (ensemble-like behavior), smoothness (functional regularity), and stochasticity (noise robustness).

Result: The proposed Bayesian methods (DVIP, VaLLA, FMGP) equip pretrained deterministic networks with calibrated uncertainty estimates. The theoretical framework unifies diversity, smoothness, and stochasticity under PAC-Bayesian/large-deviation theory to explain generalization in overparameterized networks.

Conclusion: Bayesian principles provide both practical tools for uncertainty quantification in deep learning and a coherent theoretical lens — via PAC-Bayes and large-deviation theory — to resolve the apparent paradox of generalization in heavily overparameterized neural networks.

Abstract: This thesis investigates how Bayesian principles can deepen our understanding of modern deep learning systems. While neural networks achieve remarkable predictive performance, their ability to generalize and to quantify uncertainty remains only partly understood. This thesis approaches this challenge from both methodological and theoretical angles: unifying Bayesian inference, function-space modeling, and large-deviation theory under a common probabilistic perspective.
  On the methodological side, the thesis introduces the Deep Variational Implicit Process (DVIP), a scalable Bayesian framework that extends implicit processes to deep architectures. Complementing this, two post-hoc methods -- the Variational Linearized Laplace Approximation (VaLLA) and the Fixed-Mean Gaussian Process (FMGP) -- are proposed to equip pretrained deterministic networks with calibrated uncertainty estimates.
  The theoretical contributions focus on one of the central open questions in modern machine learning: why do large, over-parameterized neural networks generalize so well? To address this, the thesis develops a unified probabilistic framework that connects three key mechanisms -- diversity, smoothness, and stochasticity -- within the language of PAC-Bayesian and large-deviation theory.

</details>


### [57] [Attention-Based Estimation of the Individual Treatment Benefit Probability under Dose Variation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13821)
*Lev V. Utkin,Andrei V. Konstantinov,Stanislav K. Kogan,Natalya M. Verbova,Maksim I. Goriunov*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes Dose-AIPTB, a framework for estimating Individual Probability of Treatment Benefit (IPTB) with ordinal outcomes under discrete dose assignments, extending beyond binary treatment settings by reframing the problem as binary classification over unobserved individual treatment effects using attention-based aggregation of pseudo-labels.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing IPTB estimation methods are limited to binary treatment settings, yet clinical practice frequently involves dose-varying interventions. There is a need for a framework that can estimate individual-level treatment benefit across multiple discrete dose levels.

Method: The method recasts IPTB estimation as binary classification over the unobserved sign of the individual treatment effect. Pseudo-labels are constructed from covariate-similar pairwise comparisons among patients receiving different doses. These pseudo-labels are then aggregated using attention mechanisms or Nadaraya-Watson kernel regression to estimate the probability that one dose outperforms another for a given individual.

Result: Numerical experiments on real-world and synthetic data — under covariate shift, varying sample sizes, and heterogeneous outcomes — demonstrate that attention-based aggregation consistently outperforms kernel-based alternatives (Nadaraya-Watson).

Conclusion: The Dose-AIPTB framework provides a foundation for personalized dose selection grounded in individual-level benefit probabilities, with attention mechanisms proving superior to kernel regression for aggregating pairwise comparison signals.

Abstract: Estimating the probability that a treatment outperforms a control for an individual patient, called the Individual Probability of Treatment Benefit (IPTB), offers a clinically intuitive alternative to population-average metrics. However, existing methods for IPTB estimation are largely confined to binary treatment settings, despite the prevalence of dose-varying interventions in clinical practice. We propose a general framework for IPTB estimation with ordinal outcomes under discrete dose assignments, called Dose-AIPTB (Dose Attention-based IPTB). Our approach recasts the problem as binary classification over the unobserved sign of the individual treatment effect, constructing pseudo-labels from covariate-similar pairwise comparisons and aggregating them via attention mechanisms or Nadaraya-Watson kernel regression. This formulation naturally accommodates multiple discrete dose levels, extending beyond the binary treatment paradigm. Through numerical experiments on real-world and synthetic data under covariate shift, varying sample sizes, and heterogeneous outcomes, we demonstrate that attention-based aggregation consistently outperforms kernel alternatives. The framework provides a foundation for personalized dose selection grounded in individual-level benefit probabilities. Codes implementing the model are publicly available at https://github.com/NTAILab/AIPTBDose.

</details>


### [58] [A Stationarity-and-Coupling Criterion for Training-Free Time-Lagged Spectral Embeddings of Multivariate Time Series](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13823)
*Siddharth Pal,Viktoria Rojkova*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper introduces D(τ), a training-free multivariate time series descriptor based on eigenvalues of a time-lagged correlation matrix truncated at the Marchenko-Pastur edge. Its main contribution is a falsifiable applicability criterion derived from a stationary Gaussian VAR(1) model, operationalized as a two-part pre-flight test (ADF stationarity check + power-baseline saturation check) that predicts when the descriptor will succeed or fail before training.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing time series descriptors often lack transparency about their domain of applicability — they are evaluated on performance alone without understanding *when* they can be expected to work. The authors seek not just another descriptor, but a principled, falsifiable criterion that predicts success or failure conditions a priori, making the method's limitations known in advance rather than discovered post hoc.

Method: The descriptor D(τ) is constructed from eigenvalues of a time-lagged correlation matrix, truncated at the Marchenko-Pastur edge to retain only signal-bearing eigenvalues. Classification uses cosine similarity to class centroids with zero learned parameters. The theoretical analysis starts from a stationary Gaussian VAR(1) model, yielding three semi-formal consequences: a distinguishability condition, collapse of static (τ=0) covariance to chance, and failure under power-discriminated stationary paradigms. The pre-flight test combines an Augmented Dickey-Fuller stationarity check and a power-baseline saturation check.

Result: On four paradigms satisfying the criterion (Sleep-EDF, BCI-IV-2a, MIT-BIH, ESC-50), D(τ) is competitive with strong baselines at a fraction of their cost — achieving 88.5±4.5% on Sleep-EDF under leave-one-subject-out with 20 subjects on a single CPU thread. On three violating paradigms (non-stationary ERPs, financial-volatility, wearable-stress), the descriptor fails exactly as the pre-flight predicts. The negative results are emphasized as being more informative than the positives.

Conclusion: D(τ) is explicitly not claimed as the most accurate representation. Its value lies in being a compact, training-free embedding with a known domain of validity. The falsifiable pre-flight criterion allows practitioners to determine, before training, whether the descriptor is appropriate for their data, representing a shift from performance-centric evaluation to applicability-aware methodology.

Abstract: We study training-free fixed-length descriptors for multivariate time series and ask not merely whether such a descriptor performs well, but when it can be expected to work at all. Our object of study is $D(τ)$, built from a time-lagged correlation matrix truncated at the Marchenko-Pastur edge so that only signal-bearing eigenvalues survive and classified by cosine similarity to class centroids with zero learned parameters. The central contribution is not the descriptor but a falsifiable applicability criterion for it. Working from a stationary Gaussian VAR(1) model, we argue that $D(τ)$ separates two classes when the signals are approximately stationary and the class information lives in their cross-channel temporal coupling rather than in marginal per-channel power. We derive, semi-formally, three consequences: a distinguishability condition, why the static ($τ=0$) covariance collapses to chance, and why a stationary but power-discriminated paradigm defeats the descriptor. The criterion is operational: a two-part pre-flight test -- an augmented Dickey-Fuller stationarity check and a power-baseline saturation check -- predicts applicability before any training. We validate both halves on a mixed assortment. On four paradigms that satisfy the criterion (Sleep-EDF, BCI-IV-2a, MIT-BIH, ESC-50) the descriptor is competitive with strong baselines at a fraction of their cost, reaching $88.5\pm4.5\%$ under 20-subject leave-one-subject-out on Sleep-EDF on a single CPU thread. On three that violate it -- non-stationary ERPs, and financial-volatility and wearable-stress regimes that are power-discriminated -- it fails exactly as the pre-flight predicts, and these negatives are the more informative half. We are explicit that $D(τ)$ is not the most accurate representation; its value is a compact, training-free embedding whose domain of validity is known in advance.

</details>


### [59] [SuperThoughts: Reasoning Tokens in Superposition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13862)
*Zheyang Xiong,Shivam Garg,Max Yu,Vaishnavi Shrivastava,Haoyu Zhao,Anastasios Kyrillidis,Dimitris Papailiopoulos*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SuperThoughts compresses consecutive CoT token pairs into single latent representations and decodes two tokens per step via Multi-Token Prediction, achieving ~20–30% CoT length reduction with minimal accuracy loss.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long Chain-of-Thought reasoning is computationally expensive due to sequential, autoregressive token generation. Existing continuous latent-space approaches suffer from training instability and lack of supervision signals for complex long-horizon tasks.

Method: SuperThoughts compresses pairs of consecutive CoT tokens into single latent representations and uses a lightweight Multi-Token Prediction (MTP) module to decode two tokens per step. A confidence-based adaptive mechanism falls back to standard decoding when uncertainty is high. Models fine-tuned: Qwen2.5-Math-1.5B/7B/14B-Instruct.

Result: Evaluated on MATH500, AMC, OlympiadBench, and GPQA-Diamond. Achieves ~20–30% CoT length reduction while maintaining accuracy with only 1–2 points drop on most tasks.

Conclusion: SuperThoughts effectively doubles inference throughput via latent-space compression of CoT reasoning while preserving discrete token supervision, offering a practical speed-accuracy trade-off for reasoning LLMs.

Abstract: Long Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning improves LLM problem-solving but is computationally expensive due to sequential token generation. While recent works explore reasoning in continuous latent spaces to bypass discrete token generation, they often struggle with training stability and fail to scale to complex, long-horizon tasks due to lack of supervision signal. We propose SuperThoughts, which compresses pairs of consecutive CoT tokens into single latent representations and decodes two tokens per step via a lightweight Multi-Token Prediction (MTP) module. This preserves discrete token supervision at training time while doubling throughput at inference time. We finetune Qwen2.5-Math-1.5B-Instruct, Qwen2.5-Math-7B-Instruct, Qwen2.5-Math-14B-Instruct, and evaluate on MATH500, AMC, OlympiadBench, and GPQA-Diamond. With a confidence-based adaptive mechanism that falls back to standard decoding when uncertain, SuperThoughts achieves $\sim$20--30\% CoT length reduction while maintaining accuracy with minimal degradation (1-2 points accuracy drop on most tasks).

</details>


### [60] [Muon$^p$: Muon with Fractional Spectral Powers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13867)
*Yihe Dong,Will Sawin*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Muon^p introduces fractional spectral-power updates (US^p V^T) as a generalization of the Muon optimizer, interpolating between Muon (p=0) and gradient descent (p=1). The authors prove that exact fractional powers cannot be computed by fixed polynomial iterations and derive low-degree bivariate recurrences for practical approximation. They establish a theoretical connection to maximizing linear loss improvement under Schatten q-norms and demonstrate strong empirical results for large-scale finetuning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The standard Muon optimizer fully flattens the singular spectrum by replacing the gradient G=USV^T with its polar factor UV^T, but this entirely discards singular-value information that may be beneficial for adaptation, particularly during finetuning where preserving spectral structure could improve performance.

Method: The authors propose Muon^p, which applies fractional spectral power updates US^p V^T with rational p in (0,1). They prove a no-free-lunch theorem showing no fixed univariate polynomial can compute arbitrary fractional spectral powers, then derive low-degree odd bivariate recurrences that approximate these powers using only matrix multiplications. They also establish that Muon^p maximizes linear loss improvement under the Schatten q-norm for q=1+1/p, and analyze spectral geometry to characterize when Muon^p is less suitable.

Result: On billion-scale model finetuning, Muon^p improves both validation perplexity and downstream task performance compared to baselines. The method preserves Muon's matrix-multiplication-only structure and computational complexity while enabling controlled retention of singular-value information.

Conclusion: Preserving singular spectrum information through fractional spectral powers can yield significant empirical gains in optimization, particularly for finetuning. The work provides both a principled framework and practical implementation for achieving this, while also delineating conditions under which full spectral flattening (standard Muon) remains preferable.

Abstract: Muon is an increasingly widely used optimizer that replaces a gradient $G=USV^\top$ with its polar factor $UV^\top$, thereby flattening the singular spectrum. However, full flattening discards singular-value information that may matter for adaptation. We introduce Muon$^p$, a Muon-style optimizer that instead uses fractional spectral-power updates $US^pV^\top$ for rational $p\in(0,1)$, interpolating between Muon and gradient descent. To make it practical, we prove that fractional spectral powers cannot be computed by any fixed univariate polynomial iteration, and furthermore derive low-degree odd bivariate recurrences that approximate $US^pV^\top$ using only matrix multiplications, preserving Muon's matrix-multiplication-only structure and compute complexity. We show that Muon$^p$ maximizes the linear improvement in loss under the Schatten $q$-norm for $q=1+\frac{1}{p}$. Empirically, Muon$^p$ is especially effective for finetuning: on billion-scale models, Muon$^p$ improves validation perplexity and downstream task performance. We further analyze when Muon$^p$ is less suitable, through the lens of spectral geometry. Our results reveal important insights on when preserving the singular spectrum can bring significant gains, and introduce a principled way to achieve them.

</details>


### [61] [Natively Unlearnable Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13873)
*Gaurav R. Ghosal,Pratyush Maini,Aditi Raghunathan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: NULLs are LLMs trained with shared backbone neurons and sparsely activated source-specific "sinks," enabling native unlearning of individual data sources at deployment by simply disabling sinks—no gradient updates or retained data needed. The method scales to 6M Wikipedia articles, matches retraining-from-scratch fidelity, resists adversarial extraction, and preserves general language capabilities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Machine unlearning is challenging because data source contributions are entangled in model parameters. Existing solutions either isolate sources into disjoint parameters (easing removal but blocking joint learning) or perform post-hoc unlearning (gradient-based, requiring retained data, vulnerable to adversarial attacks). The authors aim to simultaneously achieve source isolation for easy removal and shared representation learning across sources.

Method: NULLs architecture comprises: (1) a set of shared backbone neurons trained on all data, and (2) a pool of sparsely activated "sinks"—specialized parameters assigned to specific data sources via a source-aware routing mechanism. During training, source-specific information naturally concentrates in the assigned sinks while shared knowledge accumulates in the backbone. Unlearning is performed at deployment by simply disabling a source's corresponding sinks, requiring no gradient updates and no access to retained data.

Result: (1) Scaled to Wikipedia's ~6M articles, treating each as an independent source. (2) Unlearning a single article removes source-specific knowledge while preserving shared facts with semantically related articles, closely matching retraining from scratch. (3) In a Harry Potter case study, NULLs resisted adversarial extraction and relearning attacks that typically reverse post-hoc unlearning. (4) Preserved general language capabilities, matching standard transformers on downstream benchmarks.

Conclusion: Source-level unlearning can be built natively into LLM training rather than applied post-hoc, simultaneously achieving the opposing goals of source isolation for clean removal and joint learning for shared representations. This suggests unlearning should be a first-class design consideration in model training.

Abstract: Unlearning aims to remove the influence of specific training data sources, but this has proved challenging because the contributions of different sources are entangled within the model. Isolating source contributions to disjoint parameters makes removal easier, though it obstructs joint learning across sources. We propose NULLs (Natively Unlearnable LLMs), a model class that satisfies the two opposing goals of isolating source-specific contributions and learning jointly across sources, by training a set of shared backbone neurons alongside a pool of sparsely activated sinks. During training, information specific to a source naturally concentrates in its sinks while information shared across sources accumulates in the backbone. A source is then unlearned at deployment by disabling its corresponding sinks, with no gradient updates and no access to the retained data. We show that NULLs scales to Wikipedia's ~6M articles, isolating each as an independent source. Unlearning a single article removes knowledge specific to it while preserving facts shared with semantically related articles, closely matching retraining from scratch. We note that unlearning with NULLs is also robust: in a case study of unlearning the Harry Potter books, NULLs resists both adversarial extraction and relearning that reverses post-hoc unlearning. Finally, NULLs preserves general language capabilities, matching a standard transformer on downstream benchmarks. Together, these results suggest that source-level unlearning need not be an afterthought. It can be built natively into LLM training while retaining the benefits of shared representation learning.

</details>


### [62] [A Longitudinal Attribute-Conditioned Neural Network for Modeling Health-State Transition Probabilities in Temporally Irregular Data: The LANTERN Framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13880)
*Bright Kwaku Manu,Beckett Sterner,Petar Jevtic*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A structured machine learning estimator for long-term care transition probabilities is developed that handles irregular longitudinal health data, conditions on individual covariates and history, and aggregates into cohort-level transition matrices. Evaluated against multiple benchmarks using HRS data, it achieves superior calibration, improved severe disability discrimination, and the lowest transition matrix error.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Classical multi-state models (Markov, semi-Markov, proportional hazards) used in disability insurance are restrictive for irregular longitudinal health data exhibiting nonlinear aging patterns and heterogeneous covariate histories. There is a need for estimators that provide both individual-level accuracy and aggregate-level fidelity for actuarial cohort projection.

Method: A multi-state transition probability estimator is built that: (1) learns from individual health history and elapsed time between observations; (2) conditions on demographic and socioeconomic attributes; (3) outputs a valid probability distribution over four states (healthy, mild disability, severe disability, death); and (4) aggregates individual probabilities by age group and origin state into cohort transition matrices. The method is benchmarked against logistic regression, gradient-boosted trees, a recurrent neural network, and a last-state persistence model using Health and Retirement Study (HRS) longitudinal data.

Result: The proposed estimator improves severe disability discrimination over logistic regression and gradient-boosted trees, maintains strong calibration, and achieves the lowest transition matrix error among all evaluated models in held-out testing.

Conclusion: A structured machine learning approach can effectively support long-term care transition modeling when evaluated on both calibration and projection fidelity, not solely on discrimination. This has practical implications for disability insurance pricing, reserving, and solvency assessment.

Abstract: Accurate estimation of long-term care transition probabilities is central to disability insurance pricing, reserving, and solvency assessment. Classical actuarial multi-state models commonly rely on Markov, semi-Markov, or proportional-hazard specifications, which provide a direct connection to cohort projection but may be restrictive for irregular longitudinal health data with nonlinear aging patterns and heterogeneous covariate histories. This paper develops a well-calibrated estimator of multi-state transition probabilities for irregular longitudinal health data. The model learns from individual health history, incorporates the time elapsed between observations, and conditions transition probabilities on demographic and socioeconomic attributes. It produces a valid probability distribution over the next observed health state, with four possible states: healthy, mild disability, severe disability, and death. Individual probabilities are aggregated by age group and origin state to form transition matrices compatible with actuarial cohort projection. Using longitudinal data from the Health and Retirement Study, we compare the proposed estimator with logistic regression, gradient-boosted trees, a recurrent neural network, and a last-state persistence benchmark. The evaluation considers probabilistic accuracy, endpoint discrimination and calibration for severe disability and death, risk concentration, and transition matrix error after aggregation. The proposed estimator improves severe disability discrimination relative to logistic regression and gradient-boosted tree benchmarks, maintains strong calibration, and yields the lowest transition matrix error among the evaluated models in the held-out test analysis. Results show that a structured machine learning estimator can support long-term care transition modeling when judged by calibration and projection fidelity, beyond discrimination.

</details>


### [63] [Gefen: Optimized Stochastic Optimizer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13894)
*Nadav Benedek,Tomer Koren,Ohad Fried*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Gefen is a memory-efficient optimizer that reduces AdamW's memory footprint by ~8x (6.5 GiB per billion parameters) by sharing second-moment estimates across parameter blocks and quantizing the first moment using a learned histogram-based dynamic-programming codebook, all while maintaining AdamW-level performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AdamW stores first and second moment buffers, each roughly the size of the model parameters, doubling or tripling training memory. Reducing this memory overhead would enable larger models, larger batch sizes, and higher throughput without sacrificing optimizer performance.

Method: Gefen is grounded in a theoretical result: large mixed Hessian entries constrain the ratio of squared gradients toward one, implying Hessian-aligned parameters can share second-moment statistics. Since full Hessians are impractical, Gefen infers block structure from initial squared gradients without architecture-specific metadata. It shares second-moment estimates across these blocks and quantizes the first moment using an exact histogram-based dynamic-programming codebook, reusing block assignments for first-moment scaling. No hyperparameters beyond AdamW defaults are required.

Result: Gefen achieves ~8x memory reduction over AdamW (6.5 GiB saved per billion parameters) and the lowest peak optimizer memory among comparable AdamW-like methods, while maintaining AdamW-level performance. In FSDP and DDP training, the reduced memory enables larger microbatches and significantly improves throughput.

Conclusion: Gefen is a practical drop-in replacement for AdamW, delivering substantial memory savings without performance degradation. It enables training larger models or using larger batch sizes with improved throughput. A complete Python implementation with fused CUDA kernels is open-sourced on GitHub.

Abstract: AdamW is a default optimizer for modern deep learning, but its first and second moment states add roughly two parameter-sized buffers to training memory. We propose Gefen, a memory-efficient optimizer that automatically shares second-moment estimates across parameter blocks and quantizes the first moment using a learned codebook, thereby reducing AdamW's memory footprint by ~8x while maintaining the same performance, corresponding to a reduction of 6.5 GiB per billion parameters. The method is motivated by a theoretical result showing that large mixed Hessian entries constrain the ratio of squared gradients toward one, suggesting that Hessian-aligned parameters are natural candidates for sharing second-moment statistics. Since computing Hessians is impractical at scale, Gefen infers block structure from the initial squared gradients, requiring no architecture-specific metadata or hyperparameters beyond AdamW defaults. Gefen learns an exact histogram-based dynamic-programming quantization codebook and reuses the same blocks for first-moment scaling. Across diverse experiments, Gefen achieves the lowest peak optimizer memory among the compared AdamW-like methods while maintaining AdamW-level performance. In FSDP and DDP training, the reduced memory footprint enables larger microbatches and improves throughput significantly over AdamW, providing a practical drop-in replacement with lower memory usage that can increase throughput and enable training larger models or using larger batch sizes. We provide the complete Python implementation, including fused CUDA kernels at https://github.com/ndvbd/Gefen

</details>


### [64] [SpikF-GO: Spiking Fourier Graph Operators for Multivariate Time Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13901)
*Jafar Bakhshaliyev,Niels Landwehr*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SpikF-GO introduces graph-based multivariate modeling to spiking neural networks for time series forecasting, using spike-driven spectral processing with learnable frequency gates and complex-valued spiking neurons, achieving state-of-the-art SNN performance and outperforming its ANN counterpart at lower energy cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing SNN-based time series forecasting methods process variables independently, lacking mechanisms to model inter-variable (cross-variable) dependencies. This is a critical gap in multivariate forecasting where correlations across variables carry significant predictive information.

Method: SpikF-GO introduces: (1) a hypervariate graph formulation where every scalar observation becomes a graph node; (2) spike-driven spectral graph processing in the Fourier domain; (3) a Hard Concrete frequency gate for learnable sparse frequency selection; (4) a Complex LIF gate applying independent spiking neurons to real and imaginary Fourier components, preserving binary event-driven computation throughout. A variant with Central Pattern Generator-based positional encodings is also proposed for stronger long-range temporal modeling.

Result: Evaluated on eight benchmarks under a unified experimental protocol, SpikF-GO achieved the best average rank among all SNN methods and outperformed its ANN counterpart (FourierGNN) at reduced energy cost. It maintained competitive accuracy even at substantially smaller embedding dimensions, yielding significant energy reductions.

Conclusion: This is among the first works to bring graph-based multivariate modeling into the spiking domain for time series forecasting and the first to provide a unified comparison across SNN forecasting architectures under a common experimental protocol.

Abstract: Spiking Neural Networks (SNNs) have emerged as an energy-efficient alternative to conventional neural networks, demonstrating strong performance in computer vision and robotics. More recently, SNNs have been applied to time series forecasting (TSF), with methods exploring spiking temporal backbones, spike-compatible positional encodings, Fourier-domain processing, and redesigned neuron dynamics. However, existing SNN forecasting approaches process variables independently, lacking explicit mechanisms for modeling inter-variable dependencies. This is a critical limitation in multivariate settings, where cross-variable correlations carry substantial predictive information. We propose Spiking Fourier Graph Operators (SpikF-GO), which addresses this gap by combining a hypervariate graph formulation in which every scalar observation becomes a graph node with spike-driven spectral processing. SpikF-GO introduces a Hard Concrete frequency gate for learnable sparse frequency selection and a Complex LIF gate that applies independent spiking neurons to real and imaginary Fourier components, preserving binary, event-driven computation throughout the spectral domain. We further present a variant incorporating Central Pattern Generator-based positional encodings for stronger long-range temporal modeling. Evaluated on eight benchmarks under a unified experimental protocol, SpikF-GO achieves the best average rank among all SNN methods and outperforms its ANN counterpart, FourierGNN, at reduced energy cost. SpikF-GO maintains competitive accuracy even at substantially smaller embedding dimensions, thereby achieving significant energy reductions. To our knowledge, this is among the first works to bring graph-based multivariate modeling into the spiking domain for TSF and the first to provide a unified comparison across SNN forecasting architectures under a common experimental protocol.

</details>


### [65] [Smoothing Dark Areas in Molecular Latent Diffusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13955)
*Xi Wang,Jiahan Li,Yuxuan Xia,Yingcheng Wu,Shaoyi Zheng,Shengjie Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: TopVAE introduces a topology-optimized VAE for 3D molecular generation that reduces "dark areas" in the latent space—regions that decode to invalid or disconnected molecules—by internalizing structural and chemical constraints during training. When combined with a DiT decoder, it achieves significant improvements in validity, connectivity, and stability over prior methods on QM9 and GEOM-Drugs benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing molecular VAEs trained with reconstruction objectives produce latent spaces with "dark areas"—regions reachable during diffusion sampling that decode to chemically invalid or disconnected molecules. Unlike images, molecular generation demands strict structural/chemical precision, so even small latent perturbations cause catastrophic failures. This motivates a VAE designed explicitly for robust off-posterior decoding in latent diffusion pipelines.

Method: TopVAE is a topology-optimized variational autoencoder that jointly trains the encoder and decoder with explicit structural and chemical constraints, forcing the decoder to internalize validity requirements during training rather than relying on post-hoc correction. This reduces dark areas in the latent space. The model is paired with a standard Diffusion Transformer (DiT) for 3D molecular generation and evaluated on tasks including unconditional generation and zero-shot scaffold inpainting.

Result: TopVAE paired with a DiT achieves: 77% lower FCD-3D on QM9 (state-of-the-art validity & connectivity), 52% lower FCD-3D on GEOM-Drugs, and 1.29× more stable and connected molecules on zero-shot scaffold inpainting compared to baselines.

Conclusion: Incorporating structural and chemical constraints directly into VAE training—rather than applying test-time corrections—is essential for building latent diffusion pipelines that produce valid, stable, and connected 3D molecules. TopVAE demonstrates that topology-aware decoder training effectively eliminates dark areas in the latent space, enabling robust and scalable molecular generation.

Abstract: Latent diffusion is a promising framework for scalable 3D molecular generation, but it requires a latent space that remains smooth, valid, and navigable beyond posterior samples. Existing molecular VAEs, however, are typically learned through reconstruction-based objectives, which do not guarantee such a latent space. We show that this leads to dark areas: regions of latent space that are reachable during diffusion sampling but decode to disconnected or chemically invalid molecules. Unlike in image generation, molecular decoding requires strict structural and chemical precision, so even small latent perturbations can produce catastrophic failures. We therefore propose TopVAE, a topology-optimized VAE that reduces dark areas by making the decoder internalize structural and chemical constraints during training, eliminating the need for test-time chemical correction. TopVAE greatly improves off-posterior robustness, and when paired with a standard DiT, achieves $77\%$ lower FCD-3D on QM9, the highest V&C, $52\%$ lower FCD-3D on GEOM-Drugs, and $1.29{\times}$ more stable and connected molecules on zero-shot scaffold inpainting.

</details>


### [66] [Can Machine Learning Forecast Rice Yields in Data-Constrained Settings? Satellite Climate Data, National Crop Statistics, and Lessons from Sierra Leone](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13959)
*Ibrahim Denis Fofanah*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Sierra Leone lacks data-driven agricultural decision support. This study tests whether rice yields can be forecast from available data. Crop-only ML models fail to beat persistence, but adding satellite climate data (CHIRPS rainfall, NASA POWER temperature) enables an XGBoost model to reduce RMSE by one-third (284 vs 428 kg/ha). Early-season rainfall is the dominant predictor. The 2018 yield collapse was institutionally rather than climatically driven. Findings are translated into policy recommendations for the Feed Salone Strategy with an open-source pipeline.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Sierra Leone's agricultural sector operates with virtually no data-driven decision support, and no published machine learning study has examined the country's crop yields. The authors aim to determine whether readily available data can produce actionable yield forecasts to inform national food security strategy.

Method: Using 25 years (2000–2024) of FAOSTAT production data for nine major crops, the authors train XGBoost, Gradient Boosting, and Random Forest models under a strict anti-leakage protocol with expanding-window walk-forward validation across seven held-out years. Models are evaluated against a naive persistence baseline. Two feature sets are compared: crop statistics alone, and crop statistics augmented with free satellite climate data (CHIRPS rainfall, NASA POWER temperature). A climate-only XGBoost variant is also tested, and robustness is checked by excluding the anomalous 2018 season.

Result: No model trained on crop statistics alone outperforms persistence. Augmenting with climate data reverses this result: the climate-only XGBoost reduces forecast RMSE by approximately one-third (284 vs 428 kg/ha). This improvement holds for a linear model and is robust to excluding 2018. Early-season (May–June) rainfall emerges as the dominant predictor, indicating seasonal yield risk is observable months before harvest. No model anticipated the 2018 yield collapse, which the authors attribute to institutional rather than climatic causes.

Conclusion: Freely available satellite climate data can enable meaningful rice yield forecasting in data-sparse contexts like Sierra Leone. Early-season rainfall provides an early-warning signal for food security planning. The 2018 anomaly underscores that non-climatic (institutional) factors must also be monitored. The authors translate findings into policy recommendations for the Feed Salone Strategy and release a fully open-source pipeline to facilitate adoption.

Abstract: Sierra Leone's agriculture operates with almost no data-driven decision support, and no published machine learning study has examined the country's crop yields. We ask whether rice yield can be forecast from data Sierra Leone currently has. Using 25 years of FAOSTAT production data (2000-2024) for nine major crops, we train XGBoost, Gradient Boosting, and Random Forest under a strict anti-leakage protocol with expanding-window walk-forward evaluation across seven held-out years, benchmarked against naive persistence. No model trained on crop statistics alone outperforms persistence. Augmenting with free satellite climate data (CHIRPS rainfall, NASA POWER temperature) reverses this result: a climate-only XGBoost reduces forecast error by one third (RMSE 284 vs 428 kg/ha), a gain that holds for a linear model and is robust to excluding the anomalous 2018 season. Early-season (May-June) rainfall is the dominant predictor, implying seasonal yield risk is observable months before harvest. No model anticipated the 2018 collapse, whose origins were institutional rather than climatic. We translate the findings into policy recommendations for Sierra Leone's Feed Salone Strategy, with a fully open-source pipeline.

</details>


### [67] [PostDeg: Placement Beats Parameterization in LayerNorm GNNs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14022)
*Yash Tomar,Aryav Das*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Post-LayerNorm (not pre-LayerNorm) is the correct slot for injecting per-node topology scalars in GNN residual blocks, because pre-LN scalars are divided out during normalization. The authors prove this with PostDeg (inverse-degree scaling) and validate with four pre-registered falsification tests, achieving consistent gains across three graph tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LayerNorm-based GNNs erase topology signals (degree, centrality, k-core) that node-selection policies should depend on, yet the literature has not identified precisely where in the residual block this erasure occurs. The authors aim to locate the surviving insertion slot and provide a principled rule for preserving topology information.

Method: The authors theoretically analyze where a positive per-node scalar survives in a LayerNorm residual block. A scalar inserted before LayerNorm is divided out (up to a stabilizer term), while one inserted after LayerNorm reaches the score head as representation magnitude. They instantiate this with PostDeg, a parameter-free post-LayerNorm inverse-degree scale, and pre-register four falsifiers: graphwise scalars, extra LayerNorm, expressive same-slot capacity, and backbone-agnostic source — any of which would reject the placement rule if successful.

Result: PostDeg achieves +3.5% on influence maximization, +2.5% on network dismantling, and +5.6% on maximum independent set over the LN backbone, with 10/10 paired-seed wins per task. None of the four pre-registered falsifiers fired, confirming that placement — not parameterization — drives the gain.

Conclusion: The key takeaway is that post-LayerNorm placement of topology scalars carries the performance gain, generalizing to any positive topology scalar in any normalized residual stack. This is a small invariance check rather than a new architecture.

Abstract: LayerNorm-based GNNs routinely erase the topology signals (degree, centrality, $k$-core) that node-selection policies should depend on, but the literature has not located where in the residual block the erasure happens. We answer that question: a positive per-node scalar inserted before LayerNorm is divided out up to a stabilizer term, while the same scalar inserted after LayerNorm reaches the score head as representation magnitude. The surviving slot is the post-LayerNorm position. We instantiate it with PostDeg, a parameter-free post-LayerNorm inverse-degree scale, and pre-register four falsifiers (graphwise scalars, extra LayerNorm, expressive same-slot capacity, backbone-agnostic source) that would reject the rule. PostDeg gains $+3.5\%/+2.5\%/+5.6\%$ over the LN backbone on influence maximization, network dismantling, and maximum independent set, with $10/10$ paired-seed wins per task; none of the four falsifiers fires. The takeaway is that placement, not parameterization, carries the gain -- a small invariance check that generalizes to any positive topology scalar in any normalized residual stack.

</details>


### [68] [Utility-Constrained Policy Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14029)
*Mehrdad Moghimi,Bernardo Avila Pires*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper introduces a practical methodology for Utility-Constrained MDPs (UCMDPs), extending beyond risk-neutral CMDPs to support risk-sensitive constraints, enabling flexible constraint limits without retraining, and achieving strong empirical performance on Safety Gymnasium benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard CMDPs only handle risk-neutral constraints, allowing policies that mix rare catastrophic failures with frequent conservative behavior — an unacceptable outcome in safety-critical applications. Risk-sensitive constraints can also improve performance under risk-neutral evaluation, yet no practical UCMDP solutions existed.

Method: The authors propose a simple methodology for solving UCMDPs that incorporates risk-sensitive constraints (via utility functions). The framework does not require fixing constraint limits before training, as long as a sensible range is known, allowing post-hoc limit adjustments without extra training cost.

Result: The proposed agent consistently matches or outperforms existing baselines across several Safety Gymnasium benchmark tasks.

Conclusion: The UCMDP framework with risk-sensitive constraints is both practical and effective, offering greater policy flexibility, eliminating the need for pre-specified constraint limits, and delivering strong empirical results that validate its generality.

Abstract: Constrained MDPs (CMDPs) are a widely adopted framework for incorporating safety into RL agents; however, the framework does not support risk-sensitive constraints. This can be problematic: For example, CMDPs allow for optimal solutions that, in order to satisfy the risk-neutral constraints, mix infrequent catastrophic behaviors and frequent, overly conservative ones. Moreover, prior empirical results suggest that enforcing stricter, risk-sensitive constraints can improve performance even under risk-neutral evaluation. The natural framework to incorporate risk-sensitive constraints is utility-constrained MDPs (UCMDPs), but no practical solutions for this problem existed. In this work, we introduce a simple yet powerful methodology for UCMDPs and constrained RL. Besides allowing for risk-sensitive constraints, our framework does not require us to fix constraint limits in advance of training the agent, provided that a sensible range is known. This increases policy flexibility and, in practice, allows for adjustments to these limits at no extra training cost. Besides benefiting from the generality of the framework, our agent shows strong performance in practice, consistently matching or outperforming existing baselines in several Safety Gymnasium benchmark tasks.

</details>


### [69] [Decompose Sparsely Where You Should, Absorb Densely Where You Should No](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14040)
*Ruixuan Deng,Zehao Jin,Zekun Wang,Zihan Dong*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Standard sparse autoencoders assume all activation content is amenable to sparse decomposition, but this paper identifies a low-rank, dense "computational scaffold" in residual streams that resists sparse representation. Adding a small rank-r linear bottleneck in parallel dramatically reduces persistent dense latents and improves SAE performance, while the absorbed scaffold proves structurally identifiable, causally necessary, and redundantly encoded by sparse features.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: SAEs are widely used for mechanistic interpretability under the assumption that all activation content can be sparsely decomposed into monosemantic features. However, persistent dense latents in trained SAEs suggest this assumption may be flawed, motivating an investigation into whether a fundamentally dense, low-rank component exists in residual stream activations that is important but unsuitable for sparse representation.

Method: The authors augment standard SAEs (BatchTopK and Matryoshka) with a small rank-r linear bottleneck in parallel. This bottleneck absorbs dense structure before sparse reconstruction. Experiments are conducted on Gemma-2-2B layer 12, with the absorbed component analyzed structurally (PCA, outlier dimensions), causally (ablation impact on next-token cross-entropy), and representationally (sparse feature ablation vs. scaffold removal, MMLU topic classification).

Result: A rank-24 bottleneck reduces dense latent count by up to 84% while improving sparse probing and targeted probe perturbation at matched sparsity. The absorbed component: (i) is structurally identifiable as top principal components and outlier dimensions; (ii) raises next-token cross-entropy by 7.5× when removed vs. 2.8× for top-24 PCA removal; (iii) is redundantly encoded — ablating 787 maximally aligned sparse features raises CE by only 2.9×, and removing 2,048 topic-aligned features barely affects MMLU classification (98.7% to near chance when scaffold removed instead).

Conclusion: The findings reveal a compact, semantically informative, and causally critical low-rank component (the "computational scaffold") that sparse dictionaries encode inefficiently and redundantly. This challenges the foundational assumption of sparsity-based interpretability and suggests the scope of SAE methods warrants careful re-examination, advocating for hybrid dense-sparse approaches.

Abstract: Sparse autoencoders (SAEs) are typically trained to reconstruct the \textbf{entire} residual stream through a sparse dictionary, implicitly assuming that all activation content is amenable to sparse, monosemantic decomposition. We question this assumption and hypothesize that activations contain a low-rank, dense component that is computationally important to the model yet inherently unsuitable for sparse representation, which serves as a major source of the persistent dense latents widely observed in trained SAEs. To test this, we add a small rank-$r$ linear bottleneck in parallel with standard SAEs (BatchTopK and Matryoshka), allowing dense structure to be absorbed before sparse reconstruction. On Gemma-2-2B layer 12, a rank-24 bottleneck reduces dense latent count by up to 84\% while improving sparse probing and targeted probe perturbation on both architectures at matched sparsity. The absorbed component is (i) \textbf{structurally identifiable} as the top principal components and outlier dimensions; (ii) \textbf{causally necessary}, with removing it raising next-token cross-entropy by 7.5$\times$, far exceeding the 2.8$\times$ from removing the geometrically near-identical top-24 PCA directions; and (iii) \textbf{redundantly encoded by sparse dictionaries}, with ablating 787 maximally aligned sparse features raising cross-entropy by only 2.9$\times$ and ablating 2,048 topic-aligned features leaving MMLU topic classification virtually unchanged, whereas removing the scaffold drops it from 98.7\% to chance.
