Together, our findings identify a compact, semantically informative and causally important component of residual stream activations (which we term a \textbf{computational scaffold}) that standard sparse dictionaries represent inefficiently, suggesting that the scope of sparsity-based interpretability methods warrants careful re-examination.

</details>


### [70] [Non-Parametric Machine Text Detection via Multi-View Gaussian Processes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14060)
*Aleem Khan,Nicholas Andrews*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A multi-view, non-parametric detection framework that extracts complementary feature views from documents and aggregates them via a Gaussian process ensemble, achieving robust machine-text detection under adversarial attacks with calibrated probabilities and principled out-of-distribution abstention.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing machine-text detectors degrade sharply under adversarial conditions (paraphrasing, style transfer) and parametric classifiers make confidently wrong predictions under distribution shifts (novel attacks, unseen LMs). Since documents carry multiple complementary signals, an attack suppressing one may leave others intact, motivating a multi-view approach that raises evasion cost.

Method: A multi-view, non-parametric detection framework: (1) extracts complementary feature views (stylistic, likelihood/rank-order, structural) from the same document; (2) aggregates per-view evidence through a Gaussian process ensemble; (3) provides calibrated probabilities and enables principled abstention on out-of-distribution inputs.

Result: Evaluated on three benchmarks—DetectRL, RAID, and the PAN2025 shared task—spanning diverse generators and attacks. The multi-view detector maintains strong performance under considered attacks and outperforms existing approaches against held-out attacks.

Conclusion: The multi-view, non-parametric Gaussian process ensemble framework effectively resists adversarial evasion by forcing adversaries to defeat multiple independent detection axes simultaneously, while providing reliable uncertainty quantification for high-stakes deployment.

Abstract: Adversarial conditions such as paraphrasing and targeted style transfer sharply degrade the accuracy of machine text detectors. A document, however, carries multiple complementary signals (e.g., stylistic features, likelihood and rank-order features, and structural features), and an attack that suppresses one may leave others intact. While a parametric classifier can learn to combine these features given sufficient supervision, classifiers are prone to making confidently incorrect predictions when the distribution shifts (e.g., novel attacks or unseen language models). To address this, we propose a multi-view, non-parametric detection framework that extracts complementary feature views from the same document and aggregates per-view evidence through a Gaussian process ensemble. By aggregating evidence across views, an adversary must simultaneously defeat multiple independent axes of detection, substantially raising the cost of evasion. The Gaussian process formulation additionally provides calibrated probabilities and principled abstention on out-of-distribution inputs, supporting reliable deployment in high-stakes settings. We evaluate on three benchmarks spanning diverse generators and attacks: the DetectRL and RAID benchmarks, and the PAN2025 shared task and demonstrate that our multi-view detector maintains strong performance under the considered attacks, outperforming existing approaches against held out attacks.

</details>


### [71] [Rethinking Backdoor Adversarial Unlearning through the Lens of Catastrophic Forgetting in Continual Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14078)
*Zhenqian Zhu,Yamin Hu,Yujiang Liu,Luping Wei,Wenbo Hou,Bin Li,Haodong Li,Wenjian Luo*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper frames backdoor unlearning as a continual learning problem, derives necessary conditions for complete backdoor removal via catastrophic forgetting, and proposes BI-BAU — a method using blind inversion and EM-based adversarial training to eliminate backdoor effects across diverse attack types and modalities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current backdoor defenses lack robustness and only provide superficial protection, failing to completely eliminate backdoor effects. There is a need for a principled framework that achieves complete backdoor unlearning rather than mere mitigation.

Method: The authors formalize backdoor learning/unlearning as a three-stage continual learning process and define conditions for complete backdoor unlearning based on catastrophic forgetting. They propose BI-BAU, which casts adversarial example generation as a blind inversion problem and solves it by embedding bi-level adversarial training optimization within an Expectation-Maximization (EM) framework to maximize a MAP objective. The method is extended to untargeted adversarial scenarios and multi-modal contrastive learning.

Result: Extensive experiments show BI-BAU achieves general applicability across a wide spectrum of backdoor attacks and effectively, thoroughly eliminates backdoor effects from compromised models.

Conclusion: BI-BAU provides a theoretically grounded, practical solution for complete backdoor unlearning, with broad applicability to real-world deployment scenarios involving compromised pre-trained models.

Abstract: Existing studies reveal that current backdoor defenses exhibit limited robustness and often fail against specific types of attacks. More concerningly, prevailing safety tuning strategies tend to provide only superficial safety protection, as they fall short of completely eliminating the backdoor effects. In this work, we present a novel formulation of backdoor learning and unlearning as a sequential, three-stage process from a continual learning perspective. Within this framework, we formally define complete backdoor unlearning and further derive the necessary conditions for achieving it based on the mechanism of catastrophic forgetting. Guided by these insights, we propose Blind Inversion-Backdoor Adversarial Unlearning (BI-BAU), which formulates the generation of adversarial examples satisfying the unlearning conditions as a blind inversion problem. We solve this by integrating the bi-level optimization process of adversarial training into an Expectation-Maximization (EM) algorithm framework to optimize the maximum a posteriori (MAP) objective. Furthermore, BI-BAU is extended to untargeted adversarial scenarios with unknown target classes, as well as to multi-modal contrastive learning tasks, enhancing its applicability to real-world deployment scenarios where pre-trained models may be compromised. Extensive experiments demonstrate that our method exhibits general applicability across a wide spectrum of backdoor attacks and can effectively and thoroughly eliminate the backdoor effects from a backdoor model.

</details>


### [72] [Deep Spectral Learning of Embedded Latent Transfer Operators for Stochastic Dynamical Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14079)
*Ryogo Tanaka,Yoshinobu Kawahara*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: DSE learns a latent state-space model by combining neural feature maps with transfer operators, solved via functional CCA and ridge regression, enabling Bayesian filtering and Koopman mode decomposition in deep feature spaces.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Stochastic nonlinear dynamical systems are challenging to model, especially under noise and partial observability. Existing methods struggle to simultaneously learn compact Markovian latent states and linear operators that faithfully capture dynamics. The authors aim to bridge spectral learning (transfer operators) with deep learning to obtain a principled, closed-form estimation framework.

Method: The method, Deep Spectral Encoder (DSE), uses a time-invariant neural encoder to map observations into a learnable feature space. Functional canonical correlation analysis (fCCA) in a Galerkin-projected feature space yields state coordinates from past/future observation windows. The transfer operator (dynamics) and observation operator are then estimated as ridge-regularized closed-form solutions — equivalent to Galerkin projections of covariance operators. This representation supports sequential Bayesian filtering and Koopman spectral mode decomposition in feature space.

Result: DSE demonstrates stable and superior performance compared to sequential Bayesian filtering and dynamic mode decomposition baselines across multiple scenarios, including settings with noise and partial observability.

Conclusion: A spectral learning approach that embeds transfer operators in deep feature spaces yields a robust latent state-space model, overcoming limitations of purely data-driven or purely operator-theoretic methods for stochastic nonlinear systems.

Abstract: We propose a spectral learning method for stochastic nonlinear dynamical systems represented with embedded latent transfer operators in deep feature spaces. We instantiate the method as Deep Spectral Encoder (DSE), an operator-based latent state-space model in which a time-invariant neural encoder implements learnable nonlinear feature maps from observations, and these features define Markovian latent states whose temporal evolution and observation mapping are described by the transfer and observation operators, respectively. Functional canonical correlation analysis in a learnable Galerkin-projected feature space provides state coordinates from past and future observations, and the two linear operators are estimated on the state coordinates as ridge-regularized closed-form solutions that coincide with Galerkin projections of the associated covariance operators. On this representation, we generalize sequential Bayesian filtering and Koopman spectral mode decomposition in feature space. Experiments on several scenarios show stable and superior performance with sequential Bayesian filtering and dynamic mode decomposition baselines even under noise and partial observability.

</details>


### [73] [Lyapunov-Based Sample Complexity Analysis for Weakly-Coupled MDPs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14095)
*Tianhao Wu,Matthew Zurek,Weina Wang,Qiaomin Xie*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper establishes polynomial sample complexity bounds for learning near-optimal policies in average-reward weakly-coupled MDPs and restless bandits under a generative model, overcoming the exponential blowup of naive tabular approaches. A novel Lyapunov-based analysis framework is introduced, replacing traditional bias-function methods with drift transfer and fine-grained LP perturbation analysis.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Learning in weakly-coupled MDPs (WCMDPs) and restless bandits (RBs) is challenging because naive reduction to tabular MDPs yields state-action spaces exponential in the number of arms N. The authors aim to develop algorithms with sample and computational complexity polynomial in N, providing finite-sample PAC guarantees that scale favorably.

Method: The paper employs a plug-in approach: first estimate an empirical model from generative samples, then apply an efficient planning algorithm to this estimated model. The core technical innovation is a Lyapunov-based analysis framework that uses an explicitly constructed Lyapunov function and a drift transfer technique to relate the true and empirical models, avoiding reliance on the bias function. A fine-grained perturbation analysis of the underlying LP relaxation serves as a key analytical tool for LP-based policies in weakly-coupled systems.

Result: For fully heterogeneous WCMDPs, the authors establish the first finite-sample PAC guarantee with polynomial complexity and an O(1/sqrt(N)) optimality gap. For homogeneous RBs, they prove a smaller optimality gap is achievable under mild structural assumptions.

Conclusion: The Lyapunov-based analysis framework provides a general and powerful alternative to classical bias-function methods for analyzing LP-based policies in weakly-coupled systems. The polynomial sample complexity results demonstrate that the exponential state-space barrier can be circumvented by exploiting structure.

Abstract: We study the sample complexity of learning in average-reward weakly-coupled Markov decision processes (WCMDPs) and Restless Bandits (RBs) under a generative model. Naive reduction to a tabular MDP leads to high complexity bounds as the state-action space is exponentially large in the number of arms $N$. By exploiting the weakly coupled structure, we show that near-optimal policies can be learned with sample and computational complexities that are polynomial in $N$. Specifically, we analyze the plug-in approach, which applies an efficient planning algorithm to an empirical model estimated from data. For fully heterogeneous WCMDPs, we establish the first finite-sample PAC guarantee with polynomial complexity and an $O(1/\sqrt{N})$ optimality gap. For homogeneous RBs, we further prove that a smaller optimality gap is achievable under mild structural assumptions. A primary technical contribution of our work is a novel Lyapunov-based analysis framework. Unlike classical approaches that rely on the difficult-to-control bias function, our framework uses an explicitly constructed Lyapunov function along with a drift transfer technique between the true and empirical models. A key step of independent interest in our framework is a fine-grained perturbation analysis for the underlying linear programming (LP) relaxation, which provides a general tool for analyzing LP-based policies and weakly-coupled systems.

</details>


### [74] [Numbers Already Carry Their Own Embeddings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14108)
*Suhyun Bae,Donghun Lee*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: AOE is a training-free numerical embedding that jointly encodes real values and p-adic (modular) signatures while preserving addition and multiplication. It plugs into existing architectures and achieves state-of-the-art results on algebraic combinatorics benchmarks, including perfect accuracy on the Weaving Pattern task.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Neural networks struggle with numerical reasoning — the "number problem" — because standard embeddings fail to capture both magnitude and modular arithmetic structure. Prior solutions require task-specific retraining. The authors aim to create a universal, plug-and-play numerical representation that inherently respects both real-valued and modular (p-adic) properties.

Method: Adelic Operation-preserved Embeddings (AOE) use the adelic framework from number theory, combining real embeddings with p-adic embeddings for multiple primes. The construction is designed such that addition and multiplication in the embedding space correspond to the same operations on the represented numbers. Crucially, AOE requires no training — it is computed deterministically from the number itself and can be dropped into any architecture as a replacement for standard numerical encodings.

Result: AOE delivers consistent performance gains on algebraic combinatorics benchmarks. Notably, it achieves the first-ever perfect accuracy on the Weaving Pattern task, demonstrating that the embedding captures structural properties essential for mathematical reasoning that prior approaches missed.

Conclusion: By jointly encoding real and p-adic structure in an operation-preserving manner, AOE offers a principled, training-free solution to the number problem. Its plug-and-play nature and strong empirical results suggest a promising direction for integrating genuine mathematical structure into neural architectures.

Abstract: We introduce Adelic operation-preserved embeddings (AOE), a training-free representation that captures both a number's real value and its modular (p-adic) signatures. This construction preserves additive and multiplicative structure by design, turning numerical input into embeddings that "speak in the language of mathematics." Unlike prior approaches that rely on task-specific retraining, AOE is plug-and-play and drops seamlessly into existing architectures. On algebraic combinatorics benchmarks, it delivers consistent gains including the first-ever perfect accuracy on the Weaving Pattern task-while suggesting a principled path forward for overcoming the long-standing "number problem" in AI.

</details>


### [75] [DTVEM-RE: A Hierarchical Random-Effects Extension of the Differential Time-Varying Effect Model for Person-Specific Multi-Lag Estimation in Intensive Longitudinal Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14116)
*Amartya Bhattacharya*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: DTVEM-RE extends the Differential Time-Varying Effect Model to allow person-specific lag structures via hierarchical Bayesian VAR (discrete-time) and Ornstein-Uhlenbeck models (continuous-time). Simulations show good parameter recovery, and empirical application demonstrates substantial between-person heterogeneity in lag-1 effects, improved prediction, and detection of multi-lag individual differences that single-lag methods miss.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Jacobson et al. (2019) DTVEM assumes a homogeneous lag structure across individuals, which contradicts the person-specific premise of modern clinical research. The original authors flagged this limitation as future work. The motivation is to develop a method that lets each person have their own lag coefficients while retaining DTVEM's lag-detection strengths.

Method: Two complementary approaches: (1) a discrete-time hierarchical Bayesian VAR implemented in Stan, pooling across persons with full posterior uncertainty; (2) a continuous-time per-person Ornstein-Uhlenbeck model in ctsem for irregularly spaced measurements. The confirmatory step of DTVEM is adapted to accommodate random effects on lag coefficients. A multi-lag extension estimates person-specific tau_k parameters across multiple lags simultaneously.

Result: (1) Simulation: Bayesian version recovers between-person spread tau_a with bias < 0.01 and coverage of 90–93%. (2) Fisher et al. (2017) EMA data (N=40): person-specific lag-1 effects vary by an order of magnitude; Bayesian and GAMM estimates correlate r=0.87–0.92. (3) DTVEM-RE achieves best one-step-ahead prediction among four discrete-time methods. (4) Multi-lag version: all nine tau_k credible intervals exclude zero; the lag of maximal between-person differences varies across items. (5) Discrete- and continuous-time versions agree almost identically (r ≥ 0.995).

Conclusion: DTVEM-RE is the first person-specific implementation of DTVEM-style lag detection, subsuming standard DTVEM as a special case. It successfully captures meaningful individual differences in temporal dynamics that homogeneous models miss, with strong agreement between discrete- and continuous-time formulations.

Abstract: The Differential Time-Varying Effect Model (DTVEM) of Jacobson et al. (2019) is a popular tool for finding the best time lag in intensive longitudinal data, but it assumes everyone shares the same lag structure. The original authors named fixing this as future work, and it clashes with the premise of modern clinical research, which is that people differ. We present DTVEM-RE, an extension that lets each person have their own lag coefficients, with two versions of the confirmatory step: a discrete-time hierarchical Bayesian VAR in Stan, which pools across people and gives calibrated uncertainty, and a continuous-time per-person Ornstein-Uhlenbeck model in ctsem, which handles unevenly spaced beeps directly. We report four results. A simulation shows the Bayesian version recovers the between-person spread tau_a with bias below 0.01 and coverage of 90 to 93 percent. On the Fisher et al. (2017) EMA dataset (N=40), person-specific lag-1 effects vary by an order of magnitude across three mood items, the Bayesian and GAMM estimates agree closely (r=0.87 to 0.92), and DTVEM-RE gives the best one-step-ahead prediction among four discrete-time methods. A multi-lag version shows all nine tau_k values have credible intervals excluding zero, and the lag where people differ most changes across items, something lag-1-only methods like mlVAR cannot detect. Finally, the two versions agree almost exactly on person-specific lag-1 estimates (r >= 0.995), differing only as shrinkage predicts. DTVEM-RE is, to our knowledge, the first person-specific implementation of DTVEM-style lag detection, and it contains standard DTVEM as a special case.

</details>


### [76] [Recovering Stranded Discrimination in Knowledge Tracing: Per-Item Bias Correction via Empirical-Bayes Shrinkage](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14123)
*Xiaoran Yan,Cheng Tang,Atsushi Shimada*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SLC (State-space Logit Correction) addresses per-item logit bias in frozen knowledge-tracing models using Gaussian pseudo-observations, empirical Bayes shrinkage via a Kalman smoother, and an offset-Platt link, improving both AUC and NLL—especially for sparse items—unlike monotone global calibrators that only improve calibration without affecting discrimination.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deployed knowledge-tracing models are frozen after training but suffer from systematic per-item logit bias due to limited per-item expressivity in backbone architectures and post-deployment distribution shifts. While global post-hoc calibrators (Platt scaling, temperature scaling, isotonic regression) improve probability calibration, they leave AUC unchanged because monotone score-only transforms preserve ranking. Recovering stranded discriminative ability requires conditioning on item identity.

Method: SLC operates in three stages: (1) converting binary observations to Gaussian pseudo-observations via Laplace approximation / IRLS; (2) applying empirical Bayes shrinkage through a Kalman smoother over a state-space model to borrow strength across items over time; (3) fitting an offset-Platt link function for calibration. The state-space formulation also yields a detectability bound characterizing the Bernoulli information floor, explaining why temporal tracking fails at current data densities.

Result: Across four datasets, five backbones, and three seeds, SLC improved AUC on all four datasets and NLL on three, with gains concentrated on sparse items. Cross-domain control experiments suggest the phenomenon generalizes beyond education whenever a deployed backbone leaves entity-level bias.

Conclusion: SLC recovers item-conditional discrimination that monotone global calibrators cannot, demonstrating that post-hoc per-item correction via state-space modeling is both feasible and beneficial. The detectability bound further provides a theoretical explanation for when temporal item tracking can or cannot help, suggesting broad applicability beyond educational knowledge tracing.

Abstract: Deployed knowledge-tracing models are typically frozen after training, yet systematic per-item logit bias arises, from limited per-item expressivity in backbone architectures and from post-deployment shifts in item properties, degrading prediction quality. Global post-hoc calibrators such as Platt scaling, temperature scaling, and isotonic regression improve probability estimates but leave discriminative ability, as measured by AUC, unchanged. This AUC invariance is a structural consequence of monotone score-only transforms; recovering the stranded discrimination requires conditioning on item identity. We propose SLC (State-space Logit Correction), which converts binary observations to Gaussian pseudo-observations via Laplace/IRLS, applies empirical-Bayes shrinkage through a Kalman smoother, and fits an offset-Platt link. The state-space formulation also yields a detectability bound that characterizes the Bernoulli information floor, explaining why temporal tracking provides no benefit at current data densities. Across four datasets, five backbones, and three seeds, SLC improves AUC on all four datasets and NLL on three, with the advantage concentrating on sparse items. Cross-domain controls suggest that the same phenomenon can arise beyond education when the deployed backbone leaves entity-level bias.

</details>


### [77] [Decoupled Latent Optimization of Diffusion Models for Full Waveform Inversion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14139)
*Chen Min,Zheng Ma*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: DLO reformulates latent-optimization FWI into a quadratic-penalty objective that decouples data-fidelity gradients (physical space) from the diffusion prior (latent space), outperforming both classical regularizers and existing diffusion-based methods on clean, noisy, and missing-trace data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Classical FWI regularizers fail to produce realistic geological structures, while recent diffusion-prior methods suffer from a fragile trade-off between data fidelity and prior consistency. A robust approach is needed that preserves both realism and fidelity without mutual interference.

Method: Decoupled Latent Optimization (DLO) introduces a quadratic-penalty formulation with an auxiliary physical variable and a latent variable. Data-fidelity gradients act strictly in physical space, while the diffusion sampler contributes solely via decoded prior samples. Standard smoothed-velocity FWI initialization is preserved. The diffusion prior is trained on 70×70 OpenFWI models.

Result: DLO outperforms classical regularizers and existing diffusion-based methods on the OpenFWI benchmark under clean, noisy, and missing-trace acquisitions. It transfers zero-shot to Marmousi and Overthrust benchmarks, recovering intricate fault structures while remaining robust to initialization smoothing and measurement noise.

Conclusion: Decoupling the data-fidelity and prior-consistency terms resolves the fragility of diffusion-prior FWI, enabling robust, high-fidelity subsurface recovery that generalizes across datasets and acquisition conditions.

Abstract: Full waveform inversion (FWI) recovers subsurface velocity from seismic recordings by solving a severely ill-posed, nonconvex PDE-constrained optimization. Classical regularizers stabilize the inversion but fail to reproduce realistic geological structures; recent diffusion-prior methods improve realism at the cost of a fragile trade-off between data fidelity and prior consistency. We propose Decoupled Latent Optimization (DLO), which relaxes the standard latent-optimization formulation into a quadratic-penalty objective over an auxiliary physical variable and a latent variable. The data-fidelity gradient acts in physical space, the diffusion sampler contributes only through a decoded prior sample, and the standard smoothed-velocity initialization of classical FWI is preserved. On the OpenFWI benchmark, DLO outperforms classical regularizers and existing diffusion-based methods under clean, noisy, and missing-trace acquisitions. The prior, trained on 70*70 OpenFWI models, transfers directly to the Marmousi and Overthrust benchmarks, where DLO recovers intricate fault structures and remains robust to initialization smoothing and measurement noise.

</details>


### [78] [Trust but Verify: Mitigating Medical Hallucinations via Post-Hoc Adversarial Auditing and Multi-Agent Feedback Loops](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14149)
*Muhammad Osama,Maheera Amjad,Zartasha Mustansar,Arslan Shaukat,Muhammad U. S. Khan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LLMs frequently recommend banned or withdrawn drugs in clinical settings. A five-agent "Trust but Verify" system reduced hallucination errors by ~53%, shifting outputs from unsafe recommendations toward appropriate refusal, offering a model-agnostic safety layer for healthcare AI deployment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs deployed in healthcare risk patient safety by hallucinating outdated regulatory knowledge — specifically, recommending pharmaceuticals that have been recently banned or withdrawn. Existing models lack mechanisms to stay current with evolving drug safety regulations, creating dangerous gaps in clinical decision support.

Method: The authors created an adversarial dataset of 103 clinical MCQs where historically correct answers now refer to banned substances, spanning multiple therapeutic classes for statistical significance. They developed a five-agent "Trust but Verify" architecture using a single LLM backbone, where agents cross-check outputs against regulatory data. Three open-access model families (GPT-OSS, Llama-3, Falcon-3) and proprietary models were evaluated under vanilla and agentic conditions using pointwise score, label accuracy, Hallucination Error Rate (HER), and Component Fidelity (CF) score.

Result: In default configurations, all models exhibited high hallucination rates, consistently selecting banned drugs aligned with training data. The agentic architecture reduced HER by approximately 53% across models. Pointwise scores shifted from -0.25 (unsafe recommendation) toward 0.0 (appropriate refusal). The safety audit successfully intercepted dangerous outputs even when parametric knowledge favored banned substances. Clinical safety regression was also observed in proprietary models.

Conclusion: The multi-agent "Trust but Verify" framework provides a model-agnostic, practical method for enforcing regulatory compliance in LLM-based clinical decision support, prioritizing patient safety over fluent generation and demonstrating how real-time regulatory data can be integrated into LLM pipelines.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed in healthcare settings, yet their tendency to hallucinate poses risks when clinical decisions are involved. This study examine whether LLMs recommend recently banned or withdrawn pharmaceuticals when answering clinical questions and tests an agent-based method for reducing such errors. We developed a five-agent "Trust but Verify" system using a single LLM backbone. To measure regulatory knowledge obsolescence, we created an adversarial dataset of 103 clinical MCQs where historically correct answers now refer to banned substances. This scale ensures statistical significance across various therapeutic classes. We evaluated three open-access model families (GPT-OSS, Llama-3, Falcon-3) under vanilla and agentic conditions. Performance was measured via pointwise score, label accuracy, Hallucination Error Rate (HER), and Component Fidelity (CF) score. We also observed clinical safety regression in proprietary models. In default configurations, all models showed high hallucination rates, consistently selecting banned drugs that matched training data patterns. Our proposed agentic architecture reduced HER by approximately 53% across models. Pointwise scores shifted from -0.25 (unsafe recommendation) toward 0.0 (appropriate refusal). The safety audit intercepted dangerous outputs even when models' parametric knowledge favored the banned substance. The proposed multi-agent framework offers a model-agnostic method for enforcing regulatory compliance that prioritizes patient safety over fluent text generation. Our work demonstrates a practical approach for deploying autonomous AI systems in safety-critical healthcare settings. It shows how real-time regulatory data can be integrated into LLM pipelines to support clinical decision-making.

</details>


### [79] [Small LLMs: Pruning vs. Training from Scratch](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14150)
*Yufeng Xu,Taiming Lu,Kunjun Li,Jiachen Zhu,Mingjie Sun,Zhuang Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper investigates whether pruning large pretrained models (using Llama-3.1-8B at pruning ratios 0.5–0.8 across six methods) yields better small language models than training from scratch, under token-matched comparisons. Results show pruning is advantageous under limited token budgets but can be matched or surpassed by training from scratch when token budgets are abundant, especially for coarser structured pruning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To rigorously evaluate the claim that pruning large pretrained models offers a "shortcut" to strong small language models, by disentangling the effects of the parent model's learned knowledge from the training token budget, which prior work often conflates.

Method: The authors prune Llama-3.1-8B at ratios from 0.5 to 0.8 using six pruning methods (depth, width, and sparse granularities). They compare pruned finetuning against random initialization (training from scratch) under two controlled token-matched settings: (1) equal training token budgets, and (2) training from scratch receiving the full token budget of the entire prune-then-finetune pipeline.

Result: (1) Under equal training token budgets, pruned initialization consistently outperforms random initialization, but the margin narrows with larger budgets and higher pruning ratios, nearly vanishing at the highest ratios. (2) Under the full pipeline token budget, fine-grained pruning retains an advantage over training from scratch, while coarser structured pruning can be matched or surpassed by training from scratch.

Conclusion: Pruning is recommended when a large pretrained model is available and the training token budget is limited. When the training budget is not constrained, training from scratch can be competitive for coarser pruning, making a large pretrained parent not strictly necessary.

Abstract: Pruning promises a shortcut to strong small language models. In this work, we examine this promise by pruning Llama-3.1-8B at pruning ratios of 0.5--0.8 with six methods spanning depth, width, and sparse granularities, under two controlled token-matched settings. (1) With the same training token budget, pruned initialization consistently outperforms random initialization. This shows that the parent model provides a strong starting point, although the advantage narrows as the training token budget grows and as the pruning ratio rises, nearly vanishing at the highest pruning ratio we study. (2) When training from scratch is instead given the full token budget consumed by the whole pipeline, pruning at finer granularities still retains an advantage, while coarser structured pruning can be matched or surpassed. This suggests that the parent model transfers knowledge that additional training tokens alone cannot fully recover, but only at fine granularity. Taken together, our results yield a clear recommendation: with a large pretrained model in hand and a limited training token budget, pruning is better than training from scratch; when the training budget is not limited, training from scratch can be competitive for coarser pruning, so a large pretrained parent is not always necessary.

</details>


### [80] [Graph-based Target Back-Propagation for Context Adaptation in Multi-LLM Agentic Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14155)
*Tan Zhu,Tong Yao,Kananart Kuwaranancharoen,Amit Singh,Yushang Lai,Deepa Mohan,Shankara Bhargava*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: GTBP is a graph-based context adaptation framework for multi-LLM agentic workflows that uses backward propagation of target outputs to guide stage-wise prompt updates, offering convergence guarantees and outperforming baselines across benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Extending context adaptation (automated prompt engineering) to multi-LLM agentic systems is challenging because existing methods suffer from inaccurate credit assignment among agents and lack convergence guarantees.

Method: GTBP models agentic workflows as directed acyclic graphs (DAGs). It propagates local target outputs backward through the workflow graph and computes target–output discrepancies to drive a stage-wise prompt update mechanism. The framework iteratively revises tunable prompts without modifying model weights.

Result: Theoretically, GTBP's stage-wise prompt updates stabilize over iterations, and a sufficiently capable LLM optimizer provably decreases the overall objective. Empirically, GTBP consistently outperforms strong baselines on three benchmarks while maintaining comparable computational cost.

Conclusion: GTBP provides a principled, convergence-guaranteed solution for context adaptation in multi-agent LLM systems, advancing the paradigm beyond single-agent prompt tuning to complex agentic workflows.

Abstract: Context adaptation automates prompt engineering in LLM-based systems by iteratively revising tunable prompts from task feedback, without modifying model weights. Extending this paradigm to multi-LLM agentic systems is crucial: existing methods suffer from inaccurate credit assignment and lack convergence guarantees. We propose \textbf{G}raph-based \textbf{T}arget \textbf{B}ack-\textbf{P}ropagation (GTBP), a context adaptation framework for agentic workflows modeled as directed acyclic graphs. GTBP propagates local target outputs backward through the workflow graph and uses target--output discrepancies to guide a stage-wise prompt update mechanism. Theoretically, we show that GTBP's stage-wise prompt updates become stable over iterations, and that a sufficiently capable LLM optimizer can decrease the overall objective. Empirically, GTBP consistently outperforms strong baselines across three benchmarks while maintaining comparable computational cost.

</details>


### [81] [Learning High Coverage Discriminative Parsimonious Rulesets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14156)
*Mariamma Antony,Raman Sankaran,Chiranjib Bhattacharyya,Uma Satya Ranjan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper introduces CDPR, a novel approach for learning classification rule sets that balances accuracy and interpretability. Using submodular maximization, it provides provable coverage guarantees and produces discriminative, parsimonious rules. Experiments show a 2.5× improvement in average coverage over the next best method.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing IF-THEN rule learning algorithms prioritize predictive accuracy but underperform on interpretability metrics such as coverage and parsimony. There is a gap in methods that simultaneously optimize for both high accuracy and high interpretability.

Method: The authors propose CDPR, grounded in two algorithms based on submodular maximization. These algorithms provide provable guarantees on rule set coverage while ensuring the resulting rule sets are both discriminative (high accuracy) and parsimonious (compact and interpretable).

Result: Empirical results demonstrate that CDPR-learned rule sets achieve higher accuracy and interpretability compared to state-of-the-art alternatives, with over a 2.5-fold improvement in average coverage rates relative to the next best algorithm.

Conclusion: CDPR is the first approach to successfully combine submodular maximization with rule learning to deliver provably high-coverage, accurate, and parsimonious rule sets, setting a new benchmark for interpretable classification.

Abstract: Learning systems based on IF-THEN rule representations readily offer interpretability, making them a crucial focus in contemporary AI research. A key objective for such rule sets is to achieve both high discriminative power and interpretability. While existing state-of-the-art algorithms implicitly prioritize predictive accuracy, they often fall short on one or more quality metrics that ensure interpretability, such as coverage and parsimony of rule sets. Motivated by this, this paper propose the development of CDPR, which aims to create highly accurate and interpretable rule sets for classification problems. To the best of our knowledge, this represents the first attempt to establish such an approach. In this study, we introduce two algorithms rooted in submodular maximization, which not only provide provable guarantees on coverage but also yield rule sets that are both discriminative and parsimonious. We empirically demonstrate that rule sets learned through our approaches achieve higher accuracy and interpretability and has more than a 2.5-fold improvement in average coverage rates when compared to the next best algorithm.

</details>


### [82] [Learning Urban Access Costs from Origin-Destination Flows via Inverse Optimal Transport](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14157)
*Paula Joy B. Martinez*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper models school choice in the Philippines as an entropic optimal transport problem, using inverse optimal transport to recover latent cost functions from observed enrollment flows. Two complementary models—a distance-banded interpretable model and a neural cost model with differentiable Sinkhorn—estimate a "subsidy-equivalent distance" metric that quantifies how many kilometers of perceived travel cost the education subsidy offsets, enabling accessibility-aware urban service planning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Planners in mixed public-private urban service systems observe where households go but not the latent cost function governing their trade-offs among distance, price, and institutional access. In the Philippines, a national education subsidy aims to redirect learners from congested public schools to private schools, but the behavioral impact — how households actually weigh these factors — remains unobserved, limiting effective policy design.

Method: The authors frame school-to-school enrollment flows as an entropic optimal transport plan and apply two inverse optimal transport models to recover latent choice costs: (1) an interpretable distance-banded model incorporating a subsidy term, and (2) a neural cost model trained via a differentiable Sinkhorn forward pass. The analysis uses 283,016 learner trips across 23,820 observed origin-destination flows in the Philippines' most populated region.

Result: The framework produces a metric λ⁽ᵏ⁾ — the subsidy-equivalent distance — interpreted as kilometers of perceived travel cost offset by the subsidy. This transforms administrative origin-destination data into an interpretable planning metric quantifying how the subsidy alters household travel behavior.

Conclusion: Inverse optimal transport with administrative OD data yields actionable, interpretable metrics for accessibility-aware subsidy design, facility siting, and urban service allocation, as demonstrated through the Philippine school choice case study.

Abstract: Cities deliver basic services through mixed public-private facility networks, including schools, clinics, transit providers, and subsidized service points. In these systems, planners often observe where households go, but not the latent cost function through which they trade off factors such as distance, price, and institutional access. We study this urban problem through school choice in the Philippines, where the country's largest national education subsidy is intended to redirect learners from congested public schools to participating private schools. Treating school-to-school enrollment flows as an entropic optimal transport plan, we recover latent choice costs using two complementary inverse optimal transport models: an interpretable distance-banded model with a subsidy term, and a neural cost model trained through a differentiable Sinkhorn forward pass. Applied to 283{,}016 learner trips across 23{,}820 observed flows in the most populated region, the framework estimates a subsidy-equivalent distance, $λ^{(k)}$, interpreted as the kilometers of perceived travel cost offset by the subsidy. The case demonstrates how administrative origin-destination data can be transformed into interpretable planning metrics for accessibility-aware subsidy design, facility siting, and urban service allocation.

</details>


### [83] [Curvature-Guided Geometric Representation for Protein-Ligand Binding Affinity Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14159)
*Shuai Li,Chuan-Xian Ren,Yuhao Li,Ziqi Huang,Yue Pan,Mingzhe Tang,Hong Yan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: RicciBind integrates Ricci curvature-guided hierarchical structure learning with optimal transport-based cross-domain alignment to jointly model local geometric organization and global cross-molecular interactions for protein-ligand binding affinity prediction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing PLA methods fail to jointly capture local geometric organization within molecules and globally coordinated cross-molecular interactions, limiting their ability to model complex binding mechanisms.

Method: RicciBind uses (1) Ricci curvature to quantify local interaction tightness and build curvature-aware hierarchical atomic representations, and (2) optimal transport-based cluster matching to align protein and ligand clusters across heterogeneous domains under geometric constraints, enabling globally consistent correspondences and higher-order interaction patterns.

Result: RicciBind achieved superior predictive performance and generalization on PLA benchmarks and virtual screening tasks; ablation studies confirmed Ricci curvature's essential role in enhancing molecular interaction representations.

Conclusion: By coupling curvature-guided encoding with OT-driven alignment, RicciBind effectively models complex interaction semantics, substantially improving both accuracy and interpretability of binding affinity prediction.

Abstract: Protein-ligand binding affinity (PLA) prediction is critical in drug discovery. Despite the notable advancements in machine learning-based approaches, existing methods struggle to jointly characterize local geometric organization and globally coordinated cross-molecular interactions, limiting their ability to model complex binding mechanisms. Here, we propose RicciBind, a geometric representation framework that integrates curvature-guided hierarchical structure learning with optimal transport (OT)-based cross-domain alignment to model molecular interactions. Specifically, RicciBind leverages Ricci curvature to capture local interaction tightness within molecular structures, enhancing structural awareness and organizing atomic interactions into curvature-aware hierarchical representations. An OT-based cluster matching mechanism then aligns protein and ligand clusters across heterogeneous domains under geometric constraints, enabling globally consistent correspondences and revealing higher-order interaction patterns beyond local neighborhoods. By coupling curvature-guided structure encoding with OT-driven cross-domain alignment, RicciBind effectively models complex interaction semantics and substantially improves both the accuracy and interpretability of binding affinity prediction. Extensive experiments demonstrate that RicciBind achieved superior predictive performance and generalization across PLA benchmarks and virtual screening tasks. Ablation studies further confirmed the essential role of Ricci curvature in enhancing molecular interaction representations.

</details>


### [84] [Machine Learning for Biomedical Raman Spectroscopy: From Spectral Acquisition to Clinical Translation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14169)
*Bogdan Oancea,Ana Maria Seciu-Grama,Nicoleta Siminea,Laura Mihaela Stefan,Alice Stoica,Joel Sjoberg,Marian Necula,Ana-Maria Prelipcean,Corneliu Ovidiu Vrancianu,Eduard Milea,Andrei Păun,Ion Petre,Mihaela Păun*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A review examining machine learning's role across the biomedical Raman spectroscopy pipeline — from preprocessing to clinical deployment — highlighting both methodological advances and translational barriers such as limited datasets, inter-instrument variability, and insufficient validation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Biomedical Raman spectra are high-dimensional, noisy, and confounded by fluorescence background, acquisition variability, and biological heterogeneity. Robust computational analysis is essential for realizing Raman spectroscopy's potential in cancer diagnosis, molecular subtyping, microbiological identification, and intraoperative guidance. Machine learning offers a path to address these analytical challenges while enabling biologically interpretable, clinically actionable outputs.

Method: This is a comprehensive review paper that surveys machine learning approaches across the entire biomedical Raman spectroscopy pipeline: (1) preprocessing and signal correction, (2) unsupervised structure discovery, (3) supervised diagnosis and molecular stratification, (4) representation and transfer learning, (5) explainability and biomarker discovery, and (6) multimodal integration with imaging, pathology, and molecular profiling. The review synthesizes methodological, biomedical, and translational perspectives.

Result: Key findings include: machine learning enhances every stage of the Raman analysis pipeline; emphasis should extend beyond classification to interpretable, actionable analysis; major barriers to clinical translation are limited dataset sizes, inter-instrument variability, inconsistent preprocessing, insufficient external validation, reproducibility issues, and poor sharing of software/data/metadata. Progress requires both methodological advances and standardization with robust validation frameworks.

Conclusion: Developing reliable, clinically deployable Raman-AI systems requires integrating advances in machine learning methodology with standardization, rigorous external validation, explainability, and deployment-ready analytical frameworks. The review outlines key directions for achieving this integration across methodological, biomedical, and translational dimensions.

Abstract: Raman spectroscopy provides label-free, chemically specific characterization of biological systems and has become an important tool for cancer diagnosis, molecular subtyping, microbiological identification, and intraoperative decision support. Biomedical Raman spectra are, however, high-dimensional, noisy, and affected by fluorescence background, acquisition variability, and biological heterogeneity, making robust computational analysis essential.
  This review examines the role of machine learning across the biomedical Raman spectroscopy pipeline, from preprocessing and signal correction to unsupervised structure discovery, supervised diagnosis and molecular stratification, representation and transfer learning, explainability, biomarker discovery, and multimodal integration with imaging, pathology, and molecular profiling. Emphasis is placed on the use of machine learning not only for diagnostic classification, but also for biologically interpretable and clinically actionable analysis.
  We also discuss the main barriers to clinical translation, including limited dataset sizes, inter-instrument variability, inconsistent preprocessing, insufficient external validation, reproducibility concerns, and limited sharing of software, data, and metadata. We argue that progress will require methodological advances together with standardization, robust validation, explainability, and deployment-ready analytical frameworks. By integrating methodological, biomedical, and translational perspectives, this review outlines key directions for developing reliable and clinically deployable Raman-AI systems.

</details>


### [85] [Context-aware Modality-Topology Co-Alignment for Multimodal Attributed Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14172)
*Sirui Zhang,Xu Wang,Zhengyu Wu,Xunkai Li,Hongchao Qin*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CoMAG is a unified Multimodal Attributed Graph backbone that learns task-adaptive reliable contexts and preserves modality-specific representations, achieving state-of-the-art performance on graph prediction, cross-modal matching, and graph-conditioned generation tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing MAG methods suffer from task-agnostic propagation (using fixed or uniformly fused graph contexts regardless of downstream tasks) and over-compressed fusion (losing modality-specific evidence when merging heterogeneous attributes). These limitations hinder both structure-centric tasks requiring discriminative representations and modality-centric tasks requiring fine-grained cross-modal alignment.

Method: CoMAG has two core modules: (1) Reliable Context Learning — estimates edge reliability from multimodal semantic consistency, augments raw topology with semantic neighbors, and uses a task-aware gate to select relevant context components; (2) Modality-preserving Hop-token Alignment — maintains modality-specific multi-hop trajectories, matches modality-hop tokens across modalities, and decouples shared vs. private representations. Both graph and modality representations are produced in a single forward pass.

Result: Experiments on nine OpenMAG datasets against feature-only, graph-only, multimodal, and unified MAG baselines show CoMAG achieves best reported performance across graph-level prediction, modality matching, and graph-conditioned generation, while retaining sparse edge-linear complexity.

Conclusion: Task-adaptive reliable contexts and modality-preserving alignment effectively improve structural prediction, cross-modal matching, and graph-conditioned generation. CoMAG also provides theoretical analysis on stable propagation, over-smoothing mitigation, and modality-collapse control.

Abstract: Multimodal Attributed Graphs (MAGs) model real-world entities by coupling graph topology with heterogeneous attributes such as text and images. They support graph-centric tasks requiring structural and class-discriminative representations, and modality-centric tasks requiring fine-grained cross-modal correspondence. However, existing MAG methods often rely on fixed graph contexts or uniformly fused representations, causing task-agnostic propagation and over-compressed fusion that hinder diverse task requirements and modality-specific evidence preservation. To address this, we propose CoMAG, a unified MAG backbone that learns task-adaptive reliable contexts and modality-preserving alignment within them. CoMAG first conducts Reliable Context Learning by estimating edge reliability from multimodal semantic consistency, complementing raw topology with semantic neighbors, and selecting context components through a task-aware gate. It then performs Modality-preserving Hop-token Alignment by maintaining modality-specific multi-hop trajectories, matching modality-hop tokens across modalities, and decoupling shared and private representations. Thus, CoMAG produces graph and modality representations from one forward pass while retaining modality-specific cues. We further analyze stable propagation, over-smoothing mitigation, and modality-collapse control. Experiments on nine OpenMAG datasets compare CoMAG with feature-only, graph-only, multimodal, and unified MAG baselines across graph-level prediction, modality matching, and graph-conditioned generation. Results show that CoMAG achieves the best reported performance, demonstrating that task-adaptive reliable contexts and modality-preserving alignment improve structural prediction, cross-modal matching, and graph-conditioned generation while retaining sparse edge-linear complexity.

</details>


### [86] [Simplex-Constrained Sparse Bagging: Transitioning from Uniform Priors to Sparse Posteriors in Ensemble Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13589)
*Meher Sai Preetam,Meher Bhaskar*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SCSB is a post-training framework that jointly optimizes ensemble pruning and probability calibration by solving a simplex-constrained sparse optimization problem using a concave quadratic penalty (since L1 fails on the simplex), achieving up to 96% compression with improved calibration and maintained accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard bagging ensembles assign uniform weights to all base estimators, ignoring their varying local competence and leading to model overconfidence. Existing pruning and calibration methods lack a unified, mathematically rigorous framework that operates on the probability simplex while inducing true sparsity.

Method: SCSB formulates ensemble pruning and calibration as a joint optimization problem over the probability simplex, minimizing OOB loss. To overcome the "L1-simplex paradox" (L1 norm is constant on the simplex and cannot induce sparsity), a concave quadratic penalty is introduced. The method is model-agnostic and applies to any bootstrap-based bagging ensemble (Random Forests, Bagged SVMs, Bagged Neural Networks).

Result: SCSB achieves up to 96% ensemble compression, yielding linear inference speedups. It delivers superior probability calibration (lower Expected Calibration Error) while preserving or enhancing generalization accuracy.

Conclusion: SCSB provides a rigorous and practical solution for simultaneously compressing and calibrating bagging ensembles, overcoming fundamental limitations of L1 regularization on the simplex through a concave quadratic penalty, with strong empirical results across multiple model types.

Abstract: We present Simplex-Constrained Sparse Bagging (SCSB), a mathematically rigorous framework for post-training compression and probability calibration of bootstrap-based bagging ensembles. Standard bagging ensembles (such as Random Forests, Bagged SVMs, and Bagged Neural Networks) assign uniform voting power to all constituent estimators. However, this naive uniform prior ignores the varying local competence of base estimators and contributes to model overconfidence. We formulate ensemble pruning and calibration as a joint optimization problem over the probability simplex by minimizing the Out-Of-Bag (OOB) loss. To induce sparsity, we address the theoretical "L1-simplex paradox" -- the mathematical reality that the L1 norm is constant on the simplex and fails to prune -- by introducing a concave quadratic penalty. SCSB is model-agnostic and achieves up to 96% ensemble compression, yielding linear inference speedups and superior probability calibration (lowered Expected Calibration Error) while preserving or enhancing generalization accuracy.

</details>


### [87] [Zeta: Dual Whitening for Matrix Optimization via Coordinate-Adaptive Preconditioning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14187)
*Kaiwen Chen,Shuhai Zhang,Qiuwu Chen,Zimo Liu,Linxiao Li,Ying Sun,Yuchen Li,Yifan Zhang,Bo Han,Mingkui Tan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Zeta is a dual whitening optimizer that applies coordinate whitening before spectral whitening (Newton-Schulz iteration) to correct intra-matrix scale heterogeneity in momentum matrices, improving orthogonalization reliability and achieving faster convergence across LLMs, MoE, and vision tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing matrix-aware optimizers like Muon rely on Newton-Schulz iteration, which is critically sensitive to input conditioning. The raw momentum matrices they operate on exhibit severe coordinate-wise scale heterogeneity (variance imbalance across rows/columns), undermining the reliability of spectral whitening. This vulnerability is underappreciated and unaddressed in prior work.

Method: The authors first verify scale heterogeneity via a chi-square uniformity test across Transformer layers. They then propose Zeta, a strictly ordered dual whitening pipeline: (1) coordinate whitening normalizes per-row/per-column statistics to establish statistical isotropy, and (2) spectral whitening (Newton-Schulz) then orthogonalizes the now well-conditioned matrix. They mathematically prove this ordering reduces orthogonalization error by improving the input condition number, and the ordering is not a tunable choice but a mathematical necessity.

Result: Zeta matches or surpasses strong baselines on language modeling (0.6B to 8B parameters), mixture-of-experts architectures, and vision tasks, demonstrating faster convergence and better generalization when scale imbalance is resolved prior to orthogonalization.

Conclusion: Coordinate-wise scale heterogeneity in momentum matrices is a real and prevalent issue that degrades matrix-aware optimization. The proposed dual whitening pipeline (coordinate then spectral) provides a principled, mathematically grounded fix that strictly reduces orthogonalization error and yields consistent empirical gains across diverse training settings.

Abstract: Large-scale neural network training increasingly relies on matrix-aware optimizers that exploit the structure of weight parameters beyond element-wise adaptation. However, existing matrix-aware methods such as Muon have an underappreciated vulnerability: their core operation, Newton-Schulz iteration, depends critically on input conditioning, yet the raw momentum matrices exhibit severe coordinate-wise scale heterogeneity. In this paper, we first verify this scale heterogeneity through a chi-square uniformity test, showing that intra-matrix scale imbalance is prevalent across Transformer layers and that coordinate whitening effectively corrects it. Motivated by this finding, we propose Zeta, a dual whitening optimizer that applies coordinate whitening and spectral whitening in a strictly ordered pipeline. The ordering is not a tunable choice but follows from a mathematical dependency: coordinate whitening establishes the statistical isotropy that spectral whitening requires to function reliably. We further prove that this dual pipeline strictly reduces orthogonalization error relative to pure spectral methods by improving the condition number of the input. Empirically, Zeta matches or surpasses strong baselines across language modeling (0.6B to 8B parameters), mixture-of-experts architectures, and vision tasks, demonstrating that resolving scale imbalance before orthogonalization leads to faster convergence and better generalization. Code is available at https://gitcode.com/kevin259/MindSpeed.

</details>


### [88] [DRIVE: Distributional and Retrieval-Augmented Bidding with Value Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14192)
*Miduo Cui,Haochen Wang,Shangqin Mao,Xun Yang,Qianlong Xie,Xingxing Wang,Xuri Ge,Ying Zhou,Zhiwei Xu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: DRIVE is a Transformer-based offline auto-bidding framework that decouples candidate action generation from decision making, using distributional action modeling, retrieval-augmented candidate generation from historical data, and value-based evaluation to select optimal bids, outperforming unimodal parametric methods especially under sparse/long-tail traffic.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing offline RL and Transformer-based bidding policies are unimodal and purely parametric, causing them to collapse multiple effective bidding strategies into suboptimal averaged actions. They also perform unreliably under sparse or long-tail traffic conditions, which are common in real-time advertising.

Method: DRIVE is a unified Transformer-based framework with three components: (1) distributional action modeling to capture the full distribution of viable bids; (2) retrieval-augmented candidate generation that retrieves high-quality historical decisions; and (3) value-based evaluation to select the most promising candidate bid at inference time. This decouples candidate generation from final decision making.

Result: Extensive experiments on AuctionNet and additional offline RL benchmarks show that DRIVE consistently improves bidding performance and generalizes well across multiple Transformer-based methods.

Conclusion: Decoupling candidate generation from decision making via distributional modeling, retrieval augmentation, and value-based evaluation effectively addresses the averaging and reliability issues of prior unimodal approaches, yielding robust improvements in offline auto-bidding.

Abstract: Auto-bidding is a core component of real-time advertising systems, where decisions must optimize long-term performance under budget and cost constraints, while online exploration is prohibitively risky. Offline reinforcement learning and, more recently, Transformer-based sequence modeling have shown promise for learning bidding policies from logged data, but their unimodal and purely parametric formulations often collapse multiple effective bidding strategies into suboptimal averaged actions and perform unreliably under sparse or long-tail traffic. To mitigate these limitations, we propose DRIVE (Distributional and Retrieval-Augmented Bidding with Value Evaluation), a unified Transformer-based framework that decouples candidate action generation from decision making for offline auto-bidding. DRIVE combines distributional action modeling, retrieval-augmented candidate generation from high-quality historical decisions, and value-based evaluation to select the most promising bid at inference time. Extensive experiments on AuctionNet and additional offline reinforcement learning benchmarks demonstrate that DRIVE consistently improves bidding performance and generalizes well across multiple Transformer-based methods.

</details>


### [89] [Structured Noise Adaptation for Sequential Bayesian Filtering with Embedded Latent Transfer Operators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14195)
*Naichang Ke,Pongpisit Thanasutives,Yoshinobu Kawahara*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes an ELTO-based Bayesian filter with structured noise parameterization that combines learning a time-invariant noise model with dynamic adaptation to non-stationary changes, improving state estimation in time-varying environments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing Kalman filters based on Embedded Latent Transfer Operators (ELTO) rely on simplified noise models that cannot dynamically adapt to non-stationary processes, limiting their performance in real-world, time-varying scenarios.

Method: The authors introduce a novel structured parameterization for the filter's noise model within an ELTO-based Bayesian filtering framework. This parameterization enables structured noise adaptation, which couples data-driven learning of an optimal time-invariant noise model with dynamic parameter adaptation to track changes in the underlying dynamics of non-stationary processes.

Result: Empirical results demonstrate that the proposed structured noise adaptation approach yields improved dynamic state estimation performance in noisy, time-varying environments compared to existing methods.

Conclusion: Structured noise adaptation effectively addresses the key limitation of ELTO-based Kalman filters, enabling robust state estimation in non-stationary settings by integrating time-invariant optimal noise learning with responsive dynamic adaptation.

Abstract: Kalman filters based on the Embedded Latent Transfer Operators (ELTO) emerge as novel statistical tools for sequential state estimation. However, a critical limitation stems from their use of simplified noise models, which fail to dynamically adapt to non-stationary processes. To address this limitation, we introduce an ELTO-based Bayesian filtering approach with a new structured parameterization for the filter's noise model. This parameterization enables structured noise adaptation, which couples the data-driven learning of an optimal time-invariant noise model with dynamic parameter adaptation that responds to changes in dynamics within non-stationary processes. Empirical results show that our structured noise adaptation improves the filter's dynamic state estimation performance in noisy, time-varying environments.

</details>


### [90] [LapidaryEngine: Fully Conversational Crystal Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14215)
*Yusei Ito,Yuta Suzuki,Tomoya Murata,Masaki Adachi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LapidaryEngine introduces a conversational generative model for crystal materials that uses a pivot representation to enable bidirectional translation between free-form natural language and crystal structures, overcoming limitations of prior one-directional, formula-constrained approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing text-to-crystal models require rigid input formats (e.g., chemical formulas) and only support one-way generation (text→crystal), preventing iterative, conversational refinement that aligns with users' ambiguous and evolving design goals.

Method: The authors propose a pivot representation—an intermediate embedding space that bridges natural language and crystal structures without requiring direct paired text–crystal datasets. This enables bidirectional translation: text→crystal generation and crystal→text description, supporting iterative dialogue-like refinement and editing.

Result: LapidaryEngine is demonstrated on diverse tasks: insulator discovery, stability optimization, compositional modification, and structural editing, showing effective alignment of generated materials with user intent through interactive, conversational workflows.

Conclusion: A pivot-based bidirectional architecture enables the first fully conversational crystal generation system, allowing free-form natural language input, iterative refinement, and precise structural control—advancing toward intuitive, dialogue-driven materials design.

Abstract: The emergence of Large Language Models (LLMs) has inspired the vision of generating bespoke crystal materials directly from natural-language instructions, enabling users to design materials through intuitive, conversational interaction. Existing text-to-crystal generative models represent important early steps toward this goal, but they suffer from two critical limitations: (i) restricted input formats that require highly structured descriptions (e.g., chemical formulas), and (ii) one-directional generation, where models can map text to crystal but cannot perform the inverse. These limitations prevent fully conversational workflows and hinder alignment with users' inherently ambiguous and evolving desiderata. We address these challenges with LapidaryEngine, the first model to support fully conversational crystal generation. LapidaryEngine accepts free-form natural-language requests and performs iterative refinement and editing in a dialogue-like manner. The key innovation is a pivot representation, a third, intermediate form that enables bidirectional translation between text and crystal structures despite the absence of direct paired datasets. Leveraging this pivot allows robust interpretation of user feedback and precise structural control. We demonstrate LapidaryEngine across diverse tasks, including insulator discovery, stability optimization, compositional modification, and structural editing, showcasing its ability to align generated materials with user intent in an interactive manner.

</details>


### [91] [Curvature-Informed Potential Energy Surface for Protein-Ligand Binding Affinity Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14217)
*Peng-Fei Sun,Chuan-Xian Ren,Hong Yan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CPES proposes a curvature-informed potential energy surface graph neural network that combines static interaction geometry with physics-based curvature descriptors derived from the Hessian of the potential energy surface, using spectral cross-attention to capture binding-induced conformational dynamics for improved protein-ligand binding affinity prediction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing geometric deep learning methods for binding affinity prediction rely solely on static interaction geometry from a single bound conformation, ignoring molecular flexibility and conformational changes induced upon binding, which limits predictive accuracy.

Method: CPES operates in three stages: (1) curvature spectral descriptors are derived from the Hessian matrix of the potential energy surface at equilibrium, with eigenvalues defining local principal curvatures; (2) spectral cross-attention compares unbound ligand/protein states with the bound complex to capture binding-induced dynamic changes, while parallel hierarchical interaction representations are learned via geometry-aware message passing, soft clustering, and bidirectional cross-attention on static structural features; (3) curvature-informed dynamic and static representations are fused for affinity regression.

Result: Extensive evaluations on multiple benchmark datasets demonstrate that CPES achieves improved predictive performance compared to existing methods and provides physical interpretability of the learned representations.

Conclusion: Incorporating physics-informed curvature representations that model conformational flexibility significantly enhances protein-ligand binding affinity prediction, combining dynamic and static structural information yields both superior accuracy and interpretability.

Abstract: Accurate prediction of protein-ligand binding affinity is essential for structure-based drug discovery. Recent geometric deep learning methods have achieved promising performance by representing protein-ligand complexes as three-dimensional graphs. However, most existing approaches mainly rely on static interaction geometry from a single bound conformation, while neglecting molecular flexibility and binding-induced conformational changes. To address this limitation, we propose a curvature-informed potential energy surface (CPES) graph neural network for protein-ligand binding affinity prediction, which incorporates physics-informed curvature representations to model conformational flexibility. CPES first derives curvature spectral descriptors from the Hessian of the potential energy surface evaluated at equilibrium configurations, whose eigenvalues define the local principal curvatures of the potential energy surface. It then uses spectral cross-attention to compare the unbound ligand and protein with the bound complex, thereby capturing binding-induced changes in conformational dynamics. In parallel, hierarchical protein-ligand interaction representations are learned from static structural features through geometry-aware message passing, soft clustering, and bidirectional cross-attention. Finally, CPES fuses the curvature-informed dynamic representations with static interaction representations for affinity regression. Extensive evaluations on multiple benchmark datasets demonstrate that CPES achieves improved predictive performance and offers physical interpretability.

</details>


### [92] [Learning the Context of Errors: Black-Box Online Adaptation of Time Series Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14222)
*Xilin Dai,Yiding Liu,Hongjie Xia,Yifan Hu,Zewei Dong,Jiang-Ming Yang,Qiang Xu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ORCA introduces black-box online adaptation for Time Series Foundation Models by learning to predict residual errors conditioned on the base model's input-output context, demonstrating effectiveness across 5 TSFMs and 8 datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Future TSFMs may be deployed as closed-source API services, creating a paradigm mismatch with existing white-box adaptation methods (parameter fine-tuning, gradient backpropagation). The paper asks: in black-box online adaptation for TSFMs, what should we learn?

Method: ORCA (Online Residual Contextual Adaptation) formulates black-box adaptation as learning to predict the base model's predictive errors, conditioned on both the input and output of the base model (the error context). An adapter model is trained online using only the base model's predictions and ground truth, without requiring access to model parameters or gradients.

Result: Extensive experiments across 5 state-of-the-art TSFMs and 8 datasets validate ORCA's effectiveness. Ablation studies quantitatively analyze how different adapter learning hypotheses impact black-box online adaptation performance.

Conclusion: The paper establishes that in black-box online adaptation for TSFMs, learning residual errors conditioned on the base model's input-output context is an effective strategy, enabling model-agnostic adaptation without white-box access.

Abstract: The rapid evolution of Time Series Foundation Models (TSFMs) has advanced zero-shot forecasting across diverse domains. Inspired by the current form of Large Language Models, future TSFMs may be offered as commercialized, closed-source API services. However, many existing online adaptation methods still rely on white-box access for parameter fine-tuning or gradient backpropagation. This paradigm mismatch raises a question: In black-box online adaptation for TSFMs, what should we learn? We answer this with an insight: the predictive errors of the base model are conditioned on both the input and output of the base model (i.e., the context of errors). To validate this insight, we propose ORCA (Online Residual Contextual Adaptation). We conduct extensive experiments across 5 state-of-the-art TSFMs and 8 datasets to demonstrate the effectiveness of our approach. Furthermore, through ablation studies, we quantitatively analyze the impact of different adapter learning hypotheses on the final adaptation performance in black-box online adaptation. Code available at https://github.com/Fifthky/ORCA.

</details>


### [93] [Implicit Variational Rejection Sampling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14235)
*Jian Xu,Shigui Li,Wei Chen,Jiacheng Li,Zhiqi Lin,Delu Zeng,Xinghao Ding,John Paisley,Qibin Zhao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes Implicit Variational Rejection Sampling (IVRS), combining neural-network-based implicit proposals with rejection sampling and a discriminator network to refine posterior approximations, yielding the tighter IR-ELBO bound and outperforming traditional VI methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional mean-field VI and even neural-network-based implicit VI suffer from limited expressiveness, leading to inaccurate posterior approximations. The authors aim to overcome these limitations by integrating rejection sampling to correct and refine implicit distributions.

Method: IVRS uses neural networks to construct implicit proposal distributions, then applies rejection sampling with a discriminator network that estimates the density ratio between the implicit proposal and the true posterior. The Implicit Resampling Evidence Lower Bound (IR-ELBO) is introduced as a quality metric and a tighter variational lower bound.

Result: Experimental results demonstrate that IVRS outperforms traditional variational inference techniques in posterior approximation quality.

Conclusion: Integrating implicit distributions with rejection sampling via a discriminator-based density ratio estimation provides a more flexible and accurate framework for variational inference, achieving tighter lower bounds and superior empirical performance.

Abstract: Variational Inference (VI) is a fundamental inference technique in Bayesian machine learning for approximating complex posterior distributions. Traditional VI often relies on the mean-field factorization, which can inadequately capture true posterior complexity. Recent advancements have leveraged neural networks to model implicit distributions, offering increased flexibility. However, the practical constraints of neural network architectures still produces inaccuracies. In this paper, we propose a method called Implicit Variational Rejection Sampling (IVRS), which integrates implicit distributions with rejection sampling to improve the posterior approximation. Our method uses neural networks to construct implicit proposal distributions, and rejection sampling with a discriminator network that estimates the density ratio between the implicit proposal and the true posterior for refining the approximation. Towards this end, we introduce the Implicit Resampling Evidence Lower Bound (IR-ELBO) as a metric to characterize the resampled distribution's quality and derive a tighter variational lower bound.
