Experimental results demonstrate that our method outperforms traditional variational inference techniques.

</details>


### [94] [Where Black-box Drug-Target Interaction Prediction Models Look: Cross-Method Explainability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14245)
*Ali Vefghi,Zahed Rahmati,Mohammad Akbari*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: An interpretability audit of the BridgeDPI architecture across three datasets reveals modality dominance, padding artifacts, and chemistry-consistent motifs. By combining multiple gradient-based attribution methods with occlusion and intersection consensus, the study demonstrates that XAI serves as model criticism, generating testable hypotheses for downstream validation in drug discovery pipelines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Despite strong benchmark performance of DTI/DTA predictors, the internal use of sequence, fingerprint, and graph features remains opaque. There is a need to audit these models to understand what representations they actually rely on, beyond aggregate metrics.

Method: An interpretability audit of BridgeDPI using three datasets (Gao, Human, C. elegans). The approach combines five gradient-based attribution methods (integrated gradients, saliency, LRP, SmoothGrad, SmoothGrad-IG) with feature-wise occlusion ablation. Strict intersection consensus across methods is applied to reduce single-explainer bias. Analyses span raw inputs, the bridge similarity scaffold, and graph convolution layers, including edge-level sensitivities and targeted edge removals.

Result: Explainability reveals modality dominance, padding and special-token artifacts, dataset-dependent cooperative vs. suppressive effects across layers, and chemistry-consistent fragment and composition motifs where attribution methods agree. These findings serve as model criticism rather than ground truth.

Conclusion: Applying modern XAI to contemporary DTI/DTA models, while still nascent, provides a valuable first layer of scrutiny. It helps researchers relate predictions to drug- and target-side representations and prioritize external validation, generating testable hypotheses for computational drug discovery pipelines.

Abstract: Drug-target interaction (DTI) and affinity (DTA) predictors increasingly achieve strong benchmark scores, yet their internal use of sequence, fingerprint, and graph features often remains opaque. We present an interpretability audit of BridgeDPI architecture on three different datasets including Gao, Human, and C.elegans. This study combines gradient-based attributions -- integrated gradients, saliency, layer-wise relevance propagation, SmoothGrad, and SmoothGrad-IG -- with feature-wise occlusion ablation and strict intersection consensus across methods to reduce single-explainer bias. We summarize sensitivity and signed effects at raw inputs, at the bridge similarity scaffold, and through the graph convolution, including edge-level sensitivities and targeted edge removals. The results show that explainability is most informative when treated as model criticism: it reveals modality dominance, padding and special-token artifacts, dataset-dependent cooperative versus suppressive effects across layers, and chemistry-consistent fragment and composition motifs where methods agree. These analyses do not substitute for structural or experimental ground truth, yet they can provide testable hypotheses for downstream validation in computational drug discovery pipelines. More broadly, applying modern XAI to contemporary DTI/DTA models is still an early pass over the rich structure implicit in trained weights and data -- yet even this first layer of scrutiny already helps researchers relate predictions to drug- and target-side representations and to prioritize external validation.

</details>


### [95] [Beyond a Single Explanation of the Adam--SGD Gap](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14259)
*Chenxiang Zhang,Rustem Islamov,Enea Monzio Compagnoni,Jun Pang,Aurelien Lucchi,Antonio Orvieto*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The Adam-SGD performance gap arises from complex data-architecture interactions rather than a single factor, with a consistent crossover batch size phenomenon where SGD is better at small batches and Adam dominates at large batches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multiple hypotheses exist for the Adam-SGD performance gap (data distribution, architecture design, optimization properties), but they are studied in isolation, making their relative importance unclear. The authors aim to systematically disentangle these factors through controlled experiments.

Method: The authors conduct a controlled empirical study across diverse domains (vision, language, genomics, graphs), spanning both modern and classical architectures. They systematically vary factors such as vocabulary distribution (uniform vs. heavy-tailed), attention mechanisms (softmax vs. alternatives), and nonlinearities (ReLU vs. GeLU) to isolate their effects. They also develop a theoretical model that predicts a batch-size-dependent crossover in relative optimizer performance.

Result: No single factor consistently explains the gap. Key findings: (1) Adam's advantage persists under uniform vocabulary but nearly vanishes under heavy-tailed distributions; (2) the advantage reverses in favor of SGD for softmax-attention models; (3) architectural choices like GeLU vs. ReLU amplify Adam's advantage. A robust pattern emerges: a crossover batch size exists where the relative advantage shifts from SGD (small batches) to Adam (large batches). The theoretical model captures this crossover.

Conclusion: The Adam-SGD gap is driven by nontrivial interactions between data properties and architecture components, not a single common cause. The crossover batch size phenomenon helps reconcile prior conflicting hypotheses and offers practical guidance for optimizer selection across domains.

Abstract: Prior work has identified several factors that can contribute to the performance gap between Adam and SGD, spanning data aspects, architecture design, and optimization properties. Yet these explanations are often studied in isolation, leaving their relative importance unclear. In this work, we revisit these hypotheses through a controlled empirical study across vision, language, genomics, and graph tasks, spanning modern and classical architectures, and carefully designed training setups. Our results suggest that no single factor consistently explains the Adam--SGD gap. For instance, the Adam advantage can (1) persist under a uniform vocabulary distribution yet nearly disappear under a heavy-tailed one; (2) reverse in favor of SGD in softmax-attention models; and (3) become larger under soft architectural modifications, e.g., when ReLU is replaced by a GeLU nonlinearity. This suggests that the gap arises from nontrivial data and architecture interactions, rather than from a single common factor. Yet, we observe a pattern across our settings: a \emph{crossover batch size} at which the relative advantage shifts from SGD to Adam as the batch size scales. These empirical results are captured by our theoretical gap model, which predicts this batch-size-dependent crossover. Our perspective helps reconcile several existing hypotheses while offering practical insights across domains.

</details>


### [96] [DIFF-ERO: A Conformance-Aware Loss for Deep Learning in Process Mining](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14283)
*Johannes De Smedt,Jari Peeperkorn,Artem Polyvyanyy,Jochen De Weerdt*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: DIFF-ERO is a differentiable, conformance-aware loss function for deep process analytics that incorporates control-flow structural information via batch-level stochastic transition matrices with soft edge memberships, enabling structural precision and recall to directly inform backpropagation. Applied to transformer-based next-activity prediction, it improves predictive performance on structurally meaningful tasks while achieving convergence toward ground-truth process models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard deep learning objectives like cross-entropy optimize local next-step likelihoods and only implicitly capture control-flow structure, allowing models to achieve high token-level accuracy while exhibiting imprecise global behavior. The authors aim to explicitly incorporate process model structure into the training signal.

Method: DIFF-ERO is a differentiable formulation of entropy-based stochastic conformance checking. It constructs batch-level stochastic transition matrices using soft edge memberships, yielding differentiable structural precision and recall signals that inform backpropagation. The loss is model-agnostic and can be applied whenever the final representation parameterizes stochastic transitions. It is instantiated in transformer encoder-decoder pipelines jointly with cross-entropy for next-activity prediction.

Result: Across benchmarks comparing other loss functions and targets, DIFF-ERO shows improved predictive performance where structure matters most while maintaining parity elsewhere. The learned stochastic automaton converges toward the structural ground truth, indicating that the network internalizes process model structure.

Conclusion: DIFF-ERO successfully bridges deep learning and formal process model structure by making stochastic conformance differentiable, enabling neural networks to internalize control-flow constraints during training without sacrificing predictive accuracy on non-structural dimensions.

Abstract: Deep learning has driven many recent advances in process analytics, especially for predictive and prescriptive monitoring. However, standard objectives such as cross-entropy optimize local next-step likelihoods and only implicitly capture control-flow structure. As a result, models can achieve high token-level accuracy while permitting imprecise global behaviour. We introduce DIFF-ERO, a conformance-aware loss function for deep learning models on process data. DIFF-ERO is a differentiable formulation of entropy-based stochastic conformance that incorporates control-flow information during training. Our approach constructs batch-level stochastic transition matrices with soft edge memberships, allowing structural precision and recall signals to directly inform backpropagation. The loss is model-agnostic and can be applied whenever the final representation parametrizes stochastic transitions. We instantiate DIFF-ERO in transformer encoder-decoder pipelines for next-activity prediction and use it jointly with cross-entropy to analyse its theoretical components with respect to convergence. Across benchmarks comparing other loss functions and targets, DIFF-ERO shows improved predictive performance where structure matters most while maintaining parity elsewhere. At the same time, the learned stochastic automaton converges towards the structural ground truth, indicating that the network internalizes process model structure.

</details>


### [97] [Hierarchical ODE: Learning Continuous-Time Physical Prototypes for Early Link Failure Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14284)
*Jiaen Lv,Leran Qi,Shaowei Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes a hierarchical ODE-based clustering network for time series prototype learning that uses Neural ODEs to model continuous latent dynamics, disentangling smooth trends from noise, while adaptively determining the number of prototypes without closed-set assumptions. Validated on early link failure detection with irregularly sampled data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Time series prototype learning suffers from observational ambiguity because discrete architectures cannot separate stochastic noise from continuous dynamics, and rigid closed-set assumptions fail to capture unseen diversity in real-world data.

Method: A hierarchical ordinary differential equation clustering network is proposed. Neural ODEs model latent state evolution as continuous integral curves, enforcing temporal continuity to disentangle smooth feature trends from stochastic noise. An adaptive hierarchical mechanism autonomously determines the appropriate number of prototypes without rigid prior constraints.

Result: The method was validated on an early link failure detection task using irregularly sampled time series. It effectively extracted underlying physical prototypes and enabled robust failure detection.

Conclusion: By combining continuous-time modeling via Neural ODEs with adaptive hierarchical clustering, the approach resolves key limitations of discrete architectures and closed-set assumptions, demonstrating practical efficacy in failure detection scenarios with irregularly sampled data.

Abstract: Time series prototype learning is fundamentally challenged by observational ambiguity. Discrete architectures fail to resolve this, as they lack the capacity to decouple stochastic noise from continuous dynamics. Furthermore, rigid closed-set assumptions fail to capture unseen diversity. To address these limitations, we propose a hierarchical ordinary differential equation clustering network, which utilizes neural ordinary differential equation to model latent state evolution as a continuous integral curve. This formulation enforces temporal continuity to effectively disentangle smooth feature trends from stochastic noise, while our adaptive hierarchical mechanism autonomously determines the appropriate number of prototypes without rigid prior constraints. Validated on the early link failure detection task with irregularly sampled time series, the proposed method effectively extracts underlying physical prototypes, thereby enabling robust failure detection. Our code is available at https://github.com/NJ-LNN/Hierarchical-ODE.

</details>


### [98] [Riemannian Metric Matching for Scalable Geometric Modeling of Distributions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14334)
*Jacob Bamberger,Adam Gosztolai,Pierre Vandergheynst,Michael Bronstein,Iolo Jones*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes Riemannian metric matching, a neural network framework that learns the carré du champ operator to estimate Riemannian geometry from data. By reformulating this operator as a conditional expectation over random perturbations, it enables amortized inference without explicit kernel construction, matching k-NN accuracy while being up to 400× faster and viable for high-dimensional image data where nearest-neighbor methods fail.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: High-dimensional data often concentrate near low-dimensional manifolds, but existing geometry estimation methods (graphs, kernels) scale poorly with dataset size and ambient dimension. Nearest-neighbor-based diffusion geometry estimators become unreliable in high dimensions due to the breakdown of nearest-neighbor meaningfulness. There is a need for scalable, graph-free methods to learn Riemannian geometry for downstream tasks.

Method: The authors propose learning the carré du champ operator — a key object in diffusion geometry — using neural networks. The central insight is that this operator can be expressed as a conditional expectation over random perturbations of the data. This enables (1) sample-wise stochastic training without constructing a full kernel or graph, (2) amortized inference at constant cost per sample, and (3) direct access to the Riemannian geometry toolkit (metric tensor, Laplacian, geodesics, etc.) for downstream tasks.

Result: Metric matching rivals or exceeds the estimation accuracy of k-NN-based diffusion geometry estimators. It achieves up to 400× speedup via amortized inference. Crucially, it enables graph-free geometric analysis on high-dimensional image datasets where nearest-neighbor methods break down.

Conclusion: Riemannian metric matching provides a scalable, accurate, and fast alternative to traditional kernel/graph-based geometry estimators, unlocking Riemannian geometric analysis for high-dimensional domains (e.g., images) where conventional approaches fail.

Abstract: High-dimensional datasets often concentrate near low-dimensional structures, but estimating their geometry from samples typically relies on graphs and kernels that scale poorly with dataset size and dimension. We propose Riemannian metric matching: a denoising probabilistic framework for learning the Riemannian geometry of data using neural networks. Specifically, we learn the carré du champ operator, which, using diffusion geometry, gives us access to the Riemannian geometry toolkit for downstream machine learning and statistical tasks. Our key observation is that the carré du champ operator can be formulated as a conditional expectation over random perturbations of the data, which can be exploited for sample-wise training and constant cost, amortized inference without explicit kernel construction. Empirically, metric matching rivals or improves the accuracy of $k$-NN-based diffusion geometry estimators, while enabling amortized inference that is up to $400\times$ faster, and supports graph-free geometric analysis on high-dimensional images where nearest neighbors break down.

</details>


### [99] [More with LESS -- Local Scene Representations for Tactile Imaging](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14344)
*Zohar Rimon,Elisei Shafer,Tal Tepper,Daniel Kozin,Alon Malka,Roy Holland,Aviv Tamar*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LESS introduces an object-centric, local-encoder-based tactile representation that models touch as a grid of recurrent encoders with local receptive fields, enabling strong generalization from single-inclusion to multi-inclusion imaging, spatial uncertainty estimation, hand-held palpation, and full 3D reconstruction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing self-supervised tactile imaging methods rely on global, unstructured representations and robot-controlled sensing, which limits generalization to complex multi-inclusion objects and prevents practical hand-held use. The authors aim to exploit the inherent local nature of touch to overcome these limitations.

Method: LESS models the tactile scene as a grid of recurrent encoders, each with a local receptive field. These local encoder states are fused to reconstruct 2D or 3D images of internal object structure. Hand-held operation is enabled via external pose tracking and human-like palpation data collection. The local structure also facilitates spatial uncertainty estimation.

Result: Models trained solely on single-inclusion phantoms successfully image objects with multiple inclusions and varying sizes. The method supports hand-held tactile imaging and full 3D reconstruction of internal structure.

Conclusion: The compositional, object-centric design of LESS enables superior generalization in tactile imaging compared to global representation methods. Local receptive fields unlock practical capabilities including hand-held use, uncertainty estimation, and 3D reconstruction, advancing tactile imaging toward real-world medical and robotic applications.

Abstract: Tactile imaging seeks to reconstruct the internal structure of soft objects through touch sensing, with applications in medical diagnosis and robotic manipulation. Recent self-supervised learning approaches have shown promising results, but rely on global, unstructured representations and robot-controlled sensing, limiting generalization and practical use. We propose Local Encoder for Spatial Sensing (LESS), an object-centric tactile representation that exploits the local nature of touch. The tactile scene is modeled as a grid of recurrent encoders with local receptive fields, whose states are fused to reconstruct 2D or 3D images of internal structure. This compositional design enables strong generalization: models trained on single-inclusion phantoms accurately image objects with multiple inclusions and varying sizes. The local structure further supports spatial uncertainty estimation. In addition, we enable hand-held tactile imaging via external pose tracking and human-like palpation data, and extend tactile imaging to full 3D reconstruction.

</details>


### [100] [Squeeze-Release: Iterative Pruning with Exact Structural Minimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14346)
*Roman Denkin,Ida Akerholm,Prashant Singh,Ida-Maria Sintorn*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Squeeze-Release cycle iteratively prunes and structurally rewrites masked networks into smaller dense ones via "minimization," achieving up to 39× compression on FC networks and 14.8× on ConvNeXt-Tiny with comparable accuracy, and introduces CompensatedLayerNorm for channel reduction across residual streams.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard unstructured pruning reduces the number of non-zero weights but retains original tensor shapes, yielding no actual reduction in deployed model size. The authors aim to convert sparse masked networks into genuinely smaller dense networks without altering the forward function, and to recover capacity otherwise wasted by exact-zero positions.

Method: The Squeeze-Release cycle: (1) pruning produces a sparse mask; (2) "minimization" structurally rewrites the masked network into a smaller dense network with identical forward function (up to floating-point rounding); (3) a "release" step re-enables exact-zero positions as small calibrated noise to regain trainable capacity; (4) iteration discovers structural redundancy unreachable in one pass. CompensatedLayerNorm extends minimization to channel reduction across LayerNorm-equipped residual streams.

Result: The method achieves 39× compression on a fully-connected model and 14.8× compression on ConvNeXt-Tiny, at comparable accuracy to the unpruned baseline. The rewrite is also proven extensible to transformer architectures.

Conclusion: Squeeze-Release demonstrates that iterative pruning-minimization-release cycles can substantially reduce deployed model size while preserving accuracy, and the minimization rewrite generalizes to modern architectures including transformers.

Abstract: Unstructured pruning produces sparse weight tensors, but the standard implementation keeps tensor shapes unchanged so the deployed model is no smaller than before pruning. We present an exact structural rewrite, which we call minimization, that converts a masked network into a smaller dense network with the same forward function up to floating-point rounding. The Squeeze-Release cycle iterates pruning and minimization with an intermediate release step that re-enables the exact-zero positions inside the compacted tensors as small calibrated noise, turning otherwise wasted capacity back into trainable parameters. Successive cycles use that capacity to find structural redundancy a single pass cannot reach. We additionally introduce CompensatedLayerNorm, a function-preserving replacement for LayerNorm that extends minimization to channel reduction across LayerNorm-equipped residual streams. Squeeze-Release compresses the deployable network to 39x smaller than the unpruned model on a fully-connected model network and 14.8x smaller on modern CNN (ConvNeXt-Tiny), at comparable accuracy. In addition we prove that the rewrite can be extended to transformer architectures.

</details>


### [101] [When Language Representations Interact: Separability and Cross-Lingual Effects in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14347)
*Boris Marinov,Angira Sharma,Christian Schroeder de Witt,Philip Torr,Anisoara Calinescu,Jialin Yu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper applies causal-geometric analysis to multilingual LLMs across 28 bilingual contrasts in three models, finding that language concepts form stable, approximately separable linear representations under a covariance-adjusted inner product, with residual structure reflecting linguistic similarity and hierarchical (simplex-like) organization within language families.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While causal-geometric interpretability has explained how concepts are encoded as linear separable directions in monolingual models, it remains underexplored whether this framework extends to multilingual LLMs where language identity is inherently correlated and hierarchically structured. Understanding these representations is critical for ensuring reliable, trustworthy behavior in multilingual systems.

Method: The authors apply causal-geometric analysis to multilingual LLMs, studying 28 bilingual contrasts across three different models. They examine whether language representations are linearly separable under a covariance-adjusted (causal) inner product and analyze residual structure to identify dependencies reflecting linguistic similarity and hierarchical clustering.

Result: Language concepts admit stable linear representations that are largely separable under the causal inner product. Structured deviations from perfect separability correlate with linguistic similarity. Languages within the same family (e.g., Germanic, Romance) exhibit a simplex-like geometric structure, indicating hierarchical organization of language representations.

Conclusion: The findings extend causal-geometric interpretability to multilingual settings, demonstrating that separability and structured similarity coexist in multilingual LLM representations. This has implications for trustworthy deployment, as residual inter-language structure may cause unintended cross-lingual effects during model monitoring or intervention.

Abstract: Large language models exhibit strong multilingual capabilities, however, their internal representations are difficult to interpret. Understanding these interactions is important for ensuring reliable behavior in multilingual systems. Recent work has shown that causal-geometric structure can explain how certain concepts are encoded as approximately linear and separable directions, but whether this framework extends to multilingual models, where language identity is correlated and hierarchical, is underexplored. We apply causal-geometric analysis to multilingual LLMs, studying 28 bilingual contrasts across three models, allowing us to analyze when languages behave as approximately independent factors and when structured dependencies persist. We find evidence that language concepts admit stable linear representations that are largely separable under a covariance-adjusted (causal) inner product, with structured deviations reflecting linguistic similarity. Moreover, languages within the same family (such as Germanic or Romance) exhibit a simplex-like geometric structure, suggesting hierarchical organization. These results extend causal-geometric interpretability to multilingual settings and provide insight into how separability and similarity may exist in multilingual LLM representations, motivating interpretability analyses that diagnose when and how structured dependencies between concepts can be anticipated. This has implications for trustworthy deployment, as residual structure between languages may lead to unintended cross-lingual effects when models are monitored or intervened upon.

</details>


### [102] [Can Deep Neural Networks Improve Compression of Very Large Scientific Data?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14353)
*Muhannad Alhumaidi,Guozhong Li,Spiros Skiadopoulos,Panos Kalnis*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper investigates whether modern ML predictors (foundation weather models) can improve error-bounded lossy compression for scientific data. Surprisingly, despite achieving up to 91% better reconstruction quality and 9.6× higher compression ratios on individual variables, ML predictors fail to improve overall dataset-level compression ratios due to unfavorable residual spatial structure affecting entropy coding efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: State-of-the-art error-bounded lossy compressors rely on the prediction-residual paradigm, where better predictors yield smaller, more compressible residuals. With the emergence of highly accurate pretrained ML models (especially in climate/weather domains), a natural question arises: can these ML models serve as superior predictors for scientific data compression? Directly investigating this is resource-intensive, so the authors exploit the climate domain where pretrained foundation models already exist as an ideal testbed.

Method: The authors propose a framework that integrates spatial and temporal deep learning models into a conventional error-bounded compression pipeline. It supports auto-regressive forecasting models while avoiding error accumulation. Using ~1.7 TB of ERA5 climate data, they evaluate three ML predictors — CRA5 (VAEformer-based codec), GraphCast (graph neural network), and Aurora (vision transformer) — against the SZ3.1 compressor. All methods share identical quantization and entropy-coding backends for fair comparison.

Result: ML predictors produce more accurate predictions than traditional methods. For highly predictable variables, reconstruction quality improves by up to 91% and compression ratios increase up to 9.6×. However, at the overall dataset level, ML predictors do not improve the compression ratio. The key finding is that prediction accuracy alone is insufficient: the spatial structure of residuals critically determines entropy coding efficiency, and ML-generated residuals exhibit less favorable spatial patterns for compression.

Conclusion: ML-based predictors can dramatically improve per-variable compression but fail at dataset-level gains due to unfavorable residual spatial structure. This reveals that prediction accuracy must be complemented by residual structure awareness for effective entropy coding, highlighting a new research direction for designing compression-oriented ML predictors.

Abstract: Error-bounded lossy compression is a fundamental technique for managing the rapidly growing volumes of scientific data produced by modern simulations and observational instruments. Most state-of-the-art-compressors follow a prediction-residual paradigm, where compression effectiveness depends on the quality of the predictor: more accurate predictions generate smaller residuals that are easier to compress. This observation raises a question: can modern machine learning models serve as superior predictors for scientific data compression? Answering this question directly is challenging because developing compression-specific ML predictors requires substantial resources. Instead, we leverage the climate domain where highly accurate pretrained weather forecasting foundation models already exist, making them an ideal testbed. We present a framework that integrates spatial and temporal deep learning models into a conventional error-bounded compression pipeline. The framework supports auto-regressive forecasting models and avoids error accumulation. Using ERA5 climate data as a representative large-scale scientific dataset, we evaluate three distinct ML predictors: a VAEformer-based codec (CRA5), a graph neural network forecaster (GraphCast), and a vision-transformer forecaster (Aurora), against the state-of-the-art compressor SZ3.1 under identical quantization and entropy-coding backends. Our evaluation over approximately 1.7 TB of data reveals a surprising result: although ML predictors generate more accurate predictions and can improve reconstruction quality by up to 91% while achieving up to 9.6x higher compression ratios for highly predictable variables, they do not improve overall dataset-level compression ratio. We show that prediction accuracy alone is insufficient: the spatial structure of the resulting residuals plays a decisive role in entropy coding efficiency.

</details>


### [103] [Be My Tutor: On-Policy Co-Distillation for Mutual LLM Improvement via Peer Feedback](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14368)
*Woohyeon Byeon,Jiwon Jeon,Jeonghye Kim,Youngchul Sung*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes On-Policy Co-Distillation (OPCoD), a multi-domain LLM training method where two specialized models co-evolve through mutual tutoring with cognizance-based gating and feedback anchoring, achieving Pareto improvement across domains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard approaches like one-way distillation or single-model fine-tuning fail to achieve mutual improvement across domains without catastrophic forgetting. The goal is mutual Pareto improvement: each model improves in its partner's domain while retaining its own specialized strength.

Method: OPCoD employs two key mechanisms: (1) each student performs self-distillation conditioned on its own correct rollouts and peer feedback; (2) cognizance-based gating determines when peer feedback should be provided; (3) feedback anchoring grounds the feedback in the original problem to improve relevance and effectiveness of the cross-model teaching signal.

Result: On Science Q&A tasks, OPCoD consistently outperforms baselines and achieves Pareto improvement across all evaluated domain pairs and student models, demonstrating mutual gains without sacrificing original domain strengths.

Conclusion: OPCoD enables effective multi-domain co-evolution of LLMs through structured on-policy peer tutoring, with cognizance gating and feedback anchoring proving critical to successful knowledge transfer and Pareto improvement.

Abstract: We study multi-domain LLM training in which two models, each stronger in a different domain, co-evolve by tutoring each other through on-policy feedback. Unlike one-way distillation or single-model fine-tuning, our goal is mutual Pareto improvement: each model improves across domains without losing its original strength. To this end, we propose On-Policy Co-Distillation (OPCoD), where each student's self-distillation is conditioned on its own correct rollout and feedback from its peer. To make feedback exchange effective, OPCoD uses cognizance-based gating to decide when to give feedback and feedback anchoring to ground feedback in the problem. On Science Q\&A tasks, OPCoD consistently outperforms baselines and achieves Pareto improvement across all evaluated domain pairs and students.

</details>


### [104] [MUFFLe: Efficient Model Update Compression via Generalized Deduplication for Federated Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14354)
*Xiaobo Zhao,Daniel E. Lucani*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: MUFFLe is a communication-efficient federated learning scheme that applies generalized deduplication (GD) to compress model updates, achieving significantly lower uplink communication than quantization, sparsification, and uncompressed FedAvg.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Federated learning in edge environments is bottlenecked by the high uplink cost of transmitting model updates. Existing compression methods (quantization, sparsification) reduce this cost, but there is room for further improvement by exploiting redundancy patterns within update vectors.

Method: MUFFLe integrates generalized deduplication (GD) into the FedAvg pipeline. It deduplicates repeated patterns across the update vector, producing a fixed-rate, variable-count compression scheme that transmits only unique patterns and their references.

Result: On IID MNIST with 20 clients, MUFFLe reaches 92.93% target accuracy using only 38 MB cumulative uplink communication, versus 75 MB for 8-bit quantization, 86 MB for Top-k sparsification, and 310 MB for uncompressed FedAvg.

Conclusion: Generalized deduplication is a feasible and effective approach for communication-efficient federated learning, offering substantial bandwidth savings over established compression baselines.

Abstract: Federated learning is well suited to edge environments but is often limited by the uplink cost of transmitting model updates. This Work-in-Progress paper presents MUFFLe, a communication-efficient update compression scheme that integrates generalized deduplication (GD) into the FedAvg pipeline. MUFFLe deduplicates repeated patterns across the update vector, yielding a fixed-rate, variable-count compression scheme. Preliminary experiments on IID MNIST with 20 clients show that MUFFLe reaches the target accuracy of $92.93\%$ with 38~MB cumulative uplink communication, compared with 75~MB for 8-bit quantization, 86~MB for Top-$k$ sparsification, and 310~MB for uncompressed FedAvg. These results demonstrate the feasibility of applying GD to communication-efficient federated learning.

</details>


### [105] [SemPiper: Interactive Code Synthesis for Semantic Operators in Machine Learning Pipelines](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14361)
*Olga Ovcharenko,Luciano Duarte,Sebastian Schelter*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SemPipes introduces a declarative programming model that integrates LLM-powered semantic operators into ML pipelines, enabling developers to use natural language for data-centric tasks while maintaining control, optimizability, and seamless interoperability with standard Python data science code.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: ML pipeline development is tedious and error-prone due to extensive data preparation and heterogeneous source integration. Existing LLM chat-based assistants offer limited control over pipeline behavior and generate code that is difficult to optimize or deploy in production.

Method: SemPipes proposes a programming model with declarative, LLM-powered semantic data operators specified via natural language. These operators are synthesized into specialized implementations at pipeline training time, conditioned on dataset characteristics and pipeline context. The system also employs an evolutionary search procedure for iterative optimization and provides SemPiper, an interactive visualization interface for computational graphs and optimization trajectories.

Result: The authors demonstrate SemPipes through SemPiper, showcasing three end-to-end scenarios where attendees can modify pipelines, inspect generated code, and observe the synthesis and iterative optimization of semantic operators.

Conclusion: Declarative semantic operators provide a practical, controllable, and optimizable approach to integrating LLMs into ML pipeline development, bridging the gap between high-level natural language intent and production-ready code.

Abstract: Machine learning (ML) pipelines require extensive data preparation, feature engineering, and integration across heterogeneous sources, making them tedious and error-prone to develop. While large language models (LLMs) have recently shown promise for assisting programming tasks, chat-based interfaces provide limited control over pipeline behavior and often produce code that is difficult to optimize or integrate into production systems. We demonstrate SemPipes, a novel programming model that extends ML pipelines with declarative, LLM-powered semantic data operators. SemPipes allows developers to specify high-level natural language instructions for data-centric operations, while seamlessly combining these operators with arbitrary Python code from standard data science libraries. For the semantic operators, it synthesizes specialized implementations at pipeline training time, conditioned on dataset characteristics and pipeline context, enabling the flexible yet controlled integration of LLM capabilities. We demonstrate SemPipes through SemPiper, an interactive interface that visualizes computational graphs of the pipelines, synthesized operator implementations, and optimization trajectories produced by an evolutionary search procedure. Attendees can explore three end-to-end scenarios, modify pipelines, inspect generated code, and observe how semantic operators are synthesized and iteratively optimized. The demonstration highlights how declarative semantic operators enable controllable, optimizable, and practical integration of LLMs into ML pipeline development.

</details>


### [106] [Discovery under Hypothesis Redundancy: A Geometric Theory of Discovery Bottlenecks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14386)
*Li Xia,Baoxun Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proposes the Search Compression Hypothesis, formalizing when hybrid discovery systems (LLM-generated proposals + local search) outperform purely local search. Hybrid advantage requires three simultaneous geometric conditions: spectral compression, orthogonal escape from the explored span, and residual signal alignment with the target. When these fail—particularly as the hypothesis space approaches full rank—LLM-driven exploration provides no benefit over random novelty search.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Scientific discovery saturates despite large nominal hypothesis spaces because new hypotheses stop providing independent information. The authors aim to understand when LLM-generated non-local proposals actually improve discovery beyond what structured local search achieves alone, moving beyond treating LLM-guided discovery as generic novelty search.

Method: The authors formalize three necessary geometric conditions for hybrid advantage: spectral compression, orthogonal escape, and residual signal alignment. They derive theoretical necessary conditions and validate through controlled synthetic experiments (signal-planting, directed-vs-random jumps), large-scale A-share factor discovery, LLM-SRBench symbolic regression tasks, and a public tabular operational sanity check for budget allocation.

Result: Random orthogonal jumps expand coverage but fail to improve yield without predictive alignment. Hybrid gains concentrate in weakly represented but target-bearing directions and vanish as the hypothesis space approaches full rank. Novelty alone is insufficient; the three geometric conditions must co-occur for LLM-driven proposals to provide advantage.

Conclusion: The framework reframes LLM-guided discovery as a diagnostic procedure: one should test for spectral compression, orthogonal escape, and residual alignment before allocating budget to non-local exploration. When the hypothesis space nears full rank, directed non-local exploration is unwarranted and resources should shift to local search.

Abstract: Scientific discovery saturates when new hypotheses cease to provide independent information, even if the nominal hypothesis space remains large. We study hybrid discovery systems that combine structured local search with LLM-generated non-local proposals and pose the Search Compression Hypothesis: non-local exploration helps only when three geometric conditions co-occur: spectral compression, orthogonal escape from the explored span, and residual signal alignment with the target. We formalize these conditions, derive necessary conditions for hybrid advantage, and test the mechanism in controlled synthetic environments, large-scale A-share factor discovery, and symbolic-regression benchmarks; a public tabular operational sanity check tests the associated budget-allocation implication. Signal-planting and directed-versus-random experiments show that novelty alone is insufficient: random orthogonal jumps expand coverage but do not improve yield without predictive alignment. Across compression sweeps, real factor archives, and LLM-SRBench tasks, hybrid gains concentrate in weakly represented but target-bearing directions and vanish as the hypothesis space approaches full rank. The framework turns LLM-guided discovery from generic novelty search into a diagnostic procedure for deciding when directed non-local exploration is warranted.

</details>


### [107] [A Low-Rank Subspace Analysis of LLM Interventions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14388)
*Angira Sharma,Christian Schroeder de Witt,Philip Torr,Anisoara Calinescu,Jialin Yu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LLM behaviors like refusal and sycophancy share internal representations in activation space. Interventions targeting one behavior produce asymmetric side effects on others, with "upstream" behaviors propagating broadly. These effects correlate with geometric properties: subspace overlap (principal angles) and proximity to the decision boundary. Reliable targeted control is thus fundamentally challenging.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current safety interventions on LLMs (e.g., reducing sycophancy, enforcing refusal) lack precision — modifying one behavior often unintentionally alters others. This side-effect problem undermines reliable safety control. The authors aim to systematically diagnose and explain why and how these behavioral interactions occur.

Method: The authors introduce a diagnostic framework that: (1) models LLM behaviors as low-rank subspaces in activation space; (2) measures how interventions on one behavior subspace affect other behaviors; (3) quantifies two geometric properties — subspace overlap via average squared cosine of principal angles, and the angle between each behavior subspace and the "decision subspace" (which captures the model's final output choice, e.g., refuse vs. comply). Experiments span instruction-tuned models from 7B to 70B parameters across refusal, jailbreak, and sycophancy settings.

Result: Different behaviors share internal representations. Interventions produce asymmetric propagation: some behaviors act as upstream control points (interventions spread broadly), while others remain isolated. Larger intervention side effects correlate with (i) higher subspace overlap between behavior pairs, and (ii) source behavior subspaces lying closer (smaller angle) to the decision subspace.

Conclusion: Targeted behavior control in LLMs is fundamentally difficult because behaviors are not independently modifiable — they share representations and exhibit asymmetric interaction dynamics governed by the geometry of activation subspaces.

Abstract: Interventions designed to modify a particular behavior in LLMs, such as refusal or sycophancy, often produce unintended changes in other behaviors. This lack of targeted control makes it difficult to design and implement reliable safety controls. To understand these side-effects, we introduce a diagnostic framework for analyzing interacting behaviors in LLMs. We model behaviors as low-rank subspaces in activation space, and study how interventions influence across behaviors. Across multiple instruction-tuned models (7B-70B) and across refusal, jailbreak, and sycophancy settings, we find that different behaviors share internal representations, and intervening on one behavior alters others in asymmetric ways. Some behaviors act as upstream control points whose interventions propagate broadly across other behaviors, while others remain more isolated. We relate these effects to two geometric quantities: (i) the overlap between behavior subspaces, measured as the average squared cosine of principal angles, and (ii) the angle between each behavior subspace and the decision subspace (capturing the model's final decision e.g., refuse vs. comply). Empirically, intervention effects on other behaviors tend to be larger for behavior pairs with higher subspace overlap, and for source behaviors whose subspaces lie closer (smaller angle) to the decision subspace. These findings highlight a challenge for targeted behavior control: behaviors are difficult to modify independently, as interventions can propagate through shared representations and asymmetric interactions.

</details>


### [108] [Flood and Harvest: The Provable Necessity of Trivia for Generating Valuable Mathematics via the Lens of Language Generation in the Limit](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14688)
*Xiaoyu Li,Andi Han,Dai Shi,Zheng Gao,Jiaojiao Jiang,Junbin Gao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper formalizes AI-generated mathematics as nested language generation in the limit, where a verifiable formal language F contains an unknown valuable sublanguage H, with access only through a core C ⊆ H of density α. The authors prove four main results: (1) the verifier does not provide taste—generation breadth is characterized by Angluin's condition; (2) the verifier enables sound coverage, relocating errors from false statements to trivial ones; (3) a sharp dichotomy exists where finite trivia yield optimal coverage α/2, while any infinite trivia allowance (even at vanishing rate) jumps the optimum to 1−α/2; (4) both regimes instantiate in a compression model of mathematics. The central insight: an unbounded stream of correct-but-worthless (trivial) statements is provably necessary to cover unrecorded valuable mathematics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The gap between what a proof checker can verify and what a mathematician would value has become the binding constraint in AI-generated formal mathematics. The paper aims to formally model this gap and determine whether a perfect verifier can substitute for taste in discriminating valuable mathematical statements from trivial ones.

Method: The authors model the problem as nested language generation in the limit: a verifiable formal language F (accessed via membership oracle/proof checker) contains an unknown valuable language H, revealed only through an adversarial enumeration of a core C ⊆ H with exact density α. Every output is either valuable (∈H), trivial (∈F\H), or a hallucination (∉F). The framework uses learning-theoretic analysis with Angluin's condition, density arguments, and compression models to prove tight bounds on achievable coverage.

Result: Four key results: (1) Verifier-free characterization holds—collections admitting generation with breadth are exactly those satisfying Angluin's condition fiber-wise. (2) Sound coverage is possible with the verifier but impossible without it; errors are relocated from false to trivial. (3) A sharp dichotomy: finite trivia → optimal coverage α/2; infinite trivia (any amount) → optimal coverage 1−α/2, with both bounds tight. The transition depends on trivia count, not rate; the gap 1−α represents unrecorded mass. (4) Both regimes instantiate in a compression model of mathematics, showing certified trivia are provably necessary.

Conclusion: A perfect verifier cannot substitute for taste. The unbounded stream of correct-but-worthless statements is not an engineering accident but a provable necessity: covering unrecorded valuable mathematics requires an infinite, asymptotically negligible, stream of certified trivia. The gap between verification and valuation is fundamental and inescapable.

Abstract: AI systems coupled to proof assistants now generate formal mathematics at scale, and the gap between what a checker can verify and what a mathematician would value has become the binding constraint. We model the generation of valuable mathematics as nested language generation in the limit: a verifiable formal language $F$, accessed through a membership oracle (the proof checker), contains an unknown valuable language $H \in \mathcal{H}$ revealed only through an adversarial enumeration of a core $C \subseteq H$ of exact density $α$ (the literature). Every output is valuable ($\in H$), trivial ($\in F \setminus H$), or a hallucination ($\notin F$). We settle four questions. First, the verifier is not taste: the collections admitting generation with breadth are exactly those of the oracle-free model, characterized fiber-wise by Angluin's condition. Second, the verifier does buy sound coverage, covering all unseen valuable statements while asserting only valid ones: possible with it, impossible without it; it relocates unavoidable errors from false to trivial. Third, and centrally, a sharp dichotomy on the tight family: generators emitting finitely many trivia achieve optimal coverage $α/2$, while any infinite trivia allowance, even at vanishing rate, jumps the optimum to $1-α/2$ (both tight, for cores presented as the candidate intersection), and one generator attains both ends. The transition is in trivia count, not rate; the gap $1-α$ is the unrecorded mass. Fourth, both regimes instantiate in a compression model of mathematics. A perfect verifier cannot substitute for taste: the unbounded stream of correct-but-worthless statements is not an engineering accident but a provable necessity, since covering unrecorded valuable mathematics requires an infinite, but asymptotically negligible, stream of certified trivia.

</details>


### [109] [Running the Gauntlet: Re-evaluating the Capabilities of Agents Beyond Familiar Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14397)
*Mykola Vysotskyi,Runqi Lin,Grzegorz Biziel,Michal Zakrzewski,Sebastian Montagna,Damian Rynczak,Shreyansh Padarha,Kumail Alhamoud,Zihao Fu,William Lugoloobi,Kai Rawal,Hanna Yershova,Xander Davies,Taras Rumezhak,Guohao Li,Fazl Barez,Baoyuan Wu,Arkadiusz Drohomirecki,Yarin Gal,Chris Russell,Christopher Summerfield,Adam Mahdi,Volodymyr Karpiv,Philip Torr,Adel Bibi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: GauntletBench is a web-based benchmark with 100 vision-intensive tasks across 5 professional applications, targeting underexplored capabilities (temporal perception, graphical understanding, 3D reasoning). The best frontier agent achieves only 19.1% success vs. >80% for non-expert humans, revealing a large capability gap in challenging real-world scenarios.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current agent benchmarks suffer from saturation — they focus on simple tasks, narrow capabilities, and popular applications, failing to probe agent limitations in realistic, complex settings. There is a need for a benchmark that tests generalization on underexplored dimensions.

Method: GauntletBench provides a modular pipeline: (1) an environment compatible with both open- and closed-source agent frameworks, (2) controlled web-based applications across 5 professional domains (Video Editor, Workflow Builder, 3D Modeller, Flight Analyser, Circuit Designer), (3) 100 vision-intensive tasks targeting temporal perception, graphical understanding, and 3D reasoning, and (4) an automated evaluation engine with diverse metrics.

Result: The state-of-the-art frontier agent achieves only a 19.1% success rate on GauntletBench. In contrast, non-expert human annotators surpass 80% success on the same tasks, demonstrating a substantial performance gap between current agentic systems and human-level capability.

Conclusion: Frontier agentic systems remain far from human-level performance on challenging, vision-intensive professional tasks. The overlooked capabilities of temporal perception, graphical understanding, and 3D reasoning represent significant limitations that current benchmarks fail to capture, underscoring the need for more demanding evaluation frameworks like GauntletBench.

Abstract: As agentic systems continue to evolve and are widely deployed in real-world scenarios, there is a growing demand to faithfully evaluate their capabilities. However, current benchmarks are typically built on popular applications with relatively simple tasks and focus on a narrow set of capabilities while overlooking broader dimensions, resulting in saturated performance on modern agents and failing to probe their limitations. To this end, we introduce GauntletBench, a web-based benchmark for evaluating agent generalisation in challenging scenarios, focusing on three underexplored capabilities (temporal perception, graphical understanding, and 3D reasoning), across five less-covered professional applications (Video Editor, Workflow Builder, 3D Modeller, Flight Analyser, and Circuit Designer), each with 20 vision-intensive tasks (100 in total). Our benchmark provides a modular pipeline that comprises an environment compatible with both open- and closed-source agent frameworks, a controlled web-based application, a well-structured task suite, and an automated evaluation engine with diverse metrics. Contrary to widespread expectations, our empirical results reveal that frontier agentic systems remain far from achieving human-level performance. Even the state-of-the-art agent achieves only a 19.1% success rate on our GauntletBench, highlighting the limitations in these overlooked capabilities and generalisation. By comparison, non-expert human annotators achieve over 80% success on our challenging yet feasible tasks, revealing the substantial gap between current agent capabilities and those required for complex real-world scenarios.

</details>


### [110] [Persona-Pruner: Sculpting Lightweight Models for Role-Playing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14695)
*Jinsu Kim,Jihoon Tack,Noah Lee,Jongheon Jeong*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Persona-Pruner selectively prunes language models to create lightweight, persona-specific role-playing chatbots by isolating only the sub-networks essential to a given character, dramatically reducing computational cost while preserving role-playing performance far better than general pruning methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deploying full-scale LMs for every character in multi-NPC ecosystems is computationally prohibitive. The authors hypothesize that a single persona requires only a fraction of a model's capacity, but naive pruning degrades role-playing performance because it fails to distinguish between redundant general knowledge and essential persona-specific traits.

Method: Persona-Pruner: a framework that identifies and extracts persona-specific sub-networks from a single character description, sculpting a lightweight role-playing model. The method isolates weights critical to embodying a given persona while discarding irrelevant capacity.

Result: Persona-Pruner reduced the performance drop from the dense model by up to 93.8% over the strongest baseline on RoleBench (LLM-as-a-judge metric), substantially outperforming state-of-the-art LLM pruning techniques. It also preserves general LLM capabilities.

Conclusion: Dedicating full generalist models to single personas is unnecessary; persona-specific sub-network isolation enables efficient, high-fidelity role-playing with dramatically lower computational cost.

Abstract: Language Models (LMs) have shown remarkable potential as role-playing chatbots, delivering consistent, stylized interactions when given a specification of a character or user persona. However, applying these capabilities to real-world applications (e.g., ecosystems with numerous NPCs interacting simultaneously) exposes a critical inefficiency due to the excessive computational cost. In this paper, we question the necessity of dedicating a full, generalist model to a single persona, hypothesizing that a specific character identity relies on only a fraction of the model's total capacity. We observe that naively pruning LMs often severely degrades the role-playing performance for a specific persona; it does not distinguish between redundant knowledge and essential character traits. We propose Persona-Pruner, a framework that sculpts a lightweight role-playing model by isolating persona-specific sub-networks from a single description. Our experiments consistently show that Persona-Pruner preserves role-playing performance substantially more effectively than existing state-of-the-art LLM pruning techniques, reducing the performance drop from the dense model by up to 93.8% over the strongest baseline on RoleBench in LLM-as-a-judge score, while still maintaining general LLM capabilities. Code is available at https://github.com/jsu-kim/Persona-Pruner.

</details>


### [111] [A theoretical model for task routing in mixture-of-expert transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14398)
*Yongli Xiang,Vinoth Nandakumar,Yunzhi Yao,Peike Li,Tongliang Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper provides a theoretical framework using discrete language models to explain task-expert specialization in MoE transformers, proving that experts can encode knowledge with sizes dependent only on intrinsic task complexity, supported by experiments on MoE loss functions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing theoretical analyses of task-expert specialization in MoE transformers rely on continuous mixture models unsuitable for natural language. There is a gap in formally explaining this phenomenon using discrete models of language.

Method: The authors represent structured knowledge through syntactic templates and finite key-value dictionaries, then formally prove that a single-layer MoE transformer can encode knowledge via task-specialized experts, where queries are routed to unique experts whose size depends solely on the intrinsic complexity (combined size of syntactic templates and factual dictionary) of the given task.

Result: The construction theoretically supports empirical findings on localized knowledge circuits in MoE models. Additional experiments evaluate model performance under varying MoE loss functions, corroborating the theoretical results.

Conclusion: Task-expert specialization in transformer MoE models can be formally explained using discrete models of language, with expert size bounded by intrinsic task complexity rather than overall model scale.

Abstract: Mixture-of-experts (MoE) layers enable the scaling of transformer models while keeping the inference compute fixed. While task-expert specialization has been observed in empirical studies of frontier MoE transformer models, existing theoretical work analyzes this using continuous mixture models that cannot be used to model natural language effectively. An important open question is to \textit{theoretically explain task-expert specialization in transformer MoE models using discrete models of language}. To address this, we represent structured knowledge via syntactic templates and finite key-value dictionaries, and prove formally that a single-layer MoE transformer can encode knowledge by using experts that specialize in the corresponding tasks. Our construction shows how queries are routed to unique, task-specific experts whose size depends solely on the intrinsic complexity of the given task (i.e. the combined size of its syntactic templates and factual dictionary). Our construction provides a theoretical support for empirical results on localized knowledge circuits in MoE models. We support our theoretical findings with experiments evaluating model performance under varying MoE loss functions.

</details>


### [112] [Federated Learning for Feature Generalization with Convex Constraints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14416)
*Dongwon Kim,Donghee Kim,Sung Kuk Shyn,Kwangsu Kim*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: FedCONST is a federated learning method that adaptively modulates local model update magnitudes based on global parameter strength using linear convex constraints, mitigating overfitting and improving generalization across heterogeneous client data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Federated learning suffers from poor generalization due to heterogeneous client data distributions. Local models overfit their own data, and even transferable features can become distorted during the aggregation phase, creating a misalignment between local and global objectives.

Method: FedCONST introduces two key mechanisms: (1) adaptive modulation of update magnitudes based on the parameter strength of the global model — well-learned parameters receive smaller updates while underdeveloped parameters are reinforced; (2) linear convex constraints applied during aggregation to ensure training stability and preserve locally learned generalization capabilities. The approach is further validated through Gradient Signal to Noise Ratio (GSNR) analysis.

Result: FedCONST achieves state-of-the-art performance across diverse FL environments. GSNR analysis confirms enhanced feature transferability and robustness compared to baseline methods.

Conclusion: By adaptively constraining updates and aligning local-global objectives, FedCONST effectively mitigates overfitting, preserves transferable features, and promotes stronger generalization in heterogeneous federated learning settings.

Abstract: Federated learning (FL) often struggles with generalization due to heterogeneous client data. Local models are prone to overfitting their local data distributions, and even transferable features can be distorted during aggregation. To address these challenges, we propose FedCONST, an approach that adaptively modulates update magnitudes based on the parameter strength of the global model. This prevents over-emphasizing well-learned parameters while reinforcing underdeveloped ones. Specifically, FedCONST employs linear convex constraints to ensure training stability and preserve locally learned generalization capabilities during aggregation. A Gradient Signal to Noise Ratio (GSNR) analysis further validates the effectiveness of FedCONST in enhancing feature transferability and robustness. As a result, FedCONST effectively aligns local and global objectives, mitigating overfitting and promoting stronger generalization across diverse FL environments, achieving state-of-the-art performance.

</details>


### [113] [EM-NeSy: Expectation Maximization for Neurosymbolic Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14463)
*Annegret Seibt,Luc De Raedt,Giuseppe Marra*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: EM-NeSy reformulates probabilistic neurosymbolic learning as an Expectation-Maximization algorithm, decoupling symbolic inference from neural gradient computation. This enables natural extension to approximate reasoning without requiring a differentiable symbolic component, while recovering exact end-to-end gradient-based NeSy as a special case.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: State-of-the-art neurosymbolic models require the symbolic component to be differentiable, which complicates the use of approximate inference. The authors aim to overcome this limitation by reformulating NeSy learning in a way that eliminates the differentiability constraint on the symbolic reasoner.

Method: The authors propose EM-NeSy, which casts probabilistic NeSy learning as an instance of the Expectation-Maximization algorithm. The E-step computes the posterior over neurally predicted symbols conditioned on the label via probabilistic inference. The M-step updates neural parameters based on this posterior using gradient descent solely through the neural component, without backpropagating through the symbolic reasoner.

Result: Experimental results demonstrate that EM-NeSy achieves scalability and computational efficiency. It naturally supports approximate reasoning without additional modifications and recovers the standard end-to-end gradient-based NeSy setting under exact inference.

Conclusion: EM-NeSy unlocks the full potential of the EM algorithm for neurosymbolic learning by removing the differentiability requirement from the symbolic component, enabling seamless integration with approximate reasoning methods.

Abstract: Neurosymbolic (NeSy) models integrate neural networks and symbolic reasoning for robust and interpretable AI. State-of-the-art NeSy models require that the symbolic component is expressed in a differentiable way, often complicating the use of approximate inference. We propose EM-NeSy which casts probabilistic NeSy learning as an instance of the Expectation-Maximization (EM) algorithm. In the expectation step, we compute the posterior over the neurally predicted symbols conditioned on the label via probabilistic inference. In the maximization step, we update the neural parameters based on this posterior using gradient descent only through the neural component. This formulation unlocks the full potential of the EM algorithm for NeSy learning. It allows NeSy to extend naturally to approximate reasoning without any additional modifications or differentiability requirements of the symbolic component. Furthermore, it recovers the standard end-to-end gradient-based NeSy setting under exact inference. Our experimental results demonstrate the scalability and computational efficiency of EM-NeSy.

</details>


### [114] [Recipe-Controlled Decoder Audit for Structural Knowledge-Graph Completion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14492)
*Xihang Shan,Ye Luo*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes Recipe-Controlled Decoder Audit (RCDA), a protocol for evaluating structural transductive KGC that isolates decoder effects by swapping decoders (ComplEx vs DistMult) under identical training recipes. Key findings: decoder differences are modest on standard KGs (+0.005–0.012 MRR), pronounced on small KGs (Kinship: +0.143 MRR), and interact with encoder depth. The authors recommend reporting matched decoder rows, logging small-KG provenance, and sweeping decoder × depth before attributing gains to encoders.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior KGC literature often attributes performance gains to encoder architectures or training recipes without isolating the effect of the decoder (scoring function). The paper aims to fill this gap by providing a systematic, controlled protocol (RCDA) to audit whether observed improvements stem from the decoder, the encoder, or their interaction, thereby preventing misattribution in benchmark comparisons.

Method: RCDA fixes the training recipe (optimizer, negative sampling, regularization, etc.) and swaps only the decoder —primarily ComplEx vs DistMult—while keeping the encoder identical. Targeted spot-checks use RotatE and TransE. The audit spans seven benchmarks (five standard KGs: WN18RR, FB15k-237, YAGO3-10, etc., plus two small KGs: Kinship, UMLS). It further examines decoder × encoder depth interactions (L=0 through L>0) and evaluates CompGCN-style encoder variability across datasets. Multiple seeds and provenance tracking are used for small KGs.

Result: On five standard KGs, ComplEx slightly but consistently outperforms DistMult (+0.005 to +0.012 MRR), while CompGCN encoder effects vary more by dataset. On small KGs, decoder effects dominate: Kinship shows a stable ComplEx advantage of +0.143 MRR (6 seeds); UMLS favours ComplEx by +0.022 MRR in controlled reruns but reverses in an earlier provenance variant, highlighting provenance sensitivity. Decoder choice interacts with encoder depth on WN18RR. Under the RCDA recipe with L=0, ComplEx on YAGO3-10 achieves 0.6971 ± 0.0048 MRR at d=128.

Conclusion: Decoder effects are non-negligible and can dominate on small KGs, but are recipe- and provenance-sensitive rather than fixed. The paper prescribes a three-part audit protocol: (1) always report matched decoder rows in comparisons, (2) log provenance details for small-KG experiments, and (3) sweep decoder × depth configurations before drawing encoder-level conclusions. This promotes more rigorous and reproducible KGC benchmarking.

Abstract: We present a recipe-controlled decoder audit (RCDA) for structural transductive knowledge-graph completion (KGC). The audit asks a simple reporting question: before attributing gains to an encoder or training recipe, what changes when the decoder is swapped under the same recipe? Using ComplEx and DistMult as the primary controlled pair, with targeted RotatE/TransE spot-checks, we evaluate seven benchmarks. On five standard KGs, ComplEx-vs-DistMult differences are modest but consistent under our recipe (+0.005 to +0.012 MRR), whereas CompGCN-style encoder effects vary more by dataset. On small KGs, decoder effects become the main diagnostic: Kinship shows a stable ComplEx advantage of +0.143 MRR (6 seeds), while UMLS favours ComplEx by +0.022 MRR in a clean 6-seed server rerun but reverses in an earlier provenance variant. We therefore treat small-KG decoder choice as recipe- and provenance-sensitive rather than as a fixed dataset winner. We further show that decoder choice interacts with encoder depth on WN18RR, and that under our recipe L=0 ComplEx on YAGO3-10 reaches 0.6971 +/- 0.0048 MRR at d=128. The result is a compact audit protocol: report matched decoder rows, log small-KG provenance, and sweep decoder x depth before making encoder-level claims.

</details>


### [115] [PepALD: Macrocyclic Peptide Generation via Autoregressive Latent Diffusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14510)
*Junming Zhang,Siyu Yi,Wei Ju,Zhonghui Gu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: PepALD is an Autoregressive Latent Diffusion model for de novo macrocyclic peptide design that uses structured chemical embeddings, context-conditioned latent diffusion per residue, R-group-aware ring closure prediction, and winner-protected preference optimization to align with affinity rewards.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Macrocyclic peptide design for intracellular targets requires simultaneous control over non-natural monomer chemistry, ring topology, membrane permeability, and target binding. Existing SMILES/HELM generative models either operate in long atom-level sequences or treat monomers as symbolic tokens with limited chemical grounding, motivating a chemically informed generative approach.

Method: PepALD combines (1) structured chemical embeddings for HELM monomers, (2) context-conditioned latent diffusion to generate each residue autoregressively, (3) R-group-aware ring closure prediction during generation, and (4) winner-protected diffusion-adapted preference optimization (a variant of DPO) to align the denoiser with affinity rewards.

Result: In silico experiments show PepALD outperforms representative peptide generation baselines in both generation quality and reward-optimization performance.

Conclusion: PepALD demonstrates that chemically grounded autoregressive latent diffusion with preference optimization is an effective paradigm for macrocyclic peptide generation, addressing key limitations of prior token-based or atom-level approaches.

Abstract: Macrocyclic peptides are promising therapeutic candidates for intracellular targets, but their design requires simultaneous control over non-natural monomer chemistry, ring topology, membrane permeability, and target binding. Existing SMILES- or HELM-string generative models either operate in long atom-level sequence spaces or treat monomers as symbolic tokens with limited chemical grounding. We introduce PepALD, an Autoregressive Latent Diffusion (ALD) foundation model for \textit{de novo} macrocyclic peptide generation. The model represents HELM monomers with structured chemical embeddings, generates each residue through context-conditioned diffusion in chemically informed latent space, predicts R-group-aware ring closures during autoregressive generation, and aligns the denoiser to affinity rewards using winner-protected diffusion-adapted preference optimization. In silico experiments demonstrate PepALD's generation quality and reward-optimization performance against representative peptide generation baselines.

</details>


### [116] [Behavioral Audit of Machine Unlearning Has a Privacy Cost](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14518)
*Liou Tang,James Joshi,Ashish Kundu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proves that auditing machine unlearning under mutual distrust (dishonest model owner, honest-but-curious auditor) faces a fundamental privacy-audit tradeoff: behavioral query-based audit schemes cannot reliably detect insufficient unlearning without leaking membership information about the retained dataset.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing MU auditing lacks a standardized scheme, and prior work has separately considered dishonest model owners and curious auditors. However, the realistic scenario of mutual distrust—where the owner may falsify evidence and the auditor may infer sensitive data properties—has not been studied. The authors aim to formally characterize the inherent tension between audit reliability and data privacy in this setting.

Method: The authors provide an information-theoretic proof for convex ML models, demonstrating that any generic audit scheme relying solely on behavioral query signals (model outputs) cannot distinguish insufficiently unlearned models from properly unlearned ones without leaking membership information of the retained set. They validate this result empirically on convex models and extend experiments to non-convex models to show the tension persists.

Result: The information-theoretic proof establishes that in the convex setting, a privacy-audit tradeoff is unavoidable under behavioral-only auditing. Empirical results on convex models strongly confirm this tradeoff, and additional experiments on non-convex models demonstrate that the same privacy-audit tension persists beyond the convex case.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that auditing MU under mutual distrust inherently involves a privacy-audit tradeoff. This calls for more careful consideration of realistic auditor threat models and scrutiny of privacy-preserving audit designs for MU pipelines. The authors release their code to facilitate further research.

Abstract: The removal of learned data from Machine Learning models through Machine Unlearning (MU) has been widely studied; however, there has yet to be an agreed-upon scheme for auditing MU. Existing work has shown that a dishonest model owner can falsify evidence to avoid executing MU, while curious auditors (and adversaries) can infer the privacy-sensitive properties of the model and its training data even with limited access. Yet auditing of MU under mutual distrust between the model owner and the auditor remains unexplored. We provide an information-theoretic proof for this scenario: for convex ML models, a generic audit scheme that relies solely on querying the model for \textit{behavioral} signals cannot identify insufficiently unlearned models without revealing membership information of the retained set. Therefore, auditing MU under the assumption of a dishonest model owner and an honest-but-curious auditor faces an inherent privacy-audit tradeoff. Our empirical results on convex models strongly supports this result, while further experiments demonstrate that this privacy-audit tension persists in non-convex models. Our results call for a more careful consideration of the privacy-audit tension under a realistic auditor threat model, and serve as a foundation for more scrutiny of designs of privacy-preserving audit schemes for the MU pipeline. We also release our code implementation at https://github.com/LiouTang/Behavioral-Unlearn-Audit.

</details>


### [117] [Code Correctness Signals in LLM Hidden States: Pre-Generation Probing and Repair Geometry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14530)
*Carlo Di Cicco*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Hidden states of Qwen3-4B-Instruct-2507 linearly encode pre-generation code correctness (AUC 0.931), robust to prompt-length residualization. However, a contrastive direction linked to repair success vanishes after residualizing against repair-context covariates, revealing it as a contextual correlate rather than an isolated repair-comprehension feature.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate whether code correctness is legible from the hidden states of a large language model both before code generation and during self-repair, using a methodologically rigorous confound-control framework (residualization) that can report negative as well as positive results.

Method: Linear probing on hidden states of Qwen3-4B-Instruct-2507 across 444 LiveCodeBench tasks. For pre-generation correctness: logistic regression from prompt-final hidden states with 50 outer splits, using nested cross-validation for layer selection. Prompt-length confound controlled via per-dimension linear residualization, compared against a prompt-length baseline. For repair: contrastive direction analysis on 236 cleaned repair cases, tested via magnitude and split-half tests against label-shuffled nulls, then subjected to conditional residualization against repair-context covariates.

Result: Pre-generation correctness probe achieves leakage-free AUC 0.931 ± 0.008; after residualizing out prompt length, AUC remains 0.911 ± 0.010, substantially above the prompt-length baseline of 0.754 ± 0.014.
