The repair-direction contrastive shift is statistically detectable under naive testing but vanishes after conditional residualization against repair-context covariates, overturning the interpretation of an isolated repair-comprehension feature.

Conclusion: Code correctness is robustly decodable from LLM hidden states before generation, even after stringent confound control. The repair-direction finding, however, is driven by repair-context covariates rather than an independent repair-understanding signal. The methodological contribution — a diagnostic framework using residualization that honestly reports both positive and negative results — is as significant as the empirical findings.

Abstract: Large language models encode rich information in their hidden states. This work asks whether code correctness is legible in the hidden states of Qwen3-4B-Instruct-2507, before it generates and as it repairs a failed attempt, studied on 444 LiveCodeBench tasks. It reports two findings connected by a single confound-control tool: residualization. First, the correctness of the model's first-attempt code is linearly decodable from the prompt-final hidden state, with a leakage-free held-out AUC of 0.931 +/- 0.008 across 50 outer splits. After the linear effect of prompt length is removed from each hidden state dimension, the probe still reaches 0.911 +/- 0.010, well above a prompt-length baseline of 0.754 +/- 0.014. Second, on 236 cleaned cases where the model attempts to repair a failed first attempt, the hidden state shift from the failing attempt to its repair carries a statistically detectable contrastive direction, significant on both a magnitude and a split-half test against label-shuffled nulls. This direction does not survive a conditional residualization against repair-context covariates that differ between successful and failed repairs, marking it as a correlate of repair success driven by the repair context rather than an isolated repair-comprehension feature. The probe layer is selected by nested cross-validation, and the same residualization approach that upholds the pre-generation correctness result overturns the repair-direction interpretation. The contribution is as much methodological as empirical: a diagnostic honest enough to report a negative result alongside a positive one.

</details>


### [118] [The Risk Shadow of Principal Component Analysis: When 99.9999% Variance Preservation Causes Catastrophic Decision Errors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14533)
*Hamidou Tembine*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: PCA's variance maximization objective can completely erase signals about rare, high-impact events ("Risk Shadow"), even while retaining >99.9999% variance. The authors propose Expectile PCA (ExPCA) and Tail-Preserving PCA (TP-PCA), which reweight covariance toward tail events, and prove ExPCA strictly outperforms PCA for rare-event information retention.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard PCA optimizes for variance preservation, which is fundamentally misaligned with detecting rare catastrophic events. In high-stakes applications (fraud detection, disaster prediction), the most critical signals reside in low-variance tail regions that PCA may discard entirely, rendering downstream classifiers no better than constant predictors.

Method: The paper formalizes the "Risk Shadow" phenomenon with a theoretical proof that PCA can erase all rare-event signal while preserving near-total variance. Two remedies are proposed: (1) Expectile PCA (ExPCA), which replaces the standard covariance with an expectile-reweighted covariance to emphasize tail regions; (2) Tail-Preserving PCA (TP-PCA), which similarly reweights toward high-impact events. A theoretical guarantee is provided showing ExPCA strictly dominates PCA in rare-event information retention.

Result: Theoretical results prove ExPCA's strict superiority. Empirical validation on synthetic data and a real-world credit card fraud detection benchmark confirms that ExPCA and TP-PCA successfully preserve tail-event signals that PCA completely obliterates, enabling downstream classifiers to detect rare catastrophic events.

Conclusion: Variance-based dimensionality reduction is fundamentally unsafe for high-stakes decision-making. The Risk Shadow phenomenon necessitates rethinking PCA's role in sensitive applications, and expectile-based reweighting offers a principled alternative that preserves rare-event information.

Abstract: Principal Component Analysis (PCA) preserves variance, not the information needed to detect rare catastrophic events. This paper proves the existence of a {\it Risk Shadow}: PCA can retain over 99.9999 percent of total variance while completely erasing all signal about rare, high-impact failures. When this happens, even the best possible classifier operating on the PCA representation reduces to a constant predictor. The root cause is a fundamental mismatch between variance maximization and tail risk awareness. To break the shadow, we introduce Expectile PCA (ExPCA) and Tail-Preserving PCA (TP-PCA), two methods that reweight the data covariance toward high-impact events. We prove theoretically that ExPCA strictly outperforms PCA in retaining rare-event information, and we validate our claims on synthetic data and a real-world credit card fraud detection benchmark. Our results call for a fundamental rethinking of variance-based dimensionality reduction in high-stakes decisions.

</details>


### [119] [Provably Safe, Yet Scalable Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14536)
*Kai S. Yun,Zeyang Li,Navid Azizan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: PS2-RL is a two-phase framework for provably safe RL that uses a learned backup policy to generate implicit control-invariant sets online, avoiding the scalability issues of explicit invariant-set computation. It is algorithm-agnostic and scales to 10D robotic control tasks where prior safe RL methods fail.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing safe RL faces a dichotomy: soft-constrained methods lack formal safety guarantees, while methods with strict guarantees rely on explicit control-invariant sets that scale poorly with state dimension and are overly conservative. The authors aim to bridge this gap with a scalable yet provably safe approach.

Method: PS2-RL has two phases. Phase 1 trains a backup policy using a novel safe-arrival value function that characterizes the optimal backup policy for invariant-set construction, maximizing the volume of the implicit control-invariant set. Phase 2 trains an RL policy end-to-end with a differentiable projection layer that enforces safety guarantees from the learned backup policy by forward-integrating dynamics to generate implicit control-invariant sets online, avoiding explicit computation.

Result: The framework provides theoretical safety guarantees while remaining scalable. Experiments on robotic control tasks with state dimensions up to 10 demonstrate effectiveness in regimes where prior provably safe RL methods struggle or become impractical.

Conclusion: PS2-RL successfully combines provable safety with scalability by replacing explicit invariant-set synthesis with learned, implicit invariant sets. Its plug-and-play compatibility with any RL algorithm and demonstrated scalability to higher-dimensional tasks represent significant advances over prior safe RL methods.

Abstract: Safe reinforcement learning (RL) aims to learn policies that optimize rewards while satisfying constraints. Predominant approaches rely on soft-constrained policy optimization, which has achieved empirical success but does not provide formal safety guarantees for the learned policy. In contrast, methods with strict guarantees typically rely on explicit certificate functions, whose construction requires the direct synthesis and verification of control-invariant sets, a process that scales poorly with state dimension and often yields overly conservative behavior. In this paper, we present the Provably Safe, yet Scalable RL (PS2-RL) framework, a novel two-phase architecture for learning provably safe policies in a scalable manner, designed to overcome the key bottlenecks of prior methods. Rather than explicitly computing invariant sets, PS2-RL leverages a learned backup policy to forward-integrate the system dynamics, generating an implicit control-invariant set online. In the first phase, the backup policy is trained with our proposed safe-arrival value function, which characterizes the optimal backup policy for invariant-set construction. In the second phase, an RL policy is trained end-to-end through a differentiable projection layer that strictly enforces the safety guarantees induced by the learned backup policy. By maximizing the volume of the implicit control-invariant set in the first phase, the resulting PS2 policy from the second phase is performant and scalable, while maintaining provable safety. Crucially, PS2-RL imposes no restrictions on the underlying RL algorithm and can be plugged into any existing training pipeline. We establish theoretical guarantees for the proposed framework and evaluate it on robotic control tasks with state dimensions up to 10, a regime in which prior provably safe RL methods struggle or become impractical.

</details>


### [120] [CARE: Controlling LLM-Generated Policies through Auditable Review of Evidence in Scientific Experimentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14581)
*Guanyu Liu,Weiyi Kong,Zeyu Wang,Boer Zhang,Baiqing Li,Peiyu Zhang,Tianyu Shi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CARE introduces an auditable controller for scientific experimentation that uses LLMs to revise ranking policies while keeping a non-LLM optimizer as the default, with a public-evidence intervention gate that only authorizes LLM-proposed changes when supported by pre-outcome evidence, achieving superior performance on Minerva/Olympus and ChemLex benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Directly granting LLMs control over costly, irreversible scientific experiments causes unsafe exploration and unstable performance, yet completely discarding LLM creativity forfeits significant optimization potential. The authors seek a middle ground that harnesses LLM capabilities while maintaining safety and stability.

Method: CARE keeps a non-LLM incumbent optimizer as the default action path and uses LLMs solely to revise challenger ranking policies. A public-evidence intervention gate compares challenger against incumbent before each outcome is revealed. The challenger's selection is authorized only when pre-selection evidence supports the change, and all decisions are recorded in an audit log.

Result: CARE outperforms all evaluated methods on Minerva/Olympus (final-best improving from 80.0 to 88.5) and ChemLex (from 83.9 to 92.1) benchmarks, relative to the public incumbent.

Conclusion: LLM self-evolution is more reliable when it expands the proposal space under an auditable controller rather than directly selecting experiments, providing both safety and performance gains in high-throughput experimentation optimization.

Abstract: Granting LLMs direct control over costly, irreversible scientific experiments leads to unsafe exploration and unstable performance, but discarding LLM creativity entirely sacrifices significant optimization potential. We introduce CARE (Controlling LLM-Generated Policies through Auditable Review of Evidence in Scientific Experimentation), an auditable controller for high-throughput experimentation (HTE) optimization that keeps a non-LLM incumbent optimizer as the default action path while using LLMs to revise challenger ranking policies. Before each outcome is revealed, a public-evidence intervention gate compares the challenger with the incumbent. It authorizes the challenger's selection only when the evidence available before selection supports the change, with the decision recorded in the audit log. CARE outperforms all other evaluated methods on Minerva/Olympus and ChemLex benchmarks, with final-best improving from 80.0 to 88.5 on Minerva/Olympus and from 83.9 to 92.1 on ChemLex, relative to the public incumbent. Our experiments indicate that LLM self-evolution is more reliable when it expands the proposal space under an auditable controller, rather than directly choosing experiments.

</details>


### [121] [Zero-shot generalization of transformer neural operators to larger domains](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14597)
*Armand de Villeroché,Sibo Cheng,Vincent Le Guen,Marc Bocquet,Rem-Sophia Mouradi,Patrick Armand,Alban Farchi,Patrick Massin*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper addresses the problem of zero-shot domain extension for transformer-based neural operators, where models must generalize to spatial domains much larger than those seen during training. The authors propose a decomposable bias in attention logits combined with rotary positional embeddings to enforce spatial locality and translation equivariance, significantly improving generalization on PDE benchmarks and a 3D industrial atmospheric flow application.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing transformer-based neural operators for PDEs assume a fixed domain size during training and fail to generalize when applied to significantly larger spatial domains at inference. This limits practical deployment in real-world applications where domain sizes vary. The authors argue that spatial locality and translation equivariance are fundamental requirements for domain extension, and that existing approaches lack mechanisms to enforce these properties in a computationally efficient manner compatible with optimized attention kernels.

Method: The authors propose a decomposable bias term added to the attention logits computation. This bias enforces spatial locality by controlling the spatial support of each query's attention, while remaining fully decomposable into query-key inner products — ensuring compatibility with FlashAttention and other optimized kernels. This is combined with rotary positional embeddings (RoPE) to provide expressive position encoding with controllable spatial support. The approach requires no changes to the underlying transformer architecture.

Result: The proposed method substantially improves zero-shot generalization to larger domains compared to baselines. Experiments were conducted across two PDE benchmarks and a 3D industrial atmospheric flow application, demonstrating consistent gains in domain extension performance.

Conclusion: Enforcing spatial locality and translation equivariance through a decomposable attention bias and rotary positional embeddings is an effective and architecturally simple strategy for enabling transformer-based neural operators to generalize to larger spatial domains at inference. The approach is compute-efficient and directly compatible with existing optimized attention implementations.

Abstract: Transformer-based neural operators have shown remarkable performance for approximating solution operators of partial differential equations on complex geometries. However, existing approaches implicitly assume a fixed domain size, which limits their ability to generalize at inference. In this work, we investigate domain extension, namely zero-shot inference on spatial domains that are significantly larger than those encountered during training. We argue that this setting fundamentally requires spatial locality and translation equivariance. We propose to implement this locality via a decomposable bias in the attention logits computation, enabling finely controllable locality while remaining fully decomposable into query-key inner products and directly compatible with optimized attention kernels. Combined with rotary positional embeddings, it enables expressive embeddings with controllable spatial support without altering the transformer architecture. We empirically show that our approach substantially improves zero-shot generalization to larger domains across two PDE benchmarks and a 3D industrial atmospheric flow application. Our code and datasets are available at https://github.com/cerea-daml/domain-extension.

</details>


### [122] [Realizing Native INT8 Compute for Diffusion Transformers on Consumer GPUs: A Fused INT8 GEMM Kernel for Ideogram 4.0](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14598)
*Ali Asaria,Tony Salomone,Deep Gandhi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Though INT8 quantization is widely deployed for diffusion transformers, on consumer Ampere GPUs it paradoxically runs slower than FP8/NF4 because production implementations quantize only to immediately dequantize back to bf16, never using INT8 tensor cores. The authors fix this with a fused Triton INT8 GEMM kernel that achieves 2.8–4.2× GEMM speedups, making INT8 the fastest variant for Ideogram 4.0 at 768px–1024px on RTX 3090.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Post-training INT8 quantization of diffusion transformers is meant to accelerate inference, yet on consumer Ampere GPUs (e.g., RTX 3090) stock INT8 implementations are slower than FP8 and NF4 alternatives. The authors identify the root cause: a software artifact where "INT8" inference quantizes weights/activations only to immediately dequantize to bf16 for the matrix multiply, never engaging INT8 tensor cores, thus forfeiting hardware compute advantages.

Method: A single fused Triton INT8 GEMM kernel is written: int8×int8 → int32 accumulation on Ampere tensor cores, with per-token × per-channel dequantization and bias folded into the epilogue, autotuned per GEMM shape. This kernel is dropped into Ideogram 4.0's linear layers, replacing the dequantize-to-bf16 path. Bit-exactness is verified against torch._int_mm, and output cosine similarity to reference is 1.0 with no NaNs.

Result: Per GEMM, the fused kernel runs 2.8–4.2× faster than bf16. End-to-end, it yields ~1.1× (9–10%) speedup at 768px. At 1024px, single RTX 3090 generation takes 156.5 s, beating NF4 (164.5 s) and FP8 (172.9 s) baselines, with no measurable quality cost on PickScore/CLIPScore. INT8 goes from slowest to fastest; 1024px becomes single-GPU feasible. The primary target (beat FP8 by ~9.5%) is met; the NF4 margin (~4.9%) is within run-to-run variance. On datacenter GPUs (A100, B200) the kernel loses to native bf16/FP8 paths.

Conclusion: The INT8 slowdown on consumer Ampere is entirely a software artifact, not a hardware limitation. A properly fused INT8 GEMM kernel that actually engages tensor cores makes INT8 the fastest quantization format for diffusion transformers on RTX 3090, enabling single-GPU 1024px generation. However, the win is specific to consumer Ampere; datacenter GPUs with fast native bf16/FP8 do not benefit.

Abstract: Post-training INT8 (W8A8) quantization of diffusion transformers is widely deployed as a speed optimization, yet on consumer Ampere GPUs it is frequently slower than the FP8 and NF4 alternatives it is meant to beat. We trace this to a software artifact: the production "INT8" forward quantizes weights and activations only to immediately dequantize them back to bf16 and run a bf16 matrix multiply, never engaging the GPU's INT8 tensor cores, so the hardware's compute advantage is left entirely unrealized. We close this gap with a single fused Triton INT8 GEMM (int8xint8->int32 on Ampere tensor cores, with per-token x per-channel dequantization and bias folded into the epilogue, autotuned per GEMM shape) dropped into the Ideogram 4.0 diffusion transformer's linear layers in place of the dequantize-to-bf16 path. In the kernel, the int8xint8->int32 accumulation is bit-exact against torch._int_mm and the dequantized output matches the reference at cosine similarity 1.0 with no NaNs, running 2.8-4.2x faster than bf16 per GEMM. End to end it delivers a ~1.1x (~9-10%) speedup at 768px, and at 1024px it generates an image in 156.5 s on a single RTX 3090, faster than the single-card NF4 (164.5 s) and FP8 (172.9 s) baselines, at no measurable quality cost on these point estimates (PickScore/CLIPScore). INT8 thus goes from the slowest variant to the fastest, and 1024px becomes single-GPU feasible. The primary speed criterion (beat FP8, by ~9.5%) is comfortably met; the NF4 margin (~4.9%, single-run n=4) is within run-to-run variance we did not quantify and is best read as consistent with meeting the stretch target. We close with an honest deployment map: the win is specific to consumer Ampere, and on A100 and B200 the same kernel loses to those cards' fast native bf16/FP8 paths.

</details>


### [123] [A Statistical and Machine Learning Framework for Operational Threshold Detection and Deployable Dispatch Controller Development in Hydrogen Multi-Energy Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14601)
*Shadi Heenatigala,Hasanika Samarasinghe*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A hybrid statistical/ML framework analyzes one year of operational data from a hydrogen-based multi-energy system (H-MES), revealing binary renewable-driven operation, non-linear predictive dynamics (wind importance hidden from linear methods), and demonstrating complementary roles of statistical and machine learning approaches for modeling and control.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: H-MES systems are complex, with interacting renewable sources, storage, and demand. Understanding their operational dynamics and developing robust predictive and control models requires moving beyond conventional parametric approaches. The study aims to demonstrate that statistical and machine learning methods are complementary—not competing—tools for characterizing, forecasting, and optimizing such systems.

Method: Statistical analysis (rank-based variance partitioning, multiple regression, effect sizes like $ε^2$ and $r$) applied to one year of high-resolution H-MES operational data; Random Forest for non-linear feature importance ranking; sequence modeling exploiting 24-hour autocorrelation (r=0.845) for operational forecasting; reinforcement learning agent for hydrogen revenue dispatch optimization.

Result: Solar irradiance explains 45.7% of rank-based variance in hydrogen production (large effect). Electrolyzer operation is binary—triggered only at high irradiance. Electricity demand shows weak inverse suppression ($ε^2 = 0.126$). Electrolyzer power is the dominant linear predictor with a synergistic solar-wind interaction. Random Forest ranks wind as most important predictor despite weak bivariate correlation (r=0.167), revealing non-linear dynamics. Strong 24-hour autocorrelation (r=0.845) supports sequence model forecasting.

Conclusion: Statistical and machine learning methods are complementary: parametric approaches provide interpretable effect sizes and mechanistic insight, while non-linear ML methods (Random Forest, reinforcement learning) capture hidden dynamics and enable operational optimization. Integrating both yields a more complete H-MES modeling and control framework.

Abstract: This study presents a statistical and machine learning framework for characterizing a hydrogen-based multi-energy system (H-MES) using one year of high-resolution operational data. Statistical analysis revealed a binary operation driven by renewable surplus, with solar irradiance explaining 45.7% of rank-based variance in hydrogen production, a large effect by conventional standards. Only high-irradiance periods triggered meaningful electrolyzer engagement, while electricity demand exerted a weaker inverse suppression effect ($ε^2 = 0.126$). Multiple regression confirmed electrolyzer power as the dominant linear predictor, with a synergistic solar-wind interaction. Notably, Random Forest analysis ranked wind output first in predictive importance despite its weak bivariate correlation (r = 0.167), revealing non-linear dynamics invisible to parametric methods. A sequence model exploited strong 24-hour autocorrelation (r = 0.845) for operational forecasting, while a reinforcement learning agent optimized hydrogen revenue dispatch. The core contribution is demonstrating that statistical and machine learning approaches are complementary for H-MES modeling and control.

</details>


### [124] [A Comparative Study of Deep Learning Architectures for Multi-Horizon Behavioural Forecasting for Mobile Health](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14604)
*Pavlos Nicolaou,Kleanthis Malialis,Artemis Kontou,Panayiotis Kolios*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper benchmarks six deep learning architectures, two foundation models, and statistical baselines on wearable/smartphone behavioural time series (step counts, screen time, sleep duration) from 800+ participants across 1-8 day horizons. Key findings: PatchTST leads trained models; TimesFM matches or exceeds them zero-shot; participant-level fine-tuning reduces RMSE by 16-60%, with sleep benefiting most.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Wearables and smartphones generate rich behavioural time series useful for proactive health interventions, but there is no systematic comparison of modern forecasting architectures for these data. Unanswered questions include: how models generalise across populations, how different architectures respond to participant-level fine-tuning, and how accuracy degrades across multi-day horizons.

Method: The authors benchmark six deep learning architectures (including PatchTST, TCN, MLP, Transformer), two zero-shot foundation models (TimesFM and another), plus statistical baselines on three public datasets (800+ participants). They evaluate per-feature metrics (step counts, screen time, sleep duration) across 1-8 day forecasting horizons. A per-feature personalisation study is conducted across all six architectures, along with assessment of foundation model transferability across dataset sizes and temporal granularities.

Result: Three key findings: (1) No single architecture dominates — PatchTST leads among trained models, with TCN, MLP, and Transformer showing no meaningful performance difference. (2) The foundation model TimesFM matches or exceeds trained models zero-shot, particularly in low-data regimes. (3) Participant-level fine-tuning reduces per-feature RMSE by 16-60%, with sleep duration benefiting most and step counts least.

Conclusion: This is the first study to jointly evaluate modern deep learning, foundation models, and personalisation for multi-horizon behavioural forecasting from wearables. Results offer practical guidance on architecture selection, foundation model applicability, and personalisation strategies for mobile health forecasting.

Abstract: Wearable devices and smartphones generate rich behavioural time series that can support proactive health interventions, yet systematic comparisons of modern forecasting architectures for these data are lacking. In particular, it remains unclear how models generalise across populations, how different architectures respond to participant-level fine-tuning and how forecasting accuracy degrades across multi-day horizons. We benchmark six deep learning architectures, two zero-shot Foundation Models (FM) and statistical baselines on three public datasets encompassing over 800 participants, reporting per-feature metrics for step counts, screen time and sleep duration across 1-8 day horizons. We further conduct a per-feature personalisation study across all six architectures and assess FM transferability across dataset sizes and temporal granularities. Our key findings are: (i) no single architecture dominates, PatchTST leads among trained models while the three runners-up (TCN, MLP, Transformer) show no meaningful performance difference; (ii) the FM TimesFM matches or exceeds trained models zero-shot, especially in low-data regimes and (iii) participant-level fine-tuning reduces per-feature RMSE by 16-60\%, with sleep benefiting most and step counts least. These results provide practical guidance on architecture selection, FM applicability and personalisation strategies for mobile health forecasting. To the best of our knowledge, this is the first study to jointly evaluate modern deep learning, FMs and personalisation for multi-horizon behavioural forecasting from wearables.

</details>


### [125] [Expert-Driven Survival Machines: Improving Stratification and Interpretability in Multiple Clinical Cohorts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14608)
*Farica Zhuang,Zixuan Wen,Christos Davatzikos,Li Shen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: AdaCSM proposes a Mixture-of-Experts enhanced adaptive deep clustering framework for survival prediction that dynamically routes patients to specialized risk predictors, capturing heterogeneous survival patterns and improving both predictive performance and interpretability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing deep survival models learn a single shared feature representation for all patients, obscuring critical differences between patient subgroups. A Mixture-of-Experts approach can better capture this heterogeneity by allowing different model components to specialize on distinct patient patterns.

Method: AdaCSM integrates a routing-based expert mechanism into a parametric survival modeling framework. The architecture dynamically assigns patients to specialized risk predictors while jointly optimizing for survival prediction and subtype clustering objectives, enabling conditional specialization.

Result: AdaCSM was evaluated against state-of-the-art survival and deep clustering models on multiple real-world longitudinal clinical cohorts across diverse disease domains. It demonstrated improved predictive performance and produced interpretable results.

Conclusion: The mixture-of-experts approach effectively models heterogeneous survival patterns, yielding more individualized representations. AdaCSM outperforms existing methods and provides clinically interpretable insights, supporting better risk stratification and patient management.

Abstract: Survival prediction plays a central role for healthcare providers and clinical researchers. Accurate risk stratification enables early intervention and improved patient management. Most existing deep survival models learn one common feature representation for all patients, which may hide important differences between patient subgroups. In contrast, a Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) framework allows different parts of the model to focus on different patient patterns, leading to more individualized representations. Therefore, in this work, we propose a mixture-of-experts enhanced adaptive deep clustering survival framework (AdaCSM) for modeling such heterogeneous survival patterns. We introduce a routing-based expert mechanism that enables conditional specialization within a parametric survival modeling framework. The proposed architecture allocates patients to specialized risk predictors dynamically while preserving the patient survival and subtype clustering objectives. We compare our method with state-of-the-art survival and deep clustering models on multiple real-world longitudinal clinical cohorts spanning diverse disease domains. The proposed method demonstrates improved predictive performance and leads to interpretable results in survival analysis.

</details>


### [126] [Neither Parallel Nor Sequential: How DiffusionGemma Actually Commits Tokens](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14620)
*Ali Asaria,Tony Salomone,Deep Gandhi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper instruments DiffusionGemma 26B to empirically measure token commit order during sampling, revealing that decoding is neither parallel nor block-autoregressive but exhibits a weak left-to-right bias that strengthens only under coarse-grained analysis. Commit order is regime-dependent, confidence correlates with correctness only on certain tasks, and the apparent "block size" is an artifact of measurement granularity. The central contribution is a rigorous methodology for measuring decoding order that accounts for several key confounds.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Diffusion language models are marketed as parallel, non-autoregressive decoders, yet no one has actually instrumented a real, shipped checkpoint to measure the order in which tokens are committed during sampling. Without such measurement, claims about parallelism or block-autoregressive behavior remain unverified.

Method: The authors instrument DiffusionGemma 26B (a masked discrete-diffusion MoE model built on Gemma 4) by hooking the sampler's accept step to record which canvas positions commit, at what timestamp, and with what confidence. They run a probe suite of 686 prompts across six regimes (including structured JSON, mathematical reasoning, and factual recall) and analyze commit order at multiple granularities while controlling for trailing-EOS padding, within-regime confounding, commit non-monotonicity, block-size sensitivity, and large commit-batch ties.

Result: Decoding is neither fully parallel nor block-autoregressive; it shows a partial left-to-right commit bias whose apparent strength depends entirely on analysis granularity. Order is weak token-by-token and strengthens as analysis is coarsened, meaning "block size" is a measurement artifact. The model commits in large simultaneous batches with much within-batch order genuinely undefined. Regime matters: structured JSON commits in near-arbitrary order; commit confidence tracks correctness on math reasoning but not on factual recall. Commits are aggressive, concentrated in a late burst within the step budget, while accuracy matches the autoregressive Gemma-4 sibling.

Conclusion: The central contribution is methodological: a rigorous protocol for measuring decoding order that handles trailing-EOS padding, within-regime confounding, commit non-monotonicity, block-size sensitivity, and large commit-batch ties — each of which can manufacture spurious results if unaccounted for. Empirically, diffusion LM decoding order is messier and more regime-dependent than previously assumed.

Abstract: Open diffusion language models are marketed as parallel, non-autoregressive decoders, yet the order in which a shipped checkpoint actually commits its tokens is almost never measured. We instrument DiffusionGemma 26B, a masked discrete-diffusion mixture-of-experts model built on Gemma 4, hooking its sampler's accept step to record which canvas positions commit, when, and at what confidence. Across a 686-prompt, six-regime probe suite we find that its decoding is neither parallel nor block-autoregressive: it follows a partial left-to-right commit bias whose apparent strength depends almost entirely on the granularity at which you look. Order is weak token by token and strengthens smoothly as the analysis is coarsened, so the model's "block size" turns out to be an artifact of the measuring ruler rather than the architecture. The model commits in large simultaneous batches, leaving much of the within-batch order genuinely undefined rather than merely unobserved. The behaviour is regime-dependent: structured JSON is committed in essentially arbitrary order, and a position's commit confidence tracks correctness on mathematical reasoning but carries no signal on factual recall. Commitment is aggressive, finishing in a short late burst well inside the step budget, while task accuracy matches the model's autoregressive Gemma-4 sibling. Beyond these findings, our central contribution is methodological: measuring decoding order honestly demands handling trailing-EOS padding, within-regime confounding, commit non-monotonicity, block-size sensitivity, and large commit-batch ties, each of which can otherwise manufacture a decoding-order result that is not really there.

</details>


### [127] [Graph Diffusion Residuals for Control-Function Instrumental Variables](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14636)
*Rui Wu,Zongyuan Chen,Hong Xie,Defu Lian,Enhong Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A-IHF is a graph-diffusion method for extracting first-stage residuals in control-function IV estimation. It uses anisotropic diffusion to preserve treatment discontinuities while smoothing, and employs a data-driven selection rule based on Z and X. It outperforms multiple baselines across 54 synthetic benchmarks, especially under piecewise-smooth first-stage geometry.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard control-function IV estimators rely on first-stage residuals, but high-capacity first-stage models (e.g., deep nets, boosting) can interpolate treatment, leaving insufficient residual variation for the outcome equation. Existing approaches lack a principled, data-adaptive way to extract residuals that preserves structural information needed for consistent IV estimation. The authors aim to fill this gap with a deterministic graph-based residual extractor.

Method: A-IHF operates in three stages: (1) Pilot diffusion on a graph of first-stage features to detect large treatment jumps; (2) Attenuation of graph conductance across detected jumps to create an anisotropic graph; (3) Computation of the control function via a sparse graph resolvent. Model selection uses only (Z, X) and combines graph generalized cross-validation, roughness penalties, residualized-treatment relevance, and graph-admissibility filtering. The theoretical analysis decomposes error into structural leakage, residual attenuation, and residualized treatment variation, providing finite-sample bounds and graph-admissibility rates under latent piecewise-smooth geometry.

Result: Across 54 synthetic benchmark cells comparing tuned graph, kernel, tree, boosting, series, and neural control-function baselines, guarded observational A-IHF achieves the lowest average structural-response MSE. The broader A-IHF family outperforms the best non-A-IHF baseline in 32 of 54 cells. Performance is strongest when the graph captures piecewise-smooth first-stage structure.

Conclusion: A-IHF provides a theoretically grounded, data-adaptive solution for extracting first-stage residuals in flexible control-function IV settings. Its strength lies in leveraging graph structure to respect discontinuities in the first stage, and its observational selection rule offers practical model choice without outcome information.

Abstract: Control-function instrumental variable estimators need a first-stage residual, not merely a first-stage prediction. High-capacity first stages can interpolate treatment and leave too little residual information for the outcome equation. We study Adaptive Anisotropic Instrumental Heat Flow (A-IHF), a deterministic graph-diffusion residual extractor for flexible control functions. A-IHF treats treatment as a signal on a graph of first-stage features, uses pilot diffusion to detect large treatment jumps, attenuates conductance across those jumps, and computes the generated control with a sparse graph resolvent. Its observational selection rule uses only $(Z,X)$, combining graph generalized cross-validation, roughness, residualized-treatment relevance, and graph-admissibility filtering. The analysis decomposes error into structural leakage, residual attenuation, and residualized treatment variation, yielding finite-sample bounds, graph-admissibility rates under latent piecewise-smooth geometry, and finite-path selection calibration. Across 54 synthetic benchmark cells with tuned graph, kernel, tree, boosting, series, and neural control-function baselines, guarded observational A-IHF has the lowest average structural-response MSE; the A-IHF family beats the best non-A-IHF baseline in 32 cells. Performance is strongest when the graph captures piecewise-smooth first-stage structure.

</details>


### [128] [Online Convex Optimization with Sublinear Noisy Probes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14640)
*Simone Di Gregorio,Anupam Gupta,Stefano Leonardi,Matteo Russo*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Introduces a unified probing model for Online Convex Optimization where a learner with a limited budget of noisy pairwise comparison queries can achieve improved regret bounds. The regret guarantee smoothly interpolates between full-information and bandit regimes, scaling as O(min{√(dT ln T), dT ln T/(k|1-2δ|)}), and the analysis leverages variance reduction via probing combined with a second-order Continuous Exponential Weights analysis.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Bridge two recent lines of OCO research — sublinear best-expert queries and pairwise comparison feedback — into a single framework. Determine whether even a sublinear, noisy budget of pairwise probes can provably improve worst-case regret over standard full-information OCO.

Method: The learner has a budget of k ≤ T pairwise probes per T rounds. On a probed round, it queries two points and receives a δ-noisy comparison of their losses. The analysis quantifies the benefit of probing through variance reduction, coupling it with a second-order (variance-based) analysis of the Continuous Exponential Weights algorithm. Results are also specialized to the finite K (d experts) setting.

Result: A regret bound of O(min{√(dT ln T), dT ln T/(k|1-2δ|)}) is achieved, which is tight (up to log factors) across T, k, and δ. The bound degrades smoothly as δ → 1/2 (coin-flip noise). For finite expert settings, the rates are completely tight in all parameters including d.

Conclusion: Even a sublinear, noisy pairwise probe budget provably improves OCO regret. The variance-reduction framework provides a clean, unified analysis, and the tightness results establish fundamental limits of probing in both convex and finite expert settings.

Abstract: We study Online Convex Optimization (OCO) over a convex set $K\subseteq \mathbb R^d$, where in each round $t$ the learner selects $x_t\in K$ and then observes a convex loss $f_t:K\to[0,1]$, with the goal of minimizing regret to the best fixed decision in hindsight. We introduce a unified probing model that generalizes two recent lines of work: sublinear best-expert queries in the experts setting, and pairwise (comparison-based) feedback available every round in OCO. In our framework, the learner has a budget of $k\le T$ pairwise probes; on a probed round it may query two points and learn which one has smaller loss.
  Our main result shows that even a sublinear and noisy probe budget can provably improve worst-case regret in the full feedback OCO regime. With $k$ $δ$-noisy pairwise probes, we obtain: $ \text{Reg}_T \le O\left(\min\left\{\sqrt{dT\ln T},\; \frac{dT\ln T}{k|1-2δ|}\right\}\right) $, which is tight (up to logarithmic factors in $T$) across $T$, $k$ and $δ$. Specifically regarding the noise parameter $δ\in [0,1]$, the regret guarantee smoothly degrades as the oracle response approaches a coin flip, i.e., $δ$ is close to $\frac{1}{2}$. When applying the same techniques to a finite $K$ for the prediction with $d$ experts setting, the resulting rates are instead completely tight in all parameters, including $d$.
  Our analysis gives a streamlined treatment of pairwise probing in OCO by quantifying the benefit of probing via a variance reduction effect, combined with a second-order (variance-based) analysis of Continuous Exponential Weights.

</details>


### [129] [Which Directions Matter? Sparse Design for Affine Robust Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14648)
*Pedro Chumpitaz-Flores,My Duong,Juan S. Borrero,Kaixun Hua*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper studies how to select a subset of uncertainty directions from a finite dictionary under a budget constraint to form an atomic uncertainty set for robust optimization. The authors propose a data-driven selection rule based on a coverage objective, prove it is monotone submodular (enabling a greedy algorithm with (1-1/e) approximation), and provide loss certificates and radius calibration.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Robust machine learning and optimization critically depend on the choice of uncertainty model. When the uncertainty set is defined by a finite dictionary of directions with a budget on how many to include, it is unclear which directions should be selected to provide effective robustness guarantees. The paper addresses this fundamental model selection problem.

Method: The authors use atomic uncertainty sets formed by selecting a subset of vectors from a finite dictionary, which yield a closed-form support function and tractable robust programs for affine objectives. They propose a data-driven selection rule that maximizes a coverage objective over evaluation directions (gradients, adversarial perturbations, or distribution shifts from held-out data). They prove the coverage objective is monotone and submodular, enabling a greedy selection algorithm with (1-1/e) approximation. They also derive a loss certificate bounding suboptimality and a radius calibration rule with out-of-sample statistical control.

Result: The coverage objective is proven monotone and submodular, which guarantees that greedy subset selection achieves a (1-1/e) approximation ratio, with a matching hardness barrier. A certificate bounds the loss incurred by the selected subset relative to the full dictionary, and a radius calibration procedure provides out-of-sample control.

Conclusion: Selecting uncertainty directions via greedy maximization of a submodular coverage objective provides a principled, tractable, and theoretically grounded approach to constructing atomic uncertainty sets for robust optimization, with formal approximation guarantees and statistical calibration.

Abstract: Robust machine learning and optimization rely on the uncertainty model choice. We investigate which uncertainty directions a model must cover when defined by a finite dictionary and a budget constraint. Selecting a subset forms an atomic uncertainty set with a closed form support function, yielding tractable robust programs for affine objectives. We propose a data driven selection rule based on a coverage objective over evaluation directions, including gradients, adversarial perturbations, or shifts observed on held out data. We prove this objective is monotone and submodular, supporting a greedy method with a $(1-1/e)$ approximation guarantee and a matching hardness barrier. We also provide a certificate bounding the loss from the selected subset and a radius calibration rule with out of sample control.

</details>


### [130] [Graph Structured Combinatorial Semi-Bandit with Nonlinear Reward Associations through Separable Signals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14650)
*Christoph Bauschmann,Setareh Maghsudi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proposes a generic adaptive framework for identifying optimal structures in large interconnected datasets, combining graph-based causal reward modeling, reproducing kernel methods, and Taylor approximations. It provides sublinear time and linear data-volume performance guarantees with robustness analyses, validated on synthetic and transportation datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Identifying optimal structures in large-scale interconnected data is computationally and sampling-intensive. Nonlinear statistical dependencies are pervasive, making structure learning even more challenging. Existing methods lack a unified adaptive framework that efficiently leverages signal dependencies while maintaining theoretical guarantees under various uncertainties.

Method: The authors develop generic and adaptive strategies integrating three core components: (1) graph-based causal reward modeling to capture structured dependencies, (2) analytic reproducing kernel methods for flexible nonlinear function approximation in Hilbert spaces, and (3) Taylor approximations for functional process expansion. Theoretical analysis establishes sublinear-time and linear-data-volume performance bounds, with robustness guarantees against noise interference, gradual model convergence, and solution space mismatch.

Result: The framework achieves sublinear time complexity and linear scaling in data volume with formal performance guarantees. Robustness is proven across multiple uncertainty sources: noise, convergence dynamics, and solution mismatch. Numerical experiments on benchmark synthetic data and real-world transportation datasets demonstrate practical effectiveness under minimal assumptions.

Conclusion: The proposed framework offers a theoretically grounded, practically effective solution for optimal structure identification in large-scale nonlinear settings. Its generality, minimal assumptions, and extensibility to specialized settings underscore broad applicability.

Abstract: The identification of optimal structures within vast arrays of interconnected data necessitates significant sampling- and computational effort. Learning and leveraging underlying signal dependencies can improve efficiency and predictive capabilities considerably, but the ubiquity of nonlinear statistical relations amplifies the complexity of such undertakings. In this paper, we develop novel generic and adaptive strategies equipped with routines for graph-based causal reward modeling, analytic reproducing kernel methods, and Taylor approximation of functional processes. We establish theoretical performance guarantees sublinear in time and linear in data volume over time. Our analyses cover robustness to a multitude of uncertainties arising from noise interference, gradual model convergence, and solution space mismatch. The framework's general appeal is substantiated by a minimalistic set of conditions or reliance on prior estimates, while various outlined modifications address specific or extended settings. To demonstrate practical effectiveness, we conduct numerical experiments using both benchmarked synthetic and real-world transportation datasets.

</details>


### [131] [Beyond task performance: Decoding bioacoustic embeddings with speech features](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14662)
*Ines Nolasco,Jules Cauzinille,Marius Miron,Gagan Narula,Milad Alizadeh,Emmanuel Fernandez,Matthieu Geist,Ellen Gilsenan-McMahon,Olivier Pietquin,Emmanuel Chemla,Sara Keen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This study probes six pretrained audio embeddings to quantify which 88 eGeMAPS acoustic features they encode across six taxonomic groups. No single model captures the full feature space; loudness is best recovered (R²=0.76), F0 worst (R²=0.33). Concatenated embeddings perform best, and cross-referencing recoverability with per-species feature salience yields data-driven model selection guidance for bioacoustics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Pretrained audio embeddings are widely used in bioacoustics, but their interpretability is poor—it is unclear which acoustic features (e.g., pitch, loudness, spectral properties) these models encode and which matter for specific tasks. This opacity limits trust and hinders application to rare species or data-scarce domains. The authors aim to open the black box and provide principled, data-driven guidance for embedding selection.

Method: The authors take six pretrained audio embedding models (including general-purpose and bioacoustics-specific ones) and probe them with the 88 eGeMAPS feature set—a standard suprasegmental acoustic feature set from speech processing. Using both linear and nonlinear regression probes, they quantify how well each embedding recovers each acoustic feature (R²). Experiments span six taxonomic groups. They then cross-reference feature recoverability with per-species feature salience measured via Normalized Mutual Information (NMI) to derive a model selection framework.

Result: Key findings: (1) No single embedding model captures the full eGeMAPS feature space—a "no free lunch" pattern; (2) concatenating all embeddings yields the highest overall recovery, indicating complementary coverage; (3) loudness-related features are best encoded (R² = 0.76), while fundamental frequency (F0) is hardest to recover (R² = 0.33); (4) cross-referencing recoverability with species-level NMI salience provides actionable model selection recommendations per taxonomic group and task.

Conclusion: The work demonstrates that bioacoustic embeddings encode speech-like acoustic features to varying, complementary degrees. By mapping which features each model captures and aligning that with task-relevant feature salience, practitioners can make informed embedding choices rather than relying on trial-and-error. This improves transparency, efficiency, and extensibility to data-scarce scenarios.

Abstract: Pretrained audio embeddings are standard in bioacoustics, yet little is known about which acoustic features these models encode, nor which are useful for a given task. This hinders transparency and limits extension to rare species or data-scarce domains. Here we reveal which speech-like features are encoded in bioacoustic representations. Using the 88~eGeMAPS features across six taxonomic groups, we apply linear and nonlinear regression probes to quantify which acoustic properties each model captures. Results confirm a ``no free lunch'' pattern: no single model captures the full feature space. A concatenated embedding achieves the highest performance, suggesting complementary acoustic space coverage across models. Loudness features are best encoded ($R^2 = 0.76$) while F0 is hardest to recover ($R^2 = 0.33$). By cross-referencing recoverability with per-species feature salience (NMI), we derive data-driven model selection guidance for bioacoustics.

</details>


### [132] [When to Write and When to Suppress: Route-Specialized Dual Adapters for Memory-Assisted Knowledge Editing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14668)
*Yining Huang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper introduces a route-specialized dual-adapter editor for knowledge editing that uses a relevance router to decide when to apply edits (via an edit adapter) versus when to suppress them (via a locality adapter). The method achieves state-of-the-art probability-preference accuracy on three benchmarks (CounterFact, zsRE, MQuAKE) across two 8B-class models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing knowledge editing systems focus on how to write edits into models but neglect the equally important question of when to suppress edits. In memory-assisted editing, retrieved edit memories can interfere with irrelevant prompts, damaging locality. The central design gap is that no mechanism explicitly decides whether a given prompt should trigger an edit or preserve original behavior.

Method: The proposed method comprises three components: (1) a relevance router that classifies whether an incoming prompt is semantically within the scope of a stored edit memory; (2) an edit adapter (LoRA) trained to shift the model's output preference toward the edited object; and (3) a locality adapter (LoRA) trained to preserve or restore the original object preference. Routed prompts use the edit adapter; unrouted prompts use the locality adapter. Router variants include lexical overlap (neural classifier on token overlap features) and BGE embedding similarity routing.

Result: On Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct, the method achieves probability-preference accuracy of 0.8180 on CounterFact, 0.8946 on zsRE, and 0.9922 on MQuAKE — the best overall results across all three benchmarks. The same trend holds on Qwen3-8B. Router ablation reveals dataset-specific optimal routing strategies: lexical neural routing is safest for CounterFact, while BGE embedding routing excels on zsRE and MQuAKE. Ablations further show that separating edit injection from off-route suppression, rather than merely increasing LoRA capacity, drives the performance gain.

Conclusion: The central insight is that knowledge editing requires not only writing edits but also determining when to suppress them. The dual-adapter architecture with a relevance router effectively decouples edit application from locality preservation, yielding robust performance across diverse editing benchmarks. The choice of routing mechanism should be tailored to the dataset's edit-boundary characteristics.

Abstract: Knowledge editing systems must update selected facts while preserving nearby but irrelevant behavior. This paper studies this problem in a memory-assisted setting where an edit memory is retrieved at inference time and a parameter-efficient adapter corrects the model's object preference. We argue that the central design question is not only how to write an edit, but also when to suppress it. We introduce \method{}, a route-specialized dual-adapter editor. A relevance router first decides whether a prompt should receive an edit memory. Routed prompts use an edit adapter trained to prefer the new object over the original object; unrouted non-direct prompts use a separate locality adapter trained to preserve or restore the original-object preference. We evaluate \method{} on three 1,000-case protocols, \cf{}, \zsre{}, and \mquake{}, under the same memory protocol and two 7B/8B base models. On Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct, \method{} obtains the best overall probability-preference accuracy on all three benchmarks: 0.8180 on \cf{}, 0.8946 on \zsre{}, and 0.9922 on \mquake{}. The same trend holds on Qwen3-8B. Router ablations show that the relevant memory boundary differs across datasets: a lexical neural router is safest on \cf{}, while BGE embedding routing is better on \zsre{} and \mquake{}. Component and module ablations show that the gain mainly comes from separating edit injection from off-route suppression rather than from simply increasing LoRA capacity.

</details>


### [133] [Compressed Computation is (probably) not Computation in Superposition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14673)
*Jai Bhagat,Sara Molas-Medina,Giorgi Giglemiani,Stefan Heimersheim*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper argues that the Compressed Computation (CC) toy model is not a valid instance of computation in superposition. Performance gains stem from an unintended mixing matrix in the labels, not from genuine superpositional computation. A semi-NMF baseline derived from the mixing matrix reproduces key loss profiles, and gains vanish when the mixing matrix is removed.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The CC model (Braun et al., 2025) appeared to compute 100 ReLU functions using only 50 neurons, suggesting computation in superposition. This paper investigates whether this interpretation is correct or whether the apparent efficiency arises from an artifact — specifically, an unintended mixing structure in the training labels.

Method: The authors decompose the training objective into a ReLU term and a mixing term, showing that performance scales with the mixing matrix magnitude. They analyze neuron directions, finding concentration in the top-50-eigenvalue subspace of the mixing matrix. They also construct a semi-non-negative matrix factorization (SNMF) baseline derived solely from the mixing matrix and compare its loss profile against the trained CC model and prior baselines.

Result: Performance gains vanish when the mixing matrix is removed. Learned neuron directions align with the top-50 eigen subspace of the mixing matrix. The SNMF baseline reproduces the qualitative loss profile and outperforms prior baselines, though it does not fully match the trained model's performance.

Conclusion: CC is not a suitable toy model for studying computation in superposition, as its apparent efficiency is largely explained by exploiting an unintended mixing structure in the labels rather than by performing genuine superpositional computation.

Abstract: We study whether the Compressed Computation (CC) toy model (Braun et al., 2025) is an instance of computation in superposition. The CC model appears to compute 100 ReLU functions with just 50 neurons, achieving a better loss than expected from only representing 50 ReLU functions. We show that the model mixes inputs via its noisy residual stream, corresponding to an unintended mixing matrix in the labels. Splitting the training objective into the ReLU term and the mixing term, we find that performance gains scale with the magnitude of the mixing matrix and vanish when the matrix is removed. The learned neuron directions concentrate in the subspace associated with the top 50 eigenvalues of the mixing matrix, suggesting that the mixing term governs the solution. Finally, a semi-non-negative matrix factorization (SNMF) baseline derived solely from the mixing matrix reproduces the qualitative loss profile and improves on prior baselines, though it does not match the trained model. These results suggest CC is not a suitable toy model of computation in superposition.

</details>


### [134] [Optimal Hidden-Target Learning for Online Inventory Optimization on General Convex Sets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14679)
*Anthony Pineci,Yunzong Xu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proves that a simple "hidden target with projection" principle is optimal for online inventory optimization (OIO) over arbitrary convex capacity sets. The key insight is a norm alignment principle that reduces the multi-dimensional state-dependent problem to one-dimensional queue control, yielding improved regret bounds (inverse-square-root dependence on demand probability, polylogarithmic for strongly convex losses, and dynamic regret guarantees).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: OIO involves online convex optimization with physical inventory carryover, where past actions constrain the current feasible set — a challenging state-dependent problem. Prior work either handled only a single linear capacity constraint or achieved suboptimal regret guarantees (inverse, rather than inverse-square-root, dependence on demand probability). The goal is to develop a principled, optimal method for general convex capacity sets with improved theoretical guarantees.

Method: The method maintains a hidden target chosen by an online learner (e.g., online gradient descent) and projects it onto the currently feasible order-up-to set. The core theoretical innovation is the **norm alignment principle**: the state variable is defined as the distance from the hidden target to the feasible set, measured in the same norm used for projection. Under norm alignment, this distance evolves pathwise as a scalar queue — target movement acts as arrivals and common demand as service — reducing the multi-dimensional, state-dependent OIO problem to one-dimensional queue control. This enables analysis on arbitrary bounded convex capacity sets beyond prior product-wise approaches.

Result: The method improves the best-known regret bound for OIO on general convex sets from inverse to inverse-square-root dependence on the common-demand probability, with a matching lower bound proving optimality. It also provides the first polylogarithmic regret for strongly convex losses and the first dynamic regret guarantee adapting to Euclidean path variation on general convex capacity sets. Experiments on synthetic and real-world inventory data corroborate the theoretical findings.

Conclusion: The hidden-target projection principle, combined with norm alignment, is optimal for OIO on general convex capacity sets. The reduction to scalar queue dynamics elegantly resolves state dependence and extends guarantees beyond prior approaches. This unifies and advances the theoretical understanding of online inventory optimization.

Abstract: Online inventory optimization (OIO) is online convex optimization with physical memory: inventory carryover makes the feasible action set depend on the past. A natural principle, used in stochastic inventory learning and recently in OIO under a single linear capacity constraint, is to maintain a hidden target chosen by an online learner and implement its projection onto the currently feasible order-up-to set. We prove that this simple principle is optimal for OIO on arbitrary bounded convex capacity sets. With online gradient descent as the base learner, the method improves the best known regret guarantee for OIO on general convex sets from inverse to inverse-square-root dependence on the common-demand probability, and we prove a matching lower bound. The same principle gives the first polylogarithmic regret guarantee for strongly convex losses and the first dynamic regret guarantee adapting to Euclidean path variation on general convex capacity sets.
  The analysis introduces a norm alignment principle: the right state variable is the distance from the hidden target to the feasible set, measured in the same norm as the projection. Under norm alignment, this distance evolves pathwise as a scalar queue, with target movement as arrival and common demand as service. This reduction to one-dimensional queue control resolves the state dependence and extends the guarantees to general convex capacity sets, beyond the reach of prior productwise approaches. Experiments on synthetic and real-world inventory data corroborate the theory.

</details>


### [135] [A Complexity Measure for Active Learning in Multi-group Mean Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14690)
*Abdellah Aznag,Rachel Cummings,Adam N. Elmachtoub*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper establishes the first general lower bound for max-risk active learning in multi-group bandits, decomposing difficulty into budget, heteroscedasticity, and a novel Variance Local Curvature (VLC) measure. A local minimax framework and representation-based instance construction reveal near-optimality of existing methods while identifying gaps in highly heterogeneous settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work on max-risk minimization in multi-group bandits lacked general lower bounds, leaving unclear whether existing algorithms are truly optimal. The authors aim to characterize the fundamental difficulty of this problem across any finite-variance hypothesis class.

Method: A local minimax framework is developed around a family of perturbed instances near a reference distribution. The proof introduces (1) a loss-induced ℓ₁ geometry on the decision space to track uncertainty propagation, and (2) a representation-based instance generator that reduces hard-instance construction to an explicit random matrix calculation. The bound features the Variance Local Curvature (VLC), which quantifies how local variance changes affect information within the hypothesis class.

Result: The lower bound factorizes into three orthogonal components: a budget term, a heteroscedasticity index, and the VLC. For smooth classes, VLC simplifies to a variance–Fisher information reparametrization with closed forms for common families. Benchmarking against the best known upper bound shows near-optimality up to logarithmic factors in broad regimes, while exposing a systematic gap in highly heterogeneous instances.

Conclusion: The work provides the first general characterization of max-risk difficulty in multi-group bandits, establishing near-tight optimality guarantees and highlighting regimes where current algorithms fall short. The VLC and the proof techniques (ℓ₁ geometry, representation-based construction) offer new tools for future lower bounds in adaptive sampling problems.

Abstract: We study a \emph{max-risk} objective for active learning in a multi-group mean estimation $d$-armed bandits: a learner adaptively allocates a budget of $T$ samples across $d$ groups to minimize the worst-case uncertainty index $\max_{k\in[d]}σ_k^2/n_k$, where $σ_k$ is the standard deviation of the distribution of arm $d$, and $n_k$ is the number of times arm $d$ is sampled. We develop a local minimax framework and prove the first general lower bound for this objective, valid for any finite-variance hypothesis class. The bound separates difficulty into three orthogonal factors: a \emph{budget} term, a \emph{heteroscedasticity} index measuring how unevenly the uncertainty is spread across arms, and a model-dependent complexity measure, the \emph{Variance Local Curvature} ($\mathrm{VLC}$), which captures how much information a local change of variance creates inside the hypothesis class. For smooth classes, the $\mathrm{VLC}$ is a reparametrization of a variance--Fisher information, with closed-form values for common families. Benchmarking against the strongest available upper bound shows near-optimality up to logarithmic factors in broad regimes, and pinpoints a systematic gap in highly heterogeneous instances. Our proof introduces two key ingredients: a loss-induced $\ell_1$ geometry on the decision space, and a representation-based instance generator that reduces hard-instance construction to an explicit random matrix calculation.

</details>


<div id='cs.SE'></div>

# cs.SE [[Back]](#toc)

### [136] [Do programming languages still matter to your AI coding agent teammate? Evidence at scale from chess engines](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13763)
*Mathieu Acher,Jean-Marc Jézéquel*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: A study examining whether frontier AI coding agents can build complete chess engines across 17 diverse programming languages, finding they can produce working engines in every language but with significant variation in performance, cost, and feature profiles depending on language choice.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Two empirical questions drive this work: (1) Can AI coding agents program in any target language, including those with no prior open-source artifacts of comparable scope? (2) Does programming language choice still shape the resulting artifact, and along which dimensions? The authors seek to understand whether language barriers have dissolved for AI teammates, or whether language remains a meaningful design constraint.

Method: A polyglot case study centered on chess engines — non-trivial multi-component systems with language-agnostic evaluation oracles (move-generation correctness, Elo strength scale). Two frontier agents (Claude Code and Codex) were prompted without chess-specific knowledge or implementation guidance, under a documented intervention and stopping policy. The agents produced 34 chess engines across 17 languages (mainstream, domain-specific, legacy, esoteric). Analysis combines per-engine feature analysis, independent Elo assessment, session trajectory examination, and qualitative code/transcript review.

Result: Every language yielded at least one feature-rich working engine, including languages with no prior open-source chess engine of comparable scope (e.g., LaTeX). Code was synthesized from scratch, not copied. However, language choice significantly mattered: strong playing strength only emerged in mainstream compiled languages; cost and engineering effort increased sharply for exotic languages; feature choices varied across language families. Agents self-validated unprompted but their strength self-estimates were biased; a few engines cheated by calling external chess libraries.

Conclusion: Programming language is no longer a binary barrier to whether AI teammates can build a working system — the answer is yes across the board. However, language choice still critically governs performance, cost, what gets built, and how much human supervision and validation remain necessary.

Abstract: Frontier coding agents now promise end-to-end authorship of complete software systems. Two empirical questions follow: can AI coding-agent teammates program in any target language, including ones with no comparable prior open-source artefact? If so, does language choice still shape the artefact, and along which dimensions? We study both through a polyglot case study built around chess engines: non-trivial multi-component systems that admit a hierarchy of language-agnostic oracles, from exact move-generation correctness to a strength scale (Elo), observable from Rust to Brainfuck. We prompted two frontier agents (Claude Code and Codex) at the capability level, without chess knowledge or implementation guidance, under a documented intervention and stopping policy. The agents produced 34 chess engines spanning 17 primary programming languages, from mainstream to specialised, domain-specific, legacy, and esoteric targets. We combine per-engine feature analysis, independent Elo assessment, and session trajectories with qualitative analysis of code and transcripts.
  Frontier coding agents are genuinely polyglot: every language we tried produced at least one feature-rich working engine, several with no prior open-source counterpart of comparable scope (e.g., LaTeX), and the code is synthesised from scratch rather than copied. Yet language choice still matters: strong playing strength is only reachable in mainstream compiled languages, cost and engineering effort grow sharply as the language becomes more exotic, and feature choices shift across language families. Agents validate their own work unprompted, but their strength self-estimates are biased and a few engines cheated by calling a chess library. Programming language is no longer about whether AI teammates can build a working system, but about performance, cost, what gets built, and how much human supervision validation still needs.

</details>


### [137] [A Benchmark and Framework for Evaluating Next Action Predictions in Spreadsheets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13802)
*Tejas Agrawal,Vu Le,Sumit Gulwani,Gust Verbruggen*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: Introduces a benchmark for spreadsheet action prediction (code completion analogue), featuring a manually curated dataset of 52 sequences with 12K actions and an online evaluation protocol. Baselines include LLMs, SLMs, and classical models, analyzed across multiple properties.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Auto-completion is prevalent in code editors but virtually absent in spreadsheets, despite spreadsheets being far more common. The gap exists because public spreadsheet corpora lack edit histories, and spreadsheet actions span a complex spatial-temporal-composite action space.

Method: (1) Dataset: 52 manually curated action sequences (~12K actions) recreating public spreadsheets, seeded by parametrized heuristics and refined via LLMs. (2) Online evaluation: stepwise prediction after each user action, with accept/reject feedback, updating future actions upon acceptance, iterating until the target spreadsheet is reached. Multiple baselines tested: zero-shot LLMs, fine-tuned small language models (SLMs), and classical models.

Result: The benchmark reveals insights across several dimensions: saved actions vs. false positives trade-offs, prediction efficiency, influence of user profiles, trigger effects, and context effects.

Conclusion: The proposed benchmark fills a critical gap in spreadsheet auto-completion research, providing a structured evaluation framework and actionable insights into model behavior, user modeling, and context utilization for future predictive systems in spreadsheets.

Abstract: Predictive code completion greatly accelerates how quickly developers work. In spreadsheets, despite being much more common, such auto-completion features are virtually non-existent. To address this gap, we introduce a benchmark for systems that observe a sequence of user actions in a spreadsheet and predict future actions. Two challenges are (1) the absence of edit histories in public spreadsheet corpora and (2) the complex space of spreadsheet actions (spatial, temporal, composite). To address (1), we manually curate 52 sequences of 12K actions that recreate spreadsheets from public corpora, seeded by parametrized heuristics and LLM refinement. To address (2), we propose an online evaluation that expects a prediction after each user action, accepts or rejects that prediction, updates the future actions upon acceptance, and repeats this until the target spreadsheet is obtained. We use multiple baseline predictors (including zero-shot LLMs, fine-tuned SLMs, and classical models) and analyze different properties that our benchmark teaches us, including but not limited to: properties of saved actions and false positives, efficiency, effect of user profiles, effect of triggers, and effect of context.

</details>


### [138] [An Empirical Study of Gemini 3 for Detecting Natural Language Test Smells in Manual Test Cases](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13804)
*Keila Lucas,Rohit Gheyi,Márcio Ribeiro,Fabio Palomba,Luana Martins,Elvys Soares*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper investigates using the LLM GEMINI-3-PRO-PREVIEW with a prompt-based, whole-test-case analysis strategy to detect test smells in manual test cases, comparing performance against SLMs (GEMMA-3-4B, LLAMA-3.2-3B, PHI-4-14B) on 100 Ubuntu test cases across seven smell types, finding superior performance and actionable explanations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Manual testing remains critical for uncovering issues that automation misses, but manual test cases frequently contain test smells (ambiguity, redundancy, missing checks) that degrade reliability, maintainability, and reproducibility. Existing detection approaches rely on manually engineered rules that fail to generalize and scale across heterogeneous test suites. Prior SLM-based work showed feasibility but limited performance, motivating exploration of whether a contemporary LLM can achieve better results through holistic test-case analysis.

Method: The authors employ a prompt-based, whole-test-case analysis strategy using GEMINI-3-PRO-PREVIEW, evaluating entire test cases rather than isolated steps to capture inter-step relationships and dependencies. Evaluation is conducted on 100 Ubuntu test cases spanning seven test smell types. Performance is compared against three previously evaluated SLMs: GEMMA-3-4B, LLAMA-3.2-3B, and PHI-4-14B.

Result: GEMINI-3-PRO-PREVIEW outperforms all three SLMs in test smell detection accuracy. The model also produces actionable explanations to help practitioners improve clarity and consistency. Additionally, the study reveals that test smells are pervasive, with nearly one detected smell per test step on average, underscoring the need for scalable automated quality support for manual testing artifacts.

Conclusion: Contemporary LLMs using whole-test-case analysis offer a scalable and effective approach for detecting test smells in manual test cases, surpassing SLM-based methods. The pervasiveness of test smells in practice highlights a pressing need for automated quality assurance tools targeting manual testing artifacts. The actionable explanations generated by the LLM further support practitioners in revising test cases.

Abstract: Manual testing, in which testers follow natural language instructions to validate system behavior, remains essential for uncovering issues that are difficult to capture with automation. However, manual test cases often contain test smells, quality issues such as ambiguity, redundancy, or missing checks that reduce reliability, maintainability, and reproducibility. Existing detection approaches largely depend on manually engineered rules and thus struggle to generalize and scale across heterogeneous test suites. In our previous work, we assessed the feasibility of using Small Language Models (SLMs) for test smell detection by evaluating GEMMA-3-4B, LLAMA-3.2-3B, and PHI-4-14B on test steps from 143 real-world Ubuntu test cases, covering seven smell types. PHI-4-14B achieved the best performance. In this article, we investigate whether a contemporary Large Language Model (GEMINI-3-PRO-PREVIEW) available at the time of the study can identify test smells in natural language manual test cases using a prompt-based, whole-test-case analysis strategy. Unlike approaches that analyze individual test steps in isolation, our approach evaluates complete test cases, enabling the model to consider relationships and dependencies among test steps.
