We evaluate the approach on 100 Ubuntu test cases covering seven test smell types and compare its performance against previously evaluated SLMs, including GEMMA-3-4B, LLAMA-3.2-3B, and PHI-4-14B. Our results show that GEMINI-3-PRO-PREVIEW outperforms the SLMs, while producing actionable explanations that can help practitioners revise manual test cases for greater clarity and consistency. We also find that test smells are pervasive in practice, with nearly one detected test smell per step on average, highlighting the need for scalable and automated quality support for manual testing artifacts.

</details>


### [139] [A Principled Framework for Safe Algorithm Updates in Automated Insulin Delivery Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13882)
*Thomas Screven,Ziqiang "Joe" Zhu,Deniz Cengiz,Rayhan A. Lal,Korey K. Hood,Samuel T. King*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: A framework is proposed to classify bugs in AID (Automated Insulin Delivery) algorithms and evaluate the clinical equivalence of software updates, using shadow execution and paired glucose error analysis. Applied to Trio's oref algorithm port, both implementations met clinical equivalence thresholds.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In co-adapted AID systems where users fine-tune settings around existing algorithmic behavior, software updates and bug fixes can paradoxically worsen glycemic control. No principled framework existed to assess whether AID algorithm updates are clinically safe.

Method: A two-part framework: (1) Bug classification into factual, heuristic, and computational categories, each with distinct management strategies, validated by porting Trio's oref algorithm from JavaScript to Swift; (2) Clinical equivalence assessment via error analysis on paired glucose values. Methods included shadow execution on 736,480 invocations from eight Trio users, mechanistic in silico simulation, and data-driven replay simulation, with Parkes Error Grid Zones A+B as the clinical equivalence threshold.

Result: Swift and JavaScript implementations produced nearly identical Time in Range (84.9% vs. 84.9%) and Glycemia Risk Index (23.5% vs. 23.9%). Over 99% of paired glucose fell in Parkes Error Grid Zones A+B. Shadow execution mismatch rates were low across oref components (iob 0.43%, autosens 1.22%, determineBasal 0.07%, meal 0.01%), with clinically meaningful differences in only 0.03% of iob invocations. Data-driven replay also met the clinical equivalence threshold.

Conclusion: The framework integrates bug-fixing principles with multi-method clinical evaluation and is system-agnostic, applicable to all widely used OS-AID systems. Case studies underscore the need for systematic remediation of factual and computational bugs.

Abstract: Background: AID algorithms require ongoing software updates and bug fixes. In co-adapted systems, where users tune settings around existing algorithmic behavior, bug fixes can paradoxically disrupt glycemic control. No principled framework evaluates the safety of AID algorithm updates.
  Methods: Our two-part framework classifies bugs and evaluates the clinical equivalence of AID system software updates. Bugs are classified as factual, heuristic, or computational, each with distinct management strategies. Classifications were validated from porting Trio's oref algorithm from Javascript to a bug-fixed Swift implementation. We compared implementations using shadow execution on 736,480 invocations from eight Trio users. The second component assesses clinical equivalence with error analysis on paired glucose values, applied to both Trio implementations using mechanistic in silico and data-driven replay simulation.
  Results: In mechanistic in silico simulation, the Swift and Javascript implementations produced nearly identical Time in Range (84.9% vs. 84.9%) and Glycemia Risk Index (23.5% vs. 23.9%), with more than 99% of paired glucose in Parkes Error Grid Zones A and B, meeting our clinical equivalence threshold. Shadow execution showed low mismatch rates in oref components (iob 0.43%, autosens 1.22%, determineBasal 0.07%, meal 0.01%), with clinically meaningful differences in 0.03% of iob invocations. Data-driven replay simulations of bugs revealed more than 99% of downstream paired glucose in Parkes Error Grid Zones A and B, also meeting our clinical equivalence threshold.
  Conclusions: Our framework integrates bug-fixing principles with multi-method clinical evaluation to assess AID algorithm update safety. It is system-agnostic and applicable to all widely used OS-AID systems, with case studies highlighting the need for systematic remediation of factual and computational bugs.

</details>


### [140] [Bayesian-Calibrated Detection of Hallucinated Package Imports in AI-Assisted Code](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13918)
*Lom M. Hillah,Jean-Marc Richard,Ryan Hasnaoui*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: A Bayesian calibration layer is proposed for detecting hallucinated package imports (slopsquatting) in LLM-generated code. Unlike binary detectors, it outputs Beta-posterior probabilities using a 3-category epistemic taxonomy and leverages PyPI metadata to catch suspicious but registered packages that binary detectors miss. Evaluated on 1,734 Python snippets across six models, the layer matches strict-registry detections while adding well-calibrated metadata-based flags.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing slopsquat detectors emit binary decisions (flag/no-flag), which lack nuanced risk quantification and fail to detect registered-but-suspicious packages — a realistic post-LLM-emission attacker regime where malicious packages are published to registries. The work addresses the need for a principled, probabilistic, risk-aware detection primitive consumable by downstream CI/CD gates.

Method: The authors introduce a Bayesian calibration layer that emits Beta-posterior probabilities per detection, grounded in a 3-category epistemic taxonomy: empirically calibrated, constructively argued, and engineering-judgement-traced priors. Beyond the standard 200/404 registry channel, the layer exploits PyPI metadata signals (package age, release count, author descriptor, summary) to surface registered-but-suspicious packages. Calibration is assessed via flagged-subset Expected Calibration Error (ECE), full-corpus Brier score, and detector asymmetry via McNemar's paired test.

Result: Evaluated on a merged corpus of 1,734 Python snippets (189-prompt BigCodeBench slice + 100-prompt niche-library stress-test set) across six models (four cloud, two local open-weight), the calibrated layer reproduces all strict-registry detections of a re-implemented binary baseline (inspired by Mahmud et al.) while introducing well-calibrated additional flags via the metadata channel. The baseline shares the registry oracle with ground truth, making it a degenerate upper bound rather than a genuine competitor.

Conclusion: The Bayesian calibration layer successfully augments binary slopsquat detection with principled probabilistic outputs and metadata-driven detection of registered-but-suspicious packages. It enables risk-aware threshold decisions suitable for downstream CI gates, advancing beyond binary heuristics toward calibrated, epistemic-aware detection in LLM-generated code supply chains.

Abstract: We present a Bayesian calibration layer for slopsquat detectors -- those that flag hallucinated package imports in code produced by large language models (LLMs). Where existing pipelines emit binary decisions (flag / do-not-flag), our layer emits a Beta-posterior probability per detection, derived from a 3-category epistemic taxonomy that explicitly classifies each prior as empirically calibrated, constructively argued, or engineering-judgement-traced. Beyond the primary 200/404 registry channel, the calibrated layer exploits PyPI metadata signals -- package age, release count, author descriptor, summary -- to surface registered-but-suspicious packages that a binary registry detector misses, which is the realistic post-LLM-emission attacker regime. The resulting risk-aware primitive is directly consumable by downstream CI gates and supports principled threshold decisions across detection rules. We evaluate the calibration on a merged corpus of 1,734 Python snippets -- a stratified 189-prompt BigCodeBench slice plus a 100-prompt niche-library stress-test set, generated across a six-model panel spanning four cloud models (Claude-Sonnet-4.6, Mistral-Large, DeepSeek-v4-pro, DeepSeek-R1) and two local open-weight code models (Mistral Codestral, Meta CodeLlama). Against a re-implemented binary baseline inspired by Mahmud et al. -- which shares its registry oracle with our ground truth and therefore serves as a degenerate upper bound rather than a genuine competitor -- the calibrated layer reproduces the strict-registry detections and introduces well-calibrated additional flags on the metadata channel. We assess detector asymmetry with a McNemar paired test and calibration with both a flagged-subset Expected Calibration Error and a strictly proper full-corpus Brier score.

</details>


### [141] [LLM Agents Can See Code Repositories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14061)
*Dongjian Ma,Silin Chen,Yufei Yang,Yulin Shi,Yanfu yan,Xiaodong Gu*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: The paper presents the first systematic study on whether visual repository representations (e.g., folder hierarchies, dependency graphs) improve LLM-based coding agents for issue resolution. A vision-only approach hurts accuracy and increases token cost, but hybrid text+vision setups reduce token consumption by up to 26% while maintaining or improving accuracy, particularly aiding fault localization and autonomous exploration.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Human developers rely heavily on visual structures (folder hierarchies, dependency graphs) to navigate large codebases, yet most LLM-based coding agents consume repositories purely as text. With the emergence of multimodal LLMs (MLLMs), the authors investigate whether visual representations of repository structure can improve agent performance — a question that had not been systematically studied before.

Method: The authors conduct a systematic empirical study evaluating four recent multimodal LLMs on repository-level issue resolution. They compare three setups: (1) text-only baseline, (2) strictly vision-only input, and (3) a hybrid approach that supplements standard text interfaces with visual graphs of repository structure. Metrics include issue-resolution accuracy and token consumption. They further analyze when visualization helps most (fault localization, exploration depth control).

Result: A strictly vision-only setup degrades accuracy and increases token cost because agents lack symbolic detail and compensate with repeated visual queries. In contrast, the hybrid text-and-vision approach reduces input token consumption by up to 26% while maintaining or improving issue-resolution accuracy. Visualization proves most beneficial during fault localization and when the agent autonomously controls exploration depth.

Conclusion: The findings support a practical hybrid text-and-vision design paradigm for next-generation coding agents, where visual representations serve as a supplementary modality rather than a replacement for textual code input. This mirrors how human developers use both symbolic code and visual structure when navigating repositories.

Abstract: Coding agents powered by large language models have demonstrated strong performance on software engineering tasks. Yet most agents consume repositories almost entirely as text, which differs from how human developers use visual structure such as folder hierarchies and dependency relationships to orient themselves in large codebases. With multimodal large language models (MLLMs), it is an open question whether agents can effectively benefit from visual representations of repositories. This paper presents the first systematic empirical study of visual repository representations for LLM-based agents on repository-level issue resolution. We evaluate four recent multimodal models. Our results show that a strictly vision-only setup degrades accuracy and increases token cost, because agents lack sufficient symbolic detail and compensate with repeated visual queries. In contrast, integrating visual graphs of repository structure as a supplementary modality alongside standard text interfaces helps agents understand structure more efficiently: input token consumption decreases by up to 26% while issue-resolution accuracy is maintained or improved. Visualization is most useful during fault localization and when the agent autonomously controls exploration depth. These findings point to a practical hybrid text-and-vision design for next-generation coding agents.

</details>


### [142] [FastContext: Training Efficient Repository Explorer for Coding Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14066)
*Shaoqiu Zhang,Maoquan Wang,Yuling Shi,Yuhang Wang,Xiaodong Gu,Yongqiang Yao,Rao Fu,Shengyu Fu*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: FastContext introduces a dedicated exploration subagent powered by specialized 4B–30B models that separates repository exploration from task solving in LLM coding agents. It issues parallel tool calls and returns concise file paths and line ranges, improving SWE-bench resolution rates up to 5.5% while reducing token consumption up to 60%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Repository exploration is a major bottleneck in LLM coding agents: locating relevant code consumes substantial token budgets and pollutes the solver's context with irrelevant snippets. Current agents use the same model for both exploration and solving, causing exploratory reads to persist in context history. A dedicated, efficient exploration mechanism is needed.

Method: FastContext is a dedicated exploration subagent invoked on demand. It issues parallel tool calls and returns focused context as file paths and line ranges. The underlying specialized exploration models (4B–30B parameters) are bootstrapped from strong reference-model trajectories and fine-tuned with task-grounded rewards targeting three objectives: broad first-turn search, multi-turn evidence gathering, and precise citation generation. The system is evaluated by integrating into Mini-SWE-Agent across three benchmarks.

Result: Across SWE-bench Multilingual, SWE-bench Pro, and SWE-QA, integrating FastContext into Mini-SWE-Agent improved end-to-end resolution rates by up to 5.5% (absolute). Coding-agent token consumption was reduced by up to 60%, with only marginal computational overhead.

Conclusion: Repository exploration can be effectively decoupled from task solving and handled by specialized models. The FastContext architecture demonstrates that a dedicated exploration subagent with targeted training yields both higher accuracy and significantly lower token usage in LLM-based coding agents.

Abstract: Large Language Model (LLM) coding agents have achieved strong results on software engineering tasks, yet repository exploration remains a major bottleneck: locating relevant code consumes substantial token budget and pollutes the agent's context with irrelevant snippets. In most agents, the same model explores the repository and solves the task, leaving exploratory reads and searches in the solver's history. We present FastContext, a dedicated exploration subagent that separates repository exploration from solving. Invoked on demand, FastContext issues parallel tool calls and returns concise file paths and line ranges as focused context. FastContext is powered by specialized exploration models spanning 4B--30B parameters. We bootstrap them from strong reference-model trajectories and refine them with task-grounded rewards for broad first-turn search, multi-turn evidence gathering, and precise citation generation. Across SWE-bench Multilingual, SWE-bench Pro, and SWE-QA, integrating FastContext into Mini-SWE-Agent improves end-to-end resolution rates up to 5.5\% while reducing coding-agent token consumption up to 60\%, with marginal overhead. These results show that repository exploration can be separated from solving and handled effectively by specialized models. Code and data: https://github.com/microsoft/fastcontext

</details>


### [143] [Simulating Students' Java Programming Errors with Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14113)
*Ali Keramati,Jie Cao,Iman Mohammadi,Mark Warschauer,Yang Shi*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper investigates whether LLMs can simulate realistic student programming errors, evaluating five models across three prompting strategies on a large Java dataset. Results show LLMs generate diverse errors indistinguishable from real student mistakes in blind evaluation, but alignment varies by model and problem difficulty, with Claude Sonnet 4 achieving the best balance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Obtaining representative student error sets for newly designed programming tasks is slow and costly, requiring extensive classroom deployment. The authors explore whether LLMs can serve as scalable proxies to accelerate this process by generating realistic logical errors synthetically.

Method: Using the CodeWorkout dataset (74,000+ unique student Java submissions across 37 problems), five LLMs were evaluated under three prompting strategies: Input-Output (IO), Chain-of-Thought (CoT), and iterative Self-Refine. Performance was assessed on diversity (range of distinct error patterns) and alignment (fidelity to authentic student mistakes), with analysis stratified by problem struggling level. A blinded expert annotation study (N=401) qualitatively compared synthetic vs. authentic errors.

Result: All models generated diverse errors, but alignment to human submissions varied significantly. Claude Sonnet 4 achieved the most balanced diversity-alignment trade-off. The blinded expert study confirmed synthetic errors are functionally indistinguishable from authentic ones. Higher-struggling-level problems elicited more diverse but less student-like errors.

Conclusion: LLMs can serve as viable proxies for simulating student programming errors, but there are trade-offs between diversity and alignment that depend on model choice, prompting strategy, and problem difficulty. The findings inform the design of teachable agents, intelligent tutoring systems, and learning analytics that integrate synthetic errors.

Abstract: Understanding student errors in the programming is a cornerstone of programming education, yet obtaining a representative set of student errors for any newly designed task remains slow and costly, since authentic submissions only accumulate after extensive classroom deployment. This paper explores whether large language models (LLMs) can serve as scalable proxies for students by simulating realistic logical errors in code submissions. Using the CodeWorkout dataset of 74,000+ unique student Java submissions across 37 problems, we evaluate five LLMs under three mainstream prompting strategies: Input-Output (IO), Chain-of-Thought (CoT), and iterative Self-Refine. We assess performance along two key dimensions: diversity (the range of distinct error patterns) and alignment (alignment with authentic student mistakes), and examine how these vary by struggling level of programming tasks. Our quantitative findings reveal that while all models generate diverse errors, their alignment to human submissions diverges: Claude Sonnet 4 achieves the most balanced performance. In addition, we conducted a blinded expert annotation study (N = 401) comparing synthetic and authentic errors. This qualitative analysis confirms that the generated errors are functionally indistinguishable from authentic student errors. Moreover, higher-struggling-level problems elicit more diverse but less student-like errors. These results highlight trade-offs in using LLMs to simulate human learners and suggest design considerations for integrating synthetic errors into teachable agents, intelligent tutoring systems, and large-scale learning analytics.

</details>


### [144] [Investigating Metamorphic Fuzz Oracle Enhancement via Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14164)
*Ruixiang Qian,Ding Yang,Zengxu Chen,Yuxuan Gao,Chunrong Fang,Chao Zhang,Zhenyu Chen*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper introduces MetaFOE, an LLM-based framework that automatically generates metamorphic-based oracles to enhance existing fuzz drivers, demonstrating significant improvements in edge coverage (18.7%) and unique crash discovery (1,528) on OSS-Fuzz benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing greybox fuzzing relies on crash-based oracles, which overlook library functionality bugs that don't cause crashes. Metamorphic-based oracles could fill this gap, but constructing them requires substantial domain knowledge, making automation highly challenging. The paper addresses this gap by exploring whether LLMs can automate metamorphic-based fuzz oracle enhancement (MFOE).

Method: The authors propose MetaFOE, an LLM-based framework that: (1) extracts target function signatures and semantics from fuzz drivers, (2) uses LLMs with five prompt strategies to generate metamorphic relations (MRs), (3) automatically synthesizes these MRs into compilable "meta drivers" that integrate with existing OSS-Fuzz drivers, and (4) validates the generated oracles through compilation and runtime checks before fuzzing.

Result: Evaluated on OSS-Fuzz with three LLMs (GPT-4, Claude, Gemini): generated 3,475 MRs with 77.3% applicability rate; implemented 12,351 meta drivers with 6,228 valid (50.4%). Valid meta drivers improved edge coverage by 18.7% on average after three hours of fuzzing and triggered 1,528 unique crashes not found by baseline crash-only oracles.

Conclusion: Metamorphic-based oracle enhancement is both effective for improving fuzzing outcomes and feasible to automate using LLMs. The approach uncovers functional bugs missed by crash-only oracles and provides a practical path toward more comprehensive library fuzzing.

Abstract: Fuzz drivers are essential components of greybox fuzzing, as they encapsulate target interfaces, define test spaces, and largely determine fuzzing effectiveness. Existing fuzz drivers typically rely on crash-based oracles for security testing, overlooking library functionality and limiting bug detection capability.
  In this paper, we present the first study on metamorphic-based fuzz oracle enhancement (MFOE), which augments existing fuzz drivers with metamorphic-based oracles derived from metamorphic relations (MRs). Since constructing and integrating such oracles requires substantial domain knowledge, automating MFOE is challenging. To address this challenge, we propose MetaFOE, an LLM-based framework that automatically generates and integrates metamorphic-based oracles.
  We evaluate MetaFOE on OSS-Fuzz drivers using three modern LLMs and five prompt strategies. MetaFOE generates 3,475 MRs, of which 77.3% are applicable, and implements 12,351 meta drivers, with 6,228 being valid. After three hours of fuzzing, the valid meta drivers improve edge coverage by an average of 18.7% and trigger 1,528 unique crashes. Our results demonstrate both the effectiveness of metamorphic-based oracle enhancement and the feasibility of using LLMs to automate MFOE, providing valuable insights for advancing greybox fuzzing.

</details>


### [145] [Evaluating LLMs for Obfuscation Detection and Classification in Android Apps](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14233)
*Luca Ferrari,Marco Alecci,Jordan Samhi,Tegawende' F. Bissyande',Jacques Klein,Mariano Ceccato,Luca Verderame*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper presents a large-scale empirical study evaluating whether Large Language Models (LLMs) can detect obfuscation in Android apps through semantic reasoning, without relying on handcrafted rules, predefined signatures, or dedicated training. The study uses both a controlled benchmark and real-world Google Play datasets, examining prompt design, model selection, and decision thresholds across open-weight and proprietary LLMs, and compares LLM-based reasoning with existing SAST-based detection approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Android developers increasingly use code obfuscation to protect intellectual property, but this hinders static analysis and vulnerability detection. Existing obfuscation detection methods rely on handcrafted heuristics, engineered features, or task-specific pipelines that struggle to generalize across evolving obfuscation strategies. The authors aim to explore whether LLMs can overcome these limitations through semantic reasoning.

Method: A large-scale empirical study evaluating off-the-shelf LLMs (both open-weight and proprietary) for Android obfuscation detection. Evaluation uses: (1) a controlled benchmark with an app obfuscated using multiple techniques, and (2) a real-world dataset of Google Play apps. The study systematically varies prompt design, model selection, and decision thresholds, and compares LLM-based reasoning against existing SAST-based obfuscation detection approaches.

Result: Not explicitly stated in the abstract; the paper reports findings on the impact of prompt design, model selection, and decision thresholds, along with comparative analysis against SAST-based approaches and discussion of broader implications and limitations.

Conclusion: Not explicitly stated in the abstract; the paper discusses broader implications and limitations of applying LLMs to Android security analysis, particularly for obfuscation detection, and provides insights on the viability of LLM-based semantic reasoning as an alternative or complement to traditional approaches.

Abstract: Android applications (apps) developers increasingly rely on code obfuscation techniques to hinder reverse engineering and protect intellectual property. However, obfuscation also reduces the effectiveness of static analysis and vulnerability detection tools, creating challenges for Android security analysis. Existing approaches for detecting obfuscation in Android apps predominantly rely on handcrafted heuristics, engineered features, or task-specific learning pipelines, which may struggle to generalize across evolving obfuscation strategies.
  This paper presents a large-scale empirical study investigating the capability of Large Language Models (LLMs) to detect obfuscation in Android apps through semantic reasoning. Our study evaluates whether off-the-shelf LLMs can identify obfuscated code without relying on handcrafted rules, predefined signatures, or dedicated model training. The empirical evaluation is conducted on both a controlled benchmark containing an app obfuscated with multiple techniques and a real-world dataset of Android apps collected from Google Play. The study further examines the impact of prompt design, model selection, and decision thresholds across several open-weight and proprietary LLMs. Finally, the analysis compares LLM-based reasoning with existing SAST-based obfuscation-detection approaches and discusses the broader implications and limitations of applying LLMs to Android security analysis.

</details>


### [146] [I'm Sorry Driver, I'm Afraid I Can't Do That: Appraising the Safety of LLMs within Automotive Contexts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14327)
*Shaun Feakins,Ibrahim Habli,Kim Littler,Robert Palin*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: The paper examines AI frameworks integrating LLMs into automotive control tasks through a safety assurance lens. It identifies dual challenges: assuring upstream-developed general-purpose LLMs in downstream vehicle architectures, and reconciling engineering constraints (ISO21448) with LLM-specific issues like alignment (ISO/PAS8800). Using a Talk2Drive case study, the authors expose limitations via a safety argument and propose future assurance mechanisms for LLM-related hazards.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs are being rapidly integrated into automotive settings, yet their deployment in real-time safety-critical control tasks lacks rigorous safety assurance frameworks. The authors aim to systematically appraise existing integration approaches, identify gaps between current standards and LLM-specific failure modes, and motivate the development of robust assurance mechanisms before operational deployment outpaces safety considerations.

Method: The paper conducts a two-pronged analysis: (1) conceptual analysis of the upstream-downstream assurance gap — where general-purpose LLMs from large AI labs must be assured within specific vehicle architectures; (2) standards-based analysis mapping engineering constraints (e.g., latency per ISO21448/SOTIF) and LLM-specific issues (e.g., alignment per ISO/PAS8800) onto concrete challenges. This is grounded in an experimental case study using the open-source Talk2Drive repository, culminating in a structured safety argument (likely GSN/CAE-style) to make limitations explicit. The authors then synthesize potential assurance mechanisms.

Result: Current frameworks face significant challenges that limit efficacy in real-time safety-critical contexts. The dual upstream-downstream assurance problem creates a fundamental accountability and technical gap. ISO21448-relevant constraints (latency, etc.) and ISO/PAS8800-relevant issues (alignment failures) remain unresolved in existing solutions. The Talk2Drive case study empirically demonstrates these limitations, and the safety argument formalizes them, showing that no current integration approach provides sufficient safety assurance for operational deployment.

Conclusion: While LLM integration into automotive control is proceeding at both technical and operational levels, existing safety assurance frameworks are inadequate. The authors propose future assurance mechanisms targeting LLM-specific hazardous events, acknowledging that bridging the upstream-downstream gap and reconciling traditional functional safety with novel AI-specific standards (ISO/PAS8800) are critical prerequisites for safe deployment.

Abstract: This paper appraises recent frameworks within AI development to integrate LLMs into control tasks in automotive contexts from the perspective of safety assurance. This work has built upon the rapid integration of LLMs across automotive settings. However, we find that at present, these frameworks face significant challenges, limiting their efficacy in real-time safety-critical contexts. Firstly, we consider conceptual challenges, including the fact that deployers are faced with a dual challenge, wherein they must assure a model which has been developed upstream, i.e. as general-purpose tools by the large AI labs, in a downstream context, i.e. into specific vehicle architectures. Secondly, we consider concrete challenges from across existing standards. We show that there are currently both fundamental engineering constraints covered in ISO21448, such as latency, and novel LLM-specific issues, such as alignment-related issues covered in ISO/PAS8800. We ground both examples in a concrete introductory, experimental case study exploring an existing open-source repository, Talk2Drive. We present a safety argument in order to make explicit the limitations of existing solutions. Nonetheless, given that the use of LLMs in automotive contexts is being explored at a technical level and operationalised, we propose potential assurance mechanisms for LLM-related hazardous events going forward.

</details>


### [147] [No Accidental Software Agent First Canonical Code for Human Code Entropy Reduction and 30 to 500 times Lower Frontier Model Requirements](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14357)
*Jepson Taylor*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: Proposes "agent-first canonical code" — a proof-carrying substrate that rewrites software into canonical behavior profiles with explicit proof obligations, aiming for ~100-fold reduction in verified-change cost by quotienting software by behavior equivalence under a declared oracle.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current frontier coding models waste capacity learning accidental entropy (framework churn, naming drift, CI dialects, etc.) rather than true program behavior. Human repositories mix valuable signals (tests, incidents, edge cases) with non-essential artifacts, inflating the cost of verifiably correct software changes.

Method: Introduces agent-first canonical code with typed change algebra, proof lanes, constrained edit grammars, semantic patch cells, runtime negative memory, and proof-carrying change objects. Core mechanism: quotient software by behavior equivalence under a declared oracle, collapsing equivalent encodings into governed representatives. Preliminary validation via QLoRA on Qwen2.5-Coder-14B with 64,088 canonical trajectories.

Result: QLoRA experiments show 64,088 canonical trajectories are learnable and suppress tested forbidden-language markers. Reported reduction bands are hypotheses only. The proposed "No-Accident Horizon" targets ~100-fold all-in cost reduction for supported routine-product distributions — explicitly not a guarantee for all software.

Conclusion: The contribution is a falsifiable program centered on minimum functional description length and verified-change cost. Behavior preservation, scaling economics, and verified-change cost metrics remain unestablished empirically; the paper frames these as testable hypotheses rather than demonstrated results.

Abstract: Frontier coding models may spend substantial capacity learning not only program behavior, but also accidental entropy in human repositories. Such repositories contain valuable signals: tests, incidents, migrations, edge cases, product judgment, and operational history. These signals are entangled with framework churn, naming drift, generated-source ambiguity, dependency rituals, CI dialects, weak proof routes, and human-oriented review customs. We propose agent-first canonical code, a proof-carrying substrate that rewrites routine product software into canonical behavior profiles, typed change algebra, proof lanes, constrained edit grammars, semantic patch cells, runtime negative memory, and proof-carrying change objects.
  The core hypothesis is that quotienting software by behavior equivalence under a declared oracle can collapse equivalent encodings into governed representatives with explicit evidence and proof obligations. The endpoint is amortized cost per verified correct change, including source, context, reasoning, tools, verification, security, provenance, review, failed loops, defects, and foundry cost under a common oracle. Reported reduction bands are hypotheses, not measured frontier results. The proposed limit is a No-Accident Horizon: removable accident decreases until residual novelty, evidence, governance, risk, and future optionality dominate. For supported routine-product distributions, this gives a defensible planning target near 100-fold all-in cost reduction, not a guarantee for all software. Preliminary QLoRA experiments on Qwen2.5-Coder-14B show that 64,088 canonical trajectories are learnable and suppress tested forbidden-language markers, but do not establish behavior preservation, scaling economics, or verified-change cost. The contribution is a falsifiable program centered on minimum functional description length and verified-change cost.

</details>


### [148] [tap: A File-Based Protocol for Heterogeneous LLM Agent Collaboration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14445)
*Minseo Kim*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: tap is a file-based collaboration protocol enabling LLM agents from different vendors (Claude/Anthropic and Codex/OpenAI) to collaborate on a shared codebase without shared memory or identical runtime, using markdown file-based messaging with real-time notifications and isolated git worktrees. A 27-day self-applied operation showed heterogeneous model pairs had higher defect detection rates (69.8%) than homogeneous pairs (53.1%).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing multi-agent software development systems assume a common runtime, central conversation server, or shared API family, preventing LLM agents from different vendors from directly exchanging messages from their own execution environments while collaborating on shared codebases. The need is for a protocol that enables cross-vendor, heterogeneous agent collaboration without shared infrastructure.

Method: tap employs a file-first design with three key components: (1) markdown files with metadata that serve as the canonical message format; (2) a two-tier communication system combining file inspection (Tier 1) with real-time notification paths for Claude and Codex (Tier 2); and (3) isolated git worktrees for separating agent workspaces. Message files persist even when real-time notification fails or a receiver restarts, ensuring durability. The protocol was self-applied over 27 days and 37 generations to develop and review its own codebase.

Result: Over a 27-day period with 37 generations of self-application, 209 tap-related pull requests and 717 operational artifacts were collected. Analysis of 375 review artifacts revealed that heterogeneous model pairs recorded at least one defect or requested change in 69.8% of reviews, compared to 53.1% for homogeneous model pairs.

Conclusion: The tap protocol successfully demonstrates that combining file-based message preservation with real-time notification enables cross-vendor LLM agent collaboration in a real production repository. Heterogeneous model pairings yield broader review perspectives with higher defect detection rates than homogeneous pairings. tap is released as open-source (@hua-labs/tap, v0.5.2).

Abstract: Existing multi-agent software development systems have proposed many forms of agent collaboration, including role-based collaboration and automated code review. However, many systems assume a common runtime, a central conversation server, or the same API family. Under these assumptions, LLM agents from different vendors cannot easily exchange messages directly from their own execution environments while dividing development and review work on a shared codebase. This paper presents tap, a file-based collaboration protocol that allows Claude (Anthropic) and Codex (OpenAI) to collaborate on one codebase without shared memory or an identical runtime. The core of tap is a file-first design that preserves markdown files with metadata as original messages, combines a file inspection path (file communication, Tier 1) with real-time notification paths for Claude and Codex (real-time communication, Tier 2), and isolates work through separate git worktrees. Even if real-time notification fails or a receiver restarts, the message file remains available and the same content can be inspected again. In a 27-day, 37-generation self-applied operation where tap was used to develop and review itself, we collected 209 tap-related pull requests and 717 operational artifacts. An analysis of 375 review artifacts showed that the share of reviews recording at least one defect or requested change was 69.8% for heterogeneous model pairs and 53.1% for homogeneous model pairs. These results show that tap, which combines file-based message preservation with real-time notification, operates in a real production repository, and that combining heterogeneous models and execution environments can broaden review perspectives. tap is distributed as the open-source npm package @hua-labs/tap (v0.5.2).

</details>


### [149] [Security Threats and Their Impact on Blockchain Interoperability: Identification and Countermeasures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14554)
*Shawn M. Reynolds,Hassan Reza*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: A systematic taxonomy of security threats in blockchain interoperability, categorizing attacks into five areas (core blockchain, network, interoperability-specific, social engineering, and code vulnerabilities) and proposing corresponding countermeasures for each identified threat.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Blockchain interoperability lacks comprehensive security mechanisms, a weakness that has already led to hundreds of millions in asset losses. There is no systematic classification of these threats, hindering the design of secure cross-chain solutions.

Method: The authors conduct a systematic identification and classification of security threats targeting interoperable blockchain systems. Threats are organized into a structured taxonomy of five categories, with each threat's attack surface analyzed and paired with effective defensive strategies.

Result: A five-category threat taxonomy: (1) core blockchain attacks, (2) network attacks, (3) interoperability-specific attacks, (4) social engineering, and (5) code vulnerabilities (with emphasis on smart contract weaknesses). Each threat entry includes attack surface analysis and proposed countermeasures.

Conclusion: The resulting taxonomy offers a structured foundation for both designing new secure interoperability solutions and evaluating the security posture of existing cross-chain systems.

Abstract: Blockchain interoperability enables independent blockchain systems to communicate and exchange assets across heterogeneous networks. However, the lack of comprehensive security mechanisms remains a critical weakness -- one that attackers have already exploited to cause hundreds of millions of dollars in asset losses. This paper presents a systematic identification and classification of security threats facing interoperable blockchain systems, along with corresponding countermeasures for each. We organize threats into five categories: (1) core blockchain attacks, (2) network attacks, (3) interoperability-specific attacks, (4) social engineering, and (5) code vulnerabilities, with particular attention to smart contract weaknesses. For each identified threat, we analyze its attack surface and propose effective defensive strategies. The resulting taxonomy provides a structured foundation for designing and evaluating secure blockchain interoperability solutions.

</details>


### [150] [When Errors Become Narratives: A Longitudinal Taxonomy of Silent Failures in a Production LLM Agent Runtime](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14589)
*Wei Wu*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: An 8-week longitudinal study of a production LLM agent system identified 22 incidents with 28+ silent failures. The authors propose a five-class taxonomy of silent failure mechanisms (A through E), introduce the concept of "fail-plausible" (Class D) where LLMs transform errors into convincing but false narratives, and find that ~70% of silent failures were caught only by human observation, not automated tests or audits.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM agent systems are increasingly deployed as long-lived autonomous runtimes, yet their failure modes — particularly silent failures where no actionable error signal reaches humans — are poorly understood. The authors aim to systematically characterize, taxonomize, and derive defenses against these failures through empirical study of a real production system.

Method: Longitudinal case study over 8 weeks of a production personal-assistant agent runtime (continuous operation since March 2026) with ~40 scheduled jobs, 8 LLM providers, a tool-governance proxy, and a knowledge-base memory plane, defended by 4,286 unit tests and 827 governance checks. All 22 incidents underwent full root-cause postmortem analysis. The authors also performed retrospective audits on 15 incidents to assess ex-ante prevention vs. regression blocking capability.

Result: (1) A five-class, mechanism-oriented taxonomy: (A) environment/platform quirks, (B) design-assumption mismatches, (C) error swallowing/dilution, (D) chained hallucination/fabrication, (E) operational omission/forensic blind spots. (2) Class D ("fail-plausible") is unique to LLM systems: the LLM transforms errors into fluent, plausible narrative — the observer is convincingly lied to. (3) ~70% of silent failures caught by human user-view observation, not tests/audits. (4) Retrospective audit: 0% ex-ante prevention, 87% regression blocking — audits function as regression engines, not prediction engines. (5) Incident latency (13 hours to 60 days) tracks failure mechanism, not code complexity; longest-lived failures resided in seams between components.

Conclusion: LLM agent systems exhibit unique and dangerous silent failure modes, particularly fail-plausible failures where errors are masked by fluent but fabricated LLM output. Current testing and governance regimes are strong at regression but weak at prediction. The authors provide a defense framework and design principles for making agent failures loud, attributable, and boring.

Abstract: LLM agent systems increasingly run as long-lived autonomous runtimes: scheduling jobs, calling tools, maintaining memory, and pushing results to humans. We present a longitudinal study of silent failures in one such system: a personal-assistant agent runtime in continuous production since March 2026, with roughly 40 scheduled jobs, 8 LLM providers, a tool-governance proxy, and a knowledge-base memory plane, defended by 4,286 unit tests and 827 governance checks. Over eight weeks we documented 22 incidents with full root-cause postmortems, in which one meta-pattern -- a failure whose error signal never reaches a human in actionable form -- manifested at least 28 times. We derive a five-class, mechanism-oriented taxonomy: (A) environment and platform quirks, (B) design-assumption mismatches, (C) error swallowing and dilution, (D) chained hallucination and fabrication, (E) operational omission and forensic blind spots. Class D is unique to LLM systems and the most dangerous: the system does not merely fail to report an error -- the LLM transforms it into fluent, plausible narrative delivered to the user. We term this fail-plausible: gray failure's differential observability escalated -- the observer is not just blind, it is convincingly lied to by the failure itself. Three findings: about 70% of silent failures were caught by human user-view observation, not tests or audits; a retrospective audit of 15 incidents found 0% ex-ante prevention but 87% regression blocking -- audits are regression engines, not prediction engines; incident latency (13 hours to 60 days) tracks failure mechanism, not code complexity -- the longest-lived failures lived in the seams between components, where no test runs. We describe the resulting defense framework and distill design principles for agent systems whose failures are loud, attributable, and boring. All postmortems and artifacts are public.

</details>


### [151] [Regulating the Machine Contributor: Governance and Policy Alignment in Open Source](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14594)
*Jassem Manita,Aziz Amari*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper examines how autonomous/semi-autonomous AI coding agents disrupt open-source contribution norms built around human accountability. Through comparative policy analysis across six organizations (SymPy, LLVM, matplotlib, OpenInfra, Apache SF, Linux Foundation), the authors derive a six-dimensional taxonomy, a Policy Maturity Score, and trace actual AI-caused incidents. They then map policy gaps against emerging AI governance frameworks (EU AI Act, NIST AI RMF, ISO/IEC standards), finding neither side closes the gap, and propose a harmonized tiered framework.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Open-source software governance assumes human contributors who can attest to provenance, sign agreements, and answer reviewer questions. The rapid rise of AI coding agents—capable of planning changes, editing files, and submitting PRs autonomously—breaks these assumptions. Documented agent-driven incidents, nuisance AI-generated contributions, and platform shutdowns during 2025–2026 demonstrate operational consequences, yet organizational responses remain fragmented and their alignment with emerging AI governance frameworks is unmapped.

Method: The study employs Most-Similar Systems Design (MSSD) across six open-source organizations. It uses indicator-based coding to extract policy dimensions and process tracing (for SymPy and LLVM) to validate causal links between policy gaps and documented agent incidents. The derived taxonomy is then systematically mapped against four governance frameworks: the EU AI Act, NIST AI RMF with the UC Berkeley Agentic AI Profile, ISO/IEC 42001, and ISO/IEC 23894.

Result: The analysis yields: (1) a six-dimensional taxonomy of contribution governance (disclosure, responsibility, human oversight, licensing, enforcement, maintainer workload); (2) an ordinal Policy Maturity Score across organizations; (3) a mapping of real agent incidents onto dimensions each policy fails to govern; and (4) identification of overlapping regulatory/policy gaps that neither existing contribution policies nor AI governance frameworks currently address.

Conclusion: Neither open-source contribution policies nor emerging AI governance frameworks independently close the accountability gap introduced by AI coding agents. The authors propose a harmonized tiered framework addressing all six dimensions and call for empirical evaluation to calibrate it, aiming to bridge the disconnect between agent-driven software development and the human-centered governance of open-source projects.

Abstract: AI-assisted software development has moved from line-level autocomplete to agents that can plan changes, edit files, and submit pull requests with limited human supervision. Open-source software, however, evolves through a process designed for humans: contributor agreements, codes of conduct, and review norms all assume a legally accountable person who can attest to provenance and answer reviewer questions. Autonomous and semi-autonomous AI contributors strain those assumptions, and the 2025-2026 record of agent-driven incidents, AI-generated nuisance volume, and platform-level shutdowns shows that the gap is operationally consequential. Several open-source organisations have responded with contribution policies, but the result is fragmented, and its alignment with emerging AI governance frameworks (EU AI Act, NIST AI RMF with the UC Berkeley Agentic AI Profile, ISO/IEC 42001 and 23894) is unmapped at the contribution level. We compare policies across six organisations (SymPy, LLVM, matplotlib, OpenInfra, the Apache Software Foundation, and the Linux Foundation) using Most-Similar Systems Design with indicator-based coding and process tracing for SymPy and LLVM. From this we derive a six-dimensional taxonomy (disclosure, responsibility, human oversight, licensing, enforcement, maintainer workload), an ordinal Policy Maturity Score, and a mapping of documented agent incidents onto the dimensions each policy fails to govern. Aligning the dimensions with the regulatory frameworks above identifies overlapping gaps neither side currently closes, and we close by sketching the shape of a harmonised tiered framework and the empirical evaluation needed to calibrate it.

</details>


### [152] [Empowering Student Debugging in Parallel Programming with Execution Traces and Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14607)
*God'salvation F. Oguibe,Vinodh Kumaran Jayakumar,Tongping Liu,Andrew Lan,Wei Wang*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: ParaView is an educational tool that records and visualizes parallel C/C++ program execution to help students debug concurrency issues. In a study with 17 students, it significantly improved debugging and implementation success rates. The authors also explored LLMs for analyzing concurrency bugs—LLMs excelled at bug identification and trace explanation but produced inconsistent fixes for complex synchronization patterns.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Concurrent programming is a core yet notoriously difficult subject in CS curricula due to nondeterministic bugs (deadlocks, race conditions) that are hard to observe, understand, and debug. Existing instructional approaches lack sufficient tooling to make parallel execution transparent and comprehensible to students.

Method: The authors developed ParaView, a tool providing transparent execution recording and visualization for C/C++ parallel programs. They evaluated it through debugging and implementation tasks with 17 students, comparing results to previous course iterations. A student survey gathered subjective feedback. Additionally, the authors explored using LLMs to analyze concurrency bugs and suggest fixes, assessing their effectiveness on bug identification, trace explanation, and patch correctness.

Result: Students using ParaView showed significant improvement in both debugging and implementation success compared to prior course cohorts. The student survey confirmed that most participants found ParaView helpful. LLMs proved highly effective at identifying bugs and explaining execution traces, but their bug-fix correctness varied, particularly for complex synchronization patterns.

Conclusion: Recording-visualization tools like ParaView, especially when complemented by AI (LLMs), can substantially improve the teaching and learning of concurrent programming. However, LLM-generated fixes require caution for complex synchronization scenarios.

Abstract: Concurrent programming is a core component of Computer Science curricula, yet remains notoriously difficult for students to master due to its inherent complexity and the nondeterministic nature of concurrency bugs such as deadlocks and race conditions. In this work, we present ParaView, an educational tool designed to help students understand, debug, and correct concurrency issues in parallel programs written in C/C++. ParaView provides transparent execution recording and visualization to make parallel execution observable and comprehensible. We evaluated ParaView through a series of debugging and implementation tasks, with 17 students participating. Results showed a significant improvement in debugging and implementation successes compared to previous course iterations. A student survey confirmed that most participants found ParaView helpful. To further support learning outside the classroom, we explored using Large Language Models (LLMs) to analyze concurrency bugs and suggest fixes. While LLMs were highly effective in identifying bugs and explaining execution traces, the correctness of their bug fixes varied, especially for more complex synchronization patterns. Our findings suggest that recording-visualization tools like ParaView, complemented by artificial intelligence (AI), can improve teaching and learning of concurrent programming.

</details>


### [153] [GitHub Template Repositories: Served Domains, Maintenance, and Practitioner Guidelines](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14616)
*Leuson Da Silva,Altaf Allah Abbassi,Imen Trabelsi,Paulo Borba,Foutse Khomh*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: A large-scale empirical study of GitHub template repositories across five major programming languages, examining their domains, maintenance quality, and design practices. The study finds Web Development dominates, quality varies by language, and high-quality templates adopt established software engineering practices with comprehensive documentation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Despite GitHub template repositories being available since 2019 as a mechanism for scaffolding new projects, there is a significant knowledge gap regarding what domains they serve, their maintenance characteristics, and what practices lead to effective template design.

Method: The authors conducted a mixed-methods empirical study: (1) mining and categorizing templates across the five most-used programming languages using LLM-as-a-judge for domain classification; (2) statistical analysis of associations between repository characteristics/activity and quality issues (code smells, vulnerabilities, security hotspots); and (3) qualitative analysis of a representative subset to derive design guidelines and identify pitfalls.

Result: Web Development is the predominant domain across all ecosystems. Maintenance and quality issues vary significantly by programming language. High-quality templates consistently adopt established software engineering practices, provide comprehensive documentation, and offer clear usage guidance.

Conclusion: The study provides empirical insights and actionable guidance for practitioners to both design and adopt high-quality template repositories, filling a critical knowledge gap in understanding this increasingly important form of software reuse.

Abstract: Over time, GitHub has introduced different strategies for sharing reusable code artifacts. In addition to fork-based reuse, template repositories provide a distinct feature for generating new projects from scaffolding. Although this feature has been available since 2019, little is known about the domains it supports, its maintenance characteristics, or the practices that guide practitioners for effective template design. To address this gap, we conduct a large-scale empirical study of GitHub template repositories across the five most used programming languages. First, we mine and categorize templates to analyze the domains they serve, exploring the LLM-as-a-judge strategy. Next, we explore the reliability of templates by evaluating the associations between repository characteristics and activity, and quality-related issues (e.g., code smells, vulnerabilities, and security hotspots) through statistical analysis. Finally, we qualitatively analyze a representative subset of templates to derive practical guidelines and recurring pitfalls for template design and management. Our results show that Web Development is the predominant domain across ecosystems, while maintenance and quality issues vary by programming language. We further find that high-quality templates tend to adopt established software engineering practices, while providing comprehensive documentation and clear guidance for use. Overall, our findings offer empirical insights and actionable guidance to support practitioners in designing and adopting high-quality template repositories.

</details>


### [154] [Teaching Machine Learning to Software Engineers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14618)
*Nafiseh Kahani,Jason Jaskolka*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: The paper identifies gaps in undergraduate SE curricula regarding AI/ML-based software development, surveys instructors on topic importance, and derives guidelines for embedding high-priority AI/ML topics into existing SE courses.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI/ML components are increasingly embedded in software products, yet undergraduate software engineering curricula rarely provide systematic preparation for building, testing, deploying, and maintaining AI/ML-based systems. There is a need for evidence-based guidance on integrating AI/ML content into core SE education.

Method: The authors (1) compile a structured inventory of topics relevant to SE practice in AI/ML-based software; (2) map these topics against required courses in representative SE curricula to identify coverage gaps; (3) survey SE instructors on topic importance and integration constraints; and (4) perform a crosswalk analysis between topic definitions, curriculum coverage, and instructor prioritization to derive recommendations.

Result: The paper produces a structured topic inventory, a gap analysis of current SE curricula, instructor-prioritized rankings of AI/ML topics, and a guideline recommending where and how high-priority topics can be embedded within existing SE courses.

Conclusion: Undergraduate SE curricula have significant gaps in AI/ML coverage. The proposed guideline offers a practical, evidence-based roadmap for embedding high-priority AI/ML topics into existing SE courses without requiring entirely new programs, balancing educational importance with integration feasibility.

Abstract: Machine learning (ML) and Artificial Intelligence (AI) components are increasingly embedded in software products, yet undergraduate software engineering (SE) curricula rarely provide systematic preparation for building, testing, deploying, and maintaining AI/ML-based software systems. This paper aims to provide evidence-based guidance for integrating AI/MLrelevant content into core SE education. We compile and define a structured inventory of topics relevant to SE practice in AI/MLbased software, then map these topics against required courses in a set of representative SE curricula to identify coverage gaps. To assess educational priorities and feasibility, we survey SE instructors on topic importance and integration constraints. Based on the crosswalk between topic definitions, curriculum coverage, and instructor prioritization, we derive a guideline that recommends where and how high-priority topics can be embedded within existing SE courses.

</details>


<div id='cs.AI'></div>

# cs.AI [[Back]](#toc)

### [155] [A Deep Reinforcement Learning (DRL)-Based Transformer Method for Solving the Open Shop Scheduling Problem](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13682)
*Faezeh Ardali,Mwembezi A. Nyelele,Gerald M. Knapp*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A Transformer-based scheduling policy trained on small Open Shop Scheduling Problem (OSSP) instances (4x4 to 10x10) generalizes to large-scale problems (up to 100x100), achieving makespans within 12.89–15.12% of a standard lower bound and outperforming classical dispatching rules like SPT and LPT, while remaining competitive with EST.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The OSSP is computationally intractable via exact methods at scale, and classical dispatching rules or metaheuristics often require substantial tuning for large instances. A learning-based approach that generalizes from small to large instances without retraining or feature engineering is needed.

Method: A Transformer encoder-decoder architecture with multi-head attention is trained on Taillard benchmark instances (4×4, 5×5, 7×7, 10×10) using only the processing-time matrix as input. The trained policy is then applied without retraining to randomly generated instances ranging from 40×40 to 100×100, and compared against SPT, LPT, MWKR, and EST dispatching rules.

Result: The Transformer policy produces feasible schedules with makespans within 15–30% of best-known values on training-scale instances. On large 40×40 to 100×100 instances, it achieves average gaps of 12.89–15.12% relative to a standard lower bound, substantially outperforming SPT and LPT, and remaining competitive with EST within a modest margin.

Conclusion: A Transformer policy trained exclusively on small OSSP instances can generalize effectively to much larger problems, offering a feature-light, learning-based alternative to classical dispatching rules without requiring retraining or extensive tuning.

Abstract: The open shop scheduling problem (OSSP) arises in many industrial and service settings but remains computationally challenging as the number of jobs and machines increases. While exact methods quickly become intractable, classical dispatching rules and metaheuristics may require substantial tuning to maintain solution quality at large scales. This study develops a Transformer-based scheduling policy for OSSP using an encoder-decoder architecture with multi-head attention. The model is trained on Taillard benchmark instances (4x4, 5x5, 7x7, and 10x10) using only the processing-time matrix as input and produces feasible schedules with makespans typically within 15-30% of best-known values. To evaluate scalability, the trained policy is applied without retraining to randomly generated instances from 40x40 to 100x100 and compared against classical dispatching heuristics, including SPT, LPT, MWKR, and EST. Across these large instances, the Transformer achieved average gaps of 12.89-15.12% relative to a standard lower bound. Compared with EST, the Transformer remained competitive, typically within a modest margin, while substantially outperforming SPT and LPT. These results indicate that a Transformer policy trained on small OSSP instances can generalize to substantially larger problems and provide a feature-light, learning-based alternative to classical dispatching rules.

</details>


### [156] [UP-NRPA: User Portrait based Nested Rollout Policy Adaptation for Planning with Large Language Models in Goal-oriented Dialogue Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13683)
*Hui Wang,Fafa Zhang,Meng Liu,Xiangyu Chen,Chaoxu Mu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: UP-NRPA is an online framework that uses LLMs and real-time user portraits (personality, preferences, objectives) to dynamically adapt dialogue policy without offline RL training, achieving 100% task success in benchmarks and a 56.41% SL improvement in negotiation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current dialogue policy planning methods rely on offline reinforcement learning with pre-defined user group models, limiting their ability to dynamically adapt to diverse, real-time user characteristics.

Method: UP-NRPA employs a nested rollout policy adaptation mechanism powered by Large Language Models. It constructs a user portrait mapping personality, preferences, and objectives from real-time feedback, then uses this portrait to dynamically customize dialogue strategies online, eliminating the need for offline RL training.

Result: UP-NRPA achieved a 100% success rate on multiple dialogue tasks in both collaborative and non-collaborative benchmarks. In negotiation tasks, the sale-to-list ratio (SL) increased by 56.41%.

Conclusion: The framework demonstrates that leveraging LLMs with real-time user portrait adaptation enables effective, training-free dynamic customization of dialogue strategies, successfully adapting to diverse user needs without offline reinforcement learning.

Abstract: To address the challenge that current dialogue policy planning methods struggle to dynamically adapt to diverse user characteristics, this paper proposes a User Portrait based Nested Rollout Policy Adaptation (UP-NRPA) online framework with Large Language Models. In contrast to conventional approaches dependent on model training and require offline reinforcement learning policy models for user groups, UP-NRPA enables dynamic customization of dialogue strategies through an adaptive mechanism. This is achieved by leveraging real-time user feedback alongside personality, preferences, and objectives mapped from the current user portrait, thereby adapting to user characteristics without offline reinforcement learning. In collaborative and non-collaborative dialogue benchmarks, UP-NRPA demonstrated considerable benefits, achieving an impressive 100% success rate in multiple dialogue tasks. Particularly in negotiation tasks, the sale-to-list ratio (SL) increased by 56.41%. This demonstrates that UP-NRPA can adapt to diverse user needs without requiring a training mechanism, enabling the dialogue system to adapt to user characteristics.

</details>


### [157] [History of the Muddy Children Puzzle](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13703)
*Hans van Ditmarsch*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper traces the historical origins of the Muddy Children Puzzle across two centuries of logical and literary publications, surveys its numerous variations (numeric, coloured hats), and presents a novel self-referential hats puzzle.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The Muddy Children Puzzle has been foundational to epistemic logic, yet its exact origin remains unclear. The authors aim to resolve this ambiguity by historically tracing the puzzle's emergence and evolution, while also contributing a new variant.

Method: The study employs historical analysis, surveying logical and literary publications from the past two centuries to trace the puzzle's lineage. It also surveys known variations and proposes a novel puzzle construction involving self-reference.

Result: The paper documents the puzzle's trajectory through 19th- and 20th-century sources, catalogues variants including numeric and coloured-hat versions, and introduces a new self-referential hats puzzle that extends the tradition.

Conclusion: The Muddy Children Puzzle has a rich, traceable history spanning two centuries, demonstrating its enduring value for epistemic logic. Novel variants, such as the proposed self-referential puzzle, continue to deepen exploration of knowledge, ignorance, and common knowledge.

Abstract: The Muddy Children Puzzle is a puzzle about knowledge and ignorance that has been inspiring for the development of epistemic logic. Who came up with it first? This is unclear. We trace the origin of the Muddy Children Puzzle through logical and literary publications over the past two centuries. The puzzle inspired a numerous variations such as involving numbers or coloured hats. We also present a novel hats puzzle involving self-reference.

</details>


### [158] [WorkBench Revisited: Workplace Agents Two Years On](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13715)
*Olly Styles*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper revisits the WorkBench agent benchmark after two years, finding that the best agent (Claude Opus 4.8) now completes 89% of tasks (up from 43% by GPT-4) with only 2.5% harmful actions (down from 26%). Key findings: capability and safety improve together, some basic errors persist, and open-weight models have dramatically lowered costs for previously proprietary-level performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To track progress on agentic AI systems by re-evaluating the WorkBench benchmark two years after its initial release, assessing how frontier models have evolved in both task completion capability and safety (unintended harmful actions), and understanding structural shifts in the model landscape (open-weight vs. proprietary).

Method: Re-benchmarking WorkBench — a benchmark designed to evaluate AI agents on real-world tasks — in June 2026 using the then-best model (Claude Opus 4.8). The authors compare scores against the March 2024 baseline (GPT-4), analyze error classes, examine cost trends across open-weight and proprietary models, and release an updated benchmark with data/code quality improvements.

Result: Task completion rose from 43% (GPT-4, 2024) to 89% (Claude Opus 4.8, 2026), while unintended harmful actions dropped from 26% to 2.5%. Capability and safety were found to be positively correlated, not in tension. Several error classes were fully eliminated, though basic mistakes causing irreversible harm (e.g., emailing the wrong person) persist. Open-weight models now match previously proprietary-only performance at drastically lower cost, while frontier costs remained stable.

Conclusion: Agentic AI has made substantial and simultaneous progress in both capability and safety over two years. The positive correlation between the two suggests they are complementary rather than competing objectives. Persistent basic errors warrant continued caution, while the commoditization of strong performance via open-weight models represents a significant structural shift in the AI agent landscape.

Abstract: The best agent on WorkBench in March 2024, GPT-4, completed 43% of tasks and took an unintended harmful action, such as emailing the wrong person, on 26% of them. We re-visit the benchmark in June 2026 and find that the best agent to date, Claude Opus 4.8, completes 89% and takes an unintended harmful action on 2.5%. Aside from this considerable progress in frontier agent performance, three things stand out. First, capability and safety go together on WorkBench rather than trade off, so the models that finish the most tasks also do the least unintended damage. Second, while several classes of error have been totally eliminated, frontier models still make some basic mistakes that occasionally result in irreversible harm, such as sending an email to the wrong person. Third, the rise of open-weight models has drastically lowered costs for a performance level that was previously only accessible to proprietary models, while frontier costs have stayed relatively stable. We release an updated version of the benchmark with data and code quality improvements, new model scores, and analysis of agent progress on WorkBench since 2024.

</details>


### [159] [Orchestra-o1: Omnimodal Agent Orchestration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13707)
*Fan Zhang,Vireo Zhang,Shengju Qian,Haoxuan Li,Hao Wu,Jinyang Wu,Donghao Zhou,Zhihong Zhu,Zheng Lian,Xin Wang,Pheng-Ann Heng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Orchestra-o1 is an omnimodal agent orchestration framework that enables multi-agent collaboration across text, image, audio, and video modalities through modality-aware task decomposition, online sub-agent specialization, and parallel execution. It outperforms prior approaches by 10.3% on OmniGAIA and, with DA-GRPO training, achieves SOTA among open-source omnimodal agents.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM-based multi-agent orchestration frameworks are limited to narrow modalities and fail in omnimodal settings where text, image, audio, and video coexist. There is a need for a unified orchestration mechanism that can handle heterogeneous modalities for complex real-world tasks.

Method: Orchestra-o1 introduces a unified orchestration mechanism with three key components: (1) modality-aware task decomposition, (2) online sub-agent specialization, and (3) parallel sub-task execution. Additionally, Decision-Aligned Group Relative Policy Optimization (DA-GRPO) is proposed as an efficient agentic RL method for training the Orchestra-o1-8B model.

Result: Orchestra-o1 surpasses the second-best approach by 10.3% accuracy on the OmniGAIA benchmark. The DA-GRPO-trained Orchestra-o1-8B achieves state-of-the-art performance against all existing open-source omnimodal agents.

Conclusion: The scalable, modality-aware orchestration design of Orchestra-o1 effectively addresses the challenges of omnimodal multi-agent collaboration, demonstrating significant performance gains and establishing a new SOTA for open-source omnimodal agents.

Abstract: The recent success of agent swarms has shifted the paradigm of large language model (LLM)-based agents from single-agent workflows to multi-agent systems, highlighting the importance of agent orchestration for task decomposition and collaboration. However, existing orchestration frameworks are limited to a narrow set of modalities and struggle to generalize to more complex settings where heterogeneous modalities coexist and interact. This limitation becomes particularly pronounced in omnimodal scenarios, where tasks require the unified understanding and coordination of diverse inputs such as text, image, audio, and video. In this work, we propose Orchestra-o1, an omnimodal agent orchestration framework designed to support efficient agent collaboration across multiple modalities. Orchestra-o1 introduces a unified orchestration mechanism that enables modality-aware task decomposition, online sub-agent specialization, and parallel sub-task execution. This scalable design allows agent systems to effectively tackle complex real-world tasks involving heterogeneous information sources, surpassing the second-best approach by 10.3% accuracy on the OmniGAIA benchmark. Furthermore, we introduce decision-aligned group relative policy optimization (DA-GRPO), an efficient agentic reinforcement learning approach for training Orchestra-o1-8B, which also achieves state-of-the-art performance against all existing open-source omnimodal agents.

</details>


### [160] [YeasierAgent: Agentic Social Sandbox as a Canvas for Intent-Driven Creation of Platform-Agnostic Symbiotic Agent-Native Applications](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13722)
*Jory He*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: YeasierAgent proposes a new application paradigm that replaces conventional device-coupled software with collaborative spaces built on symbiotic agents, narrative worlds, and scene-aware interaction, unifying emotional companionship and practical tool execution in a single sandbox.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper is motivated by the limitations of conventional device-coupled software models, where applications are tied to specific platforms and fixed graphical layouts. The authors seek to move beyond isolated, tool-specific chatbots toward cohesive, socially embedded computational environments that integrate emotional and practical functions.

Method: The system architecture uses platform-agnostic interactive units — agents, scenes, and dialogue — instead of fixed graphical layouts. It integrates automated generation, user-created worlds, and spatial multi-agent collaboration within a unified experiential sandbox that combines companionship and tool execution.

Result: The work formalizes the category of Symbiotic Agent-Native Applications, demonstrating rapid, cross-platform construction of agent-native applications and unifying emotional companionship with practical tool execution in a single environment.

Conclusion: YeasierAgent represents a paradigm shift from device-coupled, tool-specific applications toward collaborative, symbiotic, and socially embedded computational environments, establishing a new formal category of agent-native software.

Abstract: This paper introduces YeasierAgent, an application-building paradigm based on symbiotic agents, narrative worlds, and scene-aware interaction. It challenges the conventional device-coupled model of software by redefining applications as collaborative spaces among users, agents, and worlds. We present a system architecture that achieves two primary contributions: (1) enabling the rapid, cross-platform construction of agent-native applications by utilizing platform-agnostic interactive units (agents, scenes, dialogue) rather than fixed graphical layouts; and (2) unifying the emotional companionship and practical tool execution attributes of intelligent agents within a single experiential sandbox. By integrating automated generation, user-created worlds, and spatial multi-agent collaboration, YeasierAgent formalizes the category of Symbiotic Agent-Native Applications, demonstrating a shift from isolated, tool-specific chatbots toward cohesive, socially embedded computational environments.

</details>


### [161] [Hybrid Open-Ended Tri-Evolution Makes Better Deep Researcher](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13710)
*Hongming Piao,Chi Liu,Mengzhuo Chen,Yan Shu,Derek Li,Ying Wei,Bryan Dai*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: HOTE is a hybrid reinforcement learning framework that co-evolves a proposer, solver, and judge for open-ended deep research tasks, enabling an 8B model to outperform larger static models and prior training methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current AI agents face a gap: deep research excels at open-ended retrieval/integration but relies on static model capabilities, while agent evolution works well on verifiable tasks but struggles with open-ended research. The authors aim to bridge these two paradigms.

Method: The Hybrid Open-Ended Tri-Evolution (HOTE) framework uses hybrid-mode reinforcement learning to jointly evolve three collaborative agents — a proposer (generates research directions), a solver (executes research), and a judge (evaluates outputs) — grounded in web-scale knowledge, enabling autonomous evolution in open-ended environments.
