Result: An 8B model trained via HOTE surpasses the strongest static open models in the 8-32B range and outperforms state-of-the-art deep research training methods, all with lower time overhead. Ablation confirms all three evolving modules are essential.

Conclusion: HOTE successfully bridges deep research and agent evolution through collaborative tri-evolution, demonstrating that smaller models can rival or exceed much larger static counterparts on open-ended research benchmarks when trained with this approach.

Abstract: Deep research and agent evolution serve as de-facto tasks for AI agents in real-world applications toward artificial general intelligence. The former enables autonomous retrieval and integration of information in open-ended environments to tackle open-ended research tasks, yet it is constrained by the static parametric deep research capabilities of agent systems. The latter allows agents to autonomously interact with the environment to gain experiences that evolve model capabilities. However, its effectiveness has been widely validated only on verifiable tasks with standard answers, leaving a gap with open-ended research tasks. To bridge these two critical tasks, we propose the Hybrid Open-Ended Tri-Evolution (HOTE) framework, which leverages hybrid-mode reinforcement learning to facilitate the collaborative evolution of a proposer, solver and judge based on web-scale knowledge, moving toward autonomous evolving agents in open-ended tasks and environments. Extensive experiments on three long-form deep research benchmarks demonstrate that the 8B model trained via HOTE surpasses the strongest static open 8-32B models as well as those trained by state-of-the-art deep research training methods with less time overhead, and further verify that the evolution of all three modules in HOTE is indispensable.

</details>


### [162] [TwinBI: An Agentic Digital Twin for Efficient Augmented Interactions with Business Intelligence Dashboards](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13731)
*Jisoo Jang Wen-Syan Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TwinBI is an agentic digital-twin framework that synchronizes LLM-based conversational assistance with an executable BI dashboard state, improving analytical accuracy and reducing timeouts by grounding interactions in a shared, log-reconstructed state.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In modern BI workflows, users alternate between dashboard manipulation and natural-language queries, but these two modalities easily desynchronize during multi-step analysis. There is no existing mechanism to maintain consistent analytical state—filters, hierarchies, metrics, chart context—across these interaction modes, leading to fragmented workflows and analytical errors.

Method: TwinBI introduces an agentic digital-twin framework that couples an LLM-based agent with an executable BI dashboard state. It unifies conversational interaction, dashboard manipulation, semantic grounding, and provenance tracking through a shared analytical state reconstructed from a unified interaction log. The system exposes artifacts including schema views, SQL, logs, and an /insights command for state-grounded summaries. Evaluation used a controlled A/B benchmark (TwinBI vs. Dashboard-alone, same backbone agent) and a usability study with human participants.

Result: In the A/B benchmark, TwinBI improved exact-match accuracy from 43.3% to 63.3%, partial-credit accuracy from 48.3% to 70.8%, and reduced timeout rate from 40.0% to 10.0% relative to Dashboard alone. The usability study showed high task accuracy, moderate workload, and favorable ratings for state-aware interaction mechanisms.

Conclusion: TwinBI demonstrates that grounding LLM-based agents in a visible, executable dashboard state significantly improves both agent-level analytical reliability and user-facing analytical support. The digital-twin approach provides a viable architecture for synchronizing multi-modal BI interactions.

Abstract: Business intelligence (BI) increasingly combines dashboard interaction with LLM-based assistance, but these two modes often fall out of sync during multi-step analysis. As users switch between direct dashboard manipulation and natural-language queries, it becomes difficult to preserve a consistent analytical state across filters, hierarchies, metrics, and chart context. We present TwinBI, an agentic digital-twin framework that couples an LLM-based agent system with an executable BI dashboard state. TwinBI unifies conversational interaction, dashboard manipulation, semantic grounding, and provenance tracking through a shared analytical state reconstructed from a unified interaction log. It also exposes artifacts such as schema views, SQL, logs, and an /insights command for state-grounded analytical summaries. We evaluate TwinBI in two complementary ways. In a controlled A/B benchmark with the same backbone agent, TwinBI improves exact-match accuracy from 43.3% to 63.3%, partial-credit accuracy from 48.3% to 70.8%, and substantially reduces timeout rate from 40.0% to 10.0% relative to Dashboard alone. In a usability study, participants benefited from the integrated dashboard-and-chat workflow, with high task accuracy, moderate workload, and favorable ratings for state-aware interaction mechanisms. These results suggest that TwinBI improves both agent-level analytical reliability and user-facing analytical support by turning visible dashboard state into richer actionable context. Our dataset and source code are available at: https://github.com/simonjisu/TwinBI

</details>


### [163] [Refusal Beyond a Single Direction: A Preliminary Comparison of Diff-in-Means and INLP](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13720)
*Elisabetta Rocchetti,Alfio Ferrara*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Comparison of INLP-based interventions against DiM-based methods for steering refusal in chat models. INLP counterfactual flipping matches DiM directional ablation on refusal suppression; nullspace projection is weaker. Leading INLP directions yield a tunable capability with near-baseline perplexity. Geometrically, the two INLP interventions occupy distinct regions of activation space.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Arditi et al. (2024) established that refusal in safety-tuned chat models is mediated by a single linear direction recoverable via difference-in-means (DiM). The authors ask whether Iterative Nullspace Projection (INLP), with its richer parameterization, can match or exceed DiM for steering refusal, and whether INLP's multi-directional subspace enables more fine-grained, tweakable interventions.

Method: The authors compare two DiM-based interventions (activation addition and directional ablation) against two INLP-derived interventions (nullspace projection and counterfactual flipping) across five open-weight chat models. They evaluate refusal suppression performance, perplexity impact, and geometric properties. They also test restricting INLP to its leading subspace directions to assess tunability.

Result: INLP counterfactual flipping is competitive with DiM directional ablation for refusal suppression. Nullspace projection is consistently weaker. Restricting INLP to leading subspace directions preserves most suppression effect while maintaining near-baseline perplexity, yielding a tunable capability. Geometrically, nullspace projection collapses activations between harmful/harmless clusters, while counterfactual flipping moves them into the opposite cluster — suggesting the model encodes concept absence differently from its opposite.

Conclusion: INLP offers a viable alternative to DiM for refusal steering, with counterfactual flipping matching DiM performance. The richer INLP parameterization enables tunable interventions via subspace rank restriction. The geometric distinction between the two INLP interventions — collapsing between clusters vs. flipping to the opposite cluster — reveals that models encode absence and opposition of a concept differently, an insight meriting further investigation.

Abstract: Arditi et al. (2024) has shown that refusal in safety fine-tuned chat models is mediated by a single linear direction in the residual stream, recoverable by a difference-in-means (DiM) of harmful and harmless activations. We compare DiM-based interventions (activation addition and directional ablation) with two interventions derived from Iterative Nullspace Projection (INLP) -- nullspace projection and counterfactual flipping -- on five open-weight chat models, asking whether INLP can match DiM at steering refusal and whether its richer parameterisation yields more tweakable interventions. INLP counterfactual flipping is competitive with DiM directional ablation on refusal suppression, while nullspace projection is consistently weaker. Restricting INLP to the leading directions of the extracted subspace preserves most of the suppression effect at near-baseline perplexity, giving a tunable capability. Geometrically, the two INLP interventions land in qualitatively different regions of activation space: nullspace projection collapses transformed activations \emph{between} the harmful and harmless clusters, while counterfactual flipping moves them into the opposite cluster, suggesting that the model encodes the absence of a concept differently from its opposite -- an intriguing distinction that warrants further investigation in future work.

</details>


### [164] [When Sample Selection Bias Precipitates Model Collapse](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13732)
*Xinbao Qiao,Xianglong Du,Wei Liu,Jingqi Zhang,Peihua Mai,Meng Zhang,Yan Pang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: In low-resource settings where verifiers have only fragmented local reference data, data selection—intended to prevent model collapse—backfires by pruning tail modes and accelerating collapse. The authors prove this theoretically and propose Wasserstein proxy references across silos as a mitigation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Recursive training on synthetic data is increasingly used to address data scarcity, but it risks model collapse. While data selection via a verifier is a common proposed remedy, its reliability hinges on the reference distribution. In real-world low-resource silos (e.g., healthcare, finance), raw data cannot be pooled, so local verifiers have incomplete, biased views. The authors investigate whether data selection under such fragmented verification regimes actually prevents or precipitates collapse.

Method: The authors theoretically analyze the dynamics of recursive synthetic-data training with siloed (local-reference) data selection. They prove that biased selection accelerates model collapse and yields a power-law decay in diversity. As an initial mitigation, they construct Wasserstein proxy references by aggregating information from multiple silos without sharing raw data, enabling a more globally representative verifier.

Result: Theoretical proofs demonstrate that siloed, local-reference selection accelerates collapse and produces power-law diversity decay. Empirical results confirm that local-reference selection fails on skewed distributions, while collaborative Wasserstein proxy references mitigate diversity degradation.

Conclusion: Recursive synthetic-data pipelines demand caution when real-data coverage is fragmented or scarce. Data selection, far from being a universal safeguard, can become a collapse mechanism under biased verification. Collaborative proxy references across silos offer a privacy-preserving partial remedy.

Abstract: The proliferation of recursive training on synthetic data can alleviate data scarcity but risks model collapse, where repeated training erodes distributional tails and homogenizes outputs. Data selection is widely viewed as a remedy, yet its reliability depends critically on the reference distribution used by the verifier. We show that in low-resource verification regimes, where each verifier observes only a small, fragmented, and biased slice of the target manifold, selection itself becomes biased. This situation naturally arises in low-resource data silos such as healthcare consortia or proprietary financial institutions, where raw data cannot be pooled and local references are inherently incomplete. As a result, selection preferentially retains samples aligned with the local manifold while pruning globally relevant tail modes, turning from a safeguard against collapse into a mechanism that precipitates it. We theoretically prove that such siloed selection accelerates collapse and induces power-law diversity decay. As an initial mitigation, we construct Wasserstein proxy references from multiple silos without sharing raw data. Empirical results confirm that local-reference selection fails on skewed distributions, whereas collaborative proxy references mitigate diversity degradation, suggesting that recursive synthetic-data pipelines require particular caution when real-data coverage is fragmented or scarce.

</details>


### [165] [AI Receptivity or AI Adoption Breadth? A Tool-Specific Reanalysis of the Lower-Literacy/Higher-Usage Link](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13734)
*Hristo Inouzhe*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper reanalyzes Tully et al. (2025) data and finds that the negative AI literacy–AI usage relationship masks heterogeneity: AI literacy does not significantly predict text AI tool usage but robustly predicts non-text AI tool adoption, primarily as an adoption/non-adoption pattern rather than intensity of use.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Tully et al. (2025) claimed lower AI literacy predicts greater receptivity toward AI. The authors question this generalization by testing whether the effect is uniform across different AI tool types, given that aggregate analyses may obscure important category-level heterogeneity.

Method: The authors use public data from Study 3 of Tully et al. (2025), measuring past usage of five AI tool categories on a five-point frequency scale. They reproduce the aggregate negative association using OLS on participant-level averages, binary logit, ordered logit, and multinomial logit. They then disaggregate by tool type (text vs. non-text AI) using demographic-adjusted and original control specifications, employing ordered-logit, binary logit, and multinomial logit models.

Result: In the demographic-adjusted primary specification, AI literacy does not significantly predict text AI usage (β = -0.090, p = .387), but strongly predicts non-text AI adoption (β = -0.377, p < .001). The non-text effect is robust using the original control specification (β = -0.502, p < .001). The non-text relationship is driven by adoption vs. non-adoption rather than intensive use, with a demographic-adjusted odds ratio of 0.68 for ever having used a non-text AI tool.

Conclusion: The evidence does not support a general claim that lower AI literacy predicts greater AI receptivity. Instead, it reveals a narrower pattern: lower AI literacy is associated with broader adoption specifically of lower-penetration, non-text AI tools, not text-based AI tools.

Abstract: Recent evidence reported by Tully, Longoni, and Appel (2025) suggests that lower artificial intelligence (AI) literacy predicts greater receptivity toward AI. We revisit this claim using the public data from Study 3 of that article, which measures past usage of five AI tool categories on a five-point frequency scale. We first reproduce the negative association between AI literacy and aggregate AI usage using OLS on participant-level averages, binary logit, ordered logit, and multinomial logit specifications. We then show that the aggregate relationship masks substantial heterogeneity by tool type. In our demographic-adjusted primary specification, AI literacy does not significantly predict text AI usage (ordered-logit $β$ = -0.090, p = .387), whereas it remains a strong predictor of non-text AI adoption ($β$ = -0.377, p < .001). The non-text effect is also robust under Tully et al.'s original Study 3 control specification ($β$ = -0.502, p < .001). Binary, ordered-logit, and multinomial specifications suggest that the non-text relationship is primarily an adoption/non-adoption pattern rather than evidence of intensive use: the demographic-adjusted odds ratio of ever having used a non-text AI tool is 0.68. Thus, in the study that measures self-reported past usage rather than stated preferences, the evidence does not support a simple claim that lower AI literacy predicts greater receptivity to AI in general. It points instead to a narrower pattern of broader adoption across lower-penetration, non-text AI tools.

</details>


### [166] [MA-ProofBench: A Two-Tiered Evaluation of LLMs for Theorem Proving in Mathematical Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13782)
*Lushi Pu,Weiming Zhang,Xinheng Xie,Zixuan Fu,Bingxiang He,Hongya Lyu,Xin Li,Jie Zhou,Yudong Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MA-ProofBench is the first formal theorem-proving benchmark for mathematical analysis, containing 200 theorems across 6 core topics. Even the best LLM (GPT-5.5) achieves only 16% Pass@8 on undergraduate-level problems and 5% on PhD-level problems, revealing a major gap between informal and formal mathematical reasoning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing formal theorem-proving benchmarks (e.g., for LLMs) are limited to algebra and elementary number theory — domains easier to formalize — and neglect subfields requiring deeper reasoning, particularly mathematical analysis. This gap prevents accurate assessment of LLMs' formal reasoning capabilities at advanced mathematical depths.

Method: The authors construct MA-ProofBench with 200 theorems across 6 core topics (measure/integration theory, complex analysis, functional analysis, etc.) and 27 subcategories, split into Level I (undergraduate, 100 problems) and Level II (PhD qualifying, 100 problems). Each problem undergoes a human-led, LLM-assisted formalization pipeline followed by independent expert review to ensure faithfulness to the original mathematics. They evaluate recent general-purpose reasoning models and formal theorem provers on the benchmark.

Result: Most models perform poorly. GPT-5.5 achieves only 16% Pass@8 on Level I and 5% on Level II; most models score near 0% on Level II. The two dominant failure modes are Mathlib hallucinations and incomplete proofs. An auxiliary evaluation on the natural-language version reveals a clear gap between informal and formal reasoning.

Conclusion: MA-ProofBench serves as a reliable reference for tracking progress in formal mathematical reasoning within advanced domains like mathematical analysis, and highlights the substantial shortcomings of current LLMs in formal theorem proving at deeper mathematical levels.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) have made notable progress in automated theorem proving, yet existing formal benchmarks remain limited in both mathematical coverage and difficulty. Most are concentrated in areas that are easier to formalize, such as algebra and elementary number theory, and provide limited coverage of subfields that require deeper reasoning, including mathematical analysis. To address this gap, we introduce MA-ProofBench, to the best of our knowledge, the first formal theorem-proving benchmark dedicated to Mathematical Analysis. The benchmark contains 200 formalized theorems covering 6 core topics and 27 subcategories, including measure and integration theory, complex analysis, and functional analysis. The problems are divided into two difficulty levels, an undergraduate level (Level I, 100 problems) and a Ph.D. qualifying level (Level II, 100 problems), to evaluate how well LLMs perform formal reasoning at different mathematical depths. Each problem is constructed through a human-led, LLM-assisted formalization pipeline followed by independent expert review, ensuring that the formal statements remain faithful to the original mathematics. We evaluate a range of recent general-purpose reasoning models and formal theorem provers on MA-ProofBench. However, most models perform poorly: even the best-performing model, GPT-5.5, achieves only 16% Pass@8 on Level I and 5% on Level II, while most models stay close to 0% on Level II. Further analysis identifies Mathlib hallucinations and incomplete proofs as the two dominant failure modes, while an evaluation on the natural-language version of the benchmark exposes a clear gap between informal and formal reasoning. MA-ProofBench is intended to serve as a reliable reference for tracking progress in formal mathematical reasoning in advanced domains.

</details>


### [167] [Poker Arena: Multi-Axis Profiling of Strategic Reasoning and Memory in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13815)
*Pratham Singla,Shivank Garg,Vihan Singh*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Poker Arena introduces a multi-dimensional evaluation framework for LLMs playing no-limit Texas Hold'em, revealing that scalar leaderboards misrank model capabilities by collapsing heterogeneous strategic reasoning dimensions. Claude Opus 4.6 wins the most chips but ranks only fifth on mean axis score, demonstrating that cross-dimensional consistency matters more than peak single-axis performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current game-play benchmarks reduce heterogeneous strategic reasoning dimensions (e.g., bet-sizing, positional awareness, bluffing) to a single scalar metric, obscuring the capability structure of frontier LLMs. The authors argue this prevents meaningful diagnosis of model strengths, weaknesses, and the effects of memory architectures on strategic reasoning.

Method: The authors built Poker Arena, a no-limit Texas Hold'em tournament platform featuring: (1) a three-layer memory architecture (within-hand, session-level, and cross-session memory); (2) a nine-axis cognitive profile that decomposes strategic reasoning into interpretable dimensions; and (3) controlled memory ablation studies. Seven frontier LLMs were evaluated across 50 tournament sessions of 1,000 hands each.

Result: Tournament chips and aggregate axis scores produced divergent rankings. Claude Opus 4.6 achieved the highest chip winnings (+$15,730 with 14 first-place finishes) but ranked only fifth of seven on mean axis score. Persistent memory exhibited asymmetric effects: it improved performance for some models while degrading it for others. Cross-dimensional consistency proved more predictive of overall quality than excelling on any single axis.

Conclusion: Multi-axis evaluation surfaces capability structures that scalar leaderboards systematically misrank. The Poker Arena framework demonstrates that interpretable, decomposed cognitive profiles provide richer and more actionable insights into LLM strategic reasoning than aggregate win/loss metrics alone.

Abstract: Strategic reasoning under uncertainty underpins consequential decisions in negotiation, finance, and policy, but prevailing game-play benchmarks collapse heterogeneous reasoning dimensions into a single scalar, leaving the capability structure of frontier LLMs unexamined. We introduce Poker Arena, a no-limit Texas Hold'em tournament platform that couples a three-layer memory architecture (within-hand, session, and cross-session) with a nine-axis cognitive profile decomposing strategic reasoning into interpretable dimensions such as bet-sizing calibration and positional awareness. We evaluate seven frontier models across 50 sessions of 1,000 hands and a controlled memory ablation; tournament chips and aggregate axis score order the field differently: Claude Opus 4.6 wins +$15,730 chips with 14 first-place finishes, yet ranks only fifth of seven on mean axis score, while persistent memory helps some models and hurts others. These findings show that multi-axis evaluation surfaces capability structure that scalar leaderboards systematically misrank, with cross-dimensional consistency outweighing peak performance on any single axis.

</details>


### [168] [Hyperdimensional computing for structured querying on tabular data embeddings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13871)
*Sebastián Bugedo,Stijn Vansummeren*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Current tabular data embeddings lack interpretable similarity scores, making it impossible to distinguish true matches from spurious nearest neighbors or detect zero-match queries. This paper proposes using Holographic Reduced Representations (HRR), a HyperDimensional Computing model, for tabular row embeddings that yield closed-form expected similarity values, enabling principled retrieval thresholds and reliable zero-match detection.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing tabular embedding methods produce similarity scores with no intrinsic meaning — the nearest neighbor returned may be a true match or merely the least-dissimilar item when no valid answer exists. This lack of interpretability prevents setting principled retrieval thresholds and undermines zero-match detection, limiting practical deployment in data integration pipelines.

Method: The authors apply Holographic Reduced Representations (HRR), a form of HyperDimensional Computing, to encode tabular rows as high-dimensional vectors. They exploit the algebraic properties of HDC operations (binding, bundling) to derive closed-form expected similarity values for equality and non-equality retrieval predicates. As dimensionality increases, these expected similarities converge to interpretable, fixed values, which are used to establish principled retrieval thresholds.

Result: Evaluated against EmbDI (a graph-based baseline) on two real-world datasets across varying table sizes and predicate lengths, HDC matches or outperforms EmbDI for row retrieval in all configurations, handles non-equality predicates more robustly, achieves perfect attribute projection accuracy at sufficient dimensionality, and uniquely enables reliable zero-match predicate identification through principled thresholds.

Conclusion: HRR-based HDC provides a mathematically grounded framework for tabular row embeddings that not only performs competitively on retrieval tasks but uniquely addresses the critical practical challenge of interpretable similarity and zero-match detection, which current embedding methods fail to solve.

Abstract: Tabular data embeddings have become a cornerstone of data profiling and data integration pipelines, enabling tasks such as entity annotation and resolution; schema matching; column type detection; and table search, among others. Existing approaches embed rows, columns, or entire tables into a vector space and rely on nearest-neighbor search to retrieve candidate matches. A fundamental limitation of current embedding methods is the lack of interpretable similarity scores: the concrete similarity value between a query and its nearest neighbour carries no intrinsic meaning, making it impossible to determine whether that neighbour is a true match or simply the least-dissimilar item in a corpus that contains no valid answer. This inability to set principled thresholds for retrieval undermines practical deployment, particularly for zero-match detection.
  We investigate the use of HyperDimensional Computing (HDC), specifically the Holographic Reduced Representations (HRR) model, as a framework for tabular row embeddings when the retrieval task corresponds to answering structured select-project queries in vector space. Exploiting the algebraic properties of HDC operations, we derive closed-form expected similarity values for both equality and non-equality retrieval predicates, which converge to interpretable values as dimensionality increases, and use these to identify suitable retrieval thresholds. We evaluate HDC against EmbDI, a graph-based baseline, on two real-world datasets across varying table sizes and predicate lengths. Our results show that HDC matches or outperforms EmbDI for row retrieval across all configurations, handles non-equality predicates more robustly, and achieves perfect attribute projection accuracy at sufficient dimensionality -- while uniquely enabling reliable identification of zero-match predicates through its principled thresholds.

</details>


### [169] [Capability Minimization as a Safety Primitive: Risk-Aware Causal Gating for Least-Privilege LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13884)
*Laxmipriya Ganesh Iyer,Rahul Suresh Babu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: RACG is a framework that combines causal effect estimation with risk control to gate model decisions (act/defer/abstain) based on counterfactual risk rather than confidence, providing distribution-free safety bounds and adaptive shift handling, outperforming confidence-based baselines on high-stakes decision tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Modern decision systems increasingly rely on learned components that can produce confident but incorrect predictions, exposing downstream actions to costly errors. Existing confidence-based gating and selective prediction methods conflate predictive uncertainty with causal risk, failing to provide principled safety guarantees in high-stakes settings.

Method: RACG (Risk-Aware Causal Gating) models causal pathways from candidate actions to outcomes using counterfactual risk estimation. It derives distribution-free bounds on the probability of acting under high-risk conditions, translating these into operating thresholds that satisfy user-specified safety constraints. An adaptive gating policy monitors discrepancies between predicted and realized outcomes to adjust for distribution shift, tightening the gate when causal assumptions are violated.

Result: Across simulated interventions and real-world decision benchmarks, RACG substantially reduces high-cost errors while preserving most utility of an ungated policy. It outperforms confidence-based and selective-prediction baselines at matched abstention rates.

Conclusion: Explicitly separating causal risk from predictive uncertainty yields decision systems that are both safer and more transparent, offering a principled mechanism for trustworthy automation in high-stakes settings.

Abstract: Modern decision systems increasingly rely on learned components whose outputs may be confident yet wrong, exposing downstream actions to costly errors. We introduce Risk-Aware Causal Gating (RACG), a framework that decides whether to act on, defer, or abstain from a model's prediction by combining causal effect estimation with calibrated risk control. RACG models the causal pathway from candidate actions to outcomes and gates each decision according to an estimated counterfactual risk rather than raw predictive confidence. To make gating reliable, we derive distribution-free bounds on the probability of acting under high-risk conditions and show how these bounds translate into operating thresholds that satisfy user-specified safety constraints. We further propose an adaptive gating policy that adjusts to distribution shift by monitoring discrepancies between predicted and realized outcomes, tightening the gate when causal assumptions appear violated. Across simulated interventions and real-world decision benchmarks, RACG reduces high-cost errors substantially while preserving most of the utility of an ungated policy, and it outperforms confidence-based and selective-prediction baselines at matched abstention rates. Our results indicate that explicitly separating causal risk from predictive uncertainty yields decision systems that are both safer and more transparent, offering a principled mechanism for trustworthy automation in high-stakes settings.

</details>


### [170] [A Multi-Agent AI System for Automated High School Transcript Processing: Collaborative Document Analysis at Scale](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13916)
*Ben Torkian,Jun Zhou*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A multi-agent AI system with specialized agents (Pattern Recognition, Semantic Analysis, Vision Intelligence) coordinated by an Orchestration Agent automates college transcript processing, achieving 96.7% accuracy on 40 real-world transcripts across 13 U.S. states at 45 seconds per document.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: College admissions offices are overwhelmed by manually processing millions of high school transcripts with diverse formats, grading systems, and layouts, creating operational bottlenecks that delay decisions and consume significant resources.

Method: A multi-agent architecture comprising three specialized agents: (1) Pattern Recognition Agent for format-specific parsing, (2) Semantic Analysis Agent for natural language understanding, and (3) Vision Intelligence Agent for multimodal document analysis. These are coordinated by an Orchestration Agent that manages inter-agent communication and result reconciliation. The key innovation is agent-based quality control using GPA extraction as a coordination signal to ensure reliable collaboration and prevent information loss.

Result: The system successfully processed all 40 real-world transcripts from high schools across 13 U.S. states, achieving 96.7% accuracy compared to expert manual review, with processing speeds of 45 seconds per transcript.

Conclusion: Multi-agent coordination effectively solves complex document processing challenges, offering institutions a scalable, collaborative AI solution that preserves accuracy while dramatically reducing processing time.

Abstract: Each year, college admissions offices face an overwhelming challenge: processing millions of high school transcripts, each with unique formats, grading systems, and layouts. This manual process creates operational bottlenecks that delay admissions decisions and consume valuable resources. We present a transformative solution through a multi-agent AI system where specialized agents collaborate to automatically process diverse transcript formats through intelligent coordination and communication. Our multi-agent architecture consists of three specialized agents-a Pattern Recognition Agent for format-specific parsing, a Semantic Analysis Agent for natural language understanding, and a Vision Intelligence Agent for multimodal document analysis-coordinated by an Orchestration Agent that manages agent communication and result reconciliation. Our key innovation lies in agent-based quality control using GPA extraction as a coordination signal, ensuring reliable agent collaboration and preventing critical information loss. When evaluated on 40 real world transcripts from high schools across 13 U.S. states, our agent system successfully processed every document, achieving 96.7% accuracy compared to expert manual review while maintaining practical processing speeds of 45 seconds per transcript. This work demonstrates how multi-agent coordination can solve complex document processing challenges, offering institutions a scalable, collaborative AI solution that preserves accuracy while dramatically reducing processing time.

</details>


### [171] [Sorries Are Not the Hard Part: An Expert-Review Case Study of a Semi-Autonomous Formalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13925)
*Vasily Ilin,Brian Nugent*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A case study on using LLMs to semi-autonomously formalize Grothendieck's vanishing theorem reveals that while agents can close proof gaps, they struggle with definitional choices, API design, and organizational structure. The authors argue that autoformalization systems should be evaluated by expert review survival, not just sorries-free compilation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing evaluations of LLM-based autoformalization focus primarily on whether proofs compile without "sorries" (unfilled gaps). However, a verified theorem is not equivalent to a reusable, well-designed library contribution. The authors aim to rigorously characterize this gap by studying how LLM-generated formalizations fare under expert human review.

Method: The authors conduct a detailed case study: semi-autonomous formalization of Grothendieck's vanishing theorem using LLM agents. They produce an initial version that compiles with zero sorries, submit it for expert review to identify problems (in definitions, generality, file organization, and API design), then run a review-driven refactor and compression cycle, followed by a second expert review. The before-and-after comparison isolates where agents succeed versus fail.

Result: The before-and-after comparison reveals a sharp split in agent capabilities. Agents adapted well to local, mechanically checkable feedback (e.g., fixing specific proof gaps or errors flagged by the prover). However, they remained weak at high-level design tasks: choosing appropriate mathematical definitions, designing clean APIs, ensuring theorem generality, and organizing files modularly — precisely the qualities that distinguish a library contribution from a mere verified statement.

Conclusion: Autoformalization should be evaluated not solely by whether proofs are sorries-free, but by whether the resulting formalization survives expert review — i.e., whether it meets the standards of reusable library contributions in terms of definitional choices, generality, and API design.

Abstract: Large language models can often close proof gaps in interactive theorem provers, but a verified theorem is not the same thing as a reusable library contribution. We study this distinction through a detailed case study: a semi-autonomous formalization of Grothendieck's vanishing theorem. The initial version compiles with no sorries, but an expert review found serious problems in definitions, theorem generality, file organization, and the API. We then ran a review-driven refactor and compression process and obtained a second expert review. The before-and-after comparison shows a sharp split: agents adapted well to local, mechanically checkable feedback, but remained weak at choosing definitions and designing APIs. We argue that autoformalization should be evaluated not only by closed sorries, but by whether the resulting formalization survives expert review.

</details>


### [172] [Adversarial Concept Search: Predicting Compositional Errors From Feature Geometry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13934)
*Jennifer Meng Lu,Ruochen Zhang,Isabelle Lee,David Alvarez-Melis,Ellie Pavlick,Naomi Saphra*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLMs fail to compose concepts when their linear encodings are close (causing interference), but succeed when encodings are near-orthogonal. Representational geometry can thus predict compositional failures without evaluating specific inputs, enabling proactive identification of high-risk examples.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Humans cannot reliably intuit which scenarios will challenge LLMs. Current approaches rely on human-designed hard problems or exhaustive benchmarks, which are inefficient and may miss edge cases. The authors propose instead to anticipate model failures by analyzing internal representations, specifically targeting compositional failures arising from interference between salient features.

Method: The authors analyze an LLM's representational geometry — the linear encodings of concepts in the model's activation space. They measure the similarity (e.g., cosine similarity) between encodings of concept pairs. The core hypothesis: when two concepts are encoded near-orthogonally, the model reliably composes them; when their encodings are close (producing interference), composition fails. This is tested across toy programmatic settings, multihop reasoning, and multilingual factual recall tasks.

Result: Near-orthogonal concept encodings consistently yield successful composition, while close encodings produce interference and failure. The method reliably anticipates failure modes across diverse compositional tasks without evaluating specific inputs.

Conclusion: Representational geometry serves as a principled, scalable foundation for identifying high-risk examples, constructing targeted stress tests, and enabling active learning in real-world LLM deployment.

Abstract: Humans cannot always intuit what scenarios are most challenging to LLMs. Hoping to capture challenging edge cases, developers either design problems to be difficult for humans or curate extensive benchmarks. What if we could instead anticipate which scenarios a model will fail on? In this paper, we use an LLM's representational geometry to predict which concept combinations it will fail on. We attribute this compositional failure to interference between salient features. In tasks that require systematic composition - toy programmatic settings, multihop reasoning, multilingual factual recall - we find that when a pair of concepts is encoded near-orthogonally, the model reliably composes them. When their linear encodings are close, producing interference, the model fails to compose them. Our method reliably anticipates failure modes across different compositional tasks, without evaluating specific inputs. These results lay the groundwork to use representational geometry to identify high-risk examples, construct targeted stress tests, and provide a scalable foundation for active learning in real-world deployment.

</details>


### [173] [Minim: Privacy-Aware Minimal View for Agents via Trusted Local Sanitization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13949)
*Hexuan Yu,Chaoyu Zhang,Heng Jin,Shanghao Shi,Ning Zhang,Y. Thomas Hou,Wenjing Lou*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MINIM is a client-side privacy broker for LLM agents that uses a dual-score system (sensitivity + task-necessity) grounded in Contextual Integrity to selectively disclose, abstract, or redact UI elements before sending observations to remote inference servers, reducing sensitive data leakage while preserving task-critical context.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM-powered autonomous agents often transmit full UI state to remote servers, leaking sensitive content (auth codes, private notifications, background app states). Most UI elements are task-irrelevant, yet existing deployments lack principled client-side minimization.

Method: MINIM runs as a trusted local broker. It learns a dual-score representation per UI element: an inherent sensitivity score (s) and a task-conditioned necessity score (n). These drive a ternary disclosure policy: keep essential elements, abstract sensitive attributes, or remove task-irrelevant content. Training optimizes a Contextual Integrity-aware objective that penalizes necessity errors more heavily on high-risk content.

Result: Experiments on WebArena-derived real-world UI observations show MINIM substantially reduces task-irrelevant sensitive leakage while preserving task-critical semantic context and interactive affordances required for reliable agent actions.

Conclusion: Client-side privacy minimization using CI-grounded dual scoring is an effective approach for balancing privacy and agent utility, enabling aggressive pruning without degrading agent performance.

Abstract: Modern LLM-powered autonomous agents increasingly rely on rich user interface (UI) state observations to achieve reliable action grounding in complex digital environments. However, many deployments transmit the full UI state to remote inference servers even when most elements are irrelevant to the current task, which can leak sensitive but unnecessary context such as authentication codes, private notifications, and background application states. We propose MINIM, a trusted local broker that performs privacy-aware minimization on the client side before any observation leaves the device. Grounded in Contextual Integrity (CI), MINIM learns a dual-score representation for each UI element by predicting an inherent sensitivity score (s) and a task-conditioned necessity score (n). These scores drive a ternary disclosure policy that keeps essential elements, abstracts sensitive attributes when needed, and removes task-irrelevant content. We optimize a CI-aware objective that penalizes necessity errors more strongly on high-risk content, enabling aggressive pruning while preserving task-critical information. Experiments on real-world UI observations derived from WebArena show that MINIM substantially reduces task-irrelevant sensitive leakage while preserving task-critical semantic context and the interactive affordances required for reliable agent actions.

</details>


### [174] [Formalizing Numerical Analysis: An Agent Pipeline and Quality Audit Beyond Kernel Acceptance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14000)
*Theodore Meek,Siyuan Ge,Di Qiu Xiang,Simon Chess,Vasily Ilin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper applies a coding agent to formalize numerical analysis (Numerical Methods for ODEs) in Lean 4 — a domain underrepresented in mathlib — and introduces a three-dimensional evaluation framework (semantic correctness, Mathlib reuse, cross-file reuse) using LLM-as-judge. It reveals that compilation-based metrics significantly overstate formalization quality by uncovering recurring unfaithful patterns in prior systems (RepoProver, M2F).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing autoformalization efforts (1) focus on branches of mathematics already well-represented in mathlib, and (2) measure success solely by kernel acceptance (compilation), which obscures semantic unfaithfulness. The authors aim to test whether coding agents can formalize underrepresented areas from scratch and to establish a more rigorous, reproducible evaluation framework beyond compilation.

Method: The authors deploy a coding agent to formalize a textbook on Numerical Methods for ODEs in Lean 4. They propose a three-dimensional evaluation framework assessing: (1) semantic correctness, (2) Mathlib reuse, and (3) cross-file reuse, all evaluated via LLM-as-judge methods. This framework is applied to their own formalization and to outputs from RepoProver and M2F.

Result: The evaluation uncovers recurring unfaithful formalization patterns across all systems: incomplete multi-part statements, added weakening hypotheses, and parameter restrictions — all hidden by compilation-based metrics. The results demonstrate that kernel acceptance substantially overstates true formalization quality.

Conclusion: Compilation alone is an insufficient quality metric for autoformalization. The proposed three-dimensional LLM-as-judge framework provides a reproducible audit methodology for more rigorous evaluation of future autoformalization systems, and coding agents show promise for formalizing underrepresented mathematical domains.

Abstract: Recent work has demonstrated that coding agents can formalize entire advanced mathematics textbooks in Lean 4, yet existing efforts concentrate on branches of mathematics already well-represented in mathlib and measure success solely through kernel acceptance. We address both limitations by applying a coding agent to formalize Numerical Methods for Ordinary Differential Equations, a textbook in numerical analysis that is largely absent from mathlib, stressing the agent's capacity to develop new theory from scratch. We further introduce a systematic, reproducible three-dimensional framework for evaluating the quality of agent-produced formalizations beyond compilation: semantic correctness, Mathlib reuse, and cross-file reuse via LLM-as-judge methods. Applying this framework to our own formalization and to the released outputs of RepoProver and M2F, we uncover recurring unfaithful formalization patterns, including incomplete multi-part statements, added weakening hypotheses, and parameter restrictions, that kernel acceptance entirely obscures. Our results suggest that compilation-based metrics substantially overstate formalization quality, and we provide a reproducible audit methodology to support more rigorous evaluation of future autoformalization systems.

</details>


### [175] [Applicability Condition Extraction for Therapeutic Drug-Disease Relations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14031)
*Guanting Luo,Noriki Nishida,Yuji Matsumoto,Yuki Arase*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces a new task: extracting applicability conditions (context-specific factors) under which a drug therapeutically affects a disease, rather than merely identifying binary drug-disease relations. A manually annotated dataset of 1,119 drug-disease-condition triples is created, and a novel LoRA-enhanced method is proposed that outperforms existing baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing biomedical information extraction methods focus on identifying drug-disease relations but overlook the specific conditions (e.g., dosage, patient subgroup, combination therapy) under which those relations hold. This limits clinical decision-making support, as therapeutic efficacy is often context-dependent.

Method: The authors (1) define the task of applicability condition extraction for therapeutic drug-disease relations; (2) construct the first manually annotated dataset of drug-disease-condition triples from 1,119 biomedical abstracts; (3) systematically benchmark existing methods; and (4) propose a new method that enhances Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) by incorporating relational information between drugs and diseases to better extract triples.

Result: The proposed LoRA-enhanced method consistently outperforms strong baselines across multiple evaluation settings on the newly created dataset, demonstrating the effectiveness of explicitly modeling drug-disease relations for condition extraction.

Conclusion: Applicability condition extraction is a valuable and challenging new task for biomedical NLP. Explicitly modeling relational context between drugs and diseases improves extraction performance. The released dataset and code provide a foundation for future research.

Abstract: Identifying conditions that a certain drug takes therapeutic effect on a target disease is crucial for clinical decision-making support. However, most existing biomedical information extraction methods have focused on identifying only relations between drugs and diseases, while largely overlooking the context-specific conditions where such relations can apply. To address this problem, we introduce the task of applicability condition extraction for therapeutic drug--disease relations from biomedical research literature. We create the first dataset that has manually annotated triples of drugs, diseases, and applicability conditions on biomedical paper abstracts with 1,119 drug-disease pairs. Using this dataset, we systematically evaluate the performance of a range of existing methods. In addition, we propose a new method that enhances LoRA to consider relations between drugs and diseases. Our method consistently outperforms strong baselines across different evaluation settings. The source code and dataset of this paper can be obtained from: https://github.com/guantingluo98/Drug-ACE

</details>


### [176] [FactoryLLM: A Safe and Open-Source AI Playground for Evaluating LLMs in Smart Factories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14119)
*Yash Pulse,Yong-Bin Kang,Abhik Banerjee,Abdur Forkan,Prem Prakash Jayaraman*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: FactoryLLM is an open-source, safe AI playground for evaluating LLM-based retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) models on cross-machine manufacturing documentation. It supports local/open-source LLMs to protect sensitive industrial data and uses dual evaluation metrics (RAGAS and NVIDIA LLM-as-a-Judge). A case study with 30 queries over ~600 pages of AIV documentation showed all models achieving groundedness above 0.88.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fault diagnostics and recovery in smart factories is hindered by critical information being scattered across manuals of multiple interconnected machines. There is a need for a safe, controllable environment to evaluate how LLM-based RAG models reason over multi-machine documentation without exposing sensitive industrial data.

Method: FactoryLLM, an open-source AI playground, allows users to configure LLMs and evaluate RAG-based reasoning over multiple manufacturing documents. It employs a dual evaluation setup combining RAGAS metrics and NVIDIA's LLM-as-a-Judge metrics. The framework supports running local or open-source LLMs to maintain data confidentiality.

Result: In a case study involving an Autonomous Intelligent Vehicle (AIV) and its Mobile Planner software, three LLMs were evaluated across 30 maintenance queries derived from ~600 pages of cross-machine documentation. All models achieved a groundedness score above 0.88, demonstrating effective cross-machine document reasoning.

Conclusion: FactoryLLM is effective for cross-machine document reasoning in smart factory maintenance scenarios. The framework provides a safe, configurable, and reproducible environment for evaluating LLM-based RAG systems. The code and documentation are publicly available for community use in manufacturing-specific scenarios.

Abstract: Fault diagnostics and recovery in smart factories is challenging because critical information is dispersed across manuals of multiple machines which are interconnected through the manufacturing process. Large Language Models (LLMs) can provide a promising approach. In this paper, we propose FactoryLLM, a safe and open-source AI playground designed for evaluating different LLM-based retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) models by analysing documents from multiple machines across the manufacturing process. FactoryLLM enables the user to configure the LLM, and assess performance when reasoning over multiple documents, through a dual evaluation setup using both RAGAS and NVIDIA's LLM-as-a-Judge metrics. FactoryLLM is safe because it allows users to run local or open-source LLMs without sharing sensitive industrial data, providing a controlled environment for experimentation. We demonstrate the efficacy of FactoryLLM through a case study which involves an Autonomous Intelligent Vehicle and its Mobile Planner software, evaluating three LLMs across 30 maintenance queries derived from approximately 600 pages of cross-machine documentation. The results suggest that FactoryLLM is effective in cross-machine document reasoning: every model achieved a groundedness score above 0.88. The full code and documentation for community to test FactoryLLM with their manufacturing specific scenarios are publicly available.

</details>


### [177] [VeriGeo: Controllable Geometry Question Generation with Numerical and Analytical Verification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14176)
*Xiaoxian Duan,Zequn Liu,Yingce Xia*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: VeriGeo is a controllable geometry problem generation framework that uses executable reasoning traces and a three-stage verification pipeline to ensure consistency between problem statements, diagrams, constraints, and solutions. It employs Author and Solver agents sharing a verifiable action sequence, with verification-guided reflection to repair failures. SFT on 8.7k VeriGeo-generated examples achieves state-of-the-art GeoQA performance among end-to-end multimodal LLM solvers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reliable geometry problem synthesis is challenging because problem statements, diagrams, constraints, and solutions must be mutually consistent. Existing methods trade off controllability against reliability: seed-based rewriting offers flexibility but weak verifiability, while diagram-first construction improves validity but struggles with arbitrary user-specified constraints. A framework that combines both controllability and verifiable consistency is needed.

Method: VeriGeo uses two cooperative agents — an Author that generates problems and diagrams, and a Solver that produces proof-aligned solutions — both operating on a shared action sequence linking natural language, diagrams, geometric constraints, and proof steps into a verifiable representation. A three-stage verification pipeline checks (1) numerical consistency, (2) analytical realizability, and (3) global consistency. Verification-guided reflection repairs recoverable failures and rejects unrecoverable ones. The framework also produces 8.7k high-quality examples for supervised fine-tuning.

Result: Raw generations across five LLM backbones frequently fail consistency checks, but VeriGeo repairs a substantial fraction of invalid attempts. SFT on the 8.7k VeriGeo-generated dataset achieves state-of-the-art GeoQA performance among end-to-end multimodal LLM-based solvers, with strong results also on PGPS9K and MathVista-GPS benchmarks.

Conclusion: VeriGeo demonstrates that executable reasoning traces combined with multi-stage verification and reflection provide an effective approach to controllable, reliable geometry problem generation. The resulting verified synthetic data significantly improves multimodal geometry reasoning when used for supervised fine-tuning.

Abstract: Geometry problem generation is useful for AI-assisted education and multimodal mathematical reasoning, but reliable synthesis remains difficult because the problem statement, diagram, constraints, and solution should be mutually consistent. Existing methods often trade off controllability and reliability: seed-based rewriting is flexible but weakly verifiable, whereas diagram-first construction improves validity but is less suited to arbitrary user-specified constraints. We introduce VeriGeo, a controllable geometry generation framework grounded in executable reasoning traces. Given user constraints such as target concepts and difficulty, an Author agent generates a problem and diagram, and a Solver agent produces a proof-aligned solution. Both agents use a shared action sequence that connects natural language, diagrams, geometric constraints, and proof steps into a verifiable representation. A three-stage pipeline checks numerical consistency, analytical realizability, and global consistency, using verification-guided reflection to repair recoverable failures and reject unrecoverable ones. Across five LLM backbones, raw generations frequently fail these checks, while VeriGeo repairs a substantial fraction of the invalid attempts. Supervised fine-tuning on 8.7k examples generated by VeriGeo achieves the best reported GeoQA performance among end-to-end multimodal LLM-based solvers, and obtains strong results on PGPS9K and MathVista-GPS, demonstrating the effectiveness of verified synthetic data for improving multimodal geometry reasoning.

</details>


### [178] [When Should Agent Trust Be Conditional? Characterizing and Attacking Skill-Conditional Reputation in Agent Swarms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14200)
*Yihan Xia,Taotao Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper studies skill-conditional trust for heterogeneous LLM agents, showing that conditioning on skills yields gains only in specific regimes (high heterogeneity, sparse evidence, correlated skills). However, cross-skill evidence borrowing is dual-use: it enables data efficiency but also creates a laundering channel for attackers, who can hijack conditional routers with cheap evidence in one skill. The authors quantify this trade-off rather than claiming Sybil-resistance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Open platforms increasingly route tasks among heterogeneous LLM agents with sharply varying skill-specific competence. Standard global reputation scores are inadequate because they fail to capture specialization value. The authors investigate when and whether skill-conditional trust is beneficial versus vulnerable.

Method: The authors pose three falsifiable questions: (1) when is conditioning worth it, (2) how much cross-skill evidence should be borrowed, (3) whether that borrowing is safe. They use controlled phase-diagram analysis for the first two and a novel zero-cost Conditional Information Value Test (CIVT) to assess pool safety. They evaluate on a public benchmark of 14 heterogeneous AppWorld agents and model an attacker with cheap evidence in one skill targeting another skill through the router.

Result: Conditional trust wins only under high agent heterogeneity, sparse per-skill evidence, and correlated skills. Real pools fall in the beneficial regime with small but genuine gains. However, an attacker can hijack the conditional router, driving routing regret from 0 to 0.94 on a CIVT-rated GREEN pool, while the ungated trust verdict reads -0.06 instead of +0.19. A zero-evidence gate bounds but does not eliminate the attack; residual cost is characterized under an explicit budget.

Conclusion: The authors do not claim Sybil-resistance but instead quantify the trade-off between the data-efficiency benefits of cross-skill borrowing and its vulnerability as an attack laundering channel. Skill-conditional trust offers real but modest gains in realistic settings, and must be deployed with awareness of its dual-use nature.

Abstract: Open platforms increasingly route tasks among heterogeneous LLM agents--differing in base model, scaffold, and tool stack--whose competence varies sharply by skill: an agent excellent at one skill may be useless at another. The standard reputation approach summarizes each agent by a single global trust score, but that scalar is the wrong object here, because routing every task to the globally most-trusted agent leaves the value of specialization unclaimed. We study skill-conditional trust R(i | k)--the trust to place in agent i for a task requiring skill k, rather than one score per agent--and pose three falsifiable questions: when is conditioning worth it, how much cross-skill evidence should be borrowed, and whether that borrowing is safe. A controlled phase-diagram analysis answers the first two: conditional trust wins only in a specific regime--high agent heterogeneity, sparse per-skill evidence, and correlated skills--and the coupling strength beta that buys this data efficiency is dual-use, because the same cross-skill borrowing is also a laundering channel. On a public benchmark of 14 genuinely heterogeneous AppWorld agents, real pools land inside the beneficial regime--a small but genuine gain, with the per-skill best agent genuinely changing across skills. We then show that an attacker with cheap evidence in one skill and none in a target skill hijacks the conditional router, driving routing regret from 0 to 0.94 on a pool our zero-cost Conditional Information Value Test (CIVT) rates GREEN--while the ungated trust verdict it contaminates reads -0.06 instead of the honest +0.19. A zero-evidence gate bounds the attack but does not eliminate it; we characterize the residual cost under an explicit budget. We do not claim Sybil-resistance--we quantify the trade-off.

</details>


### [179] [Closing the Reflection Gap: A Free Calibration Bonus for Agentic RL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14211)
*Yinglun Zhu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLM agents suffer from a "reflection gap" where they mis-assess their own outputs even after seeing environment feedback. The authors propose RefGRPO, which adds a calibration bonus (computed by contrasting self-reflection with actual outcomes) and a dynamic coefficient schedule to standard RL, improving both reflection calibration and task accuracy without needing extra reward models or annotations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM agents deployed in interactive environments (e.g., text-to-SQL) receive execution feedback but persistently fail to accurately self-assess their performance — a phenomenon the authors term the "reflection gap." Standard RL fails to address this due to a credit-assignment mismatch: rewards based solely on task outcomes do not incentivize accurate self-reflection. Closing this gap is crucial for enabling agents to serve as their own verifiers, facilitating self-improvement and selective prediction.

Method: RefGRPO augments standard Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) with two components: (1) a calibration bonus that is computed for free by comparing the agent's own binary reflection (correct/incorrect) against the actual task outcome, rewarding alignment without any external reward model, LLM judge, or human annotation; and (2) a dynamic schedule that adjusts the bonus coefficient over training to balance task performance and reflection calibration. The method is applied to text-to-SQL tasks across multiple benchmarks.

Result: RefGRPO significantly reduces underconfidence rate from 44.4% to 7.7% while simultaneously improving task accuracy from 75.1% to 76.5% on text-to-SQL across five benchmarks. The calibrated reflections further enable: (i) self-improvement using reflections as pseudo-rewards without outcome supervision, and (ii) effective test-time selective prediction by committing only to rollouts flagged as correct.

Conclusion: The reflection gap is a real and addressable deficiency in LLM agents. RefGRPO provides a lightweight, annotation-free fix that not only aligns self-assessment with actual outcomes but also unlocks downstream capabilities — the agent becomes its own reliable verifier, enabling autonomous self-improvement and selective deployment without external supervision.

Abstract: LLMs are increasingly deployed as agents that interact with external environments and observe feedback such as execution results, error messages, and tool outputs. A well-functioning agent should be able to leverage this feedback to accurately assess its own performance. Yet we find a persistent reflection gap: LLM agents tend to mis-assess their own outputs after observing concrete environment feedback -- even for questions they correctly answered -- and standard RL barely helps due to a credit-assignment mismatch. To close this gap, we propose RefGRPO, a simple yet effective fix that augments standard RL algorithms with two key ingredients: a free calibration bonus computed by contrasting the agent's own reflection with the actual outcome (requiring no additional reward model, LLM judge, or external annotation), and a dynamic schedule on its coefficient. Compared to standard RL baselines, our method simultaneously improves reflection calibration (e.g., reduces underconfidence rate $44.4\% \to 7.7\%$) and task accuracy (e.g., $75.1\% \to 76.5\%$) on text-to-SQL across five benchmarks. The resulting calibrated reflection turns the agent into its own verifier grounded in environment feedback, which further enables (i) better self-improvement that uses reflections as pseudo-rewards without outcome supervision, and (ii) more effective test-time selective prediction by committing only to rollouts flagged as correct.

</details>


### [180] [SkillAudit: Ground-Truth-Free Skill Evolution via Paired Trajectory Auditing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14239)
*Haowen Gao,Haoran Chen,Can Wang,Shasha Guo,Liang Pang,Zhaoyang Liu,Huawei Shen,Xueqi Cheng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SkillAudit is a zero-feedback framework for evolving LLM agent skills by comparing trajectory differences from paired task executions (with/without a skill) and using Process-Aligned Contrastive Evaluation (PACE) to generate diagnostic edit signals, achieving 73.9% average reward across 89 tasks across 8 domains without ground-truth feedback.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deployed agent skills inevitably degrade due to edge cases, API changes, and deployment constraints that only surface during use. However, existing skill evolution methods rely on privileged feedback (held-out validation, hidden tests, environment rewards) that is often unavailable to practitioners who only have a task description and workspace data. There is a need for skill evolution without ground-truth feedback.

Method: SkillAudit uses paired trajectory auditing: each task is executed twice per iteration — once with and once without the candidate skill — to isolate behavioral differences. These differences are analyzed by Process-Aligned Contrastive Evaluation (PACE), a cluster of evaluators that maps trajectory divergences to diagnostic signals tied to specific skill-document passages. A structural verifier (compiled once from the task specification) enforces task constraints and rolls back harmful edits. Two editing pipelines handle revisions: Refine removes noisy or irrelevant guidance, while Repair replaces passages conflicting with the task.

Result: Across 89 containerized tasks in 8 professional domains, SkillAudit achieves 73.9% average task reward, compared to 40.9% (no-skill agent) and 56.7% (static expert skill), without access to hidden tests, reference solutions, or external scoring functions during evolution.

Conclusion: SkillAudit demonstrates that trajectory-level self-comparison, combined with structured verification and targeted editing, can effectively evolve agent skills in the absence of ground-truth feedback, substantially outperforming both unskilled agents and static expert-crafted skills.

Abstract: Agent skills are structured procedural packages that guide frozen LLM agents in specialized workflows. Skills rarely remain sufficient after deployment: edge cases, API changes, and deployment constraints become visible only through use, making skill evolution a practical necessity. Existing methods depend on privileged feedback such as held-out validation scores, hidden test outcomes, or environment rewards -- signals often unavailable when a practitioner has only a task description and workspace data. We introduce SkillAudit, a framework for evolving agent skills without ground-truth feedback. The key idea is paired trajectory auditing: at each iteration, the same task is executed with and without the candidate skill, isolating how the skill changes agent behavior without external labels. To turn behavioral differences into edit guidance, SkillAudit uses Process-Aligned Contrastive Evaluation (PACE), a cluster of evaluators that maps trajectory divergences to diagnostic signals linked to specific passages in the skill document. A structural verifier, compiled once from the task specification and then fixed, checks task constraints and rolls back harmful updates. SkillAudit routes edits through two pipelines: Refine removes noisy or irrelevant guidance from broadly useful skills, while Repair replaces passages that conflict with the task. Across 89 containerized tasks spanning 8 professional domains, SkillAudit achieves 73.9% average task reward, outperforming an agent without skills (40.9%) and the static expert skill (56.7%). These gains are obtained without accessing hidden tests, reference solutions, or external scoring functions during evolution.

</details>


### [181] [AFFORDANCE20Q: Evaluating Affordance Reasoning from Physical Properties](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14240)
*Yifan Jiang,Meige Yang,Zitong Li,Jay Pujara*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Affordance20Q is a benchmark that evaluates LLMs' affordance reasoning through a 20-Questions game where models must infer an object's affordance from physical properties without knowing the object's identity. Experiments with 15 LLMs reveal a ~20-point gap versus humans, and the proposed KARI pipeline—which induces affordance rules from knowledge bases—improves open-source models by up to 15.2 points.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing affordance benchmarks expose explicit object identities, allowing LLMs to rely on memorized object-affordance associations rather than genuine physical reasoning. The authors aim to isolate and evaluate reasoning over physical properties (shape, material) by concealing object identity.

Method: (1) Affordance20Q benchmark: 1,009 games across 454 objects and 59 affordances, structured as a 20-Questions game where models ask yes/no questions about physical properties to identify a hidden object's affordance from a candidate set. (2) KARI (KB-Anchored Rule Induction): an LLM-based pipeline that generates affordance rules grounded in knowledge base evidence to improve reasoning performance.

Result: A ~20-point performance gap exists between 15 state-of-the-art LLMs and human performance. KL-based information-gain analysis reveals models fail to ask discriminating questions as games progress. KARI improves open-source LLMs by up to 15.2 points, though limited KB coverage constrains further gains.

Conclusion: Affordance20Q exposes a significant weakness in LLMs' ability to reason about object affordances from physical properties alone. KARI demonstrates that grounding in structured knowledge can partially address this gap, but KB coverage remains a bottleneck, suggesting future work on richer knowledge integration.

Abstract: Affordance reasoning, the inference of an object's action possibilities from its physical properties (e.g., shape and material), is fundamental to human physical understanding and increasingly critical for Large Language Models (LLMs). However, existing affordance benchmarks largely expose explicit object identities in the evaluation setup, allowing models to rely on memorized object-affordance mappings rather than reasoning over physical properties. To address this gap, we introduce Affordance20Q, a novel affordance reasoning benchmark formulated as a 20-Questions game without exposing the object's identity. In each game, the model identifies a hidden object's affordance from a candidate set by asking yes/no questions about its physical properties. Affordance20Q comprises 1,009 games over 454 objects and 59 affordances, all manually filtered, refined, and annotated. We conduct comprehensive experiments with 15 state-of-the-art LLMs and find a substantial gap (~20 points) compared to human performance. A KL-based information-gain (IG) analysis further shows that models fail to ask discriminating questions as the game progresses. To close the gap, we develop KB-Anchored Rule Induction (KARI), a pipeline based on LLMs that generates affordance rules grounded in evidence from knowledge bases (KBs). KARI improves open-source LLMs by up to 15.2 points, while the limited coverage of KBs hinders further gains. We release all our code and data at https://github.com/1171-jpg/Affordance20Q.git

</details>


### [182] [HarnessX: A Composable, Adaptive, and Evolvable Agent Harness Foundry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14249)
*Tingyang Chen,Shuo Lu,Kang Zhao,Weicheng Meng,Hanlin Teng,Tianhao Li,Chao Li,Xule Liu,Jian Liang,Zhizhong Zhang,Yuan Xie,Heng Qu,Kun Shao,Jian Luan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: HarnessX introduces a composable, adaptive agent harness foundry that uses typed primitives, a substitution algebra, and a trace-driven multi-agent evolution engine (AEGIS) to automatically improve agent performance from execution feedback, achieving an average +14.5% gain across five benchmarks without model scaling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current agent harnesses (prompts, tools, memory, control flow) are hand-crafted, static, and task-specific. Execution traces—rich sources of improvement signal—are rarely used systematically. There is a need for an automated, feedback-driven method to compose and evolve runtime interfaces, decoupling agent progress from model scaling alone.

Method: HarnessX comprises: (1) typed harness primitives composed via a substitution algebra; (2) AEGIS, a trace-driven multi-agent evolution engine that establishes an operational mirror between symbolic adaptation and reinforcement learning; and (3) a closed-loop mechanism converting execution trajectories into both harness updates and model training signal.

Result: Across five benchmarks—ALFWorld, GAIA, WebShop, τ³-Bench, and SWE-bench Verified—HarnessX achieves an average performance gain of +14.5% (up to +44.0%), with the largest improvements observed where baselines are weakest.

Conclusion: Agent progress need not rely solely on model scaling; composing and evolving runtime interfaces from execution feedback provides a complementary and actionable lever. The codebase will be open-sourced.

Abstract: AI agent performance depends critically on the runtime harness, comprising the prompts, tools, memory, and control flow that mediate how a model observes, reasons, and acts. Yet today's harnesses remain largely hand-crafted and static: each new model or task still demands bespoke scaffolding, and the rich traces produced during execution are rarely distilled back into systematic improvement. We introduce HarnessX, a foundry for composable, adaptive, and evolvable agent harnesses. HarnessX assembles typed harness primitives via a substitution algebra, adapts them through AEGIS, a trace-driven multi-agent evolution engine grounded in an operational mirror between symbolic adaptation and reinforcement learning, and closes the harness-model loop by turning trajectories into both harness updates and model training signal. Across five benchmarks (ALFWorld, GAIA, WebShop, tau^3-Bench, and SWE-bench Verified), HarnessX yields an average gain of +14.5% (up to +44.0%), with gains largest where baselines are lowest. These results suggest that agent progress need not come from model scaling alone: composing and evolving runtime interfaces from execution feedback is an actionable and complementary lever. The complete codebase will be open-sourced in a future release.

</details>


### [183] [Communication Policy Evolution for Proactive LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14314)
*Xinbei Ma,Jiyang Qiu,Yao Yao,Zheng Wu,Yijie Lu,Xiangmou Qu,Jiaxin Yin,Xingyu Lou,Jun Wang,Weiwen Liu,Weinan Zhang,Zhuosheng Zhang,Hai Zhao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper formalizes "Communication Policy" for LLM agents, comparing text-based and UI-based interaction modalities. Experiments show text aids task performance while structured UI improves response quality and persona compliance. A hybrid method and a self-evolution framework (CPE) are proposed, with CPE achieving best task success through prompt refinement alone, establishing communication behavior as a critical design dimension for LLM agents.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: A persistent information gap exists between users and LLM agents because communication is costly and users often have repetitive, identical preferences, which limits information exchange. There is a lack of systematic understanding of how agents should communicate across different modalities to bridge this gap effectively.

Method: The authors formalize the concept of Communication Policy, establishing both textual and UI-based policies. They evaluate these across diverse environments, personas, and model combinations under two complementary settings with information asymmetry: User-Agent and Planner-Executor. They propose a hybrid method combining text and UI strengths, and develop Communication Policy Evolution (CPE), a self-evolution framework that refines communication policies through rollout and prompt-level evolution without model modification.

Result: Text-based interaction facilitates task performance, while structured UI improves response quality and persona compliance. The hybrid method captures advantages of both channels. CPE achieves the best task success across multiple settings using only prompt refinement, without any model parameter changes.

Conclusion: Communication behavior is identified as a critical yet underexplored design dimension for LLM agents. The findings suggest that optimizing communication policy — particularly through self-evolving, hybrid approaches — can substantially improve agent performance without requiring model-level modifications.

Abstract: LLM agents have rapidly evolved into autonomous systems, yet a persistent information gap remains between users and agents: communication is costly, while users' identical preferences further limit information exchange.
