To investigate how agents should communicate across modalities, this paper formalizes Communication Policy, establishes textual and UI-based policies, and then evaluates communication policies across diverse environments, personas, and model combinations. Building information asymmetry for proactive agents, we set up two complementary settings, User-Agent and Planner-Executor. Experimental results reveal complementary strengths between interaction channels: text-based interaction often facilitates task performance, while structured UI improves agents' response quality and persona compliance. Motivated by that, a hybrid method combines these advantages. We further propose Communication Policy Evolution (CPE), a self-evolution framework for refining communication policies through rollout and prompt-level evolving. Without model modification, CPE achieves the best task success across multiple settings using prompt refinement alone. Our findings identify communication behavior as a critical yet underexplored design dimension for LLM agents.

</details>


### [184] [CSPO: Constraint-Sensitive Policy Optimization for Safe Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14415)
*Ayoub Belouadah,Sylvain Kubler,Yves Le Traon*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CSPO is a first-order primal-dual Safe RL method that adds a constraint-sensitive correction term to policy updates, derived from the shortest signed distance to the safety boundary, enabling faster recovery from violations and reduced oscillations while preserving optimal KKT solutions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Primal-dual methods for Safe RL scale well but suffer from delayed constraint correction due to slow Lagrange multiplier updates, causing oscillatory behavior near safety boundaries and prolonged safety violations during training.

Method: CSPO augments the standard primal objective with a constraint-sensitive correction term computed from the shortest signed distance to the safety boundary. This is a first-order method that modifies policy updates to incorporate local constraint sensitivity, enabling proactive safety recovery without waiting for multiplier adjustments. The correction preserves the KKT conditions of the original CMDP formulation.

Result: On navigation and locomotion benchmarks, CSPO achieves faster safety recovery and higher reward preservation, yielding higher constrained returns compared to state-of-the-art primal-dual and penalty-based baselines.

Conclusion: Incorporating local constraint sensitivity directly into policy updates is an effective strategy for mitigating the delayed-correction problem in primal-dual Safe RL, leading to more stable and safer learning.

Abstract: Safe reinforcement learning (Safe RL) aims to maximize expected return while satisfying safety constraints, typically modeled as Constrained Markov Decision Processes (CMDPs). While primal-dual methods scale well to deep RL, they often suffer from delayed constraint correction, leading to oscillatory behavior and prolonged safety violations. In this paper, we propose Constraint-Sensitive Policy Optimization (CSPO), a first-order primal-dual method that incorporates local constraint sensitivity into policy updates. CSPO augments the primal objective with a constraint-sensitive correction derived from the shortest signed distance to the safety boundary, enabling smarter recovery steps back to safety, compensating for delayed Lagrange multiplier updates, reducing oscillations near the boundary, and preserving the KKT solutions of the original constrained problem. Experiments on navigation and locomotion benchmarks demonstrate that CSPO achieves faster safety recovery and high reward preservation, resulting in higher constrained returns compared to state-of-the-art primal-dual and penalty-based methods

</details>


### [185] [Causal Object-Centric Models for Planning with Monte Carlo Tree Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14418)
*Rodion Vakhitov,Leonid Ugadiarov,Alexey Skrynnik,Aleksandr Panov*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: COMET introduces MuZero-style latent planning with explicit object-centric inductive biases, using a frozen object-slot encoder, action-slot fusion for transition prediction, and object-causal attention for policy/value heads, achieving faster early-training performance across diverse visual RL benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard model-based RL methods like MuZero lack explicit object-level structure, treating visual observations as monolithic representations. This makes latent planning inefficient, especially early in training, because the model does not exploit the compositional nature of visual scenes. COMET addresses this by injecting an object-centric inductive bias—actions and decisions should bind to individual task-relevant entities—directly into the latent world model and planning process.

Method: COMET consists of three core components: (1) a frozen unsupervised object-centric encoder (e.g., Slot Attention or DINOSAUR) that decomposes visual observations into a set of slot-structured latent vectors; (2) a transformer-based world model with a novel action-slot fusion mechanism where actions are bound to specific object slots for next-slot prediction, enabling object-wise transition dynamics; and (3) policy and value heads employing object-causal attention, where learned per-slot relevance scores modulate cross-slot interactions so that Monte Carlo Tree Search concentrates computation on task-relevant entities. The entire system is trained end-to-end atop the frozen encoder using MuZero-style value-equivalence objectives.

Result: Across eight tasks spanning four benchmarks—Object-Centric Visual RL, ManiSkill, Robosuite, and VizDoom—COMET achieves a higher mean normalized score during the early stages of training compared to both object-centric baselines (e.g., SMORL, OCVRL) and monolithic baselines (e.g., DreamerV3, MuZero), demonstrating faster learning and improved sample efficiency through its structured latent planning.

Conclusion: Injecting an explicit object-level inductive bias into MuZero-style latent planning via action-slot binding and object-causal attention yields substantial gains in early-training performance. COMET demonstrates that structured world models that reason about objects and their interactions can accelerate model-based RL across visually and dynamically diverse domains.

Abstract: We introduce COMET (Causal Object-centric Model for Efficient Tree search), a model-based reinforcement learning algorithm that performs Monte Carlo Tree Search in a slot-structured latent space. COMET pairs a frozen unsupervised object-centric encoder with a transformer-based world model, in which actions are bound to objects through a novel action-slot fusion mechanism that is used in slot transition prediction. Policy and value heads use object-causal attention, modulating token interactions by learned per-slot relevance scores so that decision-making concentrates on task-relevant entities. COMET adds an explicit object-level inductive bias to MuZero-style latent planning. Across eight visually and dynamically diverse tasks from the Object-Centric Visual RL benchmark, ManiSkill, Robosuite, and VizDoom, COMET achieves a higher mean normalized score during the early stages of training compared to object-centric and monolithic baselines.

</details>


### [186] [GitOfThoughts: Version-Controlled Reasoning and Agent Memory You Can Replay, Diff, and Merge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14470)
*Pavan C Shekar,Abhishek H S,Aswanth Krishnan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: GitOfThoughts stores LLM agent reasoning trees as git repositories for auditability and mergeability. Systematic experiments across substrates show memory only improves accuracy above a "copyability threshold" (similarity >0.8), where gains come from answer retrieval rather than method transfer. For novel problems, no memory format helps — test-time sampling is the only general lever.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM reasoning is ephemeral: chains of thought disappear with the context window, pruned search branches leave no trace, and memory buffers cannot be diffed, merged, or audited. While every other complex software artifact is version-controlled, reasoning lacks comparable provenance and reproducibility infrastructure. The authors aim to bridge this gap while also rigorously testing whether memory — in any substrate — actually improves reasoning accuracy.

Method: The authors introduce GitOfThoughts, which maps an agent's reasoning tree onto a git repository: each scored thought becomes a commit, scores are stored as git notes, outcomes as tags, and retrieval operates via "git log." They then conduct a systematic evaluation across five memory substrates (none, markdown, vector, graph, git), two benchmarks, and two model scales with pre-registered replications. The evaluation measures accuracy as a function of retrieval similarity to quantify a "copyability threshold" and disentangles answer retrieval from method transfer by testing larger models on worked examples.

Result: No memory format reliably improves accuracy for novel problems. A promising early result collapsed under pre-registered replication. Memory only helps above the copyability threshold (similarity >~0.8), where accuracy jumps sharply; below that threshold, no benefit. The gain is driven by answer retrieval, not method transfer — even a 4.5× larger model cannot extract a transferable method from a worked example, though it doubles the near-duplicate payoff. Test-time sampling is the only general lever for improvement. Git-as-substrate matches accuracy parity while providing auditability, provenance, and mergeability.

Conclusion: Memory is not a general-purpose lever for improving LLM reasoning on novel problems; its benefit is restricted to near-duplicate retrieval. Git-based reasoning storage offers engineering advantages (auditability, mergeability, provenance) at zero accuracy cost. The authors advocate for rigorous pre-registered replications in LLM research, self-documenting a retracted result and refuted hypothesis.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM) reasoning is ephemeral: chains of thought vanish with the context window, pruned search branches leave no record, and memory buffers cannot be diffed, merged, or audited. Every other complex software process (code, infrastructure, data, experiments) is version-controlled; reasoning is not. We introduce GitOfThoughts, which stores an agent's reasoning tree as a git repository: every scored thought is a commit, scores are notes, outcomes are tags, and retrieval is "git log" over the agent's own history. This makes reasoning replayable, auditable, and mergeable across agents at near-zero engineering cost.
  We then ask the harder question: does memory, in any substrate, actually improve accuracy? Across five substrates (none, markdown, vector, graph, git), two benchmarks, two model scales, and pre-registered replications, the answer for novel problems is no. No memory format reliably helps, and a promising early result collapsed under its own pre-registered replication. Memory pays only above what we call the copyability threshold: when the retrieved case is a near-duplicate of the current problem (similarity >~ 0.8), accuracy jumps sharply; below it, nothing. The gain is answer retrieval, not method transfer: a 4.5x larger model doubles the near-duplicate payoff yet still cannot extract a transferable method from a worked example. The only general lever we find is test-time sampling. The case for git-as-substrate is therefore auditability, provenance, and mergeability at accuracy parity. We document a retracted result and a refuted hypothesis to model the evaluation standard we hold ourselves to.

</details>


### [187] [When the Tool Decides: LLM Agents Defer Blindly to Graph Neural Network Tools, and Stronger Backbones Defer More](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14476)
*Zhongyuan Wang,Pratyusha Vemuri*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLM agents equipped with GNN tools do not exercise independent judgment; they overwhelmingly parrot the GNN's output (97.6–99.2% agreement). This deference increases with model scale, and the performance gap relative to an oracle grows because the agent's own alternatives improve while it remains pinned to the frozen tool. Selective invocation gates recover only partial gains, suggesting reliable tool judgment must be explicitly designed, not expected to emerge from scaling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work assumes LLM agents can judiciously decide when and how much to rely on GNN-based tools. This paper directly tests that assumption—measuring whether agents exercise judgment over tool invocation or merely defer to the tool's output wholesale.

Method: A frozen GNN is exposed to a ReAct-style LLM agent as an explicit callable tool for node classification on text-attributed graphs (ogbn-arxiv, replicated on WikiCS). The authors measure prediction agreement between the agent and the raw GNN across multiple seeds and model scales (Qwen2.5 0.5B–7B). They compare agent performance against a per-node action oracle and a simple neighbour-label tool, and evaluate a selective-invocation gate with held-out estimates of best-case headroom.

Result: Agent–GNN agreement reaches 97.6–99.2%, rising with model capability (0.60 at 1.5B to 0.98 at 7B). The per-node oracle outperforms the parrot by 0.09–0.18 (3B) and 0.12–0.22 (7B), with the gap roughly doubling at high homophily. At 7B, a neighbour-label tool surpasses the GNN (0.81 vs 0.71) at high homophily, yet the agent still defers to the GNN. A selective-invocation gate recovers ~half the high-homophily gap (0.71→0.83) but yields no net global gain; held-out bounds cap the best achievable gate at ≤⅓ of the oracle headroom.

Conclusion: The findings serve as a cautionary measurement: evaluations of agent+tool systems cannot assume the agent contributes independent judgment, and reliable selective invocation must be deliberately engineered rather than expected to emerge from scaling alone.

Abstract: A growing line of work equips large language model (LLM) agents with graph neural networks (GNNs) as callable tools, assuming the agent exercises judgment over when and how much to rely on such a tool. We test this directly. We expose a frozen GNN to a ReAct-style LLM agent as an explicit tool and measure, on node classification over a text-attributed graph (ogbn-arxiv, replicated on WikiCS), whether the agent uses the tool or merely obeys it. We find the agent does not exercise judgment: its predictions agree with the raw GNN's 97.6-99.2% of the time (5 seeds), collapsing into a GNN parrot that adopts the tool's output wholesale and bypasses its own reasoning. Sweeping backbone capability (Qwen2.5 0.5B-7B), the deference is not a weak-model artifact: among models able to invoke the tool, agreement rises with capability (0.60 to 0.98 from 1.5B to 7B). Crucially, the cost of deference does not shrink as capability grows and grows where alternatives emerge: a per-node oracle over the available actions beats the parrot by 0.09-0.18 at 3B and 0.12-0.22 at 7B, roughly doubling at high homophily, because the parrot is pinned to the frozen GNN while the agent's alternatives improve; at 7B a simple neighbour-label tool overtakes the GNN at high homophily (0.81 vs 0.71) yet the agent still defers. A simple selective-invocation gate recovers about half of that high-homophily gap (0.71 to 0.83) but yields no net global gain, and held-out estimates bound the best achievable gate over standard test-time features to at most a third of the oracle headroom: reliable selective invocation looks limited by available information, not merely router design. Our results are a cautionary measurement: evaluations of agent+tool systems cannot assume the agent adds judgment on top of the tool, and selective invocation must be designed in rather than expected to emerge from scale.

</details>


### [188] [From Chatbot to Digital Colleague: The Paradigm Shift Toward Persistent Autonomous AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14502)
*Yongheng Zhang,Ziang Liu,Jiaxuan Zhu,Shuai Wang,Xiangqi Chen,Haojing Huang,Jiayi Kuang,Siyu Chen,Ao Shen,Hao Wu,Qiufeng Wang,Qian-Wen Zhang,Junnan Dong,Wenhao Jiang,Ying Shen,Hai-Tao Zheng,Yinghui Li,Di Yin,Xing Sun,Philip S. Yu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper conceptualizes LLM evolution as a shift from "Chatbot" to "Digital Colleague," organized along two dimensions: (1) cognitive advancement from fast token-prediction to deliberate "Thinking LLMs" using inference-time reasoning and RL, and (2) tool-use advancement from ad hoc tool-calling Agents to persistent "Workspace + Skill" workstation systems (OpenClaw), with corresponding shifts in data construction and evaluation paradigms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs are transitioning from simple conversational interfaces to integrated AI systems capable of reasoning, action, memory, and self-improvement. The authors aim to provide a coherent conceptual framework for understanding and guiding this transformation toward persistent, reliable, colleague-like AI that can perform sustained work rather than merely generating conversational responses.

Method: The authors propose a two-dimensional analytical framework: (1) the cognitive core dimension, tracing progression from "fast thinking" (next-token prediction) to "Thinking LLMs" (inference-time computation, Chain-of-Thought, reflection, process supervision, and reinforcement learning); (2) the tool-augmented execution dimension, tracing progression from tool-calling Agents to OpenClaw-style workstation systems featuring persistent Workspaces, reusable Skills, verification loops, and governance. They also examine shifts in data construction (from instruction-response pairs to State-Action-Observation trajectories) and evaluation (from static benchmarks to sandboxed, auditable, self-evolving ecosystems).

Result: The framework identifies that the "Workspace + Skill" paradigm enables colleague-like tool use through state persistence, reusable procedures, task closure, and experience reuse. The analysis delineates a clear maturation path from ephemeral, turn-based interactions toward persistent, auditable, self-improving AI work systems.

Conclusion: The shift from Chatbot to Digital Colleague represents a fundamental re-architecture of LLM systems across cognition and tool-use dimensions. The convergence of deliberate reasoning (Thinking LLMs) and persistent task execution (OpenClaw/Workspace+Skill) points toward AI systems that function as reliable, self-improving collaborators capable of sustained, verifiable work.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) are undergoing a fundamental transformation from conversational generators into integrated AI systems capable of reasoning, action, memory, and self-improvement. We conceptualize this transition as a shift from Chatbot to Digital Colleague: from conversational answers to persistent work. We organize this transition along two tightly coupled dimensions. First, at the cognitive core level, LLMs are advancing from Chatbot-era "fast thinking" systems driven by next-token prediction toward Thinking LLMs that leverage inference-time computation, Chain-of-Thought reasoning, reflection, process supervision, and reinforcement learning to support more deliberate and reliable cognition. Second, at the tool-augmented task execution level, LLMs are progressing from tool-calling Agents that invoke external resources in an ad hoc manner toward OpenClaw-style workstation systems (OpenClaw) equipped with persistent Workspaces, skills, verification loops, and governance. The "Workspace + Skill" paradigm makes episodic tool use colleague-like via state persistence, reusable procedures, task closure, and experience reuse. We examine data construction shifts from instruction-response pairs to State-Action-Observation trajectories and evaluation from static benchmarks to sandboxed, auditable, self-evolving AI ecosystems.

</details>


### [189] [Dense Coordinate-List Fine-Tuning Induces a Controllable Interference Surface in Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14507)
*Chenyu Zhou,Qiliang Jiang,Boguang Pan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Fine-tuning VLMs with LoRA for dense coordinate output improves visual grounding but introduces structured repetition artifacts specific to bbox-coordinate lists. This interference surface is measurable, structure-bound, and controllable via object-level repeat-stop without sacrificing accuracy, generalizing across model families (Gemma 4, Qwen3-VL) and datasets (COCO 2017).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fine-tuning vision-language models to emit dense coordinate lists for visual grounding improves performance, but it also alters how models serialize, repeat, and terminate structured outputs. Understanding and controlling this generation behavior is critical for reliable structured prediction.

Method: The authors fine-tune Gemma 4 12B using LoRA adapters (q/k/v/o and q/v-only variants) with rank sweeps (4–64) for dense coordinate-list generation. They measure class-aware F1@0.3 and duplicate rate/max repeat as interference metrics. They introduce an object-level repeat-stop mechanism to suppress exact repeated records. Structure-axis probes isolate the effect by testing dense non-bbox outputs and spatial/count JSON formats. Generalization is tested on Qwen3-VL-8B and COCO 2017.

Result: High-capacity q/k/v/o LoRA raises F1@0.3 from 0.007 to 0.448 but induces a duplicate rate of 0.080 with max repeat of 23. A q/v-only rank sweep maintains max repeat at 21–22 across ranks 4–64. Object-level repeat-stop eliminates exact repeated records (duplicate rate 0.000, max repeat 1) while preserving F1 (0.494→0.490) and improving stricter F1@0.5 (0.381→0.385). The repetition effect localizes specifically to bbox-coordinate object lists; other structured formats remain clean. Qwen3-VL-8B reproduces a clean controlled endpoint (F1@0.3 0.318, duplicate rate 0.000), and COCO 2017 reproduces both acquisition gains and duplicate pressure.

Conclusion: Dense coordinate-list adaptation creates a structure-bound, cross-family interference surface—repetition artifacts tied specifically to bbox-coordinate lists—that can be precisely measured and effectively controlled via simple post-hoc repeat suppression without harming task performance.

Abstract: Fine-tuning vision-language models to emit dense coordinate lists improves visual grounding but also changes how models serialize, repeat, and terminate structured outputs. We study this behavior as a generation and control surface. In Gemma 4 12B, high-capacity q/k/v/o LoRA raises class-aware F1@0.3 from 0.007 to 0.448 while inducing repeated-tail pressure (duplicate rate 0.080, max repeat 23). A q/v rank sweep keeps max repeat at 21-22 across ranks 4-64, showing capacity persistence. The target signal is separable: object-level repeat-stop removes exact repeated records (duplicate rate 0.000, max repeat 1) while preserving F1 (0.494 to 0.490) and stricter F1@0.5 (0.381 to 0.385). Structure-axis probes localize the effect to bbox-coordinate object lists; dense non-bbox and spatial/count JSON remain repeat-clean, including under high-capacity adapters. Qwen3-VL-8B reproduces a clean controlled endpoint (F1@0.3 0.318, duplicate rate 0.000), and COCO 2017 reproduces acquisition plus duplicate pressure. Dense coordinate-list adaptation therefore creates a structure-bound, cross-family interference surface that can be measured and controlled.

</details>


### [190] [Every Eval Ever: A Unifying Schema and Community Repository for AI Evaluation Results](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14516)
*Jan Batzner,Sree Harsha Nelaturu,Anastassia Kornilova,Jon Crall,Tommaso Cerruti,Yanan Long,Yifan Mai,Sanchit Ahuja,Asaf Yehudai,Marek Šuppa,John P. Lalor,Oluwagbemike Olowe,Jatin Ganhotra,Brian H. Hu,Eliya Habba,Andrew M. Bean,Chang Liu,Sander Land,Steven Dillmann,Aniketh Garikaparthi,Elron Bandel,Saki Imai,James Edgell,Wm. Matthew Kennedy,Jenny Chim,Patrick Meusling,Asteria Kaeberlein,Venkata Ramachandra Karthik Chundi,Manasi Patwardhan,Martin Ku,Austin Meek,Leon Knauer,Brian Wingenroth,Srishti Yadav,Usman Gohar,Felix Friedrich,Michelle Lin,Jennifer Mickel,Arman Cohan,Stella Biderman,Irene Solaiman,Zeerak Talat,Anka Reuel,Mubashara Akhtar,Gjergji Kasneci,Avijit Ghosh,Leshem Choshen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces Every Eval Ever (E3), a community-driven standardized schema and crowdsourced repository for AI evaluation results, addressing inconsistencies in how evaluations are stored and reported across diverse frameworks and sources.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI evaluation results are fragmented across incompatible formats, leaderboards, papers, and custom repositories. Different evaluation frameworks produce divergent scores for nominally identical evaluations and record metadata inconsistently, hindering comparison, scientific progress, cost reduction, and reuse.

Method: A unified, source-agnostic JSON schema standardizing evaluation representation, with optional per-instance outputs. The system includes automatic converters from popular formats, evaluation harnesses, and leaderboards, plus a community-crowdsourced database hosted on Hugging Face.

Result: The repository currently spans 22,235 models, 2,273 unique benchmarks, and 31 evaluation formats, demonstrating successful community adoption and broad coverage.

Conclusion: E3 provides the first standardization effort of its kind for AI evaluation results, enabling consistent comparison, cross-community evaluation science, and reuse through a community-governed schema and crowdsourced infrastructure.

Abstract: AI evaluations are widely used for testing and understanding progress. However, the diverse evaluators bring with them inconsistencies that challenge analysis and comparison. First, results are saved in incompatible formats, scattered across leaderboards, papers, blog posts, evaluation harness logs, and custom repositories. Second, results are created by different evaluation frameworks, which produce divergent scores for nominally identical evaluations and record metadata inconsistently, hindering comparison, cross-community evaluation science, cost reduction, and reuse. We introduce Every Eval Ever, the first shared schema and community-crowdsourced repository for AI evaluation results. The schema standardizes how evaluations are represented in a unified, single JSON document. It is source-agnostic by design, ingesting results from evaluation harnesses and papers alike, and optionally stores per-instance outputs for fine-grained analysis. We contribute: (i) a community-governed metadata schema with a companion instance-level schema, the first standardization effort of its kind; (ii) automatic converters from popular formats, evaluation harnesses, and leaderboards to the unified schema; and (iii) a crowdsourced community database hosted on Hugging Face, currently spanning to date 22,235 models, 2,273 unique benchmarks, and 31 evaluation formats.

</details>


### [191] [StreamMemBench: Streaming Evaluation of Agent Memory for Future-Oriented Assistance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14571)
*Guanming Liu,Yuqi Ren,Hansu Gu,Peng Zhang,Weihang Wang,Jiahao Liu,Ning Gu,Tun Lu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: StreamMemBench is a streaming benchmark that evaluates personal-agent memory by testing whether systems can carry observed evidence and interaction feedback from an initial task forward into improved performance on a similar follow-up task, using egocentric video streams. Current memory systems struggle with evidence reuse and feedback incorporation despite local storage.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing memory benchmarks evaluate dialogue recall or task improvement in isolation, failing to capture the real-world trajectory where streaming observations and user interactions must inform future assistance. There is a gap in testing how memory systems carry evidence and feedback from current requests to similar future tasks.

Method: StreamMemBench constructs two-step task sequences around evidence anchors from EgoLife egocentric streams. The initial task tests evidence use, and the follow-up task tests whether feedback and interaction experience are reused. Four diagnostic metrics assess: evidence recall, initial evidence use, feedback incorporation, and follow-up reuse. Eight memory systems across two backbones are evaluated.

Result: Current memory systems often fail to use observed evidence or turn feedback into reliable follow-up behavior, even when evidence is successfully stored or feedback is incorporated locally. This reveals a significant gap between storage/retrieval and effective downstream utilization.

Conclusion: StreamMemBench exposes a critical weakness in current personal-agent memory systems: the inability to translate stored evidence and interaction feedback into improved future task performance, highlighting the need for better evidence utilization and feedback integration mechanisms.

Abstract: A central role of personal-agent memory is to turn stored information and prior interactions into future-oriented assistance. In daily use, useful cues come from what the agent observes and how the user interacts with the agent, and the agent must carry them forward from the current request to similar future tasks. Existing memory benchmarks usually test dialogue recall or task improvement in isolation, leaving the trajectory from streaming observations to later assistance largely untested. We introduce StreamMemBench, a streaming benchmark that constructs a two-step task sequence around each evidence anchor from EgoLife egocentric streams. The initial task tests evidence use, while the follow-up task tests whether feedback and interaction experience are reused. Four metrics diagnose evidence recall, initial evidence use, feedback incorporation, and follow-up reuse. Experiments with eight memory systems across two backbones show that current systems often fail to use observed evidence or turn feedback into reliable follow-up behavior, even when evidence is stored or feedback is incorporated locally. StreamMemBench is publicly available at https://github.com/landian60/StreamMemBench.

</details>


### [192] [VISTA: View-Consistent Self-Verified Training for GUI Grounding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14579)
*Xinyu Qiu,Yunzhu Zhang,Heng Jia,Shuheng Shen,Changhua Meng,Linchao Zhu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: VISTA constructs GRPO comparison groups from multiple geometrically different but semantically equivalent views of the same GUI instance, and adds a self-verified cross-view anchor to stabilize training, yielding consistent grounding accuracy gains across benchmarks and model scales.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard GRPO applied to GUI grounding samples rollouts from a single screenshot, causing groups to be either all-success or all-failure — eliminating meaningful relative advantage signals. The authors seek to introduce controlled intra-group variance while preserving semantic equivalence.

Method: VISTA generates multiple target-preserving views per GUI instance via crops that keep the target element visible and remap bounding boxes exactly. These views form a comparison group for GRPO. A self-verified cross-view anchor (an oracle answer trained with advantage-weighted loss) is excluded from the group baseline and activated only when the model already achieves maximum reward, preventing RL from degrading into unconditional imitation of short coordinate outputs.

Result: On ScreenSpot-Pro, VISTA improves Qwen3-VL 4B/8B/30B-A3B from 55.5/52.7/53.7 to 63.4/65.8/67.0 respectively. Consistent improvements are reported across five GUI-grounding benchmarks. Robustness analyses demonstrate higher worst-view accuracy and lower prediction flip rates.

Conclusion: Constructing GRPO groups from view-consistent crops rather than single screenshots provides meaningful relative advantage signals, and the self-verified anchor stabilizes short-coordinate generation. VISTA offers a general, effective training framework for GUI grounding.

Abstract: When applying Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) for GUI Grounding, rollouts are sampled from a single screenshot view; groups often become either all failures on difficult instances or all successes on easy ones, yielding no useful relative advantage. We propose VISTA (View-Consistent Self-Verified Training), a GRPO-based training framework that constructs each comparison group from multiple target-preserving views of the same GUI instance.Each view is generated by a crop that keeps the target element visible and remaps its box exactly, so model rollouts are compared across semantically equivalent but geometrically different inputs. To stabilize short coordinate generation without turning reinforcement learning into unconditional imitation, VISTA further adds a self-verified cross-view anchor: an oracle answer optimized with an advantage-weighted loss, excluded from the group baseline and activated only when the model has produced a maximum-reward rollout. Across five GUI-grounding benchmarks and multiple Qwen backbones, VISTA consistently improves grounding accuracy.On ScreenSpot-Pro, it raises Qwen3-VL 4B/8B/30B-A3B from 55.5/52.7/53.7 to 63.4/65.8/67.0. Robustness analyses further show higher worst-view accuracy and lower prediction flip rates.

</details>


### [193] [A Temporal Planning Framework for Disruption Aware Dynamic Route Optimization in Heterogeneous Railway Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14582)
*Pollob Chandra Ray,Sabah Binte Noor,Fazlul Hasan Siddiqui*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes a PDDL 2.1-based temporal planning framework for dynamic route optimization and disruption management in heterogeneous multi-gauge single-track railway networks, demonstrating effective generation of conflict-free operational plans across 200 benchmark instances with up to 1,000 track points and 120 trains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing railway optimization research focuses predominantly on high-level timetabling while omitting operational details like track switching coordination, leaving critical decisions to human operators and increasing safety risks. The problem is exacerbated in heterogeneous multi-gauge single-track networks where varying train speeds, stopping patterns, infrastructure compatibility constraints, and stochastic disruptions (blocked tracks, engine failures, speed slowdowns) create substantial coordination complexity.

Method: The framework formulates railway operations as a temporal planning problem using PDDL 2.1, explicitly modeling gauge compatibility constraints and diverse disruption scenarios. It generates conflict-free timestamped operational plans with both optimized schedules and executable action sequences. Evaluation used a custom benchmark of 200 instances (up to 1,000 track points, 120 trains) with two state-of-the-art temporal planners and a plan validator.

Result: Experimental results demonstrate the framework effectively generates temporal operational plans for heterogeneous railway systems, successfully handling multi-gauge constraints and disruption scenarios while reducing dependence on manual decision-making.

Conclusion: The temporal planning approach provides a viable, automated alternative to human operator decision-making for route optimization and disruption management in complex heterogeneous railway networks, enhancing both safety and operational efficiency.

Abstract: Efficient route optimization play a vital role in ensuring both safety and punctuality in railway operations. It is very crucial particularly in heterogeneous multi-gauge railway networks with varying train speed, stopping pattern, infrastructure compatibility constraints increase coordination complexity. In single-track systems these challenges are further intensify due to all trains to share the same track and requires frequent track switching.Stochastic disruptions events including blocked tracks, blocked trains, engine failure and speed slowdowns introduces additional unpredictability in operations and deviate the timetable. However, existing studies predominantly focuses on high-level timetabling, omitting operational details such as track switching coordination. As a result leaving decision to human operators, increasing safety risks into railway operations. This study proposes a framework based on temporal planning for dynamic route optimization and disruption management in heterogeneous railway systems. The framework formulates railway operations as a temporal planning problem using PDDL 2.1 with explicitly modeling gauge compatibility constraints and diverse disruption scenarios. It generates conflict-free timestamped operational plans specifying both optimized schedules and executable action sequences. To evaluate the proposed framework, we developed a benchmark problem set with 200 instances using up to 1,000 track points and 120 trains. Two state-of-the-art temporal planners and a plan validator were employed to assessed the framework. The experimental results demonstrate that the framework effectively generates temporal operational plans for heterogeneous railway systems and handles multi-gauge constraints, disruptions, and reduces dependence on manual decision making.

</details>


### [194] [Abstracting Cross-Domain Action Sequences into Interpretable Workflows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14654)
*Gaurav Verma,Scott Counts*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: WorkflowView uses LLMs to abstract noisy, low-level interaction logs into high-level activities, demonstrated across three tasks: browser log reconstruction, MOOC dropout prediction, and privacy-preserving analysis of AI tool usage in Word. The approach achieves strong performance with minimal labeled data and offers a robust, generalizable alternative to deep learning clustering methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Low-level sequential interaction logs from digital applications are granular and noisy, obscuring meaningful high-level insights into user behavior. Prior deep learning clustering methods are noise-sensitive and lack cross-application generalizability, motivating the need for a more robust abstraction approach.

Method: The WorkflowView framework leverages large language models (LLMs) to abstract low-level action sequences into high-level activities. It is evaluated on three tasks: (a) zero-shot task description reconstruction from browser logs, (b) few-shot student dropout prediction from MOOC logs, and (c) anonymized, privacy-preserving analysis of AI tool integration within Microsoft Word document workflows.

Result: On task description reconstruction, WorkflowView achieves a mean semantic similarity of 0.91. For few-shot dropout prediction with only five examples, it reaches a weighted F1 of 0.90. The framework also successfully enables privacy-preserving analysis of AI tool usage patterns in Word.

Conclusion: LLM-based abstraction is a robust and efficient method for transforming low-level behavioral data into high-level, interpretable, and actionable insights. The paper also addresses practical deployment considerations including computational efficiency and user privacy within logging infrastructures.

Abstract: Sequential or time-stamped interaction logs provide objective records of digital application usage, yet their granularity and noise often obscure meaningful insights into people's work. Such insights are essential for improving digital products in ways grounded in real-world user interactions. Prior research has applied deep learning models to cluster user actions into high-level activities, but these approaches are highly sensitive to noise and struggle to generalize across applications. To address this limitation, we introduce WorkflowView, a framework that uses large language models (LLMs) to abstract low-level action sequences into high-level activities. We establish the effectiveness and generality of our approach across three distinct, challenging sequential tasks and diverse domains: (a) zero-shot task description reconstruction from browser logs (achieving high semantic similarity, $μ_{sim} = 0.91$), (b) few-shot student dropout prediction using MOOC interaction logs (reaching weighted $F_1 = 0.90$ with only five few-shot examples), and (c) anonymized, privacy-preserving analysis of AI tool integration within document workflows in Microsoft Word. Our work demonstrates that LLM-based abstraction is a robust and efficient path forward for transforming low-level behavioral data into high-level, interpretable, and actionable insights. We also discuss practical considerations for deploying LLM-based inferences within logging infrastructures, including computational efficiency and user privacy.

</details>


### [195] [Towards Direct Latent-Space Synthesis for Parallel Branches in LLM-Agent Workflows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14672)
*Shikun Liu,Mufei Li,Dongqi Fu,Haoyu Wang,Yinglong Xia,Hong Li,Hong Yan,Pan Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Parallel-Synthesis is a framework that allows a synthesizer LLM to directly consume KV caches from parallel worker agents, bypassing text concatenation. It uses a cache mapper and a fine-tuned adapter, matching or outperforming text-based synthesis on 7 of 9 benchmarks while reducing time-to-first-token by 2.5x–11x.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Modern agentic workflows use parallel branches for subtasks, but existing systems merge results via text concatenation, discarding the parallel structure and incurring redundant prefill computation. This creates a mismatch between structured parallel execution and sequential text interfaces.

Method: Parallel-Synthesis consists of two components: (1) a cache mapper that calibrates independently generated KV caches from parallel workers so they can be jointly consumed, and (2) a fine-tuned synthesizer adapter that enables generation from this non-sequential cache interface. Training data exposes the synthesizer to parallel cache contexts, teaches aggregation across cached branches, and distills reasoning behavior from conventional text-concatenation-based synthesis.

Result: Across nine downstream datasets (math, science QA, code generation, GAIA, multi-agent database diagnosis), Parallel-Synthesis matches or exceeds text-based synthesis on seven datasets and remains close on the other two. It achieves 2.5x–11x reduction in time-to-first-token.

Conclusion: Direct cache-based synthesis is a promising interface for more native and efficient synthesis over parallel agent branches, offering both performance parity and substantial latency improvements.

Abstract: Large language models increasingly serve as execution engines for agentic systems, yet they still consume context through a sequential text interface. This creates a mismatch with modern structured agent workflows, in which independent branches explore subtasks, retrieve evidence, or generate candidate solutions before a final synthesis step. Existing systems typically merge these branches by concatenating their textual outputs, which discards the parallel structure and incurs redundant prefill computation. In this work, we introduce Parallel-Synthesis, a plug-and-play framework that enables a synthesizer to directly consume the KV caches produced by parallel worker agents. Parallel-Synthesis combines a cache mapper that calibrates independently generated branch caches with a fine-tuned synthesizer adapter that enables generation from this non-sequential cache interface. We train Parallel-Synthesis using data that exposes the synthesizer to parallel cache contexts, teaches aggregation across cached branches, and distills reasoning behavior from standard text-concatenation-based synthesis. Across nine downstream datasets spanning math, science QA, code generation, GAIA, and multi-agent database diagnosis, Parallel-Synthesis matches or outperforms text-based synthesis on seven datasets and remains close on the other two. It also reduces time-to-first-token by 2.5x-11x, suggesting that direct cache-based synthesis is a promising interface for more native and efficient synthesis over parallel agent branches.

</details>


<div id='cs.MA'></div>

# cs.MA [[Back]](#toc)

### [196] [Large Language Models as Supervised Extraction Assistants: Lowering the Barrier to Documentation Standard Adoption in Agent-Based Modelling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13749)
*Peer-Olaf Siebers,Christopher Frantz*

Main category: cs.MA

TL;DR: This paper investigates whether Large Language Models (LLMs) can semi-automate the documentation of Agent-Based Models using the RAT-RS reporting standard. Testing four LLMs on a published ABM paper, the authors find LLMs perform well on descriptive tasks but struggle with explanatory/evaluative ones, offering practical heuristics for when LLM assistance is reliable versus when human oversight is essential.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Despite established documentation standards (ODD, TRACE, RAT-RS), adoption in the ABM community remains low because producing documentation is effort-intensive and often treated as supplementary. The authors explore whether LLMs can reduce this burden, improve rigour, and encourage broader adoption of reporting standards.

Method: A feasibility study using four LLMs to extract RAT-RS reports from a single published ABM paper. The authors assess inter-model consistency and performance across different question types (descriptive, explanatory, evaluative), identifying patterns in where LLMs succeed or fail.

Result: LLMs generate coherent outputs and perform more reliably on descriptive questions than on explanatory or evaluative ones. While they can improve reporting quality and consistency, they exhibit notable limitations, particularly with tasks requiring interpretive judgment. The authors derive practical heuristics for distinguishing reliable LLM-assisted documentation from cases requiring human oversight.

Conclusion: LLMs show promise for semi-automating ABM documentation, especially for descriptive reporting tasks, but are not yet reliable for higher-order evaluative work. The authors call for systematic, community-level efforts to further explore and refine LLM-assisted approaches to enhance both rigour and adoption of ABM reporting standards.

Abstract: Agent-Based Modelling (ABM) relies on clear documentation to ensure credibility and transparency. Although standards exist for documenting models (e.g. ODD), processes (e.g. TRACE, EABSS), and data use (e.g. RAT-RS), their adoption remains limited due to the effort required to produce documentation that is often treated as supplementary. This paper explores the use of Large Language Models (LLMs) to facilitate and partially automate such processes. We conduct a feasibility study focusing on the underused Rigour and Transparency Reporting Standard (RAT-RS), using four LLMs to extract reports from a published ABM paper. We assess consistency and performance across question types, finding that LLMs generate coherent outputs and perform more reliably on descriptive than on explanatory or evaluative tasks. While LLMs can improve reporting quality and consistency, they also exhibit notable limitations. We identify practical heuristics for when LLM-assisted documentation is reliable and when human oversight is needed and call for systematic community-level exploration to enhance rigour and adoption in ABM reporting.

</details>


### [197] [Safety-Contract Graph Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning for Autonomous Network Security Response](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13832)
*Jose Luis Lima de Jesus Silva*

Main category: cs.MA

TL;DR: Presents ACD³-GAT, a safety-contract graph MARL framework for autonomous network security response that enforces operational budgets (MTTR, false positives, firewall changes). Constrained methods dramatically reduce downtime budget violations compared to reward-only MARL, which violates budgets 100% of the time.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reward-only multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL) for autonomous network security response can improve security metrics but consistently violates operational budgets (e.g., SOC downtime), rendering it non-deployable in real-world Security Operations Centres.

Method: Introduces a safety-contract graph MARL framework with ACD³-GAT architecture combining: (1) Lagrangian constrained optimization for operational budgets, (2) Graph Attention Network (GAT) state encoding, (3) budget-aware counterfactual action screening, (4) CVaR tail-risk estimation, (5) opponent-belief state modeling, and (6) Graph Counterfactual Risk Propagation (G-CRP). Evaluated against IPPO, MAPPO-GAT, and C-MAPPO-GAT baselines in CAGE Challenge 4 with three 200-episode seeds, plus topology-seed and adaptive Red-process stress tests.

Result: Every unconstrained method violated the SOC downtime budget in 100% of episodes (mean downtime proxy costs: 311–430 vs. budget of 50). C-MAPPO-GAT reduced downtime violation to 0.3% and mean downtime cost to 15.5. ACD³-GAT achieved mean downtime cost of 48.2 with 13.8% violation rate, placing it on the safety-contract frontier. Safety-constrained policies showed lower worst-case adaptive degradation under stress tests compared to reward-only MAPPO-GAT.

Conclusion: Reward-only MARL lacks operational discipline for deployable network security response. Separating reward optimization from constrained budget enforcement via safety contracts enables practical autonomy; ACD³-GAT offers a tunable safety-contract frontier rather than a single conservative compliance point.

Abstract: Autonomous network-security response systems promise to reduce Security Operations Centre (SOC) reaction latency, but reward-only multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL) can improve security reward while remaining non-deployable. We present a safety-contract graph MARL framework and instantiate it as ACD$^3$-GAT (Adaptive Constrained Counterfactual Decisioning with a Graph Attention Network encoder), an architecture that separates simulator observations from reusable operational budgets, constrained optimization, graph state encoding, and counterfactual action screening. We evaluate the method in CAGE Challenge 4, where agents operate under budgets for Mean Time to Recover (MTTR), false-positive response, and firewall change-management disruption. Across the benchmark, every unconstrained method violates the SOC downtime budget in 100% of evaluated episodes, with mean downtime proxy costs of 311-430 against a budget of 50. This complements prior CAGE Challenge 4 findings by showing that reward-only learning lacks operational discipline. Constrained MAPPO-GAT (C-MAPPO-GAT) isolates Lagrangian operational-cost control and budget-aware screening, while ACD$^3$-GAT adds budget context, CVaR tail-risk estimation, opponent-belief state, and Graph Counterfactual Risk Propagation (G-CRP). The replicated comparison includes three 200-episode seeds for IPPO, MAPPO-GAT, C-MAPPO-GAT, and ACD$^3$-GAT. C-MAPPO-GAT reduces downtime violation from 100% to 0.3% and mean downtime cost from 355.4 to 15.5 relative to MAPPO-GAT. ACD$^3$-GAT reduces mean downtime cost to 48.2 with a 13.8% violation rate, placing it on the safety-contract frontier rather than at the most conservative compliance point. Topology-seed and coupled adaptive Red-process stress tests preserve this contrast and show lower worst adaptive degradation for safety-constrained policies than reward-only MAPPO-GAT.

</details>


### [198] [Naive Visual Memory is Not Enough: A Failure-Mode Study of GUI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14106)
*Seoyoung Choi,Minseok Ko,Hyunseok Lee,Kunwoong Kim,Woomin Song,Chanseok Jeon,Jinwoo Shin*

Main category: cs.MA

TL;DR: The paper systematically analyzes visual memory in GUI agents by introducing a four-category failure taxonomy. It finds full-image memory reduces state-level failures but worsens action-level errors. The authors propose AGMem, which stores action-relevant image crops instead of full screenshots, achieving a 33.3% improvement in task success on OSWorld.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Visual memory in GUI agents remains insufficiently understood — it is unclear which failure modes it mitigates versus exacerbates. Existing approaches prepend full screenshots as memory without analyzing how this affects different stages of the perception-reasoning-action pipeline.

Method: (1) A taxonomy of four GUI agent failures is introduced: cognitive failure, visual state misunderstanding, hidden operation blindness, and grounding error — mapped to perception, reasoning, and action stages. (2) Systematic analysis reveals full-image memory has divergent effects. (3) AGMem is proposed: an action-grounded memory framework that stores image crops of local GUI regions tied to successful actions or recoveries, rather than full screenshots.

Result: Full-image memory reduces state-level failures (visual state misunderstanding) but increases action-level errors (hidden operation blindness, grounding error). AGMem achieves a 33.3% improvement in task success rate over full-image memory on the OSWorld benchmark.

Conclusion: Action-grounded visual memory (AGMem) using localized image crops is a more effective representation than full screenshots for GUI agent memory, as it preserves the benefits of visual context while mitigating the action-level failures that full-image memory introduces.

Abstract: Graphical User Interface (GUI) agents are increasingly used to automate complex computer tasks across applications, websites, and operating systems. To improve their reliability, recent work has introduced experiential memory, where agents retrieve prior trajectories to guide decision-making in similar states. More recent approaches further extend this idea to visual memory by storing and retrieving screenshots from past interactions, providing agents with richer contextual information than text-only memories. However, the effect of visual memory in GUI agents remains insufficiently understood: it is unclear which failures visual memory mitigates, or which failures it exacerbates. To systematically analyze the effect of visual memory, we introduce a taxonomy of four GUI agent failures (i.e., cognitive failure, visual state misunderstanding, hidden operation blindness, and grounding error) that map to distinct stages of the perception-reasoning-action pipeline. We find that prepending full-image memory has a divergent effect on the failure distribution: it reduces state-level failures but worsens action-level ones, and increases hidden operation blindness and grounding error. Motivated by this finding, we propose Action-Grounded Visual Memory (AGMem), an action-grounded memory framework for GUI agents. The core idea of AGMem is to store image crops that capture the local GUI region closely related to a successful action or a recovery, rather than storing full screenshots. Experiments on OSWorld show that AGMem improves task success rates by 33.3 % over full-image memory. These results demonstrate that AGMem is an effective representation for visual memory in GUI agents.

</details>


### [199] [Learning Coordinated Preference for Multi-Objective Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14693)
*Pengxin Wang,Lihao Guo,Yi Xie,Bo Liu,Siyang Cao,Jingdi Chen*

Main category: cs.MA

TL;DR: PCMA learns coordinated agent-specific preferences in cooperative multi-objective multi-agent RL, theoretically proving that preference diversity induces team improvement, and empirically demonstrating better performance and trade-off coordination.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In cooperative MOMARL, conflicts arise across both objectives and agents (due to differing observations, roles, and contributions). Existing methods lack mechanisms for agents to make complementary trade-offs through coordinated, heterogeneous preferences.

Method: The authors formulate cooperative MOMARL as a team-optimal game. They propose PCMA, which learns coordinated agent-specific preferences. They derive a first-order improvement decomposition showing that preference diversity can induce team improvement under suitable conditions.

Result: Experiments on multiple cooperative MOMA environments and a practical traffic-control scenario show that PCMA improves both overall team performance and the coordination of trade-offs among agents compared to baselines.

Conclusion: Learning coordinated, diverse preferences across agents is an effective approach in cooperative MOMARL, enabling complementary trade-offs that enhance team-level outcomes beyond what uniform preference methods can achieve.

Abstract: Cooperative multi-objective multi-agent reinforcement learning (MOMARL) models team decision making under multiple, potentially conflicting objectives. In this setting, conflicts arise not only across objectives but also across agents with different observations, roles, and contributions. We propose Preference Coordinated Multi-agent Policy Optimization (PCMA), which learns coordinated agent-specific preferences to enable complementary trade-offs among agents. Theoretically, we formulate cooperative MOMARL as a team-optimal game and show that, under suitable conditions, preference diversity can induce team improvement through a first-order improvement decomposition. Experiments on multiple cooperative MOMA environments and a practical traffic-control scenario show that PCMA improves both performance and trade-off coordination.

</details>


<div id='cs.IR'></div>

# cs.IR [[Back]](#toc)

### [200] [Personalization and Evaluation of Conversational Information Access](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13717)
*Hideaki Joko*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: This thesis addresses three core challenges in building reliable Conversational Information Access (CIA) systems: personal context extraction via conversational entity linking (ConEL, CREL), personalized response generation using large-scale datasets (LAPS), and interpretable evaluation with a reference-free metric (FACE).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Users increasingly favor direct answers over hyperlinks in information retrieval, but existing systems lack personalization. Reliable CIA systems must extract personal context, generate personalized responses, and be evaluated effectively and interpretably — three challenges this thesis tackles.

Method: (1) CREL: a novel entity linking method tailored for conversational settings, built on the introduced ConEL dataset. (2) LAPS: an efficient method for constructing large-scale, human-written, personalized conversational datasets, used to study preference-based personalized response generation. (3) FACE: an automatic, reference-free evaluation method that assesses full conversations and correlates with human judgments.

Result: The thesis introduces three key artifacts: the ConEL dataset with CREL for conversational entity linking; LAPS for scalable personalized dataset construction; and FACE, a reference-free evaluation metric that aligns closely with human judgments for whole-conversation assessment.

Conclusion: Personal context extraction, personalized generation, and interpretable evaluation are foundational for next-generation CIA systems. The proposed CREL, LAPS, and FACE each address a critical gap, collectively advancing the development of reliable, personalized conversational information access.

Abstract: Conversational interactions have reshaped information retrieval systems, as users increasingly favour direct answers over traditional hyperlinks. To build reliable Conversational Information Access (CIA) systems that account for personal context, this thesis addresses challenges: (1) personal context extraction, (2) personalized response generation, and (3) effective and interpretable system evaluation. First, we tackle personal context extraction by studying what Entity Linking (EL) in conversations entails, introducing a dataset for conversational entity linking (ConEL), and proposing CREL, a novel EL method tailored for conversational settings. Second, we focus on personalized response generation by proposing LAPS, a method for efficiently constructing large-scale, human-written, personalized conversational datasets, and using them to study how users' preferences can be utilized to generate personalized responses. Finally, we address the need for effective and interpretable system evaluation by introducing FACE, an automatic, reference-free method that assesses entire conversations and aligns closely with human judgments.

</details>


### [201] [Nomenclature Ontology for Medical And Disease names (NOMAD): taxonomy of types and origins of disease names](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13719)
*Spiros Denaxas,Cai Ytsma,Giannos Louloudis,Jackie MacArthur,Harry Hemingway*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: The paper introduces NOMAD, a meta-taxonomy that classifies disease names by their naming conventions. Using a machine learning pipeline on 22,548 ICD-10-CM entries, 99.1% were classified into 9 top-level and 20 subcategories. Key findings: anatomical names dominate (63.8%), eponymous diseases are rare (9.7%), only 57 diseases (2.6% of eponymous) are named after women, and naming profiles vary substantially across medical specialties.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Medical disease nomenclature has evolved organically across centuries with Greek, Latin, and Arabic roots, leading to heterogeneous naming practices. Despite this, no systematic framework exists to characterize the conventions underlying how diseases are named across the entire medical lexicon. The authors aim to fill this gap by providing a structured, quantitative analysis of disease naming patterns.

Method: The authors developed NOMAD, a two-level meta-taxonomy with 9 top-level categories and 20 subcategories. A three-stage machine learning pipeline (combining rule-based, supervised, and LLM-based approaches) was applied for multi-label classification of 22,548 entries from the ICD-10-CM 2026 Alphabetical Index. Manual validation was performed on a random 10% sample (n=2,255), yielding 70% full agreement and 26% partial agreement (Cohen's Kappa 0.832).

Result: 99.1% of terms were classified (mean: 2.12 labels/entry). Anatomical categories predominated (63.8%), followed by Descriptive (48.4%) and Pathophysiological (40.2%). Eponymous (9.7%) and Geographical (1.9%) labels were less frequent than cultural prominence suggests. Only 57 eponymous diseases (2.6%) are named after women. Naming conventions varied by ICD-10 chapter: infectious diseases favored etiological/geographical labels, circulatory diseases favored anatomical/pathophysiological labels, and mental disorders showed the highest socio-behavioral label prevalence.

Conclusion: NOMAD provides the first systematic framework for characterizing disease naming conventions across all of medicine. The compositional, multi-label nature of disease nomenclature is quantitatively demonstrated, and the framework reveals specialty-specific epistemological traditions. The stark gender disparity in eponymous disease names (only 2.6% female) highlights a historical imbalance. NOMAD may serve as a foundation for studying nomenclature evolution, biases, and standardization efforts.

Abstract: The nomenclature of human disease has developed organically over the past centuries using Greek, Latin, and Arabic terminology and reflects the idiosyncrasies of different eras of medical discovery. Despite evident heterogeneity in naming practices, no systematic framework exists for characterising these conventions across all diseases. In this paper, we describe the Nomenclature Ontology for Medical And Disease names (NOMAD), a meta-taxonomy that classifies disease names according to their naming conventions. We developed a two-level taxonomy comprising 9 top-level categories and 20 subcategories and applied it to 22,548 index entries from the ICD-10-CM 2026 Alphabetical Index in a scalable three-stage machine learning-driven classification pipeline. Classification was multi-label, reflecting the compositional nature of medical nomenclature. We classified 99.1% of terms with a mean of 2.12 labels per entry. Anatomical categories were the most prevalent (63.8% of entries), followed by Descriptive (48.4%) and Pathophysiological (40.2%), while Eponymous and Geographical labels were less common than their cultural prominence might suggest (9.7% and 1.9% respectively). Among all Eponymous diseases, we identified only 57 (2.6%) of diseases named after a female person. We manually reviewed a random sample of n=2,255 entries (10%) for accuracy and calculated a full agreement rate of 70% and partial agreement rate of 26% (macro-averaged Cohen's Kappa score 0.832). Naming convention profiles varied substantially across ICD-10-CM chapters, reflecting specialty-specific epistemological traditions: infectious disease chapters were dominated by etiological labels and showed the highest proportion of geographical region related labels, the circulatory chapter by anatomical and pathophysiological labels, and mental and behavioural disorders showed the highest prevalence of socio-behavioral labels.

</details>


### [202] [TASR: Training-Free Adaptive Stopping for Iterative Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13814)
*Adrian Kieback,Uyiosa Philip Amadasun,Aman Chadha,Aaron Elkins*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: TASR is a training-free, fixed-threshold stopping rule for iterative retrieval-augmented generation that halts retrieval when the model repeats its previous normalized answer and the isotonically calibrated logit margin exceeds 0.25, matching or exceeding fixed-budget baselines while using fewer calls.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Iterative RAG agents waste computation by continuing to retrieve after convergence. Existing learned stopping policies require labeled trajectories and retraining per model/task. A simple, universal, training-free stopping rule is needed.

Method: The authors exhaustively enumerate 381 candidate stopping rules and select TASR: a predicate that fires when (1) the current normalized answer matches the previous round's answer and (2) the isotonically calibrated logit margin between top-two tokens exceeds 0.25. No classifier or value head is trained; the threshold is fixed across all 24 (model, retriever, corpus) configurations.

Result: On a 3-model × 2-dataset distractor grid, TASR retains 94.8% of fixed-k=5's macro F1 at 62.6% of its calls, exceeding fixed-k=3 by +3.42 F1. On 9 open-domain BM25 cells, it achieves 55.01 F1 at 2.98 calls vs. 54.33 at 3.00 calls for fixed-k=3. On 9 dense-retrieval cells, it shows zero significant regressions. TASR Pareto-dominates all alternatives; verbalized confidence collapses on RLHF-tuned models (96.5% values =5) while the logit margin provides 44x better class-conditional separation.

Conclusion: TASR serves as an auditable, training-free Pareto baseline for comparing learned stopping controllers in iterative RAG, grounded in the measurable pathology of verbalized confidence collapse in RLHF-tuned models.

Abstract: Iterative retrieval-augmented generation agents commonly overspend by continuing to retrieve after the model has converged on an answer, incurring calls that change neither the prediction nor the supporting evidence. Existing remedies learn a stopping policy from labeled trajectories, tying the decision to a trained component that requires retraining for each new model or task. We propose TASR (Training-Free Adaptive Stopping Rule), a one-line predicate that fires when the model repeats its previous-round normalized answer and the isotonically calibrated logit margin exceeds 0.25. No classifier or value head is learned; the threshold is fixed across all twenty-four (model, retriever, corpus) configurations we evaluate. On a 3-model x 2-dataset distractor grid, TASR retains 94.8% of fixed-k=5's macro F1 at 62.6% of its calls and exceeds fixed-k=3 by +3.42 F1. The pattern holds on nine open-domain BM25 cells (55.01 F1 at 2.98 calls vs. 54.33 at 3.00 for fixed-k=3) and, with calibration locked from the distractor split, on nine dense-retrieval cells across two retriever families, with zero significant regressions in either extension. The rule was selected from an exhaustive enumeration of 381 candidate stopping rules; no alternative Pareto-dominates it on any evaluated configuration. A signal-quality analysis shows that verbalized 1-5 confidence collapses on RLHF-tuned models (96.5% of values equal 5, entropy 0.182 nats), while the logit margin achieves 44x better class-conditional separation, grounding the design in a measurable model pathology. TASR is an auditable, training-free Pareto baseline against which learned stopping controllers can be compared. Code is publicly available.

</details>


### [203] [Hybrid Neural Retrieval with Generative Query Refinement for Quranic Passage Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13837)
*Mohamed G. Salman,Mohammad E. Moftah,Ali Hamdi*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: Proposes a four-phase neural architecture for Quranic Passage Retrieval combining sparse/dense hybrid retrieval, cross-encoder reranking, confidence gating for zero-answer queries, and AraT5-based multi-verse aggregation, achieving Recall@10 of 0.7024 and MAP@10 of 0.4947 on an expanded Quran QA 2022 dataset.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quranic Passage Retrieval is challenging due to the linguistic complexity and semantic gap between Modern Standard Arabic (MSA) queries and Classical Arabic (CA) of the Quran, which hinders conventional retrieval methods. There is also a need to handle multi-verse retrieval and filter zero-answer queries.

Method: A four-phase neural architecture: (1) Hybrid candidate retrieval using AraColBERT dense indexing combined with BM25 sparse retrieval; (2) Semantic reranking via a CAMeLBERTmix cross-encoder; (3) Confidence gating mechanism to filter zero-answer queries; (4) AraT5-based refinement module for multi-verse aggregation.

Result: Recall@10 = 0.7024, MAP@10 = 0.4947, MRR = 0.5807. Shows improved performance over baselines, with a marginal tradeoff in absolute top-rank precision compared to heavily optimized single models, but provides more comprehensive and context-aware retrieval.

Conclusion: The proposed architecture offers a substantially more comprehensive, reliable, and context-aware solution for multi-verse Quranic passage retrieval, effectively bridging the MSA-CA semantic gap while handling zero-answer queries through confidence gating.

Abstract: Quranic Passage Retrieval (PR) could be a challenging task due to the linguistic complexity and the semantic gap between the Modern Standard Arabic (MSA) used in daily queries and the Classical Arabic (CA) of the Holy Quran. These factors hinder conventional retrieval methods. To handle these limitations and improve multi-verse retrieval and filter the zero-answer queries, this paper proposes a four-phase neural architecture designed to enhance retrieval accuracy and contextual understanding. The methodology combines hybrid candidate retrieval using AraColBERT dense indexing and BM25 sparse retrieval, followed by semantic reranking with a CAMeLBERTmix cross-encoder. A confidence gating mechanism is then applied to filter zero-answer queries, and an AraT5-based refinement module for multi-verse aggregation. The system is evaluated on an expanded version of the Quran QA 2022 dataset. Results show improved performance compared to the baseline models, achieving a Recall@10 of 0.7024 and a Mean Average Precision (MAP@10) of 0.4947. While the system exhibits a marginal tradeoff in absolute top-rank precision (MRR = 0.5807) compared to heavily optimised single models, the proposed architecture provides a substantially more comprehensive, reliable, and context aware solution for multi-verse Quranic passage retrieval.

</details>


### [204] [Mood-Aware Music Recommendation: Integrating User Affective Signals into Ranking Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13858)
*Terence Zeng,Abhishek K. Umrawal*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: This paper proposes a mood-conditioned ranking framework for music recommendation that integrates user affective signals into the recommendation process via softmax-based sampling in the energy-valence space. Single-blind experiments show improved perceived recommendation quality compared to a baseline.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Collaborative filtering performs poorly in music recommendation due to sparse user-item interactions. While content-based filtering examines item qualities like genre, instrumentation, and lyrics, emotional signals have been underexplored despite a user's emotional state strongly influencing music choice. This motivates incorporating mood signals for personalization.

Method: A mood-conditioned ranking framework is proposed that integrates user affective signals into the recommendation process. The method employs softmax-based sampling in the energy-valence space to condition recommendations on mood. Evaluation is conducted via single-blind experiments where participants compare recommendations from the proposed system against a baseline.

Result: The single-blind experiments indicate improved perceived recommendation quality for the mood-conditioned system compared to the baseline, providing preliminary evidence for the effectiveness of incorporating mood-based inputs into music recommendations.

Conclusion: Incorporating mood-based inputs into music recommendation systems shows promise for improving perceived recommendation quality, particularly in domains with sparse user-item interactions where collaborative filtering alone is insufficient.

Abstract: Recommendation systems are essential in modern music streaming platforms due to the vast amount of available content. While collaborative filtering is widely used to suggest items based on the preferences of others with similar patterns, it performs poorly in domains where user-item interactions are sparse, such as music. Content-based filtering is an alternative approach that examines the qualities of the items themselves. Genre, instrumentation, and lyrics have been explored; however, relatively little attention has been given to emotion recognition. Since a user's emotional state strongly influences their music choice, incorporating mood signals offers a promising direction for personalization. In this work, we propose a mood-conditioned ranking framework that integrates user affective signals into the recommendation process via softmax-based sampling in the energy-valence space. We evaluate the approach via single-blind experiments in which participants compare recommendations from the proposed system against a baseline. The results indicate improved perceived recommendation quality, providing preliminary evidence for the effectiveness of incorporating mood-based inputs into music recommendations.

</details>


### [205] [ADORE: Iterative Query Expansion with Retrieval-Grounded Relevance Feedback](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13905)
*Amin Bigdeli,Negar Arabzadeh,Radin Hamidi Rad,Sajad Ebrahimi,Charles L. A. Clarke,Ebrahim Bagheri*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: ADORE is an iterative query expansion framework that uses retrieval-grounded feedback — generating pseudo-passages, observing corpus responses, and evaluating relevance — to outperform prior methods by up to 9.2% on BRIGHT and 3.6% on BEIR.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM-based query expansion methods rely on generation-driven approaches (plausible pseudo-documents or expansions) without verifying how the target corpus actually responds, leading to retrieval drift, amplified misleading vocabulary, and missed discriminative terms. The authors argue that effective expansion requires retrieval-grounded feedback rather than single-pass generation or unverified iteration.

Method: ADORE (ADapt, Observe, Relevance Evaluate) is an iterative framework with three stages per round: (1) an LLM generates pseudo-passages to expand the query, (2) a retriever exposes the corpus response by fetching documents, and (3) a relevance assessor evaluates retrieved documents against the original query. These relevance judgments identify what to reinforce, what remains undercovered, and what to suppress, feeding back into the next expansion round.

Result: On BEIR, ADORE improves average nDCG@10 by 24.5% over BM25 and 3.6% over the strongest prior query expansion method. On BRIGHT, improvements reach 122.9% over BM25 and 9.2% over the best query expansion baseline. Consistent outperformance is reported across TREC Deep Learning, BEIR, and BRIGHT benchmarks.

Conclusion: Retrieval-grounded feedback, rather than generation alone, is critical for effective query expansion. ADORE demonstrates that iteratively adapting expansions based on actual corpus responses and relevance assessments yields substantial and consistent gains across diverse retrieval benchmarks.

Abstract: LLM-based query expansion improves retrieval by enriching the original query with additional context. Yet most methods remain generation-driven, producing plausible pseudo-documents or expansions without checking how the target corpus responds. This can introduce retrieval drift, amplify misleading vocabulary, or miss terms that distinguish relevant from non-relevant documents. We argue that effective expansion requires retrieval-grounded feedback, not just single-pass generation or unverified iteration. We introduce ADORE (ADapt, Observe, Relevance Evaluate), an iterative framework that turns retrieval outcomes into feedback for the next expansion. At each round, an LLM generates pseudo-passages, a retriever exposes the corpus response, and a relevance assessor evaluates retrieved documents against the original query. These judgments identify what to reinforce, what remains undercovered, and what to suppress. Across TREC Deep Learning, BEIR, and BRIGHT, ADORE consistently outperforms strong query expansion baselines with notable improvements across nearly all evaluation settings, improving average nDCG@10 by 24.5% over BM25 and 3.6% over the strongest prior query expansion method on BEIR, and by 122.9% over BM25 and 9.2% over the best query expansion baseline on BRIGHT. Our code and data are publicly available.

</details>


### [206] [When Recommendation Denoising Meets Popularity Bias: Understanding and Mitigating Their Interaction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14046)
*Guohang Zeng,Jie Lu,Guangquan Zhang*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: Implicit feedback denoising methods relying on small-loss heuristics can inadvertently suppress clean but hard-to-fit tail-item signals due to popularity-dependent loss patterns. The authors formalize this phenomenon, showing that loss-based reweighting increases head-tail signal imbalance, and propose Popularity-Aware Denoising (PAD) to modulate denoising strength by item popularity, improving both accuracy and diversity tradeoffs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The dominant small-loss heuristic for denoising implicit feedback treats high-loss interactions as noisy and down-weights them.
