However, tail-item positives naturally incur higher losses because they are sparsely observed (harder to fit), not necessarily because they are noisy. This causes monotone loss-based reweighting to suppress genuine long-tail user preferences, exacerbating popularity bias and reducing recommendation diversity.

Method: The authors first formalize the problem using an effective head-tail signal ratio metric to quantify how denoising weights alter the balance of supervision from head vs. tail items. They derive a conditional reallocation result: when the loss distribution of tail positives is right-shifted relative to head positives, small-loss reweighting increases the effective head-tail ratio compared to standard ERM. Building on this analysis, they propose Popularity-Aware Denoising (PAD), a lightweight plug-in framework that modulates denoising strength inversely with item popularity — applying stronger denoising to popular (high-exposure) items and conservative denoising to tail items, thereby preserving clean-but-hard long-tail signals.

Result: Experiments across three datasets and three backbone recommenders demonstrate that PAD consistently improves over representative denoising baselines. It also yields favorable accuracy-diversity tradeoffs, with particularly pronounced gains on matrix factorization (MF)-style recommenders.

Conclusion: The small-loss heuristic, while widely used for denoising, carries an underappreciated risk of amplifying popularity bias by disproportionately filtering tail-item supervision signals. Popularity-aware modulation of denoising strength offers a simple yet effective correction, and the PAD framework can be readily integrated with existing denoising recommenders to improve both robustness and diversity.

Abstract: Implicit feedback is the dominant data source for recommender systems, but behavioral logs are often contaminated by false-positive interactions caused by mis-clicks, biased exposure, and interface effects. Denoising recommendation methods improve robustness by down-weighting or filtering interactions suspected to be noisy, often relying on the small-loss heuristic. We revisit this heuristic through the lens of popularity bias. Tail-item positives can be harder to fit because they are sparsely observed, and thus may receive larger losses even when they reflect genuine user preference. Under such popularity-dependent loss patterns, monotone loss-based reweighting can suppress clean-but-hard tail signals and increase the head-tail imbalance in effective supervision.
  We formalize this interaction through the effective head-tail signal ratio induced by denoising weights and derive a conditional reallocation result: when the loss distribution of tail positives is right-shifted relative to that of head positives, small-loss reweighting increases the effective head-tail signal ratio compared with ERM. Motivated by this analysis, we propose Popularity-Aware Denoising (PAD), a lightweight plug-in framework that modulates denoising strength by item popularity. PAD applies stronger denoising to highly exposed items while being more conservative on tail items, preserving more clean-but-hard long-tail signals. Experiments on three datasets and three backbones show that PAD generally improves over representative denoising baselines and provides favorable accuracy-diversity tradeoffs, especially on MF-style recommenders.

</details>


### [207] [Knowledge Graph Enhanced Memory-Augmented Retrieval for Long Context Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14047)
*Ghadir Alselwi,Basem Suleiman,Hao Xue,Shoaib Jameel,Hakim Hacid,Flora D. Salim,Imran Razzak*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: KGERMAR introduces dynamic, context-specific knowledge graphs constructed during inference to augment language model memory, achieving up to 8.5% lower perplexity and 2–2.5× better memory efficiency than baselines across long-context tasks (1K–32K tokens).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-context language modeling suffers from the inability to maintain coherent entity states and relationships across thousands of tokens. Semantic similarity-based retrieval alone fails to capture explicit relational patterns, motivating a hybrid approach that integrates structured knowledge graphs with semantic retrieval.

Method: KGERMAR performs real-time entity and relation extraction to construct dynamic, input-specific knowledge graphs during inference. It maintains three memory banks — contextual, semantic, and structural — whose retrieval signals are fused via learned weights. Graph-structural embeddings are integrated with textual semantics in a multi-component memory architecture, enabling domain-adaptive retrieval that exploits both surface semantics and explicit entity relationships.

Result: Evaluated on SlimPajama (84.7K examples), WikiText-103 (4,358), PG-19 (100), and Proof-pile (46.3K), KGERMAR achieves up to 8.5% perplexity reduction and 2–2.5× memory efficiency gains over memory-augmented baselines across 1K–32K context lengths, with superior in-context learning on five NLU tasks.

Conclusion: Dynamic knowledge graph construction advances memory-augmented language modeling by replacing fixed knowledge bases with context-adaptive, domain-specific knowledge representations, enabling more coherent long-context understanding.

Abstract: Long-context language modeling requires not only extending context windows but maintaining coherent understanding of entity states and relationships across thousands of tokens -- a challenge that semantic similarity alone cannot address. KGERMAR addresses this by constructing dynamic, context-specific knowledge graphs from input text during inference, enabling domain-adaptive retrieval that leverages both semantic similarity and explicit entity relationships. The framework performs real-time entity and relation extraction to build contextual knowledge graphs, then integrates graph-structural embeddings with textual semantics through a multi-component memory architecture. Three memory banks -- contextual, semantic, and structural -- are maintained with retrieval signals fused via learned weights to capture both surface-level semantics and deeper relational patterns. Evaluated on SlimPajama (84.7K training examples), WikiText-103 (4,358 examples), PG-19 (100 examples), and Proof-pile (46.3K examples), KGERMAR achieves up to 8.5\% lower perplexity and 2--2.5x better memory efficiency than memory-augmented baselines across context lengths from 1K to 32K tokens, with superior in-context learning performance across five NLU tasks. The dynamic knowledge graph construction approach advances memory-augmented language modeling by enabling domain-specific knowledge representation that adapts to input contexts rather than relying on fixed knowledge bases.

</details>


### [208] [CoRe: A Continuously Reward-Finetuned LLM Query Rewriter for Multi-Stage Context-Aware Relevance in Web-Scale Video Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14127)
*Yilin Wen,Rong Yang,Xiaojia Chang,Hong Sun,Gefu Tang,Chunhui Liu,Jeffrey Chen,Zeyu Ma,Lisong Qiu,Xiaochuan Fan,Congjia Yu,Quan Zhou,Yuheng Chen,Zian Wang*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: CoRe is a weekly-retrained LLM query rewriting system for short-video search that uses the production multimodal relevance model as its reward signal with a multiplicative ratio form, employs a semi-online Mixed Preference Optimization loop for cost efficiency at scale, and was shown in production A/B tests to significantly reduce query-change rates while maintaining or improving relevance/engagement metrics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Query rewriters in production must align training rewards with how the production ranker actually consumes rewrites, but using the full production relevance model as a reward is computationally prohibitive for continuous retraining at scale. Existing offline reward proxies leave a simulation-production gap. The authors aim to close this gap affordably to support weekly redeployment under data drift.

Method: CoRe uses: (1) a reward function based on the deployed multimodal relevance model with a multiplicative ratio form mirroring production fusion algebra; (2) a semi-online Mixed Preference Optimization loop where a DPO-style pairwise objective restricts gradient computation to top-k/bottom-k sampled trajectories, with a phase structure reducing parameter syncs between trainer and inference server from per-step to per-phase; (3) an automated promotion gate monitoring reward-like and stability metrics; (4) parallel consumption of rewriter output at recall, rawrank, and finerank stages without displacing original signals.

Result: Deployed in a major short-video search engine with weekly redeployment for over five months. Two sequential production A/B launches showed statistically significant reductions in change-query rate on rewrite-impacted queries, with all headline relevance and engagement metrics moving in the expected direction. The automated promotion gate successfully detected and recovered from a real reward-hacking incident.

Conclusion: Using the production relevance model directly as a reward — with the multiplicative ratio form and the semi-online MPO loop making it affordable — closes the simulation-production gap, enabling effective and safe continuous LLM-based query rewriting at industrial scale.

Abstract: LLM-based query rewriters in production face a tension: the training reward must reflect how the rewrite is consumed by the production ranker, yet the training procedure must be cheap enough to support continuous redeployment as data drifts. We present CoRe (Context Relevance), such a system, redeployed weekly for over five months in a major short-video search engine. Our reward uses the deployed multimodal relevance model as its source and a multiplicative ratio form mirroring the production fusion algebra, closing the simulation-production gap that offline reward proxies leave open. A semi-online Mixed Preference Optimization loop makes this reward affordable at multi-million-instance weekly scale: a DPO-style pairwise objective restricts the gradient pass to a small top-k/bottom-k subset of sampled trajectories, and a phase structure reduces trainer/inference-server parameter syncs from per-step to per-phase. An automated promotion gate over reward-like and stability metrics detected and recovered from a real reward-hacking incident in production. Rewriter output is consumed as parallel relevance signals at recall, rawrank, and finerank without displacing the original signals, bounding rewriter-failure blast radius. Online A/B from two sequential production launches, first deploying the rewriter at finerank, then extending consumption to recall and rawrank, delivers statistically significant reductions in change-query rate on rewrite-impacted queries, with all headline relevance and engagement metrics moving in the expected direction.

</details>


### [209] [ChronoID: Infusing Explicit Temporal Signals into Semantic IDs for Generative Recommendation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14260)
*Dongdong Nian,Dongqi Fu,Chenliang Xu,Yinglong Xia,Hong Li,Hong Yan,Jian Kang*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: ChronoID introduces explicit temporal information into semantic ID learning for generative recommendation, addressing the core limitation that existing semantic IDs are time-agnostic and assume temporally stationary semantics. The paper systematically explores the design space of temporal signal infusion and provides a benchmark to validate effective time-aware architectures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing semantic ID learning in generative recommendation treats item semantics as temporally stationary, conflating interactions from different temporal contexts into identical representations. This ignores the evolving interaction rhythms central to real-world recommendation, where user intent and item relevance shift over time. The paper aims to explicitly incorporate time into semantic IDs to overcome this misalignment.

Method: The authors first systematically characterize the design space along three orthogonal dimensions of temporal signals. They propose ChronoID, a unified framework for time-aware semantic ID learning that explores where and how to infuse explicit temporal information. They also contribute a new time-explicit generation recommendation benchmark to evaluate design choices regarding temporal infusion strategy, architecture design, and sources of performance gain.

Result: The paper answers three key questions through the ChronoID benchmark: (1) what is the effective way of infusing time into semantic IDs, (2) how to design the architecture for time-aware semantic ID learning, and (3) where does the performance gain originate. Specific quantitative results are not detailed in this abstract.

Conclusion: Explicit temporal modeling is essential for semantic ID learning in generative recommendation. ChronoID provides both a conceptual framework and empirical evidence that time-aware semantic IDs better capture evolving user intent and item dynamics, overcoming the stationarity assumption that limits prior time-agnostic approaches.

Abstract: Semantic IDs are crucial in generative recommendation, but with a fundamental limitation: temporal information is not well incorporated into semantic IDs. Instead, time influences recommendation only implicitly (e.g., through session construction heuristics, preference alignment, or sequence order), while existing semantic ID learning remains entirely time-agnostic. This design conflates interactions occurring under distinct temporal contexts into identical semantic representations, implicitly assuming that item semantics and user intent are temporally stationary. Such an assumption is misaligned with real-world recommendation scenarios, where evolving interaction rhythms play a central role. In this work, we investigate where and how the explicit time should be incorporated into semantic ID for generative recommendation. First, we systematically characterize the design space along three orthogonal dimensions of temporal signals and present a unified framework, ChronoID, for time-aware semantic ID learning. Then, by contributing a new time-explicit generation recommendation benchmark, ChronoID answers the questions: what is the effective way of infusing time, how to design the architecture, and where does the gain come from.

</details>


### [210] [ScoreGate: Adaptive Chunk Selection for Retrieval-Augmented Generation via Dual-Score Statistical Fusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14269)
*Karamvir Singh,Arvind Jain*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: ScoreGate is a lightweight, inference-time mechanism that adaptively controls how many retrieved chunks are fed to the generator by leveraging existing bi-encoder and cross-encoder scores, eliminating unnecessary over-retrieval and under-retrieval without additional model calls.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fixed-cardinality (top-K) retrieval ignores query complexity, causing over-retrieval for simple/narrow queries (wasted computation, tokens, and latency) and under-retrieval for compositional queries (missed relevant chunks). Existing single-score thresholding fails when bi-encoder similarity misses relevant chunks due to vocabulary mismatch — a failure mode cross-encoder scores can correct.

Method: ScoreGate uses two already-computed scores — bi-encoder similarity $s_i$ and cross-encoder reranker score $r_i$ — to make a gating decision at inference time. The key insight: a cross-encoder can "affirm" semantically relevant chunks that the bi-encoder ranked poorly due to vocabulary mismatch. No additional model inference calls are required; ScoreGate simply thresholds or combines these existing scores to decide which chunks to retain, thereby dynamically adjusting retrieval cardinality per query.

Result: On MS MARCO (200 dev queries), ScoreGate achieves MRR@10 = 0.401 with 35% fewer retained chunks than standard Top-K. On an internal benchmark (n=300, Fleiss' κ=0.87), it yields zero false positives (95% CI [96.4%, 100%]) at 97.77–99.34% recall, with 34.8% fewer tokens per query and only 31ms added latency. Production traffic results corroborate these findings.

Conclusion: Adaptive retrieval cardinality via ScoreGate can improve retrieval efficiency (fewer chunks, fewer tokens, negligible latency overhead) without degrading retrieval quality, as validated on both public benchmarks and real-world production traffic.

Abstract: Fixed-cardinality retrieval injects a constant top-K chunks into the generator regardless of query complexity, causing over-retrieval for narrow queries and under-retrieval for compositional ones. We describe ScoreGate, a lightweight score-space decision mechanism that controls retrieval cardinality at inference time using two scores already produced by the standard pipeline: bi-encoder similarity s_i and cross-encoder reranker score r_i, with no additional model inference calls required. Its core insight is that cross-encoder affirmation can rescue semantically relevant chunks that bi-encoder retrieval ranks poorly due to vocabulary mismatch -- a failure mode unaddressed by fixed-K or single-score thresholding. On MS MARCO (200 dev queries), ScoreGate achieves MRR@10 = 0.401 with 35% fewer retained chunks than Standard Top-K. On an internal benchmark (n=300, Fleiss' kappa=0.87), ScoreGate observed zero false positives (95% CI [96.4%, 100%]) at 97.77-99.34% recall, with 34.8% fewer tokens per query and only 31ms added latency. Results on both MS MARCO and real-world production traffic suggest that adaptive retrieval cardinality can improve retrieval efficiency without degrading retrieval quality.

</details>


### [211] [Verifiable User Simulation for Search and Recommendation Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14474)
*Chenglong Ma,Xinye Wanyan,Danula Hettiachchi,Ziqi Xu,Yongli Ren,Jeffrey Chan*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: This tutorial introduces an auditable framework for LLM-based user simulation, decomposing simulators into seven verifiable components to enable detection of biases, consistency checking, and fidelity assurance, with hands-on labs for recommendation and search evaluation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM-based user simulators are increasingly used to evaluate search, recommendation, and RAG systems, but they remain opaque — it is unclear why simulated users make choices or whether those choices align with intended profiles. Furthermore, LLMs exhibit demographic biases linked to language, education, and cultural context, raising equity concerns for minority groups.

Method: A design-and-audit framework treating user simulators as verifiable engineering artefacts with seven auditable components: (1) structured Persona, (2) task-aware Contract, (3) matched human-vs-agent Execution, (4) auditable Trace, (5) persona-aligned Verification, (6) structured Feedback, and (7) a Refinement loop. The tutorial distinguishes diagnostic discrepancy analysis from statistical validation and applies checks for fidelity, credibility, and demographic bias via two hands-on mini-labs.

Result: Participants gain the ability to inspect simulator behaviour end-to-end, apply fidelity/credibility/bias checks, and distinguish between diagnostic discrepancy analysis and statistical validation through practical exercises on recommendation-list evaluation and search-query formulation.

Conclusion: The framework offers a principled approach to making LLM-based user simulators transparent, auditable, and equitable, targeting IR and RecSys researchers and practitioners invested in responsible AI and user behaviour simulation.

Abstract: Large-language-model (LLM) based user simulation is increasingly adopted for evaluating search engines, recommender systems, and retrieval-augmented generation pipelines, yet most simulators remain opaque: it is difficult to determine why a simulated user made a particular choice or whether that choice is consistent with the intended user profile. Compounding this, recent research shows that LLMs can produce biased or discriminatory responses depending on user background characteristics such as language, education level, and cultural context, raising concerns about the equitable treatment of minority and disadvantaged groups. This half-day, in-person tutorial introduces a proposed design-and-audit framework that treats a user simulator as a verifiable engineering artefact composed of seven auditable components - structured Persona, task-aware Contract, matched human-vs-agent Execution, auditable Trace, persona-aligned Verification, structured Feedback, and a Refinement loop that updates personas and contracts. Through two hands-on mini-labs on recommendation-list evaluation and search-query formulation, participants will inspect simulator behaviour end-to-end, distinguish diagnostic discrepancy analysis from statistical validation, and apply checks for fidelity, credibility, and demographic bias. The tutorial targets information retrieval and recommender systems researchers and practitioners interested in user behaviour simulation and responsible AI.

</details>


### [212] [Private Information Retrieval for Large-Scale DNA-Based Data Storage](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14557)
*Gökberk Erdoğan,Daniella Bar-Lev,Rawad Bitar,Antonia Wachter-Zeh,Zohar Yakhini*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: The paper explores adapting two-server Private Information Retrieval (PIR) protocols to synthetic DNA-based data storage, addressing the unique biochemical challenges and privacy-efficiency trade-offs inherent in this novel storage medium.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While PIR is well-established for digital databases, synthetic DNA-based storage introduces fundamentally different constraints due to biochemical query mechanisms (e.g., hybridization, PCR, sequencing). The authors aim to bridge this gap by investigating how information-theoretic PIR can be realized in DNA storage systems, where querying is inherently a wet-lab process with distinct complexity and feasibility limitations.

Method: The authors propose two approaches for adapting two-server PIR protocols to DNA-based storage. These approaches are designed to navigate the tension between privacy guarantees, operational efficiency, and biochemical feasibility, illustrating how information-theoretic privacy trade-offs concretely manifest within DNA storage architectures.

Result: The paper demonstrates two distinct adaptation strategies for two-server PIR in DNA storage contexts. Each approach embodies different points on the privacy-efficiency-feasibility spectrum, revealing that biochemical constraints fundamentally reshape the trade-off landscape compared to purely digital PIR settings.

Conclusion: Information-theoretic PIR can be meaningfully adapted to DNA-based storage, but the biochemical nature of query execution introduces trade-offs not present in digital systems. The two proposed approaches offer different balances, suggesting that practical DNA-PIR will require careful co-design of cryptographic protocols and biochemical processes.

Abstract: We investigate Private Information Retrieval (PIR) in the context of synthetic DNA-based data storage. While PIR is a well-studied primitive for digital databases, extending it to DNA-based databases presents unique challenges arising from biochemical query mechanisms and their complexity. We propose two approaches for adapting two-server PIR protocols to DNA-based storage, balancing privacy, efficiency, and feasibility. These approaches illustrate how information-theoretic privacy trade-offs manifest in DNA-based storage systems.

</details>


<div id='cs.CR'></div>

# cs.CR [[Back]](#toc)

### [213] [FreoStream:Enhancing Stream Guardrails via Future-Aware Reasoning and Safety-Aligned Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13737)
*Jianwei Wang,Guoyang Shen,Yanhong Wu,Haoran Li,Hao Peng,Huiping Zhuang,Cen Chen,Ziqian Zeng*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: FreoStream is a streaming guardrail framework that uses Future-Aware Reasoning with a LoRA module and Safety-Aligned Optimization to reduce over-refusal and improve jailbreak detection, outperforming existing streaming guardrails on safety benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing streaming guardrails for LLM safety suffer from two key problems: (1) **over-refusal** — conservatively blocking tokens that appear sensitive but are actually safe, due to incomplete context at the token level; and (2) **failure to detect implicitly harmful content** from jailbreaking, again due to lack of full response context. These limitations motivate a method that can reason about future tokens before making safety decisions.

Method: FreoStream consists of two main components: (1) **Future-Aware Reasoning**: When the base guardrail flags a token as unsafe, a fine-tuned LoRA module is activated to perform a Future-Reason-Judge paradigm — it first predicts future tokens, then reasons about the full context, and finally issues a safety judgement, thereby reducing over-refusal by incorporating anticipated future information. (2) **Safety-Aligned Optimization**: This module extracts the safety-aligned component from the reasoning gradients and uses it to update the base guardrail model, enhancing its intrinsic streaming safety detection capability.

Result: Extensive experiments on various safety benchmarks show that FreoStream achieves lower over-refusal rates and better jailbreak defense compared to existing streaming guardrail methods.

Conclusion: FreoStream effectively addresses the dual challenges of over-refusal and implicit harm detection in streaming guardrails by leveraging future-aware reasoning and safety-aligned gradient optimization, establishing a new state-of-the-art for token-level LLM safety moderation.

Abstract: Stream guardrails enable token-level safety detection before full responses are generated. However, they often make overly conservative judgements and block those sensitive but safe tokens, which is known as over-refusal. Due to lack of full context, they also fail to detect implicitly harmful content from jailbreaking. To address these challenges, we propose FreoStream, a novel streaming guardrail framework. Specifically, FreoStream fine-tunes a LoRA module to perform Future-Aware Reasoning when the base guardrail detects unsafe tokens. The reasoning process follows a Future-Reason-Judge paradigm: predict the future, reason about the full context and give the final judgement. This design can effectively reduce over-refusal by incorporating the future information. Moreover, we introduce the Safety-Aligned Optimization module that extracts the safety-aligned component from the reasoning gradients to update the base guardrail model, thereby enhancing streaming safety detection. Extensive experiments on various safety benchmarks demonstrate that FreoStream achieves lower over-refusal rates and better jailbreak defense compared to existing streaming guardrails.

</details>


### [214] [SEVRA-BENCH: Social Engineering of Vulnerabilities in Review Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13757)
*Rui Melo,Riccardo Fogliato,Sean Zhou,Pratiksha Thaker,Zhiwei Steven Wu*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: SEVRA-BENCH evaluates whether LLM-based code reviewers can detect malicious pull requests where attackers control both the code change and the PR description. Using 1,062 PRs derived from real CVE fixes (inverted to reintroduce vulnerabilities) across the top 10 CWEs, wrapped in 15 social-engineering framings, the benchmark reveals a significant security gap between closed-source and open-source LLMs when acting as automated reviewers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing benchmarks for LLMs focus on static vulnerability detection or code generation, but fail to address a realistic threat: an automated PR reviewer that must reject a malicious contribution where the attacker controls both the vulnerable code and the accompanying natural-language narrative. As LLM reviewers are increasingly deployed in PR workflows with merge authority, this gap represents a concrete security risk.

Method: The authors construct SEVRA-BENCH by: (1) collecting real project commits that fixed CVEs from the top 10 CWE categories of the 2025 CWE Top 25; (2) automatically inverting those fixes to restore the original vulnerable code; (3) wrapping each malicious change in one of 15 social-engineering framings that manipulate claims, evidence, urgency, prior-approval signals, and authority appeals; (4) generating 1,062 adversarial PRs; and (5) evaluating 8 current LLMs as review agents in a realistic setting to measure approval rates.

Result: The evaluation of 8 LLMs reveals a sharp security capability gap between closed-source and open-source models in identifying and rejecting adversarially crafted PRs. The social-engineering framings prove effective at manipulating reviewer behavior.

Conclusion: SEVRA-BENCH provides a novel, realistic benchmark for measuring LLM reviewer robustness against adversarial PRs. The authors hope it will serve as a resource to improve open-source models and close the security gap with proprietary counterparts.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM) reviewers are increasingly used in pull-request (PR) workflows, where their approvals help decide which code is merged into a repository. This raises a question that benchmarks for static vulnerability detection or code generation do not address: can an automated reviewer reject a malicious contribution when the attacker controls both the code change and the accompanying PR text? We introduce SEVRA-BENCH (Social Engineering of Vulnerabilities in Review Agents), a benchmark that measures how often an automated reviewer approves such adversarial pull requests. Each malicious PR in SEVRA-BENCH is built from a real project commit that previously fixed a vulnerability listed in the Common Vulnerabilities and Exposures (CVE) database. We automatically invert that fix to restore the original vulnerable code and submit it as a pull request wrapped in one of 15 social-engineering framings, which vary the claims made, the supporting evidence, the urgency conveyed, signals of prior approval, and appeals to authority. SEVRA-BENCH contains 1,062 malicious PRs drawn from Common Vulnerabilities and Exposures (CVE)-linked fixes across the top 10 entries of the 2025 Common Weakness Enumeration (CWE) Top 25. In a realistic setting, we evaluate 8 current LLMs as code review agents on PRs that introduce vulnerabilities previously reported in public disclosures. Our results reveal a sharp gap in security capabilities between closed- and open-source models. We hope SEVRA-BENCH will serve as a valuable resource for advancing open-source models and narrowing this gap.

</details>


### [215] [Smart Blockchain-Based Access Control for the Internet of Things](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13798)
*Mahdi Manavi,Yunpeng Zhang,Guoning Chen*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: Proposes a risk-adaptive access control layer for Hyperledger Fabric that uses an off-chain LSTM oracle to tier requests (Low/Moderate/High) and enforce tier-conditioned state-based endorsement (SBE) policies, achieving stronger validation for risky requests without sacrificing latency for benign traffic.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Static endorsement policies in permissioned blockchains like Hyperledger Fabric apply uniform validation depth to all access control requests, irrespective of risk. In large-scale IoT deployments, this creates a tension between security (deep validation) and latency (fast processing of benign requests).

Method: A two-layer architecture: (1) an off-chain LSTM-based risk oracle that classifies each request into Low, Moderate, or High risk tiers and issues a signed attestation bound to client identity and target key/version; (2) on-chain chaincode at endorsing peers that verifies the attestation and enforces tier-conditioned state-based endorsement (SBE) policies, without altering the ordering service or consensus layer.

Result: Tier-conditioned endorsement strengthens validation for higher-risk requests while maintaining low confirmation latency for benign workloads, as demonstrated on a Fabric testbed.

Conclusion: The proposed risk-adaptive enforcement layer successfully balances security and latency in Hyperledger Fabric by dynamically scaling endorsement depth based on real-time risk assessment, making it suitable for latency-sensitive, large-scale IoT access control scenarios.

Abstract: Securing access control in large-scale Internet of Things (IoT) deployments requires mechanisms that adapt to risk while preserving low latency for benign traffic. Permissioned blockchains such as Hyperledger Fabric offer auditability through smart contracts, but static endorsement policies impose the same validation depth on all requests, regardless of security posture. We propose a risk-adaptive enforcement layer for Hyperledger Fabric that couples an off-chain LSTM-based risk oracle with deterministic on-chain checks. The oracle assigns each request to a tier (Low, Moderate, High) and issues a signed attestation bound to the client identity and target key/version. Endorsing peers verify the attestation in chaincode and enforce tier-conditioned SBE policies without modifying the ordering service or consensus. Experiments on a Fabric testbed show that tier-conditioned endorsement strengthens validation for higher-risk requests while retaining low confirmation latency for benign workloads.

</details>


### [216] [Information Flow Paths from RTL Traces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13860)
*Calvin Deutschbein,Owyn Wyatt*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: This paper presents a novel automated approach for constructing complete information flow paths from RTL trace data, addressing the manual and error-prone nature of hardware security validation by combining information flow tracking with specification mining to trace how sensitive information propagates through a design.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Hardware security validation is largely manual, requiring engineers to define threat models, specify security properties, detect violations, and assess consequences — each step being labor-intensive and error-prone. Prior work on information flow tracking at the RTL level could only identify that information flowed between registers but failed to reconstruct full propagation paths, limiting understanding of how sensitive data actually moves through the design.

Method: The authors leverage new hardware analysis acceleration technologies to develop a method for constructing complete information flow paths from RTL trace data, going beyond mere detection of inter-register flows to capture the full propagation trajectory of sensitive information through the hardware design.

Result: The paper proposes a system that automates property generation and security validation by reconstructing complete information flow paths, overcoming the limitation of prior approaches that only identified pairwise register flows without full path reproduction.

Conclusion: Automated construction of full information flow paths from RTL traces represents a significant advancement over prior pairwise detection methods, enabling more thorough and less error-prone hardware security validation while reducing the manual burden on validation engineers.

Abstract: Security validation is an important yet challenging part of the hardware design process, yet, by convention, validation engineers are tasked with defining the threat model, specifying the relevant security properties, detecting any violations of those properties, and assessing the consequences to system security, each of which is manually intensive and may introduce errors. The combined technologies of information flow tracking and specification mining represent an automated approach to property generation and validation, but prior work on information flow tracking on RTL trace data was limited to find cases under which information flowed between registers, without reproducing full paths to capture how sensitive information propagates through a design. With the introduction of new technologies accelerating hardware analysis, we develop a novel approach for constructing information flow paths from register transfer level (RTL) trace data.

</details>


### [217] [RTL-Arrow: Hardware-to-Cloud Bridge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13865)
*Calvin Deutschbein,Jimmy Ostler*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: The paper proposes RTL-Arrow, a framework that converts hardware simulation outputs (value change dumps) into dataframes for data science workflows, bridging the gap between hardware execution traces and software-level program verification in security research.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Researchers in hardware security spend excessive development time learning to understand and manually parse traces-of-execution from hardware designs. This manual process is inefficient for identifying vulnerabilities at the hardware, software, or interface levels. There is a need to standardize and streamline program verification across these boundaries.

Method: The RTL-Arrow framework compiles performant binaries that translate simulated hardware execution outputs — specifically value change dumps (VCDs) — into cloud-ready dataframes compatible with modern data science workflows. This bridges the hardware/data divide, enabling standardized program verification across hardware and software levels.

Result: The paper describes the framework's approach, its benefits in streamlining security analysis workflows, and lessons learned from packaging and distributing these libraries within the Willamette University hardware security research program.

Conclusion: RTL-Arrow successfully lowers the barrier to entry for hardware security analysis by converting low-level VCD traces into accessible dataframe formats, enabling researchers to leverage modern data science tooling for cross-layer vulnerability identification.

Abstract: Hardware Security at Willamette is a Willamette University affiliated research group studying the hardware-software interface of security critical services. Within our program, we noticed many researchers spent considerable development time learning to understand and manually parse traces-of-execution of hardware designs which are used to identifying whether vulnerabilities or weaknesses arise at the hardware, software, or interface level. We propose the "RTL-Arrow" framework, a framework to compile performant binaries which bridge the hardware/data divide. We translate the outputs of simulated hardware execution, as "value change dumps" into modern data science workflows as cloud-ready "dataframes", to standardize program verification across the hardware and software levels. We describe our approach, its benefits, and lessons learned from the process of packaging and distributing these libraries for our security research program.

</details>


### [218] [Crypto x AI, AI x Crypto: A Survey](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13892)
*Sarah Allen,Pranay Anchuri,James Austgen,Maryam Bahrani,Samuel Breckenridge,Aaron Buchwald,Christian Cachin,Andrés Fábrega,Jared Fernandez,James Hsin-yu Chiang,Marwa Mouallem,Roi Bar-Zur,Neil DeSilva,Ittay Eyal,Giulia Fanti,Ari Juels,Andrew Miller,Christian Sillaber,Dani Vilardell,Pramod Viswanath,Wenhao Wang,Matt Weinberg,Sen Yang,Jianzhu Yao,Fan Zhang*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: A survey systematizing the intersection of AI and crypto (blockchain technologies), examining both directions—AI for crypto and crypto for AI—summarizing existing work, identifying open research questions, and concluding that meaningful integration remains in early stages.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The growing hype around crypto × AI has created confusion about what has actually been accomplished, what the genuine opportunities and challenges are, and which open questions deserve research attention.

Method: A systematic literature and ecosystem survey that categorizes existing work along two axes: (1) AI applied to blockchain/crypto systems, and (2) blockchain/crypto applied to AI systems. The paper distills key takeaways and critically examines industry misconceptions.

Result: The survey reveals that despite substantial hype, concrete and meaningful integration between AI and crypto is nascent. Several promising directions exist in both directions (e.g., AI for smart contract auditing, decentralized AI training), but fundamental challenges remain unresolved.

Conclusion: AI and crypto remain in very early stages of meaningful integration, with considerable open research questions. The authors caution against pervasive industry misconceptions that overstate current capabilities.

Abstract: The intersection of crypto x AI is spawning papers, products, online posts, and companies. All the surrounding buzz, though, obscures what exactly has been done, what the opportunities and challenges are, and what open questions deserve attention. This survey paper asks what AI can do for blockchain-based technologies (broadly construed as "crypto") (crypto x AI), and vice versa (AI x crypto). We systematize existing work, summarize key takeaways, highlight open research questions, and offer a perspective on pervasive industry misconceptions, concluding that AI and crypto are still in the very early stages of meaningful integration.

</details>


### [219] [Side-Channel Attacks Bypass Protection in 3D Printers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13952)
*Eric Yocam,Varghese Vaidyan,Micah Flack,Gurcan Comert,Judith L. Mwakalonge*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: The paper empirically evaluates Active Motor Noise Cancellation (AMNC) in commercial FDM 3D printers. AMNC effectively neutralizes the acoustic side channel (accuracy at chance), but the vibration channel—which AMNC does not target—still leaks IP-relevant information, with temporal sequence features enabling ~61% classification accuracy. The leak is device-specific and insufficient for full geometric reconstruction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AMNC is deployed as a hardware countermeasure in commercial FDM 3D printers to defend against acoustic side-channel attacks that steal intellectual property (IP). However, no prior empirical evaluation of a deployed AMNC system exists. The authors aim to rigorously assess whether AMNC truly closes the side-channel leakage or merely shifts it to other modalities.

Method: The authors use a public dataset of synchronized acoustic and vibration recordings from two AMNC-equipped Bambu Lab printers across 12 object classes. They evaluate: (1) acoustic channel classification post-AMNC; (2) vibration channel leakage using summary statistics, frequency-only features, and full-sequence temporal models (with an order-shuffling control to isolate sequential contributions); and (3) cross-device transferability of learned classifiers.

Result: AMNC reduces acoustic classification to chance (8.33%). Vibration summary statistics yield ~31% pooled accuracy (36-47% within-printer). Frequency-only features perform at chance, indicating waveform shape carries negligible information. A full-sequence temporal model achieves ~61% accuracy, with shuffling dropping it to ~33%, confirming genuine sequential leakage tied to print progression. Cross-printer transfer is near chance, showing device-specific leakage. The vibration channel does not support full geometric reconstruction on this dataset.

Conclusion: AMNC is an effective acoustic-only defense but does not address the vibration side channel, which retains partial, geometry-correlated, device-specific leakage. Reconstruction-grade attacks would likely require magnetic or power channels, which AMNC also leaves untouched. The authors release all code for reproducibility.

Abstract: Active Motor Noise Cancellation (AMNC) ships in commercial fused deposition modeling (FDM) 3D printers as a hardware countermeasure against acoustic side-channel attacks that target intellectual property (IP). We present the first empirical evaluation of a deployed AMNC countermeasure, using a public dataset of synchronized acoustic and vibration recordings from two AMNC-equipped Bambu Lab printers across 12 object classes. AMNC fully neutralizes the acoustic channel: classification accuracy is indistinguishable from the 8.33% random baseline. The vibration channel, which AMNC does not target, still leaks. With summary statistics the leak is coarse and amplitude-driven (vibration accuracy approximately 31% pooled, 36-47% within-printer), while the waveform shape carries essentially nothing (frequency-only features at chance). A full-sequence temporal model that ingests the ordered evolution of the print raises accuracy to approximately 61%, and an order-shuffling control (approximately 33%) shows that a substantial component is genuinely sequential and tied to print progression. The leak is device-specific: a classifier trained on one printer transfers near chance to the other. We conclude that AMNC is an acoustic-only defense: vibration remains a partial, geometry-correlated side channel it does not address, but one that does not, on this dataset, support full geometric reconstruction; reconstruction-grade attacks would require the magnetic or power channels AMNC also leaves untouched. We release all code.

</details>


### [220] [Software Dark Matter: Gazing at Uncharted Files to Navigate SBOM Integrations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13966)
*Abhishek Reddypalle,Dennis Roellke,Santiago Torres-Arias*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: The paper identifies "Software Dark Matter" (SDM) — security-critical files in software artifacts that go unaccounted for by package manager metadata — revealing a fundamental flaw in current SBOM/metadata-centric approaches to supply chain transparency. Using their DARKFILES tool across DockerHub, Maven Central, plugin marketplaces, and an enterprise environment, they demonstrate SDM correlates with vulnerable software, secrets, and cryptographic keys, and introduce metrics like artifact fidelity and the concept of Packaging Lag.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current software supply chain security relies on SBOMs generated from package manager metadata, operating under the flawed assumption that an artifact's declared dependencies fully represent its actual filesystem contents. This creates a systemic visibility gap where unaccounted-for files — SDM — harbor vulnerabilities, secrets, and sensitive material that evade existing security pipelines.

Method: The authors implement DARKFILES, a reference tool that cross-references an artifact's actual filesystem contents against its associated metadata declarations. They analyze four disjoint ecosystems (DockerHub, Maven Central, Jenkins/OpenVSX plugin marketplaces, and an enterprise environment). They introduce the SDM ratio metric (untracked files / total file count) and the concept of Packaging Lag. They cross-reference untracked packages against CVE databases and directly discover CVEs.

Result: SDM is pervasive across all four ecosystems. The authors demonstrate that SDM exposes vulnerable software invisible to SBOM-driven pipelines, directly discover three confirmed high-severity CVEs, and show high correlation between SDM and sensitive information (secrets, cryptographic keys). They also identify Packaging Lag, where official metadata remains outdated across multiple versions before catching up to actual artifact content.

Conclusion: The metadata-centric SBOM paradigm is fundamentally insufficient for software supply chain transparency. SDM represents a systemic blind spot that must be addressed through filesystem-aware approaches. Artifact fidelity metrics and detection of Packaging Lag should be integrated into security pipelines to close this visibility gap.

Abstract: Modern software supply chains have evolved into vast, heterogeneous networks where transparency - the granular understanding of all software components - is now a critical security requirement. While Software Bills of Materials (SBOMs) have emerged as the primary mechanism for this transparency, current industry practices rely on a metadata-centric paradigm that assumes an artifact is defined solely by its package manager declarations. We posit that this assumption is fundamentally flawed, creating a systemic visibility gap we define as Software Dark Matter (SDM). SDM represents the set of security-critical files present in an artifact's filesystem that are unaccounted for by its associated metadata. We implement a reference tool, DARKFILES, and use it to analyze four ecosystems of disjoint nature: DockerHub, Maven Central, plugin/extension marketplaces (Jenkins plugins and OpenVSX), and a real-world enterprise environment.
  Our research makes the following contributions: we introduce a general-purpose metric for artifact fidelity calculating SDM as the ratio of untracked files per total file count. We introduce Packaging Lag, a phenomenon where official metadata remains out-of-date across multiple versions before catching up to an artifact's actual content. We demonstrate that SDM exposes vulnerable software invisible to SBOM-driven pipelines both by cross-referencing untracked packages against known CVE databases and through the direct discovery of three confirmed high-severity CVEs, showing that SDM is highly correlated with sensitive information including secrets and cryptographic keys.

</details>


### [221] [Choric Masking in Ambient Release Systems: A Finite Certificate Calculus for Trace Indistinguishability under Bounded Audiences](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13967)
*Faruk Alpay,Taylan Alpay*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: A finite certificate calculus for privacy auditing in "ambient release systems" using convex geometry, hypothesis testing, and information-theoretic bounds to verify "choric masking" — whether one can statistically distinguish a protected locus from admissible cover traces under bounded observation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Public-facing release systems (e.g., anonymized data publishing, traffic routing, credential presentation) create staged probabilistic environments where protected coordinates may be indirectly readable through side channels — timing, visible roles, repeated movement, linked rooms, post-release state. Existing privacy notions lack a finite, checkable framework that accounts for partial observability, bounded attention, and compositional release across linked contexts. The paper aims to fill this gap with a formal, decidable calculus.

Method: The authors model trace laws as polytopes over finite horizons, audiences as measurement operators that project traces into an observable subspace, and "choric masking" as polytope intersection in this projected space. Certification uses: (1) separating hyperplanes for exposure detection, (2) kernel obstructions, (3) hypothesis-testing bounds, (4) Fano-type localization lower bounds, and (5) support separation in downstream rooms. The calculus includes composition rules for linked releases, aperture identities for gaze-thinned observation, data-processing laws for attention lenses, and a "repeated-room debt theorem."

Result: The paper establishes measurement-polytope equivalence for exact and approximate masks, dual separation certificates, data-processing laws for attention lenses, aperture identities for gaze-thinned observation, lower bounds for mandatory unique gestures, composition rules for linked releases, and a repeated-room debt theorem showing that unresolved pressure broadens selection and shifts cost onto cover populations without localizing the source.

Conclusion: The calculus provides a finite, checkable language for auditing privacy, unlinkability, side-channel leakage, and accountability in public-facing release systems. It enables precise reasoning about when and how a protected locus can be statistically distinguished under bounded, compositional observation.

Abstract: This paper develops a finite certificate calculus for ambient release systems, staged probabilistic environments in which a protected coordinate is not observed directly but can remain statistically readable through visible roles, timing, repeated movement, bounded attention, linked rooms, and post-release state. The security notion, choric masking, requires the trace law induced by a protected locus to lie inside or near the convex hull of admissible cover traces under the tests available to a specified audience. For finite horizons, trace laws form polytopes, audiences induce measurement operators, and masking becomes intersection in the projected measurement space. Exposure is certified by separating hyperplanes, kernel obstructions, hypothesis-testing bounds, Fano-type localization lower bounds, and support separation in downstream rooms. The calculus distinguishes trace residue from carrier localization, full-trace exposure from attention-filtered exposure, first-room masking from delayed post-release exposure, and unresolved system pressure from carrier hazard. It proves measurement-polytope equivalence for exact and approximate masks, dual separation certificates, data-processing laws for attention lenses, aperture identities for gaze-thinned observation, lower bounds for mandatory unique gestures, composition rules for linked releases, and a repeated-room debt theorem showing how unresolved pressure can broaden selection and shift cost onto cover populations without localizing the source. The result is a finite, checkable language for auditing privacy, unlinkability, side-channel leakage, and accountability in public-facing release systems.

</details>


### [222] [Hidden in Plain Sight: Benchmarking Agent Safety Against Decomposition Attacks with DECOMPBENCH](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13994)
*Vikhyath Kothamasu,Virginia Smith,Chhavi Yadav*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: DeCompBench is a benchmark designed to evaluate LLM-agent safety under decomposition attacks, where harmful tasks are broken into benign subtasks that evade safety mechanisms but collectively achieve malicious goals. Experiments show state-of-the-art agents have high refusal rates on monolithic harmful tasks but much lower refusal rates on decomposed variants.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing agent safety benchmarks assess multi-turn and multi-tool settings but fail to explicitly capture decompositional misuse — where adversaries break harmful tasks into individually benign subtasks that cumulatively achieve malicious intent. Realistic adversarial execution flows involving such decomposition attacks are underrepresented in current evaluations.

Method: The authors introduce DeCompBench, constructed with a decomposition-by-design principle using a graphical framework. This framework enables harmful task decomposition into individually benign and executable subtasks with realistic workflows. A custom decomposer is used to evaluate state-of-the-art LLM-based agents against both monolithic and decomposed harmful tasks.

Result: State-of-the-art agents exhibit high refusal rates when presented with monolithic harmful tasks, but significantly lower refusal rates on their decomposed variants. Agents often inadvertently fulfill adversarial objectives when processing the decomposed subtasks, revealing a critical vulnerability in current safety mechanisms.

Conclusion: Current LLM-based agent safety mechanisms are inadequately prepared for decomposition attacks. There is a pressing need for safety evaluations that explicitly account for decompositional misuse and for corresponding defenses. The dataset is publicly released to facilitate further research.

Abstract: LLM-based Agents are becoming increasingly capable and widely deployed, creating growing incentives for adversarial misuse in the real-world. A key emerging threat is Decomposition Attacks \cite{glukhov2024breach, jones2024adversaries} in which a harmful task is broken into simpler, benign subtasks that evade safety mechanisms when executed separately but cumulatively fulfill the malicious intent. Although recent benchmarks assess agent safety in multi-turn and multi-tool-use settings, they do not explicitly capture this form of decompositional misuse and may not represent realistic adversarial execution flows. To this end, we introduce DeCompBench, a benchmark designed specifically to evaluate agentic safety under decomposition attacks. DeCompBench is created with a decomposition-by-design principle using a graphical framework and enables harmful task decomposition into individually benign and executable subtasks with realistic workflows. Our experiments using a custom decomposer show that state-of-the-art agents exhibit high refusal rates on monolithic harmful tasks, but significantly lower refusal rates on their decomposed variants, while often inadvertently fulfilling the adversarial objectives. These findings underscore the need for safety evaluations against decomposition attacks and corresponding defenses. Our dataset is publicly available and can be found at https://huggingface.co/datasets/decompositionbench/DeCompBench.

</details>


### [223] [Pseudonym Scheme Based on Hybrid Certificates for Security Credential Management System in Vehicular Communications](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14008)
*Abel C. H. Chen,F. J. Hwang,Yu-Chih Wei,Chin-Chen Chang,Bon-Yeh Lin*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: Proposes a hybrid ECC+PQC certificate scheme and generalized pseudonym framework for vehicular communications to resist future quantum attacks, with performance benchmarking of RSA, ECC, and NIST-standardized PQC algorithms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current IEEE/ETSI vehicular security standards (SCMS, BKE) rely on ECC, which is vulnerable to quantum computing. A post-quantum resilient solution is needed before quantum threats materialize.

Method: (1) Design of a hybrid certificate combining ECC with PQC; (2) A generalized pseudonym scheme decoupling pseudonym certificate public keys from enrollment certificate public keys, compatible with arbitrary cryptographic algorithms; (3) Comprehensive performance evaluation comparing RSA, ECC, and NIST PQC candidates on message length and computation time.

Result: The hybrid certificate design provides quantum-resilient infrastructure; the generalized pseudonym scheme eliminates linkability between pseudonym and enrollment certificates. Performance benchmarks across algorithm classes yield concrete recommendations for pseudonym scheme selection in vehicular settings.

Conclusion: Hybrid ECC+PQC certificates are a practical transitional strategy for vehicular communications. Specific pseudonym schemes using hybrid certificates are recommended based on empirical performance trade-offs for secure, efficient deployment.

Abstract: In recent years, the Institute of Electrical and Electronics Engineers (IEEE) and the European Telecommunications Standards Institute (ETSI) have developed a series of security communication standards for vehicular communications. These standards include mechanisms such as the Security Credential Management System (SCMS) and Butterfly Key Expansion (BKE) to protect vehicle privacy. However, these standards are mainly based on the Elliptic-Curve Cryptography (ECC), which may be vulnerable to attacks from quantum computing in the future. In response to this potential risk, this study proposes a hybrid certificate that combines the ECC with Post-Quantum Cryptography (PQC). This approach enables infrastructure systems to be built on cryptographic foundations that are more resilient to quantum-based attacks. Furthermore, this study presents a generalized pseudonym scheme that is compatible with various cryptographic algorithms for generating pseudonym certificates. This design aims to eliminate the possibility of inferring any correlation between the public key in a pseudonym certificate and that in an enrollment certificate. This study also conducts a comprehensive performance evaluation of the RSA, ECC, and PQC algorithms, particularly those standardized by the National Institute of Standards and Technology (NIST). The comparison considers factors such as message length and computation time. Based on the findings, this study recommends suitable pseudonym schemes that adopt hybrid certificates for secure and efficient use in vehicular communications.

</details>


### [224] [Same-Origin Policy for Agentic Browsers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14027)
*Xilong Wang,Xiaoxing Chen,Patrick Li,Dawn Song,Neil Gong*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: Agentic browsers, which use AI agents to automate web tasks via natural language, may violate the same-origin policy (SOP) by serving as unintended cross-origin data channels. The authors construct SOPBench to evaluate these violations, show that existing agentic browsers frequently violate SOP, and propose SOPGuard—an enforcement mechanism implemented in BrowserOS that effectively enforces SOP with minimal overhead.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The same-origin policy (SOP) is a foundational browser security mechanism, but its effectiveness in the emerging paradigm of agentic browsers—where autonomous AI agents execute web tasks—has never been systematically studied. The authors aim to determine whether agentic browsers inadvertently create automated cross-origin data flows that circumvent SOP protections.

Method: The authors first conduct an observational analysis identifying agentic browsers as potential automated channels for cross-origin data flows. They then construct SOPBench, a benchmark suite designed to systematically evaluate SOP violations across different agentic browser scenarios (both benign and adversarial). Based on the findings, they design and implement SOPGuard, a tailored SOP enforcement mechanism integrated into BrowserOS, an open-source agentic browser. Extensive empirical evaluation measures both security effectiveness and runtime overhead.

Result: Evaluation using SOPBench reveals that existing agentic browsers frequently violate SOP under both normal usage and attack scenarios. SOPGuard, when implemented in BrowserOS, effectively enforces SOP protections while preserving browser utility and incurring only a small runtime overhead.

Conclusion: SOP is demonstrably insufficient in current agentic browser designs, representing a nontrivial security gap. SOPGuard provides a practical, lightweight solution that restores SOP enforcement without degrading the user experience, and the open-source release of code and benchmark data facilitates further research.

Abstract: Agentic browsers integrate autonomous AI agents into web browsers, enabling users to accomplish web tasks through natural-language instructions. The same-origin policy (SOP) is a fundamental browser security mechanism that prevents unauthorized automated cross-origin data flows induced by scripts. However, whether SOP remains effective in agentic browsers is an open question that has not been systematically studied. In this work, we bridge this gap. We first observe that an agentic browser can itself serve as an automated channel for cross-origin data flows, potentially leading to SOP violations. To investigate this phenomenon, we construct SOPBench, a benchmark for evaluating SOP violations in agentic browsers. Our evaluation shows that existing agentic browsers frequently violate SOP, both in benign settings and under attacks. To address this problem, we propose SOPGuard, an SOP enforcement mechanism tailored to agentic browsers. We implement SOPGuard in BrowserOS, an open-source agentic browser. Extensive evaluations demonstrate that SOPGuard effectively enforces SOP while preserving utility and incurring only a small runtime overhead. Our code and data are available at https://github.com/wxl-lxw/BrowserOS-SOPGuard.

</details>


### [225] [Defending the Core: A Centrality-Based Protection Strategy for Supply Chain Security in npm Dependency Network](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14036)
*Zixin Wang*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: The npm dependency network exhibits a scale-free topology, making it highly vulnerable to targeted attacks on hub packages. The authors propose a defense combining centrality-based hardening of critical nodes and pruning of trivial dependency edges, showing that securing just 1% of nodes and removing 30% of weak edges prevents network collapse under attack.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Modern software supply chains depend heavily on shared open-source dependencies (e.g., npm), which accelerates development but introduces systemic vulnerabilities. The authors aim to characterize the structural fragility of the npm dependency network and develop practical mitigation strategies against targeted attacks and cascading malware infections.

Method: The authors model the npm ecosystem as a directed graph with 53,481 packages (nodes) and 78,520 dependency edges. They analyze its topology, classify it as scale-free, and simulate targeted attacks on high-degree hubs. They then propose and evaluate a dual-pronged defense: (1) Centrality-Based Node-Hardening — applying strict security protocols to the most central nodes, and (2) Dependency Weight Warning — pruning structurally trivial edges (30% threshold) to reduce cascading risk.

Result: Simulations demonstrate that hardening the top 1% of nodes by centrality, combined with pruning 30% of low-weight dependency edges, effectively prevents catastrophic network collapse and neutralizes cascading malware propagation across the npm dependency network.

Conclusion: The npm dependency network's scale-free nature demands targeted defense. A modest investment in securing a small fraction of critical packages and removing weak dependencies can dramatically improve the resilience of the entire software supply chain ecosystem.

Abstract: The modern software supply chain, taking Node Package Manager (npm) dependency network for example, relies heavily on shared open-source dependencies. While this promotes rapid development, it introduces systemic vulnerabilities as well. Concerning this potential risk, we analyze the npm dependency network by modeling 53,481 packages and 78,520 dependency edges, and classify the network as a scale-free topology. Thus, we demonstrate its inherent vulnerability to targeted attacks on high-degree hubs. To mitigate this, we propose and evaluate a dual-pronged defense strategy consisting of Centrality-Based Node-Hardening and Dependency Weight Warning system. Moreover, by simulating the network under various attack scenarios, we prove that applying strict security protocols to just the top 1% of nodes, combined with pruning 30% of structurally trivial edges, prevents catastrophic network collapse and neutralizes cascading malware infections. The source code can be found at https://github.com/5tarWhee1/Centrality-Based-Protection-Strategy-for-Supply-Chain-Security-in-npm-Dependency-Network.

</details>


### [226] [Hierarchical Identity-Based Signature with Designated Aggregator from Lattices](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14090)
*Stuti Kumari,Kunal Dey,Vikas Srivastava,Sumit Kumar Debnath*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: This paper introduces the Hierarchical Identity-Based Signature with Designated Aggregator (HIBS-DA) framework and presents the first lattice-based construction, enabling users across different hierarchical levels to generate signatures that aggregate into a single compact signature, reducing communication and verification overhead.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In hierarchical organizations, authenticating data from multiple users is complex and resource-intensive. Existing solutions lack an efficient mechanism for aggregating signatures across hierarchical levels. The authors aim to address this gap by providing a compact, secure, and efficient signature aggregation scheme tailored to hierarchical settings.

Method: The authors propose the HIBS-DA framework and construct the first lattice-based instantiation. The scheme leverages lattice-based cryptography to achieve post-quantum security. Users at different hierarchical levels generate individual signatures using their hierarchical identity-based keys, and a designated aggregator combines them into a single compact signature.

Result: The proposed lattice-based HIBS-DA construction is provably secure, correct, and resistant to forgery. It successfully reduces communication and verification costs by aggregating multiple hierarchical signatures into one compact signature.

Conclusion: The first lattice-based HIBS-DA scheme is practical and secure for large-scale hierarchical environments such as universities, corporations, and government agencies, offering significant efficiency gains in multi-user authentication scenarios.

Abstract: In hierarchical organizations, authenticating data from multiple users can be complex and resource-intensive. Hierarchical Identity-Based Signature with Designated Aggregator (HIBS-DA) provides an efficient solution by allowing users at different levels to generate signatures that can be combined into a single, compact signature. We first introduce the HIBS-DA framework and present the {\em{first}} lattice-based construction of HIBS-DA. Our scheme allows users at different hierarchical levels to generate individual signatures that can be aggregated into a single, compact signature, reducing communication and verification costs. The proposed construction is secure, correct, and resistant to forgery, making it suitable for large-scale environments such as universities, corporations, and government agencies.

</details>


### [227] [SkillMutator: Benchmarking and Defending Language-and-Code Cross-modal Attacks on LLM Agent Skills](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14154)
*Youngduk Kim,Minkyoo Song,Seungwon Shin*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: This paper introduces SkillMutator, a benchmark and defense framework for detecting cross-modal attacks on LLM Agent Skills. Attackers exploit the disconnect between natural-language specifications (SKILL.md) and executable code to hide malicious behaviors. Existing scanners detect only 2-17% of such attacks. The authors propose a reasoning-trajectory distillation method that trains small open-weight models, achieving 88.2% detection (surpassing frontier models like GPT-4o and GPT-5.4) while avoiding third-party data exposure and high API costs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM agents increasingly load Agent Skills that pair natural-language instructions with executable code. The safety assessment of such skills requires cross-modal reasoning across both language and code, creating a novel attack surface where attackers embed implicit malicious directives in documentation that steer agents toward harmful actions (e.g., data exfiltration) while scripts appear benign. Prior work treats skills solely as prompt-injection vectors or static code artifacts, failing to capture attacks emerging from cross-modal interactions. Existing scanners perform poorly (2-17% detection), motivating the need for a dedicated benchmark and defense.

Method: The authors make two contributions: (1) SkillMutator, the first benchmark for install-time detection of language-and-code cross-modal attacks. It emulates an adversarial mutation process spanning 13 attack categories, iteratively refining malicious skills using scanner feedback to make injected behaviors indistinguishable from legitimate workflows. (2) A four-phase reasoning-trajectory distillation framework that distills traces from frontier teacher models into smaller open-weight student models. This produces a locally deployable scanner that avoids third-party data exposure and excessive API costs, evaluated using Qwen2.5-Coder-7B-Instruct as the student model.

Result: On the strongest SkillMutator subset (n=76), the distilled Qwen2.5-Coder-7B-Instruct model achieves 88.2% detection accuracy, substantially outperforming GPT-4o-mini (23.7%) and GPT-5.4-mini (79.0%), and slightly surpassing the frontier-level GPT-5.4 (86.8%). In comparison, open-source and commercial skill scanners detect only 2-8% and 9-17% of cross-modal attacks, respectively.

Conclusion: Practical defense against cross-modal attacks on Agent Skills is feasible without relying on costly frontier models. The reasoning-trajectory distillation approach enables smaller, locally deployable models to match or exceed frontier model performance while preserving data privacy and reducing operational costs. This establishes a viable path toward robust, install-time safety screening for LLM agent ecosystems.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM) agents increasingly extend their capabilities at runtime by loading Agent Skills, which pair natural-language specifications (SKILL.md) with executable scripts and resources. Because a skill's behavior relies on both natural-language instructions and executable code, assessing its safety requires cross-modal reasoning, creating a new language-and-code attack surface. Attackers can present a benign workflow in SKILL.md while embedding implicit directives that steer the agent to exfiltrate sensitive files, even if the scripts appear harmless. This attack surface remains understudied; prior work treats skills merely as prompt-injection vectors or static code artifacts, leaving attacks emerging from cross-modal interactions largely unmeasured. In our evaluation, open-source and commercial skill scanners detect only 2%-8% and 9%-17% of such attacks, respectively. To address this gap, we introduce SkillMutator, the first benchmark for install-time detection of language-and-code cross-modal attacks on Agent Skills. It emulates an adversarial mutation process across 13 attack categories, iteratively refining malicious skills using scanner feedback to make injected behaviors indistinguishable from legitimate workflows. We further propose a four-phase reasoning-trajectory distillation framework to distill frontier-teacher traces into smaller open-weight models. This produces a locally deployable scanner avoiding third-party data exposure and excessive API costs. On the strongest SkillMutator subset (n=76), our distilled model (Qwen2.5-Coder-7B-Instruct) improves detection from 17.1% to 88.2%, surpassing GPT-4o-mini (23.7%) and GPT-5.4-mini (79.0%), and reaching frontier-level GPT-5.4 (86.8%). These results show practical defense against cross-modal attacks is feasible without relying on costly frontier models.

</details>


### [228] [Security Evaluation of Mobile Banking Applications in Sudan](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14165)
*Abdelmonim Naway*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: A security audit of Sudan's four leading mobile banking apps (Bankak, Fawry, Okash, Sahil) using SAST tools reveals critical vulnerabilities including absent SSL pinning, unsafe TrustManager implementations, universally flawed random number generation (CWE-330), deprecated cryptographic algorithms, and verbose debugging symbols in production APKs. Bankak exhibited the highest risk with 12 vulnerabilities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The rapid digitalization of Sudan's financial sector has led to a proliferation of mobile banking applications without commensurate security auditing, creating potential systemic risks for over 1.6 million users. The study aims to fill this gap by conducting a structured, standards-based security evaluation of the most widely adopted applications.

Method: Static Application Security Testing (SAST) was performed using Mobile Security Framework (MobSF) and Quixxi. All four applications (Bankak, Fawry, Okash, Sahil) were evaluated against the OWASP Mobile Application Security Verification Standard (MASVS), and findings were mapped to Common Weakness Enumeration (CWE) identifiers for systematic vulnerability classification.

Result: Bankak had the highest risk profile with 12 vulnerabilities, including critical absence of SSL certificate pinning and unsafe TrustManager implementations, exposing it to MitM attacks. Fawry showed relative maturity with 7 vulnerabilities. All four apps failed secure random number generation (CWE-330), risking session token integrity. Bankak and Okash used deprecated cryptographic algorithms (MD5/SHA-1). While all apps successfully disabled ADB backups, all retained verbose debugging symbols in production APKs, facilitating reverse engineering.

Conclusion: The study reveals systemic security deficiencies across Sudan's fintech ecosystem, with universal failures in critical areas like random number generation and production hardening. The authors provide actionable technical recommendations and advocate for adopting "security-by-design" principles to strengthen the national financial infrastructure.

Abstract: The rapid digitalization of the Sudanese financial sector has precipitated a surge in Mobile Banking Applications (MBAs); however, this growth has frequently outpaced rigorous security auditing. This study provides a comprehensive technical audit of the four most widely used Sudanese MBAs( Bankak, Fawry, Okash, and Sahil )collectively serving a user base of over 1.6 million. Utilizing Static Application Security Testing (SAST) via the Mobile Security Framework (MobSF) and Quixxi, the applications were evaluated against the OWASP Mobile Application Security Verification Standard (MASVS). Findings were mapped to Common Weakness Enumeration (CWE) identifiers to identify systemic vulnerabilities. Analysis revealed critical disparities in security posture. Bankak, the market leader, exhibited the highest risk profile (12 vulnerabilities), including a critical absence of SSL certificate pinning and unsafe TrustManager implementations, rendering it highly susceptible to Man-in-the-Middle (MitM) attacks. While Fawry demonstrated relative maturity (7 vulnerabilities), a universal failure was observed across all four applications regarding secure random number generation (CWE-330), potentially compromising session token integrity. Additionally, Bankak and Okash were found to utilize deprecated cryptographic algorithms (MD5/SHA-1). Notably, all applications successfully disabled ADB backups, yet 100% retained verbose debugging symbols in production APKs, significantly lowering the barrier for reverse engineering. This research addresses a critical gap in the national fintech ecosystem by providing actionable technical recommendations for developers and a strategic roadmap for implementing "security-by-design" principles across the sector.

</details>


### [229] [Security in a Workflow: Exploring Role-Based Agentic Architectures for Vulnerability Handling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14261)
*Srijita Basu,Miroslaw Staron*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: This paper proposes a role-based multi-agent workflow (Planner, Analyzer, Fixer, Verifier) for vulnerability analysis and remediation, integrating CodeQL static analysis. Evaluated on 25 real-world C/C++ vulnerabilities, it achieves 44% detection accuracy and 19% fix accuracy, highlighting gaps between LLM-based approaches and industrial practice.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLM-based software security methods focus on isolated tasks (detection or patch generation) rather than reflecting the multi-stage industrial workflow of vulnerability analysis, remediation, and verification.
