This creates a gap between research approaches and real-world practices.

Method: A role-based agentic workflow with four roles: Planner (coordinates tasks), Analyzer (identifies vulnerabilities, optionally integrated with CodeQL static analysis), Fixer (generates patches), and Verifier (validates fixes). Models used: nemotron-cascade-2:30b, qwen3-coder-next, and gpt-oss:120b. Evaluated on 25 real-world C/C++ vulnerabilities.

Result: The system achieved 44% vulnerability detection accuracy (comparable to GPT 5.5) and 19% fix accuracy on the 25 C/C++ vulnerability benchmark.

Conclusion: The role-based agentic workflow shows promise for bridging the gap between LLM-based security tools and industrial workflows, but the modest fix accuracy (19%) indicates significant room for improvement. Implications for software security practitioners are discussed.

Abstract: Secure software engineering in practice is a multi-stage workflow involving vulnerability analysis, remediation, and fix verification. However, current LLM-based software security approaches often focus on isolated tasks such as detection or patch generation, with limited attention to agentic architectures reflecting industrial workflow. This creates a gap between existing LLM-based vulnerability-handling methods and real-world practices. In this paper, we study a role-based agentic workflow for vulnerability analysis and mitigation consisting of Planner, Analyzer, Fixer, and Verifier roles. To explore the effect of static analysis tool, the analyzer agent was integrated with the CodeQL in one of the workflows. The models used include nemotron-cascade-2:30b, qwen3-coder-next, and gpt-oss:120b. Our evaluation uses 25 real-world C/C++ vulnerabilities. The study reports 44% vulnerability detection accuracy comparable to GPT 5.5 and 19% fix accuracy. We also list implications from this study in context of software security practitioners.

</details>


### [230] [From Prompts to Responses: Dual-Sided Data Leakage and Defense in Split Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14210)
*Zixuan Gu,Xiaojun Ye,Yang Liu*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: The paper introduces PIDI, a two-stage attack that simultaneously recovers both private input prompts and output responses in Split-LLM settings, and proposes ADMI, a defense combining adapter-based local warmup with mutual information regularization to protect both sides with minimal performance loss.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Split learning for LLMs reduces local computational cost but introduces privacy risks. Prior work only focuses on input prompt leakage via inversion attacks, while the potential for sensitive information leakage through generative response outputs remains unexplored. This work aims to address this overlooked bidirectional privacy threat.

Method: The attack method PIDI employs dual-sided initialization and a patched inversion strategy to recover both input prompts and output responses from split-layer intermediate representations. The defense method ADMI integrates an adapter-based local warmup strategy on the client side with mutual information regularization during training to limit information leakage in the transmitted representations.

Result: Extensive experiments across diverse tasks and models show PIDI substantially outperforms prior inversion methods in recovering both inputs and outputs. ADMI effectively defends against PIDI and other state-of-the-art inversion attacks while maintaining task performance with minimal degradation.

Conclusion: Split-LLM deployments face significant bidirectional privacy risks (both input and output leakage), and the proposed ADMI defense provides strong empirical privacy protection with negligible impact on utility, making it a practical solution for privacy-preserving split LLM inference and fine-tuning.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed in privacy-sensitive domains, where users must balance the risk of data exposure through external APIs against the high computational cost of local deployment. Split learning has therefore emerged as a promising paradigm for LLM fine-tuning and inference under limited local resources. However, it introduces new privacy risks. Prior work primarily studies leakage of private input prompts, typically via inversion attacks on intermediate representations, while the potential for sensitive information leakage through generative response outputs remains largely unexplored.
  In this work, we unveil novel vulnerabilities of Split-LLM by presenting Patched Model Inversion with Dual-Sided Initialization (PIDI), a two-stage attack that simultaneously targets both private input prompts and output responses in Split-LLM settings. It combines dual-sided initialization with a patched inversion strategy to tackle long sequences, substantially outperforming prior inversion methods. To counter threats from both sides, we further propose the Adapter-based DualGuard with Mutual Information Defense (ADMI), which integrates an adapter-based local warmup strategy and mutual information regularization to provide a strong empirical privacy protection with minimal impact on task performance. Extensive experiments across diverse tasks and models demonstrate that ADMI effectively defends against PIDI and other state-of-the-art inversion attacks. Our code is publicly available at https://github.com/FLAIR-THU/VFLAIR-LLM.

</details>


### [231] [AgentCyberRange: Benchmarking Frontier AI Systems in Realistic Cyber Ranges](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14295)
*Fengyu Liu,Jiarun Dai,Yihe Fan,Wuyuao Mai,Ziao Li,Bofei Chen,Jie Zhang,Zheng Lou,Bocheng Xiang,Qiyi Zhang,Xudong Pan,Geng Hong,Yuan Zhang,Min Yang*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: AgentCyberRange is the first open, multi-range benchmark for evaluating autonomous AI cyber attack capabilities. It features 110 vulnerabilities across 15 real web apps and 8 enterprise-like cyber ranges with 156 internal hosts. GPT-5.5 with Codex achieved the best results: 16.1% on web exploitation and 31.7% on post-exploitation (rising to 33.0% and 46.3% with hints). The benchmark also uncovered unknown vulnerabilities and payload mutations, demonstrating that open, realistic cyber-range evaluation is essential for observing emerging offensive AI capabilities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current public benchmarks for evaluating AI cybersecurity capabilities (CTF solving, vulnerability reproduction, exploit generation) abstract away realistic intrusion workflows — they fail to capture the full attack chain: discovering exposed services, gaining footholds, collecting internal intelligence, and expanding compromise across hosts. This gap prevents early observation of emerging risks because frontier AI systems are rarely tested under realistic attack conditions.

Method: The authors introduce AgentCyberRange, comprising: (1) 110 vulnerabilities across 15 real web applications and 8 enterprise-like cyber ranges with 156 internal hosts; (2) Cage, a toolchain for execution, orchestration, result collection, and verification. The benchmark covers two stages: web exploitation (exploring exposed apps and validating vulnerabilities) and post-exploitation (converting a foothold into broader internal compromise). Six frontier AI systems were evaluated under matched prompts and budgets, with and without concrete hints.

Result: GPT-5.5 with Codex achieved the highest performance: 16.1% on web exploitation and 31.7% on post-exploitation. With concrete hints, these rates rose to 33.0% and 46.3% respectively. Additional out-of-benchmark findings included unknown vulnerabilities discovered in popular projects and payload mutations that bypassed host defenses.

Conclusion: Open cyber-range evaluation under realistic, reproducible conditions is necessary for observing and measuring emerging offensive AI capabilities. The gap between abstracted benchmarks and realistic multi-host intrusion scenarios can now be addressed, and AgentCyberRange provides the infrastructure to do so, revealing both measurable progress and unexpected emergent behaviors in frontier AI systems.

Abstract: Frontier AI systems are increasingly capable of cybersecurity tasks, including codebase inspection, vulnerability detection, and exploitation. However, evaluating their offensive capabilities remains constrained by limited access to open, reproducible, multi-host cyber ranges. Existing public benchmarks capture isolated skills such as CTF solving, vulnerability reproduction, and exploit generation, but often abstract away realistic intrusion workflows: discovering exposed services, gaining a foothold, collecting internal information, and expanding compromise across hosts. This gap makes it difficult to observe emerging risks early, because frontier AI systems are rarely evaluated under realistic attack conditions.
  We introduce AgentCyberRange, the first open, multi-range infrastructure for measuring autonomous cyber attack capability in realistic cyber ranges. It combines 110 vulnerabilities across 15 real web applications and 8 enterprise-like cyber ranges with 156 internal hosts, plus Cage, a toolchain for execution, orchestration, result collection, and verification. The benchmark covers two core stages: web exploitation, where agents explore exposed applications and validate vulnerabilities, and post exploitation, where agents turn an initial foothold into broader internal compromise. We evaluate six frontier AI systems under matched prompts and budgets. GPT-5.5 with Codex performs best, solving 16.1% of web exploitation tasks and 31.7% of post-exploitation tasks; with more concrete hints, these rates increase to 33.0% and 46.3%. We also observe out-of-benchmark findings, including unknown vulnerabilities in popular projects, and payload mutation that bypasses host defenses. These results show that open cyber-range evaluation is necessary for observing emerging offensive capabilities under realistic and reproducible conditions.

</details>


### [232] [REPOSE: Quantifying the Price of Security in Weakly-Hard Real-Time Cyber-Physical Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14395)
*Vijay Banerjee,Monowar Hasan*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: REPOSE is a runtime analytical framework for weakly-hard real-time control systems that enables a bounded trade-off between safety and security by performing proactive and reactive security operations based on task behavior, achieving only 0.06% overhead at 80% utilization compared to 29% in hard-constrained systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing security approaches for IoT edge devices and RT-CPS suffer from an "all-or-nothing" problem: design-time opportunistic security mechanisms may execute no security tasks under high system utilization, while adding security to every task severely degrades performance. This motivates a framework that enables a principled, bounded trade-off between safety and security rather than treating them as binary choices.

Method: The authors propose REPOSE, an analytical framework targeting weakly-hard real-time control systems (which tolerate bounded deadline misses). REPOSE evaluates security feasibility at runtime and executes security operations in both proactive and reactive modes based on current task behavior, rather than relying solely on pessimistic design-time provisioning.

Result: REPOSE achieves a feasibility overhead of only 0.06% at 80% system utilization, dramatically outperforming hard-constrained systems that exhibit 29% overhead. A case study on a classic control system further demonstrates REPOSE's capability to analyze and calculate the safety-security trade-off.

Conclusion: REPOSE provides a robust, low-overhead framework for integrating security into weakly-hard real-time control systems at runtime, enabling a quantifiable and bounded safety-security trade-off that avoids the extremes of no security or prohibitive performance degradation.

Abstract: In contemporary IoT edge devices with real-time requirements, security is primarily enforced through design-time parameters associated with security tasks, leading to mechanisms that operate in an \emph{opportunistic} manner. As a result, security checks are often performed as secondary operations. This approach can result in systems where no security tasks are executed due to high utilization by other tasks. An alternative approach taken in prior work is to add security mechanisms to every task in the system, resulting in substantially lower performance than that of a system with no security. These approaches have resulted in an \emph{all-or-nothing} scenario for edge device security, motivating numerous studies on the safety-security trade-off in real-time cyber-physical systems (RT-CPS). This study introduces an analytical framework -- REPOSE -- for evaluating the security feasibility of real-time control systems at runtime. REPOSE is developed for \textit{weakly-hard} real-time control systems that facilitate a ``bounded trade-off'' between safety and security. In contrast to imposing additional (pessimistic) design-time overhead as considered in some real-time security literature, REPOSE performs security operations in both \textit{proactive} and \textit{reactive} manners based on the task's current behavior. Our evaluations show that REPOSE can effectively add security operations to RT-CPS with a feasibility overhead of $0.06\%$ at $80\%$ utilization, compared to a $ 29\%$ overhead observed in systems with hard constraints. Through a case study of a classic control system, we also demonstrate that REPOSE provides a robust framework to \textit{analyze and calculate} the safety-security tradeoff.

</details>


### [233] [Breaking TinyML: Why Quantized Neural Networks Need Domain-Specific Security Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14427)
*Jacob Huckelberry,Andrea Mattia Garavagno,Yuke Zhang,Peter A. Beerel,James Mickens,Vijay Janapa Reddi*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: This paper addresses the underexplored security vulnerabilities of Quantized Neural Networks (QNNs) on TinyML hardware accelerators, proposing a novel two-step attack pipeline that outperforms existing evasion attack methods and highlights the urgent need for hardware-aware security research in this domain.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: TinyML hardware accelerators overwhelmingly focus on QNN support to meet power and size constraints, yet the security implications of quantization in this context remain largely unstudied. While prior work suggests QNNs exhibit comparable or superior robustness to full-precision DNNs against evasion attacks, no attack strategies have been specifically designed for TinyML hardware. This gap motivates the need to investigate whether tailored attacks can compromise QNNs in resource-constrained environments.

Method: The authors propose a two-step attack pipeline specifically crafted for the QNN/TinyML context. The pipeline is designed to exploit hardware-specific characteristics and quantization-induced vulnerabilities, demonstrating that it can surpass the current state-of-the-art in adversarial evasion attacks against QNNs deployed on TinyML accelerators.

Result: The proposed two-step attack pipeline successfully surpasses the current state-of-the-art evasion attack methods in the QNN context, demonstrating that QNNs on TinyML hardware are more vulnerable than previously assumed when targeted with purpose-built, hardware-aware attack strategies.

Conclusion: There is a critical need for more hardware-aware security research in TinyML. The demonstrated effectiveness of tailored attacks reveals that the security assumptions about QNN robustness derived from full-precision DNN comparisons are insufficient, and TinyML-specific threat models must be developed to ensure secure deployment on resource-constrained accelerators.

Abstract: Most TinyML hardware accelerators focus on supporting Quantized Neural Networks (QNNs) to meet stringent constraints on power consumption and size. Despite this, the security aspects of quantization within TinyML hardware remain largely unexplored. Although previous studies indicate that QNNs demonstrate similar or enhanced robustness when compared to full-precision Deep Neural Networks (DNNs) against typical evasion attacks, no attack strategies tailored specifically for TinyML hardware have been proposed yet. This paper addresses this shortfall by demonstrating how a two-step attack pipeline can surpass the current state-of-the-art in the QNN context and shows the need for more hardware-aware security research.

</details>


### [234] [Securing the Future of IoMT in the Post-Quantum Era: An Edge-Native Federated Learning Approach](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14515)
*Taym Alshoghri,Deemah H. Tashman,Mohammad Reza Gerami,Soumaya Cherkaoui*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: This paper proposes a Kubernetes-based framework integrating Post-Quantum Cryptography (PQC) into Federated Learning (FL)-enabled Internet of Medical Things (IoMT) systems, validated on a Raspberry Pi testbed. Distributed cryptographic processing reduces latency versus sequential designs while maintaining feasible resource overhead.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: IoMT devices are resource-constrained yet handle sensitive health data, making security and privacy paramount. FL introduces additional risk through model updates that may leak private medical information. Conventional lightweight cryptography faces long-term threats from emerging quantum computing, necessitating the integration of PQC into IoMT systems.

Method: The authors design a scalable Kubernetes-based orchestration framework that integrates post-quantum key establishment, lightweight encryption, and edge-native orchestration into FL-enabled IoMT environments. The framework is empirically validated on a Raspberry Pi testbed, comparing distributed versus sequential cryptographic processing designs.

Result: Distributed cryptographic processing achieves significantly lower latency compared to sequential designs while maintaining acceptable resource overhead on resource-constrained edge devices.

Conclusion: The primary contribution is the design and validation of a secure orchestration and communication framework for FL-enabled IoMT. Future directions include energy-aware architectures, intelligent security optimization, and resilient next-generation Intelligent Internet of Medical Things (IIoMT) ecosystems.

Abstract: Internet of Medical Things (IoMT) devices operate under strict resource constraints while handling highly sensitive health data, making security and privacy critical concerns. Federated learning (FL) further complicates this landscape, as model updates exchanged during training may unintentionally expose private medical information. Emerging quantum computing capabilities threaten the long-term viability of conventional lightweight cryptographic mechanisms, motivating the integration of Post-Quantum Cryptography (PQC) into IoMT systems. This article discusses key enabling technologies for quantum-resilient IoMT, including post-quantum key establishment, lightweight encryption, and edge-native orchestration. We propose a scalable Kubernetes-based framework that integrates PQC into FL-enabled IoMT environments and validate it on a Raspberry Pi testbed. Results demonstrate that distributed cryptographic processing significantly reduces latency compared to sequential designs while maintaining feasible resource overhead. The primary contribution of this work lies in the design and validation of a secure orchestration and communication framework for FL-enabled IoMT systems. We conclude by outlining future directions toward energy-aware architectures, intelligent security optimization, and resilient next-generation Intelligent Internet of Medical Things (IIoMT) ecosystems.

</details>


### [235] [From Shield to Target: Denial-of-Service Attacks on LLM-Based Agent Guardrails](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14517)
*Yuguang Zhou,Xunguang Wang,Pingchuan Ma,Zhantong Xue,Zhaoyu Wang,Shuai Wang*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: The paper reveals a novel DoS vulnerability in LLM-based guardrails, where crafted payloads trap guardrails in extended reasoning loops. Using beam-search optimization and mechanism-aware mutations, the attack amplifies token generation by 13–63× across multiple model families and induces up to 148× latency amplification in real-world agent deployments, demonstrating that a single poisoned document can saturate shared guardrail infrastructure.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM-based guardrails are widely adopted to defend against prompt injection and jailbreak attacks in autonomous agents. However, the reasoning capabilities that make them effective also introduce an availability vulnerability — attackers can exploit unbounded reasoning loops to cause denial-of-service. This availability flaw remains unexplored and poses a systemic risk to agent infrastructures.

Method: Two attack frameworks are proposed: (1) a beam-search optimization framework using an LLM proposer and a strategy bank to craft natural-language payloads that maximize guardrail reasoning length; (2) a mechanism-aware structural mutation framework exploiting the schema-following nature of guardrails with lower computational cost. Evaluation spans standalone tests across diverse guardrail architectures, safety templates, and benchmarks, plus end-to-end real-world agent deployments (web, desktop, code, multi-agent).

Result: In standalone evaluations, payloads optimized on a single open-source surrogate transfer to eight model backbones (Claude, GPT, Gemini, DeepSeek, Qwen, etc.), achieving 13–63× token amplification. In end-to-end agent deployments, latency amplification reaches up to 148×. A single poisoned document can saturate shared guardrail infrastructure, starving co-located agents.

Conclusion: The work uncovers a critical availability vulnerability in LLM guardrails, demonstrating that reasoning-based defenses are susceptible to DoS via engineered reasoning-loop traps. It calls for the development of cost-bounded, reasoning-robust guardrail designs to mitigate this systemic threat.

Abstract: LLM-based guardrails have emerged as a highly effective defense against prompt injection and jailbreak attacks in autonomous agents. However, we reveal that the very reasoning and task-following capabilities enabling this protection introduce a novel vulnerability: attackers can inject crafted data to trap the guardrail in extended reasoning loops, effectuating a systematic denial-of-service (DoS) attack. To systematically expose this threat, we design a beam-search optimization framework that crafts natural-language payloads to maximize guardrail reasoning length, utilizing an LLM proposer guided by a strategy bank. Based on the observation of guardrail's schema-following nature, we also provide another attack framework driven by mechanism-aware structural mutations with less computational load. The attack efficacy is systematically evaluated in two parts. First, in standalone evaluations, the attack generalizes across diverse guardrail architectures, safety templates, and agent benchmarks. Payloads optimized on a single open-source surrogate successfully transfer to eight leading model backbones (e.g., Claude, GPT, Gemini, DeepSeek, and Qwen), achieving a 13--63$\times$ token amplification. Second, in end-to-end real-world agent deployments (web, desktop, code, and multi-agent systems), the attack reveals up to a 148$\times$ latency amplification. We show that a single poisoned document can saturate shared guardrail infrastructures, effectively starving co-located agents and paralyzing the entire system. By uncovering this availability flaw, our work underscores the urgent need to develop cost-bounded, reasoning-robust guardrails.

</details>


### [236] [Detecting Bot Detection: Prevalence, Techniques, and Implications for Web Measurement Research](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14525)
*Ralf Gundelach,Michael Mühlhauser,Dominik Herrmann*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: Browser automation tools are critical for web security research, but bot detection increasingly blocks them, skewing measurements. The authors find 83% of top papers ignore this blocking problem. Their study of 10,000 websites shows Chromium headless faces 15% soft blocking vs. 7% for other setups, with Cloudflare and Akamai causing most blocks. Header signals alone trigger 75% of headless-specific blocks, though broader JS probing exists. The work reveals systematic, unreported sample loss in web measurement research.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work only quantifies bot detection deployment, not the actual sample loss (blocking) experienced by automated browsers in measurement studies. A survey reveals 83% of top-tier security/privacy/web measurement papers do not discuss bot detection blocking, creating a critical blind spot about measurement validity.

Method: The authors conduct a measurement study crawling 10,000 websites across four browser configurations (40K total page visits), including Chromium headless, headful, and stealth-modified variants. They use custom instrumentation to detect when sites probe automation artifacts (navigator.webdriver, headless indicators, etc.), build a taxonomy of detection techniques, measure block rates per configuration, and perform a header-spoofing experiment to isolate header-level vs. JS-level signals.

Result: Chromium headless incurs a 15% soft block rate vs. ~7% for other configs. 82% of blocks stem from bot detection (59% vendor-confirmed via stack traces to known providers like Cloudflare [37% block rate] and Akamai [26%]; 23% inferred). Header spoofing shows 75% of headless-only blocks are triggered by header signals alone. JavaScript environment probing is more extensive than current blocking rates suggest, indicating future escalation potential.

Conclusion: Bot detection creates systematic, provider-correlated sample loss that the web measurement community neither measures nor reports. This threatens validity of automated browser-based studies. The downstream impact on specific measurement outcomes is identified as future work.

Abstract: Browser automation frameworks are essential tools for security and privacy research on the web, yet bot detection scripts increasingly probe their artifacts, threatening measurement validity as automated browsers may be blocked or served different content. Prior work measures detection deployment, while we measure blocking-induced sample loss. Through a literature survey of top-tier security, privacy, and web measurement venues, we find that 83% of papers omit any discussion of bot detection blocking. To address this gap, we conduct a measurement study of 10,000 websites across four browser configurations (40K page visits in total) to quantify detection prevalence and employed techniques. Using custom instrumentation to detect when sites probe for automation, we develop a taxonomy of bot detection techniques and measure how often they appear in practice. Chromium headless encounters a 15% soft block rate compared to 7% for other configurations. Across all conditions, 82% of blocks are attributable to bot detection (59% vendor-confirmed, 23% inferred from condition-dependent blocking), predominantly by providers with integrated bot detection such as Cloudflare (37% block rate) and Akamai (26%). A header spoofing experiment establishes that 75% of Chromium-headless-only blocks are caused by header-level signals alone, yet JavaScript-based environment probing is more extensive than current blocking rates suggest. These findings demonstrate that bot detection creates systematic, provider-correlated sample loss that the web measurement community neither measures nor reports. The downstream effect on specific measurement outcomes remains future work.

</details>


### [237] [When Good Verifiers Go Bad: Self-Improving VLMs Can Regress on New Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14629)
*Jianzhe Lin*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: Verifier-driven self-DPO can fail when verifier quality is task-specific; below-threshold verifiers silently regress the student model despite decreasing training loss, with damage inversely proportional to verifier accuracy in the failure regime.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper challenges the monotonicity assumption in verifier-driven self-DPO — that a stronger verifier always yields a stronger student. The authors investigate whether this holds across diverse tasks and what happens when verifiers perform poorly on target tasks.

Method: The authors test a ladder of four open-source verifiers across three benchmarks (MathVista, MMMU, BLINK) using two student models (Qwen-3-VL-2B and Qwen-2.5-VL-3B). They measure verifier rubric accuracy on each task, run DPO training loops, and compare student performance against a frozen baseline. They also provide a theoretical variance theorem explaining the failure mode of progress-gated replay.

Result: Verifier quality is highly task-specific: verifiers that improve students on MathVista (above-threshold) become sub-threshold on MMMU with rubric accuracy dropping to 8–23%. In sub-threshold regimes, every verifier silently regresses the student (3.4–10.9 pp drops) while DPO loss continues decreasing. Regression is confidence-inverted: more accurate-but-wrong verifiers cause larger damage than near-random ones. Results replicate across both student models.

Conclusion: Practitioners should measure target-task rubric accuracy before running verifier-driven loops, rank verifiers by task-specific quality rather than parameter count, and treat diminishing returns in above-threshold regimes as a compute budget cap. The variance theorem provides a mechanistic explanation for why progress-gated replay amplifies confidently wrong preference pairs.

Abstract: Verifier-driven self-DPO is a common recipe for self-improving production visual-language models. In this setup, a frozen verifier scores candidate generations, the top- and bottom-scoring candidates form a preference example, and DPO updates the learner. The deployment-time assumption is monotone: a stronger verifier should yield a stronger student. We show that this assumption can fail because verifier quality is highly task-specific. On a four-rung open-source verifier ladder across MathVista, MMMU, and BLINK, the same verifiers that are above-threshold and improve a Qwen-3-VL-2B student on MathVista become sub-threshold on MMMU, where their task-rubric accuracy drops to 8% to 23%.
  In this regime, every verifier we tested silently regresses the student, producing drops of 3.4 to 10.9 percentage points below the frozen baseline while the DPO training loss continues to decrease. The regression replicates on a second student, Qwen-2.5-VL-3B. Moreover, within the failure regime, damage is confidence-inverted: the more accurate-but-still-wrong verifier causes larger regression than a near-random verifier, suggesting that progress-gated replay amplifies confidently wrong preference pairs. We give a compact mechanistic explanation via a variance theorem for progress-gated replay and its direction-mismatch failure mode.
  The deployment message is operational rather than purely diagnostic: before running any verifier-driven loop, teams should measure target-task rubric accuracy, rank verifiers by target-task rubric quality rather than parameter count, and treat diminishing returns in above-threshold regimes as a verifier-side compute budget cap.

</details>


<div id='cs.HC'></div>

# cs.HC [[Back]](#toc)

### [238] [The Frustrometer: Detecting User Frustration in Data Visualization Tasks using Biomarkers and Interaction Patterns](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13687)
*Johannes Ellemose,Sophia Wanner,Djordje Slijepčević,Laura Cesar,Vanessa Leung,Wolfgang Aigner,Niklas Elmqvist*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: The Frustrometer uses a CNN to predict user stuckness/frustration in real-time from physiological and interaction signals. Mouse and gaze data carry the most predictive signal, while heart-rate, GSR, and head orientation provide limited insight—suggesting lightweight instrumentation may suffice for adaptive guidance in visualization systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Visualization research knows how to help stuck users (onboarding, contextual help, guidance) but not when to trigger that help. Triggering too early disrupts flow; too late means the user is already lost. The paper aims to solve the timing problem by detecting frustration and stuckness in real-time.

Method: A controlled study with 14 participants performing analytical tasks on two interactive visualization dashboards. Data captured: eye movement, pupil dilation, galvanic skin response (GSR), heart rate, head orientation, mouse dynamics, and keyboard events. Self-assessed performance and experimenter annotations of stuck episodes were collected. A convolutional neural network (CNN) classifier was trained to fuse these multimodal signals for real-time stuckness prediction.

Result: Autonomous physiological signals (heart-rate, GSR) and head orientation correlated poorly with frustration. Mouse movements and gaze data carried the majority of predictive signal; mouse movements alone showed strong correlation for some participants, indicating lightweight, non-invasive instrumentation may be sufficient for real-time frustration detection.

Conclusion: Real-time frustration detection for visualization tasks can be achieved with relatively lightweight sensors (mouse, eye-tracking), without heavy physiological instrumentation. These findings inform the design of adaptive guidance systems that use multimodal approaches to decide when to intervene.

Abstract: Visualization research has largely solved \textit{how} to help a stuck or frustrated user -- through interactive onboarding, contextual help, and active guidance. The unsolved problem is \textit{when}: trigger help too eagerly and you break the user's train of thought; wait too long and they have already gone astray. We present the \textsc{Frustrometer}, a series of experiments to predict user stuckness and frustration by fusing physiological and interaction signals. The Frustrometer consists of a convolutional neural network classifier, that in real-time estimates whether user are stuck in their task or not. We collected data from a controlled study where 14 participants performed analytical tasks on two interactive visualization dashboards while we captured eye movement, pupil dilation, galvanic skin response, heart-rate, head orientation, mouse dynamics, and keyboard events. In addition participants assessed their own performance, while we annotated when during the tasks the participants were stuck. Our results reveal that autonomous physiological responses such as heart-rate and galvanic skin response provide limited insights into the frustration level of the user. Similarly, head orientations are not easily correlated with the frustrations felt by the user during visual analysis tasks. Mouse movements and gaze data conversely carry the majority of predictive signal, with mouse movements alone having a strong correlation for some participants, suggesting that lightweight instrumentation may suffice for real-time frustration detection. We end the paper by discussing how these findings can inform the design of adaptive guidance systems for complex visualization tasks that takes a multimodal approach to frustration and stuckness detection.

</details>


### [239] [Rethinking the UI of GenUI: A Tale of Two Designs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13843)
*Xiang `Anthony' Chen,Savvas Dimitrios Petridis,Tian Deng,Humad Bari,Ruofei Du,Yang Li*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: The paper rethinks GenUI design by contrasting conventional unstructured, depth-first, high-fidelity approaches with a novel structured, breadth-first, low-fidelity paradigm, then evaluates both through a user study with 24 UX professionals to reveal trade-offs and derive design implications.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most GenUI tools inherit conversational AI conventions (unstructured prompts, depth-first exploration, high-fidelity output), but it is unclear whether this paradigm effectively supports early-stage, 0-to-1 design exploration. The authors question this assumption and seek to systematically investigate alternative design choices.

Method: The authors propose a contrastive GenUI design featuring three key shifts: (1) structured input instead of unstructured prompts, (2) breadth-first exploration rather than depth-first, and (3) low-fidelity generation instead of high-fidelity prototypes. They then conducted a comparative user study with 24 UX designers and product managers who performed mini design exploration exercises using both an existing GenUI tool and their contrastive tool.

Result: Three key findings emerged: (1) structured input surfaces key design facets but imposes higher upfront effort, raising entry barriers; (2) breadth-first workflows reveal more design possibilities, but previewing multi-screen UX ideas remains challenging; (3) while low-fidelity has recognized value, professionals strongly prefer high-fidelity because it aligns with current practice and GenAI amplifies fidelity expectations.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that neither paradigm is universally superior; each presents distinct benefits and trade-offs. The findings yield design implications for GenUI tools and broader AI-powered creativity support systems, suggesting that tool designers should thoughtfully balance structure, breadth, and fidelity based on the specific stage and goals of the design process.

Abstract: GenUI is an emergent class of AI tools that use large models to generate UI mock-ups based on users' high-level descriptions, promising to democratize UX design exploration for a broader audience. Most GenUI designs to date tend to inherit the conventions of conversational large models, such as ChatGPT and Gemini, where a user describes their design needs primarily via an unstructured prompt, and the tool then takes a depth-first approach, delving into the design right away and producing a high-fidelity prototype. In this research, we rethink how well this unstructured, depth-first, and high-fidelity GenUI design can support early-stage, 0-to-1 design exploration. To probe this question, we propose a contrastive design with structured input, breadth-first exploration, and low-fidelity generation. We then conducted a comparison study with 24 UX designers and product managers who conducted mini design exploration exercises using an existing GenUI tool and our contrastive GenUI tool. Findings reveal participants' perceived benefits and trade-offs of the two GenUI designs: structured input surfaces key facets but requires more work, raising entry barriers to start exploration; breadth-first workflow reveals more possibilities, but previewing UX ideas spanning many screens remains hard; and though low fidelity has value, professionals favor high fidelity because it fits practice and GenAI heightens fidelity expectations. We conclude with design implications for GenUI and similar AI-powered creativity support tools.

</details>


### [240] [SpheriCity: Designing Trustworthy Conversational AI for Sustainability Decision Support](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13854)
*Ahmed Qayyum,Madison Werner,Kathryn Youngblood,Jenna R. Jambeck,Tahiya Chowdhury*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: SpheriCity is a provenance-first conversational agent for synthesizing sustainability reports, evaluated with six experts who prioritized source transparency, interpretability, and workflow alignment for trust in AI-assisted circular economy decision-making.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: City-level circularity assessment reports are lengthy and heterogeneous, making cross-document synthesis difficult. While LLMs offer faster knowledge access, their opaque reasoning, hallucinations, and lack of source transparency pose trust risks in high-stakes sustainability contexts, necessitating a system that prioritizes evidence traceability and interpretability.

Method: The authors developed SpheriCity, a provenance-first conversational prototype with evidence traceability, structured synthesis, and interaction scaffolds. They conducted a formative expert review with six sustainability experts who tested the system using representative queries (cross-city comparison, policy summarization, recommendation-oriented tasks) and evaluated responses across dimensions with qualitative reflections.

Result: Experts identified transparent sourcing, contextual explanation, interpretability, and alignment with expert workflow as key factors shaping trust and perceived usefulness of the system for sustainability knowledge work.

Conclusion: The paper contributes (1) a conversational prototype for sustainability knowledge sensemaking, (2) an expert-grounded evaluation framework for AI responses in high-stakes domains, and (3) design insights linking provenance, uncertainty communication, and workflow integration to expert trust in AI-assisted sustainability decision support.

Abstract: We present SpheriCity, an expert-grounded conversational prototype designed to support trustworthy knowledge sensemaking from sustainability reports. City-level circularity assessment reports contain rich information about materials, infrastructure, and policy interventions, yet their length and heterogeneous structure make cross-document synthesis and comparison difficult for practitioners and researchers working on circular economy initiatives. While large language models (LLM) promise faster knowledge access and synthesis, their opaque reasoning, hallucinations, and lack of source transparency introduce risks for trust and interpretability, and require verification in high-stakes sustainability contexts. SpheriCity addresses these challenges through a provenance-first conversational agent that foregrounds evidence traceability, structured synthesis, and interaction scaffolds to support exploratory querying and cross-document synthesis across sustainability reports. We conducted a formative expert review with six sustainability experts using representative queries spanning cross-city comparison, policy summarization, and recommendation-oriented tasks. Experts evaluated responses across dimensions and provided qualitative reflections on the system's usefulness for sustainability knowledge work. Our results reveal that transparent sourcing, contextual explanation, interpretability, and alignment with expert workflow strongly shape expert trust and judgments of system usefulness. This work contributes (1) a conversational prototype for sustainability knowledge sensemaking, (2) an expert-grounded evaluation framework for assessing AI responses in high-stakes knowledge domains, and (3) design insights into how provenance, uncertainty communication, and integration in workflow influence expert users' trust in AI assistance for sustainability decision support.

</details>


### [241] [The Silent Cost of Artificial Intelligence Assistance: A Theory of Autonomy Surrender, the Recovery Mechanism, and the Restoration of Human Agency](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13962)
*Ancuta Margondai,Julie Rader,Emma Rader,Sara Willox,Mustapha Mouloua*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: This paper proposes a theoretical model of "autonomy surrender" — a measurable, cumulative process in which human decision-makers gradually relinquish autonomy to AI systems due to cognitive bandwidth depletion, driven by three interacting mechanisms: silent cost, surrender threshold, and recovery mechanism. It warns of a terminal state called "preference inversion" where dependence becomes a preference.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The integration of AI into human decision-making has an undertheorized cost: the gradual, often unnoticed surrender of human autonomy. Existing frameworks lack a formal model capturing how this process unfolds mechanistically over time, particularly the cognitive dynamics that make reclaiming autonomy increasingly difficult.

Method: The paper builds on the Human Identity and Autonomy Gap (HIAG) framework to advance a theoretical model identifying three interacting mechanisms: (1) the silent cost of AI assistance — incremental, awareness-free autonomy transfer; (2) the surrender threshold — a cognitive/psychological tipping point beyond which reclaiming autonomy is difficult; and (3) the recovery mechanism — structured design obligations for deliberate human re-entry into decision loops, requiring intentional bandwidth restoration.

Result: The model predicts that without structured "recovery mechanisms" built into AI system design, humans cross a surrender threshold after which functional dependence becomes entrenched. It further predicts a terminal state — "preference inversion" — where AI dependence is experienced as a preference rather than a deficit, shifting the problem from design to cultural and political domains.

Conclusion: AI system design must incorporate structured re-entry pathways (recovery mechanisms) that preserve human agency. Failing to do so risks a cultural shift where autonomy restoration becomes politically intractable. Implications span AI system design, governance frameworks, and human factors research.

Abstract: The integration of artificial intelligence into human decision-making environments has introduced a previously undertheorized cost: the gradual surrender of human autonomy in exchange for access to information and computational assistance. Building on the Human Identity and Autonomy Gap (HIAG) framework, this paper advances a theoretical model of autonomy surrender as a measurable, cumulative process driven by cognitive bandwidth depletion. The model proposes three interacting mechanisms: the silent cost of AI assistance, in which autonomy is transferred incrementally and without awareness; the surrender threshold, beyond which reclaiming autonomous function becomes cognitively and psychologically difficult; and the recovery mechanism, which establishes the design obligation and the ethical responsibility accompanying deliberate human re-assumption of control. The paper argues that human re-entry into the decision loop is not a passive option but an active cognitive event requiring intentional bandwidth restoration. The design of AI systems must incorporate structured re-entry pathways, here termed recovery mechanisms, that preserve human agency while appropriately distributing responsibility. The model further predicts a terminal state, here termed preference inversion, in which functional dependence on AI assistance is experienced not as a deficit but as a preference, transforming the restoration of autonomy from a design problem into a cultural and political one. Implications are drawn for AI system design, governance frameworks, and human factors research.

</details>


### [242] [Visible Adoption, Untracked Contribution: GitHub Evidence of the Accountability Gap Across Three Cohorts of an HCI Prototyping Course](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14054)
*Maria Teresa Parreira,Pranav Prabhat Sinha,Hauke Sandhaus,Wendy Ju*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: A longitudinal study across three cohorts (2022–2025) of a graduate HCI prototyping course analyzes 23,065 commits across 203 GitHub repositories, revealing that while AI tool disclosure rose from 0% to 66%, explicit attribution of specific contributions remains rare. The paper argues that disclosure-based accountability norms fail to capture the ambient, continuous co-creation characteristic of "vibe-coding," where AI is embedded infrastructure rather than an episodic tool.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The authors sought to empirically track how student GenAI adoption and accountability practices evolved over time, testing the adequacy of disclosure-based frameworks. A prior qualitative study of the 2023 cohort raised questions about how students document AI involvement; this work quantitatively extends that inquiry to understand whether naming AI tools (disclosure) translates into specifying what those tools contributed (attribution), and whether existing norms suffice as AI shifts from episodic assistant to ambient infrastructure.

Method: This is a longitudinal, observational case study employing computational analysis of 203 GitHub repositories containing 23,065 student commits across three cohorts (Fall 2022, 2023, 2025) of the same graduate-level HCI prototyping course. The authors computationally distinguished two levels of AI accountability trace: disclosure (naming that an AI tool was used) versus attribution (crediting a specific artifact or task to an AI tool). They tracked prevalence of each across cohorts and analyzed the gap between them.

Result: Tool disclosure grew from 0% (Fall 2022) to 66% (2025) of repositories. Explicit contribution attribution remained a minority practice across all cohorts. A persistent gap between disclosure and attribution reveals missing accountability even among students who do disclose. By 2025, AI had become infrastructure — embedded in course templates and student-built devices — with students increasingly naming tools used but rarely specifying what those tools contributed.

Conclusion: Disclosure-based accountability frameworks are insufficient for the "vibe-coding" era. The core failure is not student concealment of AI use, but that norms built for episodic, identifiable acts cannot capture continuous, ambient co-creation where AI functions as infrastructure. The case study serves as grounding for workshop conversations about what genuine co-thinking accountability should look like.

Abstract: This paper presents a longitudinal, observational case study of how student GenAI adoption shifted across three cohorts (Fall 2022, 2023, and 2025) of the same graduate-level HCI prototyping course, using computational analysis of 203 GitHub repositories with student activity and 23,065 student commits. Building on a prior qualitative study of the 2023 cohort, we distinguish two levels of AI accountability trace: disclosure (naming that an AI tool was used) and attribution (crediting a specific artifact or task to an AI tool). We find that tool disclosure grew from 0% to 66% of repositories across the three cohorts, while explicit contribution attribution remains a minority practice, and the gap between the two reveals where accountability is missing even among students who disclose. By 2025, AI is infrastructure embedded in course templates and student-built devices: students increasingly name the tools they used, but rarely specify what those tools contributed. We argue that disclosure-based frameworks are insufficient for the vibe-coding era. The failure is not that students conceal AI use; it is that a norm built for episodic, identifiable acts cannot capture continuous, ambient co-creation. We offer this case study as grounding for the workshop's conversation about what genuine co-thinking accountability looks like.

</details>


### [243] [Thinking Outside the [Chat]Box: Bridging Computer Science and Industrial Design for Cognitive-Inclusive Generative AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14306)
*Virginia Francisco,Daniel Guasch,Raquel Hervás*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: The paper reports on a co-design challenge exploring cognitively accessible GenAI interfaces beyond chat, where CS and Industrial Design students developed concepts from shared requirements. Results reveal complementary approaches — structural scaffolding (CS) and experiential scaffolding (Design) — which the authors synthesize into a dual-layer framework for supporting users with intellectual disabilities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current GenAI chat interfaces impose high cognitive demands, creating significant accessibility barriers for users with intellectual disabilities (ID). Key challenges include prompt formulation difficulties, response overload, and lack of mechanisms to assess information reliability. The work is motivated by the need to explore alternative, more cognitively accessible interaction models.

Method: A cross-disciplinary co-design challenge involving two student cohorts — Computer Science and Industrial Design — who independently developed interface concepts from an identical set of functional requirements (prompt scaffolding, structured output, GUI-based refinement, transparency, personalization). The resulting proposals were then comparatively analyzed.

Result: Both cohorts converged on foundational requirements: initial calibration, proactive prompting, and direct manipulation of response fragments. CS teams contributed structural scaffolding (reliability indicators, explicit sources, context management, predictability, navigability). Industrial Design teams contributed experiential scaffolding (pacing, attention guidance, multimodality, step-by-step flows, focus modes, proactive agency).

Conclusion: The authors synthesize findings into a dual-layer scaffolding framework — structural plus experiential — that expands the design space for cognitively accessible GenAI interaction beyond chat-centric models. Future work is needed on expert refinement, technical feasibility, and empirical validation with actual users with ID.

Abstract: Current Generative AI (GenAI) interfaces remain largely constrained to chatbox interaction, which can impose high cognitive demands on users and create substantial barriers for people with intellectual disabilities (ID), including prompt formulation difficulties, response overload, and limited mechanisms to assess information reliability. To explore alternative interaction models for cognitive accessibility, we conducted a cross-disciplinary co-design challenge in which two student cohorts (Computer Science and Industrial Design) developed interface concepts from the same set of functional requirements (e.g., prompt scaffolding, structured output, GUI-based refinement, transparency, and personalization). Comparing the resulting proposals reveals both convergence on foundational requirements (notably initial calibration, proactive prompting, and direct manipulation of response fragments) and complementary contributions that outline a multi-layered support system. Computer Science teams primarily produced structural scaffolding, emphasizing predictability, navigability, and trust through mechanisms such as reliability indicators, explicit sources, and context management for long conversations. Industrial Design teams emphasized experiential scaffolding, focusing on pacing, attention guidance, multimodality, and proactive agency, including step-by-step response flows, focus modes, and assistant-like integrations. We synthesize these findings into a dual-layer scaffolding framework that expands the design space for cognitively accessible GenAI interaction beyond chat-centric models and motivates future work on expert refinement, technical feasibility, and empirical validation with users with ID.

</details>


### [244] [Friction in AI-Assisted Clinical Decision-Making: A Case Study on The Role of Questions and 'What-if' Scenarios](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14406)
*Simon WS Fischer,Hanna Schraffenberger,Miranda L. van Hooff,Serge Thill,Pim Haselager*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: The paper explores clinicians' perceptions of two friction-based interventions (data-driven questions and what-if analysis) in a clinical DSS prototype, finding that questions served better as reminders than reflection prompts, while what-if scenarios were valued for improving patient care and training novices.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To counter overreliance on clinical decision-support systems (DSSs), the authors investigate how friction-inducing design interventions — specifically data-driven questions and what-if analysis — are perceived by medical experts, aiming to understand their effectiveness in promoting cognitive engagement and reflective thinking.

Method: The authors replicated a real-world clinical DSS and developed a prototype incorporating two friction mechanisms: (1) data-driven questions and (2) what-if hypothetical analysis. They conducted in-situ interviews with 7 clinicians to gather qualitative feedback on the prototype within the context of an authentic decision-making task.

Result: Key findings: (1) data-driven questions were not perceived as helpful for stimulating reflective thinking but were seen as useful reminders to consider relevant information; (2) what-if analysis was considered useful for potentially improving patient care; (3) clinicians viewed the prototype as a promising training tool for novices rather than for experienced practitioners.

Conclusion: The paper offers design recommendations for incorporating friction into clinical work practices and contributes to human-AI interaction research focused on encouraging reflection to mitigate AI overreliance, emphasizing that friction mechanisms may serve different purposes (reminders, training) than originally intended.

Abstract: Clinical decision-making is augmented by decision-support systems (DSSs). To counter overreliance on DSSs, several methods have been proposed that create friction in order to promote cognitive engagement and reflection. In this paper, we investigate how two such forms of friction, namely data-driven questions and `what-if' analysis, are perceived by medical experts. For a real-world decision task, we replicated a DSS used in clinical practice and gathered clinicians' feedback on a prototype through in-situ interviews (n=7). Our findings suggest that while the questions were perceived as unhelpful for reflective thinking, they could serve as reminders to consider relevant information. Furthermore, inspecting `what-if' hypotheticals was found useful for potentially improving patient care. Clinicians saw our prototype as a promising training tool for novice clinicians. From the clinicians' feedback, we make recommendations for designing friction in work practices. Our work contributes to human-AI interaction research, which aims to encourage reflection to mitigate AI overreliance.

</details>


### [245] [Fabula: Building a Narrative Storytelling Sidekick with the Writers' Community](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14411)
*Piotr Mirowski,Ben Wedin,Reinald Kim Amplayo,Rich Galt,Duncan Williams,Rida Qadri,Jaume Sanchez-Elias,Erin Drake-Kajioka,Sian Gooding,Lucia Lopez-Rivilla,Joao G. M. Araujo,Lion Schulz,Satinder Baveja,Shakir Mohamed,Edward Grefenstette,Laura Rimell,Richard Evans*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: Fabula is an interactive AI-assisted fiction writing app that structures stories hierarchically into scenes and beats using narratological theory. Evaluated with 42 experts, the study explores auto-evaluation of story quality, UI design trade-offs, cross-cultural applicability, and iterative planning's effect on creativity, ultimately using Fabula as a cultural probe for adversarial design insights.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To bridge narratological theory with participatory AI in a practical writing tool, and to critically examine design assumptions about how AI can support fiction writers' creative processes across diverse storytelling traditions.

Method: Designed Fabula, an interactive app with hierarchical narrative planning (scenes/beats). Employed participatory AI methodology: design interviews and writing sessions with 42 expert writers (casual and published), plus large-scale internal and external testing. Investigated four design questions: (1) LM-based auto-evaluator optimized on expert preferences for story quality, (2) UI exposing narrative plans alongside scripts, (3) cross-cultural/localized storytelling fit, (4) convergent iteration effects on creativity. Used adversarial design and cultural probe approaches.

Result: The study yields critical feedback on all four design interrogations: effectiveness of the expert-tuned auto-evaluator, user preferences regarding narrative plan visibility, limitations of narratological assumptions for non-Western/localized traditions and for screenwriters/playwrights, and impacts of convergent iteration on creative freedom. Fabula-as-probe revealed potentials for writing feedback mechanisms and interactive storytelling applications.

Conclusion: Participatory AI combined with adversarial design and cultural probing offers a robust framework for critically evaluating creative writing tools. Narratological structures can usefully scaffold AI-assisted writing, but must be flexibly adapted to diverse traditions and formats. Exposing narrative plans and enabling iterative refinement can support creativity if implemented with sensitivity to writer autonomy.

Abstract: We design and evaluate Fabula, an interactive app for fiction writers. Fabula uses detailed narrative plans informed by general narratological theory. Stories are structured hierarchically into scenes and beats that can be (re)generated and revised at script and story plan level. Using participatory AI, we critically evaluate and improve Fabula with casual and published writers, via design interviews and writing sessions with 42 experts, and large-scale internal and external testing. We interrogate our design choices: (1) whether a language model-based auto-evaluator, optimized on human experts' preferences, can improve story quality, (2) whether users want UI that exposes the detailed narrative plan alongside the story script, (3) to what extent our narratology assumptions fit localised storytelling traditions and serve screenwriters or playwrights, and (4) whether convergent iteration over the story plan supports writers' creativity. Building on critical feedback and concerns, we use Fabula as a cultural probe in adversarial design, and identify potentials for writing feedback and for interactive storytelling.

</details>


### [246] [The Self-Aware Body: A User-Centered Framework for Designing Therapeutic Sonic Interactions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14664)
*Prithvi Ravi Kantan,Sofia Dahl,Erika G. Spaich*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: A framework for designing movement sonification technologies for motor rehabilitation is proposed, comprising a conceptual reframing around sonic variability calibration, a practical audio-mixing-inspired design platform, and a user-centered methodology from healthcare intervention science. The HearWalk system for hemiparetic gait rehabilitation serves as an illustrative case, with discussion of where LLMs/AI can and cannot assist the design process.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Despite evidence for effectiveness, movement sonification technologies remain under-adopted in clinical practice due to the absence of standardized development methodologies and insufficient integration of clinical stakeholder perspectives into interaction design.

Method: The framework offers three interconnected contributions: (1) conceptual reframing of design as calibration of sonic variability to listener perceptual affordances and clinical context demands; (2) a practical design platform modeled on professional audio mixing workflows, imposing structured signal-flow architecture for rapid iterative exploration; (3) a user-centered development methodology adapted from healthcare intervention science, grounding design in engagement with clinicians and patients. The HearWalk biofeedback system for hemiparetic gait rehabilitation demonstrates application of the framework.

Result: The framework was instantiated through the HearWalk system for hemiparetic gait rehabilitation. Additionally, the chapter analyzes where large language models and AI tools can meaningfully assist each stage of the design process and where human clinical/perceptual expertise remains irreplaceable.

Conclusion: The proposed framework addresses barriers to clinical adoption of movement sonification technologies through structured, stakeholder-grounded design. While AI and LLMs can assist various design stages, human clinical and perceptual expertise remains essential and irreplaceable in key aspects of the process.

Abstract: This chapter presents a framework for designing therapeutic sonic interaction technologies, with a focus on movement sonification: the real-time conversion of bodily motion into sound that serves as feedback during motor rehabilitation. Despite growing evidence for their effectiveness, technologies implementing movement sonification are yet to be systematically adopted as part of clinical practice, potentially due to a lack of standardized development methodologies as well as inadequate integration of clinical stakeholder perspectives into interaction design. The framework addresses these barriers through three interconnected contributions. The first is a conceptual reframing of the design task as the calibration of sonic variability to the perceptual affordances of the listener and the demands of the clinical context. The second is a practical design platform inspired by professional audio mixing workflows, which imposes a structured and learnable signal-flow architecture on the interaction design process and enables rapid iterative exploration. The third is a user-centered development methodology adapted from healthcare intervention science, which grounds design decisions in engagement with the clinicians and patients who will use the resulting systems. The HearWalk biofeedback system for hemiparetic gait rehabilitation illustrates the framework, and the chapter concludes by examining where large language models and AI tools can meaningfully assist each stage of this design process, as well as where human clinical and perceptual expertise remains irreplaceable.

</details>
